                                 DEDICATION




For the men who let me sit at their tables before I had anything to bring to them,
and who kept a chair open long after they'd stopped needing to.
For my wife, who has watched me try to become the man in these pages for a long
time now, and who has never once pretended I'd already arrived. Every good
sentence here was lived in front of her first, and she is the reason most of them are
true.
And for our children, who are still watching. I hope you find your father in here,
and I hope you find him better than he was when you were small.

2
INTRODUCTION




Introduction
   I have spent most of my adult life sitting across tables from men. Kitchen tables,
mostly, and the ones at the back of a church fellowship hall, and the tailgates and
truck cabs and breakfast counters that serve the same purpose in this part of the
country. Some of the men were young enough to be my sons and some were old
enough to be my father, and I have come to believe that underneath the different
jobs and different decades and different troubles, they were all asking me the same
thing. They wanted to know what a man is, and whether they were one, and if not,
whether it was too late.
  This book is my long answer to that question. It is not the only answer and I
won't pretend it is, but it is the one I have watched hold up in the lives of real men
over a lot of years, and I have tried to write it the way I would say it to you if you
were sitting across from me with your hands around a cup of coffee going cold.

Who I'm writing to

  I am writing to a man in every season of life, and I mean that literally, so let me
name you.
   You may be twenty-four and single and not sure anyone has ever looked at you
and called you a man out loud. You may be twenty-nine with a baby who does not
sleep and a wife you love and cannot seem to reach anymore through the
exhaustion. You may be forty-four with three kids and a career at full throttle and a
growing sense that you are providing everything for your family except yourself.
You may be divorced, seeing your children every other weekend, sitting in an
apartment you never expected to live in and wondering whether a book like this is
even for you. You may have just watched the last child leave and found yourself
across a quiet kitchen from a woman you're not sure you know anymore. You may
have turned in a badge or a hard hat or a set of keys last year and be wondering what
a man does with his mornings now. You may have buried your wife.
   You are all in this room, and I'm not going to pretend otherwise by writing only
to the one with the mortgage and the minivan. The counsel in this book changes as



Introduction                                                                             3
your season changes, and when it does, I'll turn toward you inside the paragraph
and say so. What I won't do is stop every chapter to run down the list by age,
because you'd start skipping and so would I. In chapter 32 we'll sit down and talk
about seasons on purpose, all of them, in one place. Until then, trust that I know
you're here.

What I'm claiming

    The center of this book is one sentence. I didn't invent it, and I'm not going to
pretend I did. Robert Lewis gave it that shape years ago, and men have been
handing it to one another ever since, and I've never found a better way to say it. But
I have held it up against my own life for a long time, and against the lives of
hundreds of men in every season I just named, and I have never found a place where
it lies.
  A real man rejects passivity, accepts responsibility, leads courageously, and
expects the greater reward, God's reward.
   Those are the four marks, and chapter 3 will explain them, and chapters 4
through 7 will take them one at a time. For now I only want you to notice what
kind of sentence it is, because it isn't a sentence about what a man feels. There is a
whole shelf of books for men that start inside the chest, with your heart and your
longings and your wild places, and some of those books were good to me when I
was young, and I'm not writing against them. But this book makes a plainer claim.
The four marks are things you do, on ordinary days, and they show. They leave a
mark on a man the way a life of work leaves a mark on his hands, and you can see
them or you can see their absence, and no amount of feeling makes up for either.

How the book is built

   The book has five parts and they move in a particular order, and it helps to know
the order before you start.
   Part 1 lays out the four marks and takes each one in turn. Part 2 is about what
you inherited, the chair at the end of your childhood table and what sat in it, and
about what is yours to do with that inheritance now, including the parts of your
trouble that have nothing to do with your father and everything to do with you.
Part 3 goes back to the beginning of the whole human story and shows you where
you sit inside it, because a man who only knows his own story will read it wrong.


4                                                                             Introduction
Part 4 is about the house: the woman, the bed, the daughter, the son. Part 5 is about
the long road, the forty or fifty years of work and feeling and season and obscurity
that make up most of a man's life, and about how to finish it.
   Running through all five parts is one ordinary day. Chapter 1 is called "A Quiet
Tuesday," and the reason is that I have come to believe a man's life is decided almost
entirely on days like that, days with nothing special in them, when nobody's
watching and nothing seems to be at stake. It isn't decided at the altar, or at the
retreat, or at the ceremony, but on Tuesday, and every chapter in this book will land
at some point on a Tuesday-sized picture, and I make no apology for it.

The four lines at the end

   Every chapter ends the same way, with four short lines, and each line comes with
a plain sentence about why it matters for that particular chapter. I want to explain
them once here so I don't have to keep explaining them.
   Name it asks you to say out loud, in your own words, what the chapter touched
in you. Men are good at nodding along and terrible at saying "that's me," and
nothing changes in a man until he says it.
  Do this week gives you one thing to do before next Tuesday, not a program and
not a list, but one thing, and if you do only that one thing from each chapter, you'll
have done more than most men do with most books.
   Tell a brother asks you to say the thing out loud to another man. A mark you
keep to yourself never hardens into anything. If you don't have a brother yet,
chapter 13 is for you, and it is going to ask you for about twenty minutes a week,
which most men can find and most men have never been asked for. Until you get
there, telling a brother can mean writing it down where you'll see it and telling God,
who has heard worse.
   Carry this hands you one line to take with you into the week.
   One more thing about the four lines, because I know how a tired man reads a
book like this. You're going to get to a chapter having not done the last chapter's
thing. When that happens, don't skip it in shame and don't quit the book. Tell your
brother you didn't do it, which is itself the "tell a brother" line, and do it this week
instead. Every man in these pages has missed a week. The ones who changed are the
ones who said so and went again.



Introduction                                                                           5
The men you'll meet

   You're going to meet a number of men in these pages by name: Roy, who turned
in a state trooper's badge; Tomás, who has a baby who doesn't sleep; Kenny, who's
divorced and calls his son every Tuesday; Micah, who sat at the single men's table at
weddings for six years; and others. They are composites, which means the
conversations happened, at real tables and with real men, but the names and the
details have been mixed together so that no one can be picked out of a lineup, and
so that you might recognize yourself in one of them without recognizing anyone
else.

One more thing before you start

   I'm not going to ask you to join a program, buy a workbook, or attend anything.
I'm going to ask you to read, to do one thing a week, and to find one other man to
do it with. That's the whole ask. It is small on purpose, because I've watched too
many men leave a conference on fire and be back in the recliner by the following
Tuesday, and I've come to believe that the small steady thing outlasts the big loud
one every time.
    So find your cup of coffee. Pull up a chair. Let me tell you about a Tuesday.




6                                                                           Introduction
                     PART 1


         The man you were built to be




Part 1                                  7
8   A Quiet Tuesday
CHAPTER 1



A Quiet Tuesday
   Part 1: The man you were built to be
   If you had asked me at twenty-five to name the days that would decide what kind
of man I became, I would have given you a list of big ones: the wedding, the day the
first child came, the funeral of a father, the job I took or the job I turned down, the
morning I stood in a river and let a pastor put me under. I would have told you
those were the hinges, and I would have believed it, and I would have been mostly
wrong.
   I've had all of those days now, and I can tell you that not one of them decided
much of anything. They marked things and they announced things, and they were
stones on a riverbank that I can point to and say, that's when. But the deciding
happened somewhere else, in a long string of days I couldn't tell apart if you paid
me, and the more men I sit with, the more I'm convinced it works that way for
every one of us.
   A man's life is decided on Tuesdays. I mean that almost literally. Not on the
Sunday when he stands up in church, and not on the Saturday when he takes his
son to the lease, and not even on the Friday when the paycheck lands or the
Monday when the week's trouble starts. On the day in the middle, with nothing
special in it. The day nobody remembers. The day he comes home tired and the
house is loud and there's a conversation he could have or not have, a call he could
make or not make, a battery he could change or walk under one more time. That's
where it happens. That's where the marks get made.

What a Tuesday looks like

   Let me show you one, because a claim like that ought to be able to survive a
specific day.


A Quiet Tuesday                                                                       9
   A man I'll call Anthony left for work at 6:10 on a Tuesday in October, having
slept badly, because his wife had said something the night before about money that
wasn't quite fair and he'd let it sit, and it was still sitting there in the truck with him
as he drove. He got to the shop, and a screw stripped on the first job of the
morning, and he stood there breathing hard for a minute while his apprentice
pretended not to see. Around ten a customer called to complain about something
that wasn't Anthony's fault, and Anthony was polite to him in a way that cost
something. At lunch he ate in the truck and looked at his phone for forty minutes
and couldn't have told you afterward one thing he'd read.
   In the afternoon his father called. His father calls maybe twice a year, and every
time the phone shows that name Anthony feels nine years old for a second, and this
time he let it ring through to voicemail and told himself he'd call back later. He
finished the day's work well. He is good at his job. He drove home listening to
nothing, pulled into the garage, and stood in the doorway between the garage and
the kitchen for a minute the way he does most nights, hearing his kids be loud and
his wife tell somebody to sit down, not sure why he wasn't walking in.
   Then he walked in. He kissed his wife on the side of her head without looking at
her, and he ate, and he got the kids to bed, more or less, and by 8:40 he was in the
recliner with the game on and his phone in his hand, and his wife was in the
kitchen, and neither of them said anything about the money, and around ten he
went to bed and lay there a while before he slept.
    That's a Tuesday, and there's nothing in it you'd put in a book, and I've just put
it in a book, because I want you to see how much of a man's life was actually
decided in those sixteen hours.
   The thing he let sit from the night before was a decision. Letting his father go to
voicemail was a decision. The doorway was a decision, and so was the recliner, and
so was the kiss on the side of her head without looking, and so was the phone at
lunch and the phone at 8:40. None of them felt like decisions. That's the whole
point. They felt like a day. And a man who has ten thousand days like that will
become a particular kind of man without ever once choosing to, because the
choosing was happening the whole time, in pieces too small to notice.
   Now, Anthony's a good man. I want to be clear about that, because the point of
this book isn't to make good men feel like failures. He didn't sin against anybody on
that Tuesday, not in any way you could name. He worked hard and provided and
came home and was gentle enough. But he is becoming something, one Tuesday at


10                                                                            A Quiet Tuesday
a time, and if you asked his wife what he's becoming she could tell you, and it isn't
what he'd choose if he thought he was choosing.

Why the big days can't do it

   Here's why I've stopped trusting the big days to make men. A big day is a burst.
It gathers up everything you feel and believe and want to be, and it puts it all on the
table at once, in front of witnesses, with music playing, and that's a good thing,
because God builds days like that into a man's life on purpose, and later in this book
I'm going to tell you to make one for your son. But a burst is not a shape. It doesn't
leave a mark by itself, because a mark is what gets left on you by what you do over
and over, and a big day, by definition, is something you do once.
   Think about your hands. If you've worked with them, they have marks on them:
a ridge of callus across the palm, a finger that doesn't straighten all the way, a scar
from a fence stretcher that slipped. Nobody got those from one hard day. You got
them from a thousand ordinary ones, none of which you remember. The big days
of your working life, the first day and the last and the one when you got hurt, didn't
shape your hands. The ordinary Tuesdays did.
   The men who go to a conference and come home on fire and are back in the
recliner within two weeks aren't weak men. They've just misunderstood where a life
gets made. They put everything into the burst and nothing into the shape, and the
shape is where the marks come from.
  And there's another reason the big days can't do it, which is that on the big days
you're watched. At a wedding, at a baptism, at a ceremony, at a funeral, you know
what a man is supposed to do and everyone's looking, and most men do it. That
doesn't tell you much about the man. What tells you about the man is what he does
when the room is empty, when nobody would know if he made the call or didn't,
opened the drawer or didn't, said the true thing or let it sit. Tuesday is when
nobody's watching. That's exactly why it counts.

What God does with Tuesdays

   If you've been in church a while, you've probably absorbed the idea that the
spiritual life happens in the big moments too, at the retreat and the revival and the
altar, and God does meet men in those places; I'm not going to pretend He doesn't.
But look at how the Bible actually talks about the way a man is formed.


A Quiet Tuesday                                                                         11
     Luke 16:10 (NIV)
     Whoever can be trusted with very little can also be trusted with much, and whoever is dishonest with
     very little will also be dishonest with much.



    Very little is a Tuesday phrase if there ever was one. Jesus is saying that the man
who can be trusted in the small unwatched things is the man who can be trusted in
the large watched ones, and that you don't get the second man without the first. He
is saying that the recliner and the voicemail and the kiss without looking are not
neutral. They're the training ground. They are where the man who'll one day stand
in a hard room gets made, or doesn't. And when Jesus tells a story about a man who
built a house that held, notice what He says about how the man built it.

     Matthew 7:24 (NIV)
     Therefore everyone who hears these words of mine and puts them into practice is like a wise man who
     built his house on the rock.



   Puts them into practice. Not hears them and is moved by them. Practice is a
Tuesday word too. It's what you do again and again until it's how you do things.
The wise man in that story didn't build his house in a burst on a big day. He built it
the way houses get built, one course at a time, on days nobody photographed, and
when the rain came the house held because of all those days and not because of any
one of them.
  Most of a man's walk with God is Tuesdays. Most of his marriage is Tuesdays.
Most of his fathering is Tuesdays. If you want to know what kind of man you're
becoming, don't look at how you did at the retreat. Look at last Tuesday.

The other Tuesday

   Let me give you Anthony's Tuesday again, a year later, because I want you to see
that I'm not describing a trap. I'm describing a place where a man has more power
than he thinks.
   He left at 6:10 again, and he'd slept badly again, and there was something from
the night before sitting in the truck with him again. But somewhere on the drive he
said, out loud, "Father, I don't want to carry this all day," and then he pulled over at
a gas station and sent his wife a text that said he was sorry he'd gone quiet, and he'd


12                                                                                           A Quiet Tuesday
like to talk about it after the kids were down. It took him ninety seconds. He was
still tired. But the thing wasn't sitting in the truck with him anymore.
   The screw still stripped. He still stood there breathing hard, but this time he
walked out to the truck, waited a minute and a half for his body to come down, and
came back and finished the job. His father called in the afternoon, and Anthony felt
nine years old for a second, and then he answered, and it was an awkward six
minutes about nothing, and it was the first time in two years he hadn't let it go to
voicemail.
   When he came home he stood in the doorway for a second out of habit and then
he noticed he was doing it and walked in. He put his phone on the counter face
down, which he'd started doing a few months before after another man told him to,
and he found his wife and looked at her when he kissed her. After the kids were
down they sat at the kitchen counter for ten minutes and talked about the money,
and it wasn't a good conversation exactly, but it was one, and he laid down
something he'd wanted to say and said something truer instead. Then he did the
dishes. Then he prayed, badly and briefly, with his hand on her back. Then he went
to bed and slept.
   Nothing about that day would make a book either. His daughter, who is seven
and who watches his face when her mother walks in, would not be able to tell you
what was different. But something was different, and it was different in about six
places, each of which took less than two minutes, and if you gave that man ten
thousand of those Tuesdays instead of the other kind, you would end up with a
different man, and a different marriage, and a different girl watching a different
face.
  That's the claim of this book. Not that you need a big day. That you need to
know what a man does on a Tuesday, and then do it, and then do it again.

The same Tuesday at every age

   A Tuesday doesn't look the same at nineteen as it does at seventy, but it decides
the same things, and I want you to see yourself in one of these four men before we
go on.
  A nineteen-year-old I'll call Eli works the counter at a parts store and lives with
two roommates. His Tuesday has a moment in it around ten at night when the
apartment goes quiet and the phone is in his hand, and what he does in that


A Quiet Tuesday                                                                         13
moment is deciding what kind of man he'll be at thirty far more than anything he'll
say at church on Sunday. He told me once that he thought his real life would start
when something big happened to him. His real life is the ten o'clock moment, and
it's happening now.
   Tomás, who you'll meet more later, is twenty-nine with a baby who doesn't
sleep. His Tuesday has a moment in it at two in the morning when he hears the
crying and hears his wife start to move and could simply lie there, and he's so tired
that lying there feels like the only reasonable thing a man could do. Whether he gets
up is deciding the marriage. Not the anniversary trip they can't afford; the two
o'clock.
   Walt is fifty-five and built a business from one truck to eleven, and his Tuesday
has a moment in it at 6:15 in the evening when he could leave the shop and be home
for dinner or could stay another hour because the shop is quieter than the house.
He stayed for twenty years, one Tuesday at a time, and never once decided to. The
staying decided itself, in pieces too small to notice, until his wife said a sentence in
the kitchen that I'll tell you about in chapter 5.
   And Roy is seventy-two, retired from thirty-one years as a state trooper, and his
Tuesday has a moment in it at 5:30 in the morning when the alarm goes off and
there's no badge to put on and no reason anyone would notice if he slept until nine.
What he does at 5:30 is deciding whether his last twenty years will be a long slow
exit or the most important stretch of his life. He gets up and makes the coffee.
You'll see why that matters.
  Four men, four ages, four moments that don't feel like decisions. That's the
whole book, and that's why it starts here.

What comes next

   In two chapters I'm going to give you a sentence with four marks in it, and then
I'm going to spend the rest of the book showing you what those marks look like on
a Tuesday, in every season a man can be in, at every table I've sat at. But before I do
that I need to say one thing to the man who's reading this and suspecting that
everything he's been told about being a man is either contradictory or thin or both.
That man is not crazy. That's the next chapter.
  For now, do one thing. Think about last Tuesday. Not the highlight of your
week. The ordinary day in the middle of it. Walk through it hour by hour in your


14                                                                        A Quiet Tuesday
head, the way I walked you through Anthony's, and notice how many small
decisions were in it that didn't feel like decisions. That's your life, and that's where
the marks are being made. The good news, and it is very good news, is that it's also
where they can be remade, starting tomorrow, which is probably a Tuesday.

Before you turn the page

   I don't know which of those four you are, or whether you're somewhere in
between, but I know this about your week. It has a Tuesday in it, and the Tuesday
has a moment in it, probably several, where you'll choose without feeling like you're
choosing. I'm not going to ask you to be a different man by Sunday. I'm going to
ask you to see the moment, this once, and to do one small thing in it differently, and
to trust that the Father who made you sees the moment too and is glad when you
meet it. May He give you eyes for the ordinary day, and the small strength it takes to
walk through a doorway instead of standing in it.




A Quiet Tuesday                                                                            15
16   A Quiet Tuesday
CHAPTER 2



You Are Not Crazy
   Part 1: The man you were built to be
    A man in his early thirties sat across from me at a diner a few years ago and said
something I've heard in one form or another from men of every age since. He said,
"I feel like I've been handed six different instruction manuals for being a man and
every one of them contradicts the other five, and I'm supposed to act like that
makes sense."
   He wasn't angry when he said it. He was tired, in the particular way a man gets
tired when he's been trying to hit a target that keeps moving. He'd been told to be
strong and to be vulnerable. He'd been told to lead and to get out of the way. He'd
been told that his instincts as a man were dangerous and that his failure to act on
them was the problem with his marriage. He'd been told by one voice that
manhood was a costume the culture put on boys, and by another that it was the
most important thing about him, and by a third that it was whatever he wanted it to
be, and by a fourth that it had been settled long ago and he was simply failing at it.
He'd sat in churches that treated him as a problem to be managed and in locker
rooms that treated him as an appetite to be fed, and somewhere in all of it he'd
started to wonder whether the confusion was in the world or in him.
   I told him what I'll tell you. The confusion is in the world. You are not crazy.

The picture you were handed

   Every man alive today was handed a picture of manhood that doesn't hold
together, and most of us have spent a lot of energy trying to make it hold together
anyway, and blaming ourselves when it wouldn't. I want to spend this chapter
looking at the picture plainly, so you can stop blaming yourself for the fact that it
doesn't work.


You Are Not Crazy                                                                        17
    Start with what you got from home. Chapter 8 will go deep on this, but for now
it's enough to say that whatever sat in the chair at the end of your childhood table
taught you a picture of a man before you could read, and for most of us it was a
partial picture at best. A father who was there and silent taught you that a man is a
presence without words. A father who was gone taught you that a man is an
absence with a name. A father who was a storm taught you that a man is a weather
system to be read. Even a good father taught you a picture with gaps in it shaped
exactly like the places he couldn't reach. So you started with a fragment, and then
you went out into the world to fill it in.
   The world had plenty to offer, none of it consistent. The culture at large told
you, more or less, that manhood is a problem. That the things in you that want to
build and compete and protect and take charge are the residue of something the
species should have outgrown. That your strength is suspect and your silence is
suspect and your anger is proof, and that the best thing you can do as a man is
become less of one. Then that same culture turned around and mocked men who
were passive, who couldn't commit, who wouldn't grow up, who lived in
basements, and it never once seemed to notice it had asked for exactly that.
   The entertainment you grew up on gave you a man who was either a fool, a
brute, or a lone hero who needed no one and answered to nothing. The fool was the
father in every sitcom, well-meaning and useless, managed by his wife and
outsmarted by his kids. The brute was the villain, or sometimes the hero, and the
difference was mostly whose side he was on. The lone hero was the one every boy
wanted to be, and the trouble with him is that he has no wife who's tired, no father
who calls twice a year, no son who won't talk, no church, no brothers, and no
Tuesdays. He is a man with no house, and you were being trained to admire him
while you were living in one.
    The marketplace told you a man is what he earns and drives and owns. The
internet told you a man is what he watches and how much of it. Some corners of
the church told you a man is a spiritual leader, and then gave you a picture of
leadership so soft and so vague that you couldn't have told a grown man what to
actually do on a Tuesday if your life depended on it. Other corners told you a man
is a warrior and a king, and gave you a picture so loud and so wild that it had no
room in it for a man with a mortgage and a sick mother and a job at a wastewater
plant.
     And then there's the shelf of books for men, and I want to be careful here


18                                                                      You Are Not Crazy
because some of those books were kind to me when I was young. Many of them
start inside the chest. They tell you about your heart, your longings, your wildness,
the adventure you were made for. Some of that is true. But a great many men have
read those books and felt every wild thing they promised and gone home and let
their wives carry the whole house alone, because what's in a man's chest doesn't
leave a mark on his life by itself. Only what he does leaves a mark. A picture of
manhood that lives entirely in the interior is a picture with no Tuesdays in it, and a
man can't live in a picture with no Tuesdays.
    Put all of that together, the fragment from home and the six contradictory
manuals from the world, and you have the picture most men are trying to live up to.
It's no wonder you couldn't live up to it, because nobody could, and the reason is
that it was never a picture at all. It's a pile.

What the confusion does to a man

   I've watched the pile do three things to men, and you've probably felt all three.
   It makes a man wait. When you don't know what a man is supposed to do, the
safest thing is to do nothing and see what happens. A lot of male passivity, which
we'll spend a whole chapter on, isn't laziness at all. It's a man standing very still
because every direction he could move has been declared wrong by somebody. He
isn't on the tailgate because he's lazy. He's on the tailgate because he's confused and
he's decided that confused men shouldn't drive.
   It makes a man perform. If you can't find the real target, you learn to hit
whatever target the room in front of you seems to want. You're one man at church
and another at work and another with your buddies and another with your wife,
and none of them is quite you, and you're exhausted from the costume changes. A
performer isn't a liar, exactly. He's a man who never got a stable picture and so
learned to borrow one from whoever was watching.
   And it makes a man quietly ashamed. This is the one I see most. A man who's
been handed a contradictory picture and can't live up to it doesn't usually conclude
that the picture is broken. He concludes that he is. He carries a low hum of failure
under everything, a sense that other men must have figured out what he can't, and
he doesn't talk about it because talking about it would confirm it. The man at the
diner had that hum. Most of the men I sit with do, and it took me a long time to
realize that it wasn't a sign of anything wrong with them. It was the sound of a good


You Are Not Crazy                                                                      19
man trying to obey six manuals at once.

What's actually true

     Here is what I want to say to that hum, and I'll say it slowly.
   You are not crazy for sensing that manhood is a real thing and not a costume. It is
a real thing, and God made it. In the first chapter of the Bible He made human
beings in His image, male and female, on purpose, and He called it good before
anyone had done anything with it. Your manhood is not a problem to be solved or a
residue to be outgrown. It's a way of carrying the image of God that was His idea,
and the instinct in you that says it matters is right.
   You are not crazy for sensing that most of what you've been told about it doesn't
hold together, because it doesn't. You were handed a pile, and you've been trying to
build a house out of it, and the fact that the house keeps falling down is not
evidence against you. It's evidence against the pile.
   You are not crazy for wanting somebody to just tell you, plainly, what a man
does. That's the right want. It's the want of every boy who ever looked at his father
and the want of every man who ever sat across a table from an older one and asked a
real question. The wanting is not weakness; it's the design, because men were meant
to be handed a picture by other men, and most of us weren't, and we've been
guessing ever since.
   And you are not crazy for suspecting that the answer, when it comes, is going to
be simpler and harder than anything on the shelf. Simpler, because it can be said in
one sentence. Harder, because the sentence has to be lived on Tuesdays, and no
feeling in your chest and no burst on a big day can do that for you.

The pile at every age

  The contradiction lands differently depending on where you're standing, and I
want you to see that you aren't alone in it, whatever your age.
   At seventeen a boy I know is being told by his school that his restlessness is a
disorder, by his phone that a man is whatever gets the most attention, by his youth
pastor that a man is a servant leader, and by the older boys that a man is whoever's
had the most girls. He's trying to be all of that at once and he thinks the fact that he
can't is a problem with him. It isn't. He's a boy standing under a pile, and the pile is


20                                                                       You Are Not Crazy
the problem.
  At thirty a young father is being told to be present and to provide, to lead his
home and to defer to his wife's instincts, to be strong for his newborn and to be in
touch with his own feelings about becoming a father, all in the same week, all by
people who mean well. He's failing at most of it because it can't all be done, and the
hum of failure under his exhaustion is not evidence that he's a bad father. It's the
sound of a good man trying to obey six manuals with a baby on his shoulder.
  At forty-eight a man has quietly given up on the question. He's decided that
manhood is whatever he happens to be doing, which is mostly working and coming
home tired, and he's stopped listening to anyone who claims to know what a man is
because every one of them has contradicted the last one. His resignation looks like
peace, and it isn't peace. It's a man who set down the pile and sat on it.
   At seventy a man who's lived by a picture handed to him in the 1960s is watching
that picture get mocked on every screen his grandchildren look at, and he's begun to
wonder whether the whole thing he built his life on was a mistake. It wasn't. Some
of what he was handed was true and some of it wasn't, like every generation's
picture, and the true parts are still true. He needs somebody to sort it with him, not
somebody to tell him it was all wrong.
   Four men under the same pile. None of them is crazy, and none of them is alone.

What this book is going to do about it

   I'm going to hand you a picture. Not the whole thing at once; it'll take the rest of
the book to fill in. But the frame of it is one sentence, and I'll give you the sentence
in the next chapter and then spend four chapters taking it apart piece by piece so
you can see how each piece looks on a man in the middle of an ordinary week.
   The sentence doesn't start in your chest. It starts with what you do. It's the kind
of picture a boy could hold up against his own father and a man could hold up
against his own week and get a straight answer. It has room in it for the man with
the mortgage and the sick mother and the job at the plant, and for the single man at
the back table, and for the divorced man in the apartment, and for the old man who
just turned in his badge. It doesn't require you to feel wild or to feel anything in
particular. It requires you to get up.
   And it's older than any of the manuals you were handed. Men have been living it



You Are Not Crazy                                                                     21
for a very long time in every century and every kind of house, and the reason it has
survived is that it's true, and true things survive. You weren't given it because the
chain of men who were supposed to hand it to you got broken somewhere back
there, and nobody's fault is the point right now. The point is that the chain can be
picked up.
     I want to say one more thing to the man at the diner, and to you if you're him.
   The tiredness you feel isn't the tiredness of a man who's failed. It's the tiredness
of a man who's been carrying a contradiction. When you set the contradiction
down, which is what this chapter has been trying to help you do, you're going to
find that you have more strength than you thought, because you've been spending
most of it holding up a pile. Set it down and let the pieces fall, and then come with
me to the next chapter and pick up something that holds.

Before you turn the page

   If the hum has been under your life for a long time, I'd like you to let it get
quieter tonight, not because you've solved anything but because you've seen the pile
for what it is. You're a man who was handed a contradiction and carried it honestly,
and that isn't failure; it's strength spent on the wrong load. Set it down. The next
chapter hands you something that holds, and I've watched it hold for men of every
age you just read about. May God give you rest from carrying what was never yours
to carry, and the courage to pick up what is.




22                                                                       You Are Not Crazy
You Are Not Crazy   23
24   The Four Marks
CHAPTER 3



The Four Marks
   Part 1: The man you were built to be
   You made it through the last chapter and I hope you set the pile down. If you
did, you've got a harder problem now than the one you started with, because a man
who knows the picture he was handed is broken still has to answer the question
underneath it. If that isn't what a man is, then what is?
  I want to answer that in this chapter, in one sentence you can carry in your
pocket, and then I want to spend the next four chapters taking the sentence apart so
you can see how each piece looks on a man in the middle of an ordinary week. But
before I give you the sentence, I want to give you a picture, because the picture is
what keeps the sentence from becoming one more thing on the shelf.

What a mark is

   My grandfather's hands looked like a map of every job he ever worked. There was
a white ridge across his left palm from a fence stretcher that slipped in 1961, and his
right index finger never straightened all the way after something happened to it that
he never explained. The knuckles were wide and the skin over them was thick in the
way skin gets when a man works outside for fifty years. You didn't have to ask him
what he'd done with his life. It was written on him, and anyone who knew how to
read hands could read it.
    That is what a mark is, and I want you to hold onto the word, because it's going
to do a lot of work in this book. A mark isn't a feeling you have about yourself, and
it isn't a title somebody gives you. A mark is what gets left on you by what you've
done over and over again for a long time. My grandfather didn't get that callus from
thinking about a shovel, or from wanting to be the kind of man who used one, or
from going to a weekend event about shovels. He got it from the shovel, on ten


The Four Marks                                                                       25
thousand days that he couldn't have told apart.
   I say all that first because, as I said last chapter, there's a whole shelf of books for
men that start inside the chest, and I want you to see from the first page how this
one is different. Those books will tell you about your heart and your desires and
your longing for something wild, and some of that is true, and some of those men
were good to me when I was younger. I'm not writing against them. But this book
makes a plainer claim. The marks I'm about to give you are not things you feel.
They are things you do, and they show. A man could sit in a deer blind at dawn and
feel every wild thing a book ever promised him, and drive home and let his wife
carry the entire house alone, and what was in his chest that morning would leave no
mark on anything. What he did when he got home would.
  So here is the sentence. I didn't invent it, and I want to say so plainly. Robert
Lewis put it in these words years ago, teaching men what a man is, and the sentence
has been passed from man to man ever since because it holds. I've held it up against
my own life for a long time now, and against the lives of men in every season I can
name, and I have never found a place where it lies.
  A real man rejects passivity, accepts responsibility, leads courageously, and
expects the greater reward, God's reward.




26                                                                            The Four Marks
   Read it again, slower, and notice what kind of sentence it is. It names four marks,
not four moods and not four qualities you either have or don't, but four things a
man does, which over time leave four things you can see on him the way you could
see the fence stretcher on my grandfather's palm.

The first mark: he rejects passivity

   Every Texas town I've ever spent time in has a man on a tailgate. He is not a bad
man, and I want to be fair to him. He has opinions about the drought and the
school board and the way the city runs the water department, and he'll share all of
them with you while the work he came to do waits behind him. He's waiting for the
right time, or the right price, or the right feeling, or some sign that hasn't come, and
he has been waiting so long that the waiting has started to look like wisdom to him
and to the men who sit with him.
  Passivity is the default setting of a man. It isn't something most of us choose so
much as something we fall into, the way a truck tire falls into a rut in a caliche road
and stays there because staying there is easier than climbing out. And what makes it
dangerous is that it almost never looks like laziness. It looks like patience. It looks



The Four Marks                                                                        27
like keeping the peace. It sounds like "I didn't want to overstep," and the man saying
it usually believes he's being humble. The first man in the Bible was passive in
exactly this way, and the text goes out of its way to make sure we notice. When the
serpent came into the garden and started twisting the one command God had given,
the command the man alone had heard, the man was there.

     Genesis 3:6 (NIV)
     When the woman saw that the fruit of the tree was good for food and pleasing to the eye, and also
     desirable for gaining wisdom, she took some and ate it. She also gave some to her husband, who was
     with her, and he ate it.



   He was with her. He didn't grab the fruit and he didn't argue for it. He simply
stood there while the thing he'd been told to guard against walked in and did its
work, and his standing there was enough to break the world. Those three words,
"who was with her," have been indicting men ever since, and they indict the man on
the tailgate as surely as they indicted Adam.
   Rejecting passivity doesn't mean becoming loud, or pushy, or the kind of man
who mistakes volume for strength. It means that when something needs doing and
you know it and you're the one standing there, you get off the tailgate. It means you
bring up the hard conversation instead of hoping it gets brought up. It means you
don't require a feeling in order to start. The next chapter is about this, and I'll show
you how it looks in a house with a baby in it and in a house where the kids have
gone and in an apartment where a man lives alone.

The second mark: he accepts responsibility

    The second mark is weight. I helped a friend move a gun safe one August, four
hundred pounds of steel down a narrow hallway in a hundred and four degrees, and
I learned something about responsibility that I've never forgotten. There is a
moment with a load like that when you're committed. You've lifted it, your friend
has lifted it, and the only way through is through. You can't set it down in the
hallway because there's no room. You can't decide halfway along that this isn't really
your safe. You carry it to where it's going, and then you set it down, and that's the
only order the thing can be done in.
  That's what responsibility feels like on a man. It's a weight he picks up and
doesn't set down in the hallway. And I want you to notice that I said he picks it up.


28                                                                                          The Four Marks
A boy waits for responsibility to be assigned to him, and then argues about whether
the assignment was fair. A man looks at what's in front of him and says "that's
mine," and he says it even when it isn't his fault and even when nobody would
blame him for walking past. The bill nobody wants to open is his. The kid nobody's
talking to is his. The apology nobody's making is his. The neighbor's fence that
blew down on his side of the line is his. A man reaches for the weight, and over time
the reaching leaves a mark on him. There's a line in Lamentations that surprised me
the first time I read it slowly enough to hear it.

  Lamentations 3:27 (NIV)
  It is good for a man to bear the yoke while he is young.



   It is good, the text says, and not merely tolerable, and not a necessary evil that
builds character. The weight is part of what shapes a man into a man, and a man
who has been protected from it or who has walked past it all his life is, in a way
that's hard to name and easy to see, unshaped. Chapter 5 will show you how to pick
weight up without being crushed by it, and how to tell the difference between the
weight God gave you and the weight you grabbed in order to feel important.

The third mark: he leads courageously

    Every March there's a stock tank somewhere in this state with a group of boys
standing on the bank. The water is cold, colder than any of them expected, and
everybody is waiting for somebody to go first. That is what leadership is. It isn't the
title or the corner office or being the one in charge. It's being the one who goes first
into the cold water so that the others can follow.
   Somebody has to go first, in every house and every crew and every church.
Somebody has to be the first one to say "I was wrong" in a marriage that's been quiet
for three days. Somebody has to be the first one to pray out loud at a table where
nobody does, the first one to call the brother who's been drifting, the first one to say
the true thing in the meeting where everybody's nodding. That's what courage is
for, and I want to be clear about what courage is, because men get it wrong in a way
that keeps them on the bank. Courage is not the absence of fear. If you weren't
afraid, it wouldn't take courage to move. Courage is what happens when a man is
afraid and puts his boots on anyway and walks.



The Four Marks                                                                         29
     Joshua 1:9 (NIV)
     Have I not commanded you? Be strong and courageous. Do not be afraid; do not be discouraged, for
     the LORD your God will be with you wherever you go.



   Notice that God doesn't tell Joshua to stop being afraid, as if fear were something
a man could switch off. He tells him what to do while he's afraid, and He tells him
why he can do it. Chapter 6 is about going first, and I think you'll find it's a more
ordinary thing than you've been led to believe.

The fourth mark: he expects the greater reward

  The last mark is the one that separates a good man from a godly one, and it's the
one most men skip, so I've put it last on purpose and I'll give it the most room.
    Pecan trees don't hurry. If you plant one this fall, you'll water it for years before
it gives you a single nut worth cracking, and it won't hit its stride for a decade or
more. Which means that every man who has ever planted a pecan orchard was
planting for somebody else. For a son, or a grandson, or a family that might sell the
place before the trees mature. You don't plant pecans because you intend to eat
pecans. You plant them because you believe in a harvest you won't hold in your
own hands, and you're willing to spend your strength on it anyway.
   That's the fourth mark. A real man expects the greater reward, God's reward, and
that expectation changes what he does with his Tuesdays more than anything else in
this book. He can do the right thing when nobody's looking, because he isn't
working for the people who are looking. He can stay married through a hard
decade, because he isn't measuring the marriage by this year's return. He can give
money that won't come back and time that won't be thanked, because he believes
what Jesus said about a Father who sees what's done in secret.

     Hebrews 11:6 (NIV)
     And without faith it is impossible to please God, because anyone who comes to him must believe that
     he exists and that he rewards those who earnestly seek him.



   He rewards, the writer says, and God isn't embarrassed by that word, and you
shouldn't be either. The man who doesn't expect a reward from God will go
looking for one somewhere else, and there are only so many places to look: applause,


30                                                                                           The Four Marks
money, women, a bigger truck, a boy's approval, his own father's approval. Chapter
7 is about living for a reward that lasts, and I've come to believe it's the chapter that
makes the other three possible.

Why four, and why these

   You could argue for a different list, and men have. Let me tell you why I've
stopped arguing.
   Each of these four marks answers a specific way that men break. Passivity is how
a man breaks by doing nothing. Blame and evasion are how he breaks by refusing
weight. Cowardice, which almost always travels under a nicer name, is how he
breaks by hanging back. And living for the wrong reward is how he breaks slowly,
over forty years, while looking successful to everyone around him. I have never met
a man in trouble whose trouble didn't trace back to one of those four. Not in a
marriage, not in a business, not in a church, and not in a jail cell.
   And I want you to notice the shape of the four together. The first three are
things a good man of any faith or none might do, and plenty of men reject passivity
and carry weight and go first without ever thinking about God, and I'm grateful for
every one of them. The fourth is what makes the other three Christian. It's the
reason a man can keep doing the first three when they cost him and nobody claps.
Take the fourth mark out and you have a self-improvement list, and
self-improvement lists run out of fuel. Leave it in and you have a way of life that
will outlast you.

A word about seasons, said once

  I want to say this here so I don't have to keep saying it, and so you'll trust me
when I don't.
   You're reading this in a particular season, and I know it. You may be twenty-four
and single, and nobody has ever looked at you and called you a man out loud. You
may have a four-month-old and not have slept a full night since spring. You may be
divorced and seeing your kids every other weekend and not at all sure this book is
for you. You may be sixty-eight and have turned in a badge or a hard hat or a set of
keys last year, and be wondering what a man does with his mornings now. You may
have buried your wife.



The Four Marks                                                                         31
   You're all in the room, and I'm going to write as if you are. When your season
actually changes what I'd tell you, I'll turn and tell you, right there in the paragraph,
without making you wait for a section with your age on it. I'm not going to stop
every chapter and run down the list, because you'd start skipping and so would I.
And in chapter 32 we'll sit down and talk about seasons on purpose, all of them, in
one place, because every one of them has a job in it and the culture has been lying to
you about which ones count.
   One more thing before we go on. The men you'll meet in these pages, Roy and
Tomás and Kenny and the others, are composites, which means the conversations
happened but the names and details are mixed so that no one can be picked out of a
lineup.

The marks at every age

   The four marks don't change with age, but the garden does, and I want you to
see them on four men before we take them one at a time.
   A young man of twenty-two rejects passivity when he stops waiting for a wife or
a career to make him serious and gets serious now, in an apartment with
roommates. He accepts responsibility when he carries his own life, the rent and the
doctor and the truth, without a father standing over him. He leads courageously
when he's the first of his friends to say a hard true thing, or to pray at a table, or to
ask a woman out plainly. And he expects the greater reward when he does all of it
without an audience, because nobody claps for a single man who lives right, and
he's learning that the Father does.
    A young father rejects passivity when he turns toward his exhausted wife inside
the chaos instead of waiting for things to calm down. He accepts responsibility
when he takes the two o'clock shift without being asked. He leads courageously
when he goes first into the apology, or the prayer over the crib, or the conversation
about money that nobody wants. And he expects the greater reward when he does it
all at three in the morning with nobody watching, because he believes God sees the
three o'clock.
   A man in his fifties rejects passivity when he notices that he's drifted toward the
place that claps for him and comes home. He accepts responsibility when he says
"that's mine" about the distance in his marriage instead of explaining it. He leads
courageously when he walks across a church parking lot to a younger man who


32                                                                          The Four Marks
won't ask. And he expects the greater reward when he lets go of the scoreboard he's
been measuring himself by for thirty years.
   An older man rejects passivity when he gets up at 5:30 with no badge to put on
and finds the work anyway. He accepts responsibility for a grandson's hills and a
widow's gutters and a wife he's finally studying. He leads courageously when he says
the sentence in his daughter's driveway that he's put off for a decade. And he
expects the greater reward when he plants trees he'll never eat from, because he
knows who's watching him dig.
   Same four marks, four different gardens. That's what I mean when I say every
season is in the room.

How to read the rest of this book

   Every chapter from here on ends the same way, with four short lines, and each
line will come with a plain sentence about why it matters for that chapter. Name it
asks you to say, in your own words and out loud, what the chapter touched. Do this
week gives you one thing to do before next Tuesday; not a program, one thing. Tell
a brother asks you to say it to another man, because a mark you keep secret never
hardens into anything. Carry this hands you one line to take with you.
   And if you reach a chapter having not done the last one's thing, which will
happen, don't skip it in shame; say so to your brother and do it this week. If you
don't have a brother yet, don't skip that third line. Chapter 13 is for you, and it's
going to ask you for twenty minutes a week. Until you get there, telling a brother
can mean writing it on the back of a receipt and telling God, who has heard worse
than anything you've got.
  Now we've got four marks and a lot of Tuesdays ahead of us. Let's look at each
one the way you'd look at a man's hands.

Before you turn the page

   You've got the sentence now, and I know it's a lot to be handed on one page.
Don't try to fix all four this week. Look at your own hands, so to speak, and find the
mark that's faintest, and hold that one for a while. The men I've watched change
didn't change all at once; they worked one mark on one Tuesday and then did it
again. May the Father who made you in His image show you the mark He wants to



The Four Marks                                                                          33
press into you first, and may He be patient with you while it takes, the way He's
been patient with me.




34                                                                      The Four Marks
CHAPTER 4



Stop Waiting
   Part 1: The man you were built to be
   There's a smoke detector in a hallway somewhere near you that's chirping right
now, one chirp every forty seconds or so, because the battery's low. The man of the
house has walked under it two hundred times in the last three weeks. He knows
exactly what it is and exactly where the nine-volt batteries are kept. He is a capable
man who has rebuilt a carburetor and negotiated a raise and coached a ten-year-old's
baseball team to a losing record with real grace, and he cannot bring himself to
spend the ninety seconds it would take to make that chirp stop.
  If you asked him why, he'd tell you he's going to get to it. That's the honest
answer, and it's also the whole problem.
   I don't care about the smoke detector. I care about what it's standing in for,
because that same man has a conversation with his wife that he's going to get to. He
has a call to his brother he's going to get to, a habit he's going to deal with, a debt
he's going to face, a son who's gone quiet that he's going to sit down with, a church
he's going to start showing up at again. He's going to get to all of it. He's been going
to get to it for years, and the years are the point.
   The first mark of a man is that he rejects passivity. This chapter is about what
that looks like on a Tuesday, because the word "passivity" makes most men picture
somebody lying on a couch, and that isn't the man I'm worried about. The man I'm
worried about is busy and tired, and he is working hard on nearly everything except
the handful of things that matter most, and he's waiting, and he doesn't know it.

What passivity wears

   Passivity almost never shows up in its own clothes. If it did, we'd recognize it and



Stop Waiting                                                                          35
be ashamed, and shame would move us. Instead it borrows the wardrobe of the
virtues, and it wears them convincingly.
    It wears patience, and says "I'm giving it time." Sometimes that's true, and time
really is the right tool. More often time is doing what the man is refusing to do, and
it's doing it badly, because time has no judgment.
   It wears peace, and says "I don't want to start something." So the thing that needs
starting never starts, and the peace he's keeping is the peace of a house where
nobody talks about anything that matters.
   It wears humility, and says "Who am I to say anything?" The answer is that you're
the husband, or the father, or simply the one who's standing there, and that is
exactly who.
    It wears spirituality, and says "I'm waiting on the Lord." I'll come back to this one
at length, because there is a real kind of waiting on God, and there is a counterfeit
that borrows His name as a doctor's note, and the difference between them decides
a great deal.
   It wears research, which is another book and another podcast and another
conversation about the thing instead of the thing itself. I've known men who read
six books on marriage and never once sat down across from their wife, asked her a
real question, and listened to the entire answer.
   And it wears busyness, which is the cleverest disguise of all. A man who's out the
door at 5:40 and home at 7:15 and coaching on Saturdays and fixing the church van
on Sunday afternoon does not look passive to anyone, least of all to himself. But ask
his wife what he's avoiding and she'll tell you without taking a breath. Motion is not
the opposite of passivity. Motion is very often how a man hides from the one thing
standing still in the middle of his life. The first man did this too. When God came
walking in the garden after the fruit, Adam was hiding in the trees, and God asked
him a question that wasn't really a question.

     Genesis 3:9 (NIV)
     But the LORD God called to the man, 'Where are you?'



   God knew exactly where he was. The question was for Adam's benefit, and it
wasn't about his location. It meant: where are you, the man I put here to work this
place and to guard it, the man who was standing right there when it all went wrong


36                                                                            Stop Waiting
and said nothing? That question is still walking through gardens. It walks through
your house, and it will walk through it again tonight, and the man under the
chirping smoke detector needs to hear it as a question addressed to him.

Why we wait

  I've asked a lot of men why they didn't move when they knew they should have,
and nobody has ever said "because I'm passive." Here's what they actually say, and
you'll recognize your own voice in at least one of them.
   They say they didn't want to make it worse. This is fear of conflict, and it's the
most common reason of all. A man figures the current quiet is better than the noise
he'd make by speaking, and what he doesn't figure is that quiet has a cost too, and
that the cost compounds like interest on a debt nobody's paying down.
   They say they weren't sure they were right. This is fear of failure, and it sounds
responsible. He'd rather not act than act wrong. But not acting is also acting. It's a
decision with consequences, and he will have to answer for it just as surely as he'd
have answered for a wrong move.
    They say nobody asked them. This one runs deep in a lot of men, and it almost
always traces back to a boyhood where initiative got punished. Cole, who you'll
meet properly later in this book, rebuilt a transmission at seventeen and had his
father find the one bolt he'd torqued wrong, and that was the pattern of his whole
childhood: every time he started something, a man showed him what was wrong
with it. By ten he had learned that starting things meant being corrected, and by
thirty he was a capable man who waited to be told, at work and at church and in his
own marriage, because waiting was the only thing that had ever been safe. Maybe
your father ran everything and there was no room for a boy to start anything.
Maybe your father was gone and there was nobody to show you how a man begins a
thing. Either way you learned to wait to be invited, and now you're forty-three and
still waiting for an invitation that isn't coming, because grown men don't get invited
to lead their own lives. If you're a young man and this is you, hear it early: nobody is
going to send you an invitation. The garden's yours the day you're standing in it.
   They say it'll work itself out, and it won't, because fields don't weed themselves,
and Proverbs has a picture of this that's almost gentle in the way it's told, which is
exactly what makes it land.




Stop Waiting                                                                             37
     Proverbs 24:30-31 (NIV)
     I went past the field of a sluggard, past the vineyard of someone who has no sense; thorns had come up
     everywhere, the ground was covered with weeds, and the stone wall was in ruins.



   Notice what the sluggard didn't do. He didn't burn the field, and he didn't tear
down the wall. He just did nothing, for long enough, and the world did the rest,
because thorns don't need a man's permission. Neither does distance in a marriage,
or debt, or a teenager's silence, or a father's last years passing without the
conversation.
   And underneath all of the reasons men give, there's one they don't say out loud,
which is that they're comfortable. The tailgate is comfortable. The hallway with the
chirping detector is comfortable enough. Comfort is the most persuasive voice in a
man's life, because it never has to raise itself to be heard.

What waiting on God actually looks like

   Now, about the doctor's note. The Bible does tell men to wait on the Lord, and I
don't want to take that away from you, because there are things in your life you
genuinely cannot control and you'll wreck yourself trying. But look at how
Scripture says it.

     Psalm 27:14 (NIV)
     Wait for the LORD; be strong and take heart and wait for the LORD.



   Be strong and take heart. That isn't a man on a tailgate. That's a man on his feet
with his jaw set, doing everything that's his to do while he waits on the parts that
aren't. Abraham waited twenty-five years for a son, and while he waited he moved
his whole household across a continent, dug wells, fought a battle, and negotiated
with kings. Waiting on God never once meant sitting still on the parts that were his.
   So here's the test I use on myself, and I'd ask you to use it too. If you say you're
waiting on God, ask what you're doing while you wait. If the answer is "the next
thing I already know is right," you're waiting on God. If the answer is "nothing,
until He makes it obvious," you're waiting on comfort and calling it faith, and God
rarely makes the next step obvious to a man who won't take the one already in front
of him. Jesus never waited that way. When the time came for Him to go to


38                                                                                               Stop Waiting
Jerusalem, knowing exactly what waited for Him there, Luke reaches for a
particular word.

  Luke 9:51 (NIV)
  As the time approached for him to be taken up to heaven, Jesus resolutely set out for Jerusalem.



   He set His face and He went. He didn't feel ready in any sense a man would
recognize; we know that from the garden a few chapters later. He went anyway, and
that is the Man we're following.

How this looks in the different rooms

   I want to turn toward a few of you specifically now, because passivity puts on
different clothes in different houses, and you may not recognize it in yours.
   If you're single, the most dangerous sentence in your life right now is "when I get
married." When I get married I'll get serious about church. When I get married I'll
get my money straight, and deal with what I watch, and learn to cook, and call my
dad. But a wife is not a starting gun. The man you are on the Tuesday before you
meet her is the man she'll get, and if you wait for her to make you start, you'll hand
her a project instead of a husband. Start now, all of it, while there's nobody to blame
you for not.
   If there's a baby in your house, you're being tempted every day to wait for "when
things calm down" before you turn back toward your wife. I have to tell you plainly
that things aren't going to calm down. They're going to change shape, and then
change shape again, and the turn toward her has to happen inside the chaos or it
won't happen. Ten minutes at the kitchen counter after the baby's down, with your
phone face down on the other side of the room, isn't waiting for a better season to
arrive. It is the season, and it's the whole liturgy of this year of your marriage.
    If you're divorced, there's probably a call you've been waiting to make until the
anger cools. A conversation with your kids' mother about the schedule, or the
school, or the thing your daughter said in the truck. The anger isn't going to cool on
its own; anger doesn't work like that. It cools when a man acts like a man in spite of
it. Make the call, keep it short and kind and about the children, and notice
afterward that the anger is a little smaller than it was, because you did the thing it
was telling you not to do.


Stop Waiting                                                                                         39
   And if you're retired, you may be waiting to be asked. Waiting for the church to
call, for a younger man to seek you out, for your grandkids to want your time. Roy,
who you'll meet again and again in this book, turned in a state trooper's badge after
thirty-one years, and he told me the hardest part of the first year was realizing that
nobody was ever going to hand him an assignment again. "I sat by the phone like a
rookie," he said. "Then one morning I got up and went to find the work." He was
sixty-eight when he stopped waiting, so don't tell me you can't.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty is waiting for his life to start. He has a phrase for it,
which is "once I," as in once I finish school, once I get the job, once I meet her, once
I move out. He's a good kid, and he's been on the tailgate for three years without
knowing it, because every real thing he could start has been filed under "once I."
The takeaway for him is that there is no starting gun. The garden is the apartment
he's standing in.
   A man of fifty is waiting for a conversation with his grown son to happen on its
own. The son has gone quiet, and the father has told himself that the boy will come
around, and it has been three years. The takeaway for him is the sluggard's field:
nothing burned it down. It simply went untended for long enough. He needs to
make the call this week, badly, and then again next week.

How to get off the tailgate

   Rejecting passivity is a habit, not a moment, and I want to give you the habit in a
form you can actually run.
   Start by naming the thing. Most men carry three or four things they've been
going to get to, and they've never once said them out loud in a single sentence. Say
them, or write them down if saying them is too much at first. "I need to talk to my
wife about money. I need to call my brother. I need to deal with what I've been
watching. I need to fix the smoke detector." Passivity lives in vagueness, and it can't
survive a list.
   Then ask, for each one, what you're actually waiting for. There's an honest
reason to wait and a comfortable one, and they sound different if you listen. An
honest reason sounds like "I need one more fact" or "it's better to talk after the kids
are asleep, which is tonight." A comfortable reason sounds like "when things settle


40                                                                           Stop Waiting
down" or "when I feel ready." Feeling ready is not a prerequisite for anything a man
has to do. Nobody has ever felt ready to bury a parent or hold a newborn or tell his
wife the truth about the debt.
   Then find the smallest first move. Not the whole conversation, but the sentence
that starts it. Not the whole financial overhaul, but the one account you log into
tonight. Not a new life, but the battery. Passivity is beaten by small real motion and
not by big resolutions, because a big resolution is one more way to feel like you did
something without doing anything.
   Then move before noon. This is a Tuesday rule and I'd ask you to keep it strictly.
If you've named the thing and found the first move, do it before lunch. Waiting for
the evening is waiting. Waiting for the weekend is waiting. Men who have learned
to reject passivity have learned to hate the gap between knowing and doing, because
that gap is where whole lives disappear.
  And expect it to feel wrong. Almost every right move a man makes against his
own passivity feels awkward the first time. The conversation will start badly. Your
voice will sound strange to you. She may not respond the way you hoped, and your
brother may not call back. None of that means you shouldn't have moved. It's the
sound of a man doing something he's out of practice at, and the only cure for being
out of practice is practice.

The reward for moving

   I told you last chapter that the fourth mark makes the other three possible, so let
me show you how it touches this one. There's a line in James that most men read as
a warning, and it is one, but there's a promise hidden inside it if you look.

  James 4:17 (NIV)
  If anyone, then, knows the good they ought to do and doesn't do it, it is sin for them.



   The warning is plain enough: knowing and not doing is sin. God takes your
passivity personally, because He put you where you are on purpose, the way He put
Adam in the garden, and He's asking where you are. But the promise is this: God
sees the good you ought to do. He knows about the smoke detector and the
conversation and the call. Which means He also sees when you move. Nobody else
may notice. Your wife may not thank you for finally bringing up the money, and


Stop Waiting                                                                                41
your brother may let it go to voicemail. But the Father who sees what's done in
secret watches a man get off the tailgate, and He is not indifferent to it. That's a
reward worth working for, and it's the reason you can move on a Tuesday when
nothing in you feels like moving.
  So stop waiting. Go change the battery, and then, while you're standing there
with the old one in your hand, go make the call.

Before you turn the page

   If you've been on the tailgate a long time, I want to say something to you before
you get off it. You aren't lazy, and you probably aren't a coward. You're a man who
learned somewhere that waiting was safer than moving, and the lesson was true
once and isn't anymore. The Father who asked Adam where he was is asking you
the same thing tonight, and He isn't asking in anger. He's asking the way a father
asks a boy who's been hiding in the barn: come on out, there's work to do, and I'll
do it with you. May He give you the small strength to change the battery, and the
larger strength to make the call, and may you find that the first one makes the
second one possible.

     YOUR TURN



     NAME IT
     Because passivity can't survive being said out loud: name the one thing you've been
     going to get to the longest, and say honestly how many months or years it's been.
     DO THIS WEEK
     Because the gap between knowing and doing is where a life disappears: do the
     smallest first move on that one thing before noon on a workday. The first sentence,
     the first login, the first call.
     TELL A BROTHER
     Because a man is far more likely to move when another man knows he said he
     would: tell him what you've been waiting on and what you did about it, and give
     him permission to ask again next week.
     CARRY THIS
     Where are you? God knew where Adam was. The question was for Adam, and it's
     for you.




42                                                                             Stop Waiting
CHAPTER 5



Pick Up the Weight
   Part 1: The man you were built to be
  If you've ever built a fence that had to hold, you know that the whole thing
depends on the corners.
   Line posts you can set shallow and quick, because all they do is hold the wire up
off the ground. But a corner post takes the strain of the entire run. Every foot of
wire you stretch pulls against it, and it pulls for years, through wet winters and dry
summers and cattle leaning on it to get at the grass on the other side. So you dig it
deeper than seems reasonable, and you set it in concrete if you've got any, and you
brace it with an H and a diagonal and tamp it until your shoulders burn. And then
you never think about it again, because a corner post that does its job is invisible.
Nobody drives past a fence and admires the corners. They only notice when one
starts to lean.
   That is responsibility, and that's the second mark. A real man accepts
responsibility, and what that means on a Tuesday is that he sets himself at the
corner and takes the strain, and mostly nobody notices, and he has made his peace
with that.

The oldest excuse

   When God asked Adam what he'd done, Adam gave the first excuse in human
history, and men have been giving versions of it ever since. He said the woman gave
him the fruit. And then, as if that weren't enough, he added that the woman God
had put there with him gave it to him. In one sentence he blamed his wife and his
Creator and left himself out of the story entirely.
   I would like to tell you I've never done that. I've done it within the last month.



Pick Up the Weight                                                                      43
   Here's what blame is, underneath the words. Blame is a man setting down weight
that belongs to him and pointing at whoever happens to be standing closest. It's
fast, and it works in the moment, and it also leaves the weight sitting on the ground
where it stays and grows. Nothing in a man's life has ever gotten better because he
correctly identified whose fault it was. I've watched men win that argument for
thirty years and lose everything the argument was about. The next generation did it
too. When God asked Cain where his brother was, Cain answered with a question
that has become something like a motto for men who don't want to carry anything.

     Genesis 4:9 (NIV)
     Then the LORD said to Cain, 'Where is your brother Abel?' 'I don't know,' he replied. 'Am I my
     brother's keeper?'



   The whole rest of the Bible is going to spend its pages answering that question,
and the answer is yes. You are your brother's keeper, and your wife's, and your
children's, and your neighbor's, and not because you caused their trouble but
because you're the man standing there. That is the piece most men miss about
responsibility, and I want to slow down on it. Men tend to think accepting
responsibility means owning their fault, and it does include that. But a man who
only takes responsibility for what he caused is still measuring by fairness, and
fairness is how a boy thinks. A man takes responsibility for what's in front of him.
The bill nobody opened, the kid nobody's talking to, the widow down the road
whose gutter came off in the storm, the mess in the church parking lot after an
event he didn't even attend. Nobody would blame him for walking past any of it.
He picks it up anyway, because that's what a corner post is for.

What the weight is

   I want to be specific, because "responsibility" is one of those words that can mean
everything and therefore mean nothing, and a man can nod at it for years without
ever picking anything up.
  The first weight is your own life. Your body, your money, your time, what you
put in front of your eyes, whether you're in church or in bed on Sunday morning,
whether you've told the truth this week. Before a man can carry anything for
anyone else he has to carry himself, and a surprising number of grown men are still
waiting for somebody to do that for them. If you're single, this is where the weight


44                                                                                      Pick Up the Weight
starts, and it's not a small thing. A man who pays his own rent on the day it's due,
gets himself to the doctor, keeps his word about small things, and shows up when
he said he would is already carrying more than a good many husbands I know.
   The second weight is your house. If you're married, the state of your marriage is
your responsibility, and I don't mean half of it. I'm not saying your wife has no part;
she has a large one. I'm saying you don't get to wait for her part before you carry
yours, and a man who keeps a ledger in a marriage has already started losing it. If
