you have children, their formation is yours too. Not the school's, not the youth
pastor's, and not their mother's alone. That doesn't mean you do everything. It
means that if it isn't happening, you don't get to say "I don't know" the way Cain
did.
   The third weight is your work, whatever it is. The job isn't only how you get
paid. It's a place where somebody is trusting you with something, and how a man
handles a trust is how he handles a trust, whether it's a fleet of trucks or a
spreadsheet or a classroom or a hospital floor at three in the morning.
   The fourth weight is your people. Your church, your brothers, your street. The
older man at the end of the pew who stopped coming three weeks ago. The younger
man who keeps hovering at the edge of the group and has never been asked in.
   And the fifth weight, which is the heaviest and the quickest to set down, is what
you've done wrong. When you've sinned against somebody, the weight is naming it
without a "but" attached, and making it right where you can. Paul puts two
sentences a few lines apart in Galatians that sound like they contradict each other,
and they don't.

  Galatians 6:2 (NIV)
  Carry each other's burdens, and in this way you will fulfill the law of Christ.




  Galatians 6:5 (NIV)
  for each one should carry their own load.



   Those are two different words in the original. A burden is something crushing
that no one should have to carry alone. A load is a man's own pack, the daily weight
that's his and nobody else's. A real man carries his own load without complaint and



Pick Up the Weight                                                                     45
reaches for his brother's burden without being asked. A boy does the reverse; he
wants help with his own pack and looks the other way when his brother goes down
under something heavy.

The wrong weight

  Now I have to warn you about two ways this mark goes bad, because I've
watched both of them wreck good men who thought they were being responsible.
    The first is the man who grabs weight that isn't his, the man who has to fix
everyone: his grown daughter's marriage, his brother's drinking, every problem at
church that comes across his path. He can't sit in a room where something's wrong
without taking it onto himself, and he calls it responsibility, but it's often
something else wearing responsibility's clothes. Sometimes it's control, and
sometimes it's a way to feel needed, and sometimes it's a boy who learned early that
if he didn't hold the family together nobody would, and he's still doing it at fifty
with people who never asked him to. There is a weight God gives a man and a
weight a man grabs in order to feel important, and only the first one shapes him.
The second one just breaks his back. Moses hit this wall, trying to carry an entire
nation by himself, and he finally said so to God.

     Numbers 11:14 (NIV)
     I cannot carry all these people by myself; the burden is too heavy for me.



   God's answer wasn't "yes you can." It was seventy elders. Sometimes the most
responsible thing a man can do is admit that the weight is too big for one man and
go find the others.
   The second way this mark goes bad is quieter, and it's the one I want to spend the
rest of the chapter on, because I've watched it take down more good men than the
first.

Providing without disappearing

  Walt is fifty-five, and by every outside measure he has done it right. He built a
small business from one truck to eleven. His kids went to college without loans. His
wife has never once, in thirty years, had to worry about a bill. He'd tell you, and he



46                                                                                Pick Up the Weight
did tell me, that he has carried the weight of that house every single day of his adult
life, and he's right.
   He's also barely in it. He leaves before anyone's awake and gets home in time to
eat whatever's left in the pan, standing at the counter. He knows the names of his
daughter's boyfriends because his wife tells him. He hasn't been to a church service
in a year that didn't fall on a holiday. And when his wife finally said to him one
night in the kitchen, "I don't need more. I need you," he honestly didn't understand
the sentence. Wasn't he there, he wondered, and wasn't every hour of it for them? I
want to be careful here, because Scripture is not soft on a man who won't provide.

  1 Timothy 5:8 (NIV)
  Anyone who does not provide for their relatives, and especially for their own household, has denied the
  faith and is worse than an unbeliever.



   Provision is a weight, and a man carries it, and I'm not going to make a
hardworking man feel guilty for working hard. But look at the word Paul uses,
which is household and not paycheck. A man is responsible for a household, and a
household is people, and people need more than a roof over their heads. They need
a man in the room. Walt was providing everything for his house except himself, and
somewhere along the way he'd started to believe that the check was the man.
  Here's the plainest way I know to say it. Provision that costs your presence isn't
provision. It's a very expensive way to disappear.
    And I understand how it happens, because I've felt the pull. Work rewards you;
it has a scoreboard, and it tells you clearly when you've done well, and home mostly
doesn't. Home is a toddler screaming at a plate of food you paid for, or a teenager
who grunts at you, or a wife who's tired in a way you can't fix with a purchase. So a
man drifts toward the place that claps for him and tells himself he's carrying the
weight, and he is, but not the one that matters most.
    So how does a man provide without disappearing? He decides that presence is
part of provision and not a luxury he earns after the provision's done, and he writes
it into his week the way he'd write in a client meeting. Tomás, who you'll come to
know well in this book, has a four-month-old and works ten-hour shifts on a
pipeline crew. He can't shorten the shifts. What he did was claim the twenty
minutes between his boots coming off and dinner going on the table. His phone
goes on the charger by the door, not in his pocket. He takes the baby so his wife can


Pick Up the Weight                                                                                          47
sit down with both hands free for the first time since morning. That's twenty
minutes out of a day, and it's also the entire difference between a man who's
providing and a man who's disappearing, and his wife would tell you so.
   He learns to tell his wife the truth about his capacity. Not "I'll be home by six"
when he knows perfectly well it's eight. Not silence about the busy season until she
finds out by living through it. A man who says "the next six weeks are going to be
brutal, and here's what I'm going to protect, and here's what I'm going to need from
you" is carrying weight in a way she can lean on. A man who vanishes into the busy
season and comes out the other side expecting credit has asked her to carry the not
knowing, which is a weight nobody should carry alone.
   And he keeps a floor. A floor is the minimum that doesn't move no matter what
the job does: Sunday morning, one dinner a week with no screens on the table, the
bedtime prayer with the kids in person or by phone from a hotel room in Midland.
The floor is what tells your family that they're the corner post and the work is the
wire, and not the other way around.
   If you're the older man reading this, past the paycheck years, don't imagine
you've aged out of this one. Roy told me that after he retired he realized he'd spent
thirty years providing for a house he'd barely lived in, and that his wife had built an
entire life in the spaces he wasn't in. "I had to ask to be let back in," he said. "Not
because she was cold. Because I'd been gone." He started by making the coffee,
every morning, and sitting with her while she drank it. It sounds small until you've
been gone thirty years.

How to pick it up

   The habit of this mark is simpler than it sounds, and I'll give it to you the way I'd
give it to a son.
   Say "that's mine," out loud if you can, the moment you see the thing that's in
front of you and before your mind starts working out whose fault it is. You'll be
surprised how much a sentence changes what your hands do next.
   When you're owning something you did wrong, stop your sentence at the
period. It ends at "I did it." Not "I did it, but you." Not "I did it because." A man
who can say "I was wrong, I'm sorry, and I'm going to fix it" and then stop talking is
rarer than you'd think and more powerful than you'd guess.



48                                                                       Pick Up the Weight
   Own the outcome and not just the effort. A boy says "I tried." A man says "it isn't
done yet, and here's what I'm doing next." Effort matters, but responsibility means
staying with a thing until it holds, the way a corner post stays.
   Carry your own load before you reach for a burden. If your own life is a mess,
don't go fix your brother's marriage. Get your house in order and then reach; you'll
reach with cleaner hands.
   And when it's too heavy, ask for the seventy. Say so, to God and to men. That
isn't setting the weight down. It's being wise about how a weight gets carried.

The weight at every age

  The weight a man carries changes shape as he goes, and I want you to see four
men at the corner post before you decide where yours is.
   A young man of twenty-three is learning that his own life is a weight. He's paying
rent for the first time, and the rent is due on a day and not a season, and he's
discovering that nobody is going to remind him to change his oil or go to the doctor
or tell the truth about the ticket he got. Some men his age are still handing that load
to their mothers, and some have picked it up. The takeaway for him is that a man
who can carry himself is already carrying more than most husbands, and that this is
where a corner post gets set.
   A young father is learning that responsibility is not the same as the paycheck,
which is Tomás's whole first year. He works ten hours and comes home to a wife
who needs him to carry the baby for twenty minutes so she can use both hands, and
the temptation is to feel that he's already carried enough. The takeaway for him is
that the household is the weight, and the household is people, and the paycheck is
only part of what people need.
  A man in his fifties is discovering that he grabbed weight that wasn't his and set
down weight that was. He has fixed everyone at church for twenty years and let his
own marriage go quiet, and he's exhausted in a way that feels righteous and isn't.
The takeaway for him is Moses and the seventy: some of what he's carrying was
never his, and the thing that is his has been sitting in the hallway.
   And an older man is learning that the weight doesn't retire. Roy thought his
responsibility ended with his badge, and found that it had only changed shape into
a grandson and two younger men and a wife he'd never studied. The takeaway for



Pick Up the Weight                                                                   49
him is that the corner post doesn't get pulled when the fence gets old. It gets braced.

The weight He carried

   Every one of these marks ends at Jesus, and this one has to. The Man we follow
picked up a weight that wasn't His. He hadn't sinned, and He wasn't at fault for a
single thing that had gone wrong in the world since the garden. And He walked
toward the whole of it, on purpose, and took it onto Himself.

     Isaiah 53:4 (NIV)
     Surely he took up our pain and bore our suffering, yet we considered him punished by God, stricken by
     him, and afflicted.



  Took up and bore are carrying words. Nobody handed Him that weight and
made Him take it. He reached for it because it was in front of Him and He was the
Man who could.
   So when you set yourself at the corner, when you carry a thing nobody's going to
thank you for, you are doing more than being responsible. You're looking like Him,
and He notices, which is the fourth mark showing up inside the second. The Father
who watched His Son carry the weight of the world sees a man in Lubbock get up
at five in the morning to carry the weight of his house, and He does not forget it.
  So pick it up. Dig the post deeper than seems reasonable, and set yourself at the
corner, and take the strain.

Before you turn the page

   If you've been pointing at someone for a long time, I don't want you to feel
crushed by this chapter. Blame is the oldest habit men have, and you learned it from
a man who learned it from a man, all the way back to a garden. The Man we follow
picked up a weight that wasn't His so that you could pick up the ones that are, and
He isn't standing over you with a clipboard while you do it. He's under the other
end of the safe. May the Father give you the plain courage to say "that's mine" this
week, and may you find, as I have, that the weight gets lighter the moment you stop
arguing about whose it is.




50                                                                                       Pick Up the Weight
   YOUR TURN



   NAME IT
   Because a weight you keep pointing at never gets carried: say what you've been
   calling someone else's fault that is actually yours to pick up, in a full sentence that
   starts with "that's mine."
   DO THIS WEEK
   Because presence is part of provision and not a reward for it: choose one thing you
   provide your house that isn't money, the coffee or the bedtime prayer or the twenty
   minutes after your boots come off, and do it every day this week. If you're single,
   choose one thing in your own life you've let someone else carry, and carry it.
   TELL A BROTHER
   Because a corner post set alone tends to lean: tell him the weight you named and ask
   him to check next week whether you're still carrying it.
   CARRY THIS
   Provision that costs your presence isn't provision. It's a very expensive way to
   disappear.




Pick Up the Weight                                                                           51
52   Go First
CHAPTER 6



Go First
   Part 1: The man you were built to be
   A pickup rolled on Highway 287 north of Wichita Falls a few years back, in the
kind of rain that comes at you sideways, and I was maybe six cars behind it when it
happened. What I remember most is the slowing. Every car slowed down and every
driver looked, and the brake lights ran all the way back as far as I could see, and you
could feel forty people thinking the same thought at the same time. Somebody
ought to stop.
   One man did. He was already out of his truck and running before I'd finished
having the thought, a big man in a feed store cap, and the second his door opened,
three more doors opened behind him, and then six more, and inside of a minute
there were a dozen men in that ditch, and the one in the cap wasn't telling anybody
what to do. He didn't have to. He'd gone first, and going first turned out to be the
entire job.
   That's leadership, as far as I've been able to tell after a lot of years of watching it
and reading about it. I've sat through the seminars with the pyramids and the
principles, and some of it was useful, but I'll tell you what it comes down to for a
man on a Tuesday. Somebody has to go first. Into the ditch, into the apology, into
the prayer, into the truth. A real man leads courageously, and leading courageously
is mostly the willingness to be the first door open on a highway full of people who
are waiting to see what everybody else does.

Not in charge, but first

  I need to clear something up before we go further, because a lot of men hear the
word "lead" and picture a man at the head of a table telling other people what to do.
Some men want that picture badly and some are repelled by it, and both of them


Go First                                                                                 53
have missed the point.
   Leadership in Scripture is almost never about the head of the table. When Jesus
wanted to teach His men what leading looked like, He got up from the head of the
table, took off His outer clothes, and washed their feet. Then He told them plainly
what He'd done.

     John 13:14-15 (NIV)
     Now that I, your Lord and Teacher, have washed your feet, you also should wash one another's feet. I
     have set you an example that you should do as I have done for you.



  I have set you an example. That's the leadership sentence in the New Testament.
Not "I have given you orders," but "I have gone first into the lowest job in the room,
and now you know what it looks like." The going first was the leading. Gideon,
before his three hundred men went down against an army that outnumbered them
past counting, didn't give a speech about courage. He said something a good deal
shorter.

     Judges 7:17 (NIV)
     'Watch me,' he told them. 'Follow my lead. When I get to the edge of the camp, do exactly as I do.'



  Watch me, follow my lead, do exactly as I do. If you can't say those words to your
sons about how you treat their mother, or to the younger men at church about how
you handle your money, or to your wife about how you handle your temper, then
you're not leading them, whatever your title says. You may be managing them,
which is a different thing and a smaller one.
   So when I say a man leads, I don't mean he's in charge of everybody. I mean that
in every room he's in, he's willing to be the first one to do the right thing. And the
reason that takes courage is that first is exposed. The first man in the ditch might be
wrong about what he'll find there. The first man to apologize might not get one
back. The first man to pray out loud at a table full of relatives who don't will feel
every eye in the room on him. First is where a man can look foolish, and looking
foolish is what most of us fear more than nearly anything else.

The places a man has to go first




54                                                                                                     Go First
   Let me be concrete about where this shows up, because "lead courageously" can
float above a man's actual week if you let it.
   Every marriage has a standoff in it somewhere. Three days of politeness,
everybody knowing what happened, nobody willing to say it. Somebody has to go
first, and it has to be you, not because it was all your fault but because you're the
man and going first is what a man is for. "I was wrong about how I spoke to you
Tuesday. I'm sorry." Then you stop. You'll want to add "and I know you also," and
you must not add it, because the moment you do, you've turned an apology into an
opening statement. A man who apologizes first and cleanly changes the weather in a
house.
    I've known men who've been Christians for thirty years and have never once
prayed out loud in front of their own wives. They'll pray at church if they're called
on, and they'll pray with the men's group, but at their own table, in their own
bedroom, with the one person who knows them best, they go quiet, and the reason
is always the same: it feels awkward. Of course it does, because it's supposed to feel
awkward the first time you do anything that matters, and you go first anyway.
"Lord, thank You for this food and for this woman, and help us tonight. Amen."
That's a whole prayer, and it will take you eleven seconds, and it will do more for
your leadership in your home than a year of good intentions.
   Every family and every crew and every church has a thing nobody's saying: the
money isn't working, or Grandpa's drinking again, or the youth pastor hasn't
shown up in three weeks. Somebody has to say it first, in the room, kindly and
clearly. Nehemiah came to a city that had been sitting in rubble for decades, with
everybody stepping around the broken walls as if they were furniture, and the first
thing he did was name it out loud.

  Nehemiah 2:17 (NIV)
  Then I said to them, 'You see the trouble we are in: Jerusalem lies in ruins, and its gates have been
  burned with fire. Come, let us rebuild the wall of Jerusalem, and we will no longer be in disgrace.'



  They did see the trouble. They'd been seeing it for years. What they needed was
one man to say it and then to say "come." That is going first, and it's what your
family needs from you about the thing nobody's saying.
   In a marriage, somebody has to turn first. Toward her, when you've both been
facing your phones for a month. Toward the hard conversation about the kids.


Go First                                                                                                  55
Toward the bedroom, when it's been a long time and neither of you knows how to
bring it up. A wife should not have to ask for her husband's attention, and if she's
asking, you've already made her go first, and that's a weight she shouldn't have to
carry.
    And a house needs to know where it's going. Not a five-year plan with a logo on
it, but a man who can say "this year we're going to be a family that eats together four
nights a week, and here's how," or "we're going to get out of debt, and here's the first
step," or "we're going to start going to church again, and I'm driving." Direction is a
man saying "come" and then walking. If you're single, you still set direction, for
your own life and for the people who orbit it: your roommates, your brothers, the
younger guys at work who watch how you live whether you know it or not. A single
man who knows where he's going and says so is a leader whether anybody ever
hands him a title.

What we're actually afraid of

  I said first is exposed. Let me name what the exposure feels like, because a fear
you can name is a fear you can walk through, and a fear you can't name will keep
you on the bank forever.
  We're afraid of looking foolish. The prayer will sound clumsy, the apology will
come out wrong, the true thing will land badly and everybody will look at us.
    We're afraid of rejection, that she won't apologize back, that the younger man
won't want a mentor, that the family will go quiet and change the subject and we'll
sit there having said the thing.
  We're afraid of being wrong. What if the thing nobody's saying is actually fine
and we're the one who's off?
  And we're afraid of what it will cost, because if we go first on the truth about the
money, we're the one who has to do something about it.
  Every one of those fears is reasonable. Every one of them has come true for me at
some point. And not one of them is an excuse, because Paul told a young man who
was afraid of exactly these things what the Spirit inside him was actually like.




56                                                                               Go First
  2 Timothy 1:7 (NIV)
  For the Spirit God gave us does not make us timid, but gives us power, love and self-discipline.



   Not timid, Paul says, and notice that he doesn't say "not afraid," which I think is
deliberate. God doesn't promise a man that the fear goes away. He says the Spirit He
gave you isn't the timid kind. The fear is in you, and so is something stronger, and
courage is what happens when you let the stronger thing move your feet. And here's
the part that makes going first possible at all. You are never actually first.

  Deuteronomy 31:8 (NIV)
  The LORD himself goes before you and will be with you; he will never leave you nor forsake you. Do
  not be afraid; do not be discouraged.



   The LORD himself goes before you. Every time you're the first door open,
you're actually the second, because He's already in the ditch. A man who goes first
isn't being brave on his own account. He's following Somebody who went first into
everything, including death, and came back out. Which means the worst thing that
can happen to you for going first has already happened to Him, and He's fine.

Going first in different houses

   Going first looks different depending on where you're standing, and I want to
turn toward a few of you so you'll recognize it in your own rooms.
   If you're single and there's a woman you're interested in, go first. Ask her plainly,
for a specific day at a specific place, with your intention where she can see it. Not a
hangout and not a "we should get coffee sometime." And if it's been three months
and you know what this is, be the first to say so. Men who make women guess aren't
being careful. They're being cowards with good manners, and she deserves a man
who'll go first into the awkward conversation about what the two of you are.
   If you're a new father, go first at two in the morning. Don't wait to be asked.
Don't lie there hoping she'll get up because she's the one who can feed him. Get up,
change him, bring him to her, take him back when she's done. Your wife is learning
in these months whether she has a partner or a second child, and she's learning it in
the dark, and nobody's watching but God.



Go First                                                                                               57
   If you're divorced, going first is going to cost you more than it costs most men,
because there's history in every direction you could move, and you go first anyway.
Be the first to keep the tone civil at the exchange. Be the first to tell your kids the
truth in a way that doesn't make them carry your anger. Be the first to show up at
the school thing even though she'll be there. Kenny told me the hardest sentence he
ever said was to his ex-wife on the sideline of a soccer field: "You've done a good job
with them." It wasn't strategy, and he didn't say it to get anything. It was going first.
He told me it changed the next two years.
   And if you're an older man, going first means you initiate with younger men and
not the other way around. They're not going to ask. They think you're busy, or they
think you'd say no, or they've simply never seen it done. Roy started sitting at a
different table at his Thursday breakfast, one with two men in their thirties who
looked like they'd wandered in by accident. He asked them about their work. Then
he asked about their wives. Then he asked if they'd like to keep doing this. That's
the whole method, and it's Gideon's method: watch me, and follow my lead.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of nineteen goes first when he's the one in the truck full of friends
who says, "Let's not," about the thing everybody knows is stupid, and takes the
ribbing for it. He goes first when he asks the girl plainly instead of hovering for a
year, and when he prays out loud at a table of guys who've never done it. The
takeaway for him is that courage at nineteen is mostly the willingness to look foolish
for eight seconds, and that the other doors open after his.
   A man in his forties goes first when he calls the brother who's been drifting, and
when he says "I was wrong" in a marriage that's been polite for three days, and when
he's the one at the meeting who says the money isn't working. Anthony's first
sentence to his wife after nine years of winning fights was the hardest thing he'd
ever done. The takeaway for him is that going first in his forties is mostly going first
into the apology.

How to go first

  Prepare the first sentence and not the whole conversation. Just the sentence that
opens the door: "I need to tell you something and I'm nervous about it." "I was
wrong about Tuesday." "Can I pray for us before we eat?" A man who has the first


58                                                                                 Go First
sentence ready will say it. A man who's waiting for the whole speech to form in his
head never will.
   Go first in small rooms before you go first in big ones. Pray at your own table
before you pray at the church picnic. Tell the truth to your wife before you tell it to
the elders. Apologize to your kid before you apologize to your boss. Courage grows
in small rooms, and a man who's practiced it there will have it when the big room
comes.
   Lead with a question when you can. "What do you think we should do about
this?" is often stronger than an answer, because it goes first into the conversation
without pretending you have everything figured out. Men think leadership means
having the answer. Mostly it means being willing to start.
   Expect that nobody will follow right away. The man in the feed store cap was
alone in the rain for about eight seconds, and eight seconds is a long time when
you're the only one out of your truck, and you go anyway, because the doors open
after the first one, not before it.
   And do it on a Tuesday. Not at the retreat and not at the New Year, but at a
kitchen table or in a truck cab or on a soccer sideline with no music playing. The
rooms where going first feels least dramatic are the rooms where it counts the most.

The Man who went first

   Hebrews gives Jesus a title I didn't notice for years.

  Hebrews 12:2 (NIV)
  fixing our eyes on Jesus, the pioneer and perfecter of faith. For the joy set before him he endured the
  cross, scorning its shame, and sat down at the right hand of the throne of God.



   A pioneer is the one who goes first into a country nobody's been to and makes a
way for the rest. He went first into the garden of Gethsemane and the men He
brought with Him fell asleep. He went first into the trial and the ones who'd sworn
to die with Him ran. He went first into death, alone, and that was the whole point
of it.
  And look at what carried Him through. For the joy set before Him. That's the
fourth mark again, holding up the third. He could go first into the worst thing a



Go First                                                                                                    59
man has ever faced because He was living for a reward on the other side of it, and so
can you. The awkward prayer, the clean apology, the truth said out loud in the
room, the two o'clock feeding, the sentence on the sideline: none of it is wasted on
the Father who watched His Son go first and gave Him a throne for it.
     Somebody has to be the first door open. Let it be you.

Before you turn the page

   You've probably got a room in mind by now, one where everybody's slowing
down and looking and nobody's stopping. I'd like you to hear one more time that
you won't be alone in it. The LORD Himself goes before you, and the men who
followed Gideon into the dark didn't have to be brave on their own account; they
only had to watch him and do what he did. You have a Pioneer who went first into
everything, including the worst thing, and came out. May the Father give you the
first sentence, and the eight seconds it takes to say it, and the grace to stand there
while the other doors open.

     YOUR TURN



     NAME IT
     Because the ditch you keep slowing down for is the one you're meant to stop at: say
     where in your life everybody's looking and nobody's moving, and say that you're the
     one who should.
     DO THIS WEEK
     Because courage grows in small rooms first: pray out loud with your wife, your kids,
     or your roommate one time before Tuesday, or if you already do that, make the
     apology or say the true thing you've been waiting for someone else to say.
     TELL A BROTHER
     Because a first sentence said to one man is easier to say to the person who needs it:
     tell him the exact first sentence you're planning and who it's for, and then tell him
     afterward how it went, even if it went badly.
     CARRY THIS
     The LORD himself goes before you. You're never actually first. You're the second
     door open.




60                                                                                     Go First
CHAPTER 7



Live for a Reward That Lasts
   Part 1: The man you were built to be
   Mr. Pruitt planted forty pecan trees the spring after he buried his wife. He was
seventy-one years old.
   I helped him set a few of them, bare-root trees not much thicker than a broom
handle, each one going into a hole he'd dug himself with a post-hole digger and a
great deal of stopping to breathe. When I asked him why forty, he said that was how
many the orchard company had on the truck. When I asked him why at all, he stood
up straight and looked down the row for a long moment before he answered.
  "I won't eat one of these," he said. "You know that. My grandson might.
Somebody's grandson will." Then he bent back down and went on tamping.
   I have thought about that afternoon more than I've thought about most
sermons, because Mr. Pruitt was doing something that made no sense on any
scoreboard he would live to see. He was spending his strength, which was running
out, on a harvest he would never taste, and he wasn't sad about it. As far as I could
tell he was the most settled man in the county.
   That's the fourth mark. A real man expects the greater reward, God's reward. I
saved it for last in this part of the book because it's the mark that makes the other
three hold. You can reject passivity for a while on willpower. You can carry weight
for a while out of pride. You can go first for a while because you enjoy being seen
going first. But every one of those runs out of fuel eventually, and the only thing I
have ever seen keep a man doing the right thing for forty years when nobody claps is
that he's working for Somebody who is.

Every man lives for a reward




Live for a Reward That Lasts                                                            61
  Let me begin by taking away an excuse, because some men hear "live for a
reward" and feel a little uneasy, as if it were beneath them. Shouldn't a good man do
good for its own sake? Isn't wanting a reward a bit mercenary?
  Here is the plain truth. You already live for a reward, because every man does,
and nobody gets up at five in the morning for nothing, and the only real question is
which reward you've chosen.
   Some men live for applause. The crowd, the promotion, the compliment from
the boss, the pastor noticing, the text from a buddy that says you crushed it. It pays
out fast and it feels wonderful and it's gone by Wednesday.
   Some men live for money, and most of them aren't greedy. Money is simply the
one scoreboard that never lies about whether you won, and it pays on a schedule. It
also never says "well done." It only ever says "more."
   Some men live for being wanted, by women mostly. A single man can shape his
whole life around the next woman noticing him, and a married man can shape his
around the next woman noticing him too, and both will tell you it isn't about sex,
and both will be about half right.
  Some men live for being right: the argument won, the point proved, the doubter
shown up.
   Some men live for their father's approval, and they'll go on living for it twenty
years after the man is in the ground. I've watched sixty-year-old men still trying to
earn a nod from a grave.
   Some men live for comfort, which isn't a large reward but is paid out daily. The
recliner, the routine, the not being bothered.
   And some men live for being remembered. The building with their name on it,
the town that will tell stories, the children who'll speak of them.
   Every one of those rewards is real and every one of them pays. And every one of
them has the same two problems. It runs out, and it's controlled by someone other
than God. The crowd turns, the money goes, the woman leaves, the father dies
without saying it, and the town forgets faster than you'd believe. A man who lives
for any of them has handed the wheel of his life to something that doesn't love him.
Jesus didn't tell men to stop living for a reward. He told them to pick a better one.




62                                                              Live for a Reward That Lasts
  Matthew 6:19-21 (NIV)
  Do not store up for yourselves treasures on earth, where moths and vermin destroy, and where thieves
  break in and steal. But store up for yourselves treasures in heaven, where moths and vermin do not
  destroy, and where thieves do not break in and steal. For where your treasure is, there your heart will
  be also.



   Store up is an accumulating word. He's telling you to build a fortune. He's only
telling you to build it somewhere the weather can't get to it.

The Father who sees in secret

   The heart of this mark is in a sentence Jesus said three times in a row in one
sermon, about giving and praying and fasting.

  Matthew 6:3-4 (NIV)
  But when you give to the needy, do not let your left hand know what your right hand is doing, so that
  your giving may be in secret. Then your Father, who sees what is done in secret, will reward you.



    Do it in secret, He says, and your Father sees, and He will reward you. Sit with
that for a moment, because it's stranger than it sounds at first. The most important
audience in your life is invisible, and He's watching the things nobody else sees. The
tithe check nobody knows about. The two o'clock feeding. The lust you turned
away from in a hotel room in Odessa with no one to catch you. The kindness to
your ex-wife at the exchange that your buddies would have called weakness. The
fifteen-year-old you keep showing up for who hasn't said thank you once. Nobody
saw any of it. He saw all of it.
   And He isn't indifferent, which is the word I want to press on, because the text
says He rewards. Most Christian men, if you asked them, would say God forgives
and God loves and God is patient. Fewer would say God rewards, and fewer still
would say it like they meant it. But Scripture says it flatly and repeatedly, and the
man who doesn't believe it will go looking for his reward from an audience that can
see him.




Live for a Reward That Lasts                                                                                63
     Colossians 3:23-24 (NIV)
     Whatever you do, work at it with all your heart, as working for the Lord, not for human masters,
     since you know that you will receive an inheritance from the Lord as a reward. It is the Lord Christ
     you are serving.



   Whatever you do, Paul says, and he doesn't mean church work. He means the
pipeline, the classroom, the truck, the spreadsheet, the diaper. Work at it with your
whole heart because you're working for Him, and He pays.

Is that mercenary?

   I promised to come back to this, so let me tell you why wanting God's reward
isn't the kind of wanting a man should be ashamed of.
   There's a kind of man who marries a woman for her money, and we call that
mercenary and we're right to. There's another kind who marries a woman because
he wants her, wants the life with her, wants to wake up beside her for fifty years,
and nobody calls him mercenary for wanting his reward. The reward is the thing
itself. Wanting it is simply what love looks like.
   The reward God offers isn't a payment separate from Him. It is Him: His "well
done," His pleasure, a place at His table, more of Him to enjoy forever. When Jesus
tells the story of the servants and their master, the reward for faithfulness isn't a
bonus check.

     Matthew 25:21 (NIV)
     His master replied, 'Well done, good and faithful servant! You have been faithful with a few things;
     I will put you in charge of many things. Come and share your master's happiness!'



   Come and share your master's happiness. That's the reward: to hear Him say it,
and then to be let into His joy. Wanting that isn't mercenary. Not wanting it would
be strange. A son who works his father's land all day and doesn't want to hear "well
done" at the end of it isn't humble. He's either lying or he's given up on the father.
Moses understood this better than most. He had Egypt, the palace and the title and
everything a man could live for on this side, and Hebrews says he did the arithmetic.




64                                                                               Live for a Reward That Lasts
  Hebrews 11:24-26 (NIV)
  By faith Moses, when he had grown up, refused to be known as the son of Pharaoh's daughter. He
  chose to be mistreated along with the people of God rather than to enjoy the fleeting pleasures of sin.
  He regarded disgrace for the sake of Christ as of greater value than the treasures of Egypt, because he
  was looking ahead to his reward.



  He was looking ahead to his reward. That isn't a man who didn't care about
rewards. That's a man who cared about them so much he refused to settle for a
small one, and the Bible calls his refusal faith.

What the reward does to a Tuesday

   I want to be practical here, because this mark can sound like theology when it's
actually the most practical thing in the book. Here is what happens to a man who
really does expect the greater reward.
  He can do good that nobody sees and never need to mention it, which means he
can be generous with his money without anyone knowing, patient with his kids
when no one's watching, and faithful in a hotel room a thousand miles from anyone
who'd find out.
  He can lose an argument. If your reward is being right, every argument in your
house is a matter of life and death. If your reward is the Father's "well done," you
can let your wife have the last word and go to bed at peace, and He counts it.
  He can stay, in a hard marriage or a hard job or a hard church or a hard season
with a hard father. Men leave when the local scoreboard says they're losing. A man
with his eyes on a different scoreboard can stay through a losing decade because he
knows the game isn't over.
  He can take the low place. He can hold the baby while the other men watch the
game. He can sit with the old man nobody visits. He can coach the team that's
going to go two and nine.
   He can forgive, which is expensive, because it means giving up the reward of
seeing someone pay. A man who's expecting a greater reward can afford to give that
one up, because he was never counting on it for his happiness.
   And he can plant pecan trees at seventy-one.




Live for a Reward That Lasts                                                                                65
What it isn't

   I have to say this, because some men have been sold a counterfeit and it has cost
them their faith.
   The greater reward is not a guarantee that this life goes well. It isn't health, and it
isn't wealth, and it isn't a bigger truck. Moses, after everything, died on a mountain
looking at a land he never entered. Paul, who wrote more about the reward than
anyone, wrote a good deal of it from a jail cell. Jesus, who lived every second for the
joy set before Him, got a cross.
   So if you're expecting God's reward to arrive as things working out on your
schedule, you'll be disappointed, and you'll be tempted to quit, and I've watched
men do exactly that. The reward is real and it's coming, and some of it you'll taste
here: peace you can't explain, a marriage that heals, a son who comes back. But most
of it is stored where the weather can't reach it, and you're going to have to trust
Him with the timing.

     2 Corinthians 4:17-18 (NIV)
     For our light and momentary troubles are achieving for us an eternal glory that far outweighs them
     all. So we fix our eyes not on what is seen, but on what is unseen, since what is seen is temporary, but
     what is unseen is eternal.



  Achieving is the word to hold onto. Your troubles are producing something.
They aren't wasted, and that's why you can fix your eyes past them.

Where the small rewards find you

   The small rewards come for men differently in different seasons, and I want you
to see which one is calling loudest in yours.
   If you're young and single, the reward you're most tempted by is being wanted,
and the second is being impressive, and both of them are loud. I'm not asking you
to stop wanting a wife or a good career. I'm asking you to check, on a Tuesday, who
you're performing for. If you'd stop doing the right thing tomorrow because no
woman ever noticed, you aren't living for the greater reward yet.
   If there's a baby in your house, nobody's clapping, and nobody's going to clap
for a while. Your wife is too tired, your friends can't see it, and your own parents


66                                                                                  Live for a Reward That Lasts
think they did it harder. This is the season where the fourth mark stops being an
idea and becomes the only thing that gets you up at three. He sees the three o'clock,
and He counts it.
   If you're divorced, you may be waiting for vindication. You want people to
finally understand what happened, and her to admit her part, and your kids to see it
clearly. That may never come on this side, and I'd ask you to let it go as your reward,
because it's too small for you anyway. The Father who sees in secret knows exactly
what happened in that house, and He's the only judge whose verdict will last.
   And if you're older, watch out for the reward of being remembered. It comes for
good men late and it's subtle. You'll want your kids to be grateful, and the town to
know what you built, and the church to name a room after you. None of that is
wrong to want, and all of it is too small to live for. Mr. Pruitt didn't put a sign on
the orchard. He didn't need one. He knew who was watching him dig.
  One more man belongs in this section, because he's the one a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
    A man in his forties is most tempted by the scoreboard and by being right, which
is the reward Anthony had to lay down in his kitchen. He's measuring himself
against every man at church and every argument in his house, and every comparison
is a small reward cashed early. The takeaway for him is that a man who can lose an
argument and sleep well has found a better reward than winning.

How to expect it

   Think about it on purpose. Paul said fix your eyes, and fixing takes deciding.
Once a day, in the truck or on the porch, tell yourself the truth: what I did today
that nobody saw, He saw. Say it until you believe it.
   Do one secret good thing a week. Give something, fix something, pray for
someone, serve someone, and tell nobody, not even your wife, not even your
brother. Let it stay between you and the Father. You'll be surprised how it
recalibrates you.
   Rehearse the sentence you're hoping to hear: well done, good and faithful
servant. Imagine hearing it, not in a proud way but the way a boy imagines his
father watching from the stands. Let it pull you.
   Notice when you're performing. Every time you catch yourself telling a story so


Live for a Reward That Lasts                                                          67
someone will think well of you, that's a small reward you just cashed early. Jesus said
men who do that have already received their reward in full, and He meant that's all
they get. Don't spend it here.
  And pray with heaven in view. When you pray, don't only ask for the week to go
well. Ask for grace to be faithful whether it does or not. Ask for eyes that see what's
unseen. Ask to want the right reward, because wanting it is itself a gift.

The whole picture

   So here's a man with all four marks on him. He's off the tailgate. He's set at the
corner post. He's the first door open. And he's doing all of it for a Father who sees
in secret and rewards in the open, on a day that hasn't come yet.
   That's a steadfast man. Not a man who never fails; there's no such man. A man
who knows what he's working for and who he's working for, and who doesn't need
the local scoreboard to tell him how he's doing.
    Mr. Pruitt's trees are about knee-high on me now. They'll bear in another eight
or ten years, and he won't be here for it. Somebody will be, standing under a tree
they didn't plant and eating a harvest they didn't earn, and if they think about it at
all they'll think about an old man who spent his last strength on people he would
never meet.
  He wasn't spending it on them, though. He was spending it on the One who was
watching him dig.
     Live for a reward that lasts. Then get back to work.

Before you turn the page

   I know this chapter asks you to want something you can't see, and I know how
hard that is on a Tuesday when the visible rewards are right there. So I'll leave you
with the thing that has kept me at it. Every good thing you've done that nobody
saw, He saw. Every one. He isn't indifferent to any of it, and He isn't going to lose
track, and the day is coming when He'll say the sentence you've been hoping to hear
from someone your whole life. May the Father fix your eyes on what's unseen, and
may He let you taste, even this week, a little of the joy that's set before you.




68                                                              Live for a Reward That Lasts
   YOUR TURN



   NAME IT
   Because a man can't set down a reward he hasn't admitted he's carrying: say which
   small one has been steering you most this year, applause or money or being wanted
   or being right or your father's nod or comfort or being remembered, and say it in a
   full sentence.
   DO THIS WEEK
   Because the Father who sees in secret is the audience you're learning to work for: do
   one good thing this week that no one will ever know about, not your wife and not
   your brother, and let it stay between you and Him.
   TELL A BROTHER
   Because the small rewards lose their grip when they're named out loud: tell him
   which one you named and how it's been steering you. Don't tell him the secret
   thing; tell him you did one.
   CARRY THIS
   Every man lives for a reward. The only question is which one.




Live for a Reward That Lasts                                                               69
70   Part 2
                PART 2


   What you inherited, and what is
               yours




Part 2                               71
72   The Empty Chair
CHAPTER 8



The Empty Chair
   Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
   Anthony's family had a kitchen table with four chairs around it, and for two
years after his father left, nobody moved the fourth one.
   He was nine years old, and his mother set three plates every night, and the fourth
chair sat there at the end of the table with nothing in front of it, and every night
Anthony looked at it while he ate. Nobody talked about it. His mother didn't cry in
front of him; she cried in the laundry room with the dryer running, and he knew,
and he never said. When he was eleven she finally carried the chair out to the garage
and set a box of Christmas lights on it, and that was the end of that, as far as anyone
in the house was willing to say.
   He's thirty-eight now, with three kids and a wife who loves him and a job that
pays, and he told me that some nights he stands in the doorway between the garage
and the kitchen for a minute before he goes in. He just stands there. He couldn't tell
me why. His kids are loud at the table and his wife is asking somebody to sit down
and it's everything he ever wanted, and he stands in the doorway like a man who
isn't sure he's allowed to come in.
  He knows why, of course; he simply hasn't said it yet. There's a chair in his chest
with a box of Christmas lights on it.

Every man inherited something

  This second part of the book is about what you inherited and what is yours, and
those are two different things, and most men have never separated them, which is
what we're going to do together over the next ten chapters.
   Start with this: every man learned what a man is from the chair at the end of the



The Empty Chair                                                                      73
table, not from a book or a sermon or a movie, though all of those added to the
picture later, but from whoever sat in that chair, or didn't. Before you could read,
you were reading him, learning how he came through the door in the evening, what
his silence meant, whether his hands were safe, whether he looked at your mother
when she talked or past her, whether he came to the game, whether he came home.
You learned more about manhood before you were ten than you've learned in all
the years since, and you learned it from that chair.
   For some of you the chair was empty, through divorce or death or prison or a
man who was never there to begin with, and that was Anthony's kind of chair. You
grew up with a shape at the table where a man was supposed to be, and the shape
taught you things. It taught you that men leave. It taught you that your mother's
tiredness was somehow yours to fix. It taught you to figure it out, whatever it was,
because there was nobody to ask.
   For some of you the chair was full, and the man in it was gone anyway. He
worked, or he drank, or he watched the television with a kind of attention he never
gave you. He was in the house every night of your childhood and you couldn't tell
anyone one thing he thought about you. I want to say this carefully, because these
men often get a pass they didn't earn. A father who is present and absent at the same
time can leave a hole as deep as one who packed a bag. Deeper, sometimes, because a
boy can't even name it; after all, Dad was there, so what's your problem?
   For some of you the chair was full and it was dangerous. The man in it was a
weather system. You learned to read the forecast from the sound of the truck in the
drive. You learned that being noticed was worse than being ignored, and so you
learned to be small. Some of you learned instead to be big and loud so the storm
would land on you and not on your mother or your little brother. Either way you
learned your lessons from fear, and you're still living some of them.
   And some of you had a good man in that chair. Present, kind, showed up. I want
you to know I'm glad, and I want you to know two things. The first is that even the
best father had limits, and you inherited those too, and there's a gap in you shaped
exactly like the places he couldn't reach. The second is that you shouldn't skip this
part of the book because you think it's for men with worse fathers. Every man in the
room inherited something. You'll just have to look a little harder to find yours.

What the empty chair teaches




74                                                                     The Empty Chair
  Let me tell you what I've heard from men over a lot of years of this, because a
wound you can name is a wound you can take somewhere, and a wound you can't
name just keeps running the house.
    The empty chair teaches a boy to father himself. He learns to make his own rules,
hold his own line, comfort himself when he's scared. That sounds like strength, and
some of it is. It also means he grows into a man who can't be fathered by anyone,
including God, because he's been doing it alone so long that letting someone else do
it feels like drowning.
   It teaches him that needing things is dangerous. If you needed your father and he
wasn't there, the lesson your body learned was that needing gets you hurt, so you
stopped needing, and you became a man who doesn't ask for anything. Your wife
will tell you she can never tell what you want, and you'll say you don't want
anything, and you'll believe it.
   It teaches him to read rooms. Boys with an empty or dangerous chair become
experts on other people's moods, and it makes them good salesmen and good
pastors and good at avoiding conflict, and it makes them terrible at knowing what
they themselves feel, because they've spent thirty years pointed outward.
    It teaches him to perform. If a man's approval was scarce, a boy learns to earn it.
Grades, sports, jokes, whatever worked in that house. He grows into a man who's
still auditioning and doesn't know for whom.
    And it teaches him what he's not going to be, which sounds good and is a trap.
Anthony swore he would never leave, and he never has, and he's proud of that, and
it is also the only picture of fatherhood he has, which is a picture of a man he refuses
to be. But you can't build a house out of what you're not. A man who has only a
negative picture of fatherhood ends up standing in a doorway, sure of what he
won't do and with no idea what to do instead.

The chair explains you, and it doesn't excuse you

  Here's where I have to make the turn, and I want to make it gently, because I
know some of what you've carried.
   What sat in that chair is what you inherited. You didn't pick it. Nobody asked a
nine-year-old whether he wanted his father to leave. It happened to you, and it
shaped you, and God knows every bit of it and isn't shrugging at any of it.



The Empty Chair                                                                       75
   But what you do with it now is yours. Anthony's temper with his kids is his. It's
shaped like his father's absence, and I understand that, and it's still his. His wife
doesn't live with his father's absence. She lives with his temper. And the thing about
a wound is that it can explain a man's behavior forever and never once change it.
Plenty of men have worked out exactly why they are the way they are and have
stayed exactly that way, because understanding felt like progress and it wasn't.
   The Bible is full of men with empty chairs. Joseph's father loved him and still
couldn't protect him, and his brothers threw him in a pit. David's father didn't
bother to bring him in from the field when the prophet came looking at his sons.
Timothy's father gets half a sentence in the whole New Testament, and it's his
mother and grandmother who get the credit for his faith. Jesus Himself was raised
by a man who wasn't His father and who disappears from the story before He's
grown.
   Not one of them is excused by it. They're shaped by it, and God works in them
through it, and they are still held responsible for the men they became. That isn't
harsh; it's dignity, because God treats you like a man who can do something with
what happened to him, because you are one.

Where the chair goes

   So what does a man do with an empty chair? You start by admitting it's there.
I've watched grown men, big men, go quiet and say "I don't really think about it"
when I ask about their fathers, and I've learned that sentence means the exact
opposite. You think about it every day. You think about it in the doorway. Say it
out loud, once, in words. "My father left." "My father was there and I never knew
him." "My father scared me." "My father was good and there's still a place he didn't
reach." Whatever it is, it needs to be said by you.
   Then you grieve it, which is the thing men don't do. We get angry about it, or we
get numb about it, or we get busy, and we call all three of those "dealing with it."
Grief is different. Grief is letting yourself feel the actual size of what you didn't get:
the father who wasn't at the game, the hand that wasn't on your shoulder, the
words you needed at fourteen and are still waiting for at forty. That's a real loss, as
real as a death, and it deserves to be mourned rather than managed. David wrote a
line about this that I come back to more than almost any other.




76                                                                          The Empty Chair
  Psalm 27:10 (NIV)
  Though my father and mother forsake me, the LORD will receive me.



   David wrote that, the man his father left in the field. He doesn't pretend the
forsaking didn't happen. He names it, and then he says where he went with it.
  That's the third thing, and it's the whole of chapter 10, so I'll only point at it
now. There is a Father who does not leave. The empty chair is not the last word
about fatherhood. It's the first word, and it's a lie, and the rest of this part of the
book is about replacing it with the truth.

  Psalm 68:5-6 (NIV)
  A father to the fatherless, a defender of widows, is God in his holy dwelling. God sets the lonely in
  families, he leads out the prisoners with singing; but the rebellious live in a sun-scorched land.



   A father to the fatherless. That isn't a metaphor. It's a description of who He is.

The chair in different rooms

   The chair comes back differently at different points in a man's life, and I want to
speak to a few of you directly.
  If you're young and your father is still alive and still absent, you're probably still
hoping. Hoping he'll call, hoping he'll come to the thing, hoping the next
conversation is the one where he finally says it. I'm not going to tell you to stop
hoping. I am going to tell you to stop waiting on him to become a man before you
become one, because you can't build your manhood on a foundation that was never
poured. Build it on the Father who's here. If your dad shows up later, good; you'll
have something to offer him.
    If you've just become a father yourself, this is the season when the chair comes
back hardest. You'll hold your son and feel a fear so specific it takes your breath:
what if I do to him what was done to me? Hear this: the fear means you're paying
attention. Men who repeat the chair are almost always the ones who never looked at
it, and you're looking, and that isn't a guarantee, but it's a start, and you can do the
rest with help.
   If you're divorced, I have to say a hard thing. You may be the empty chair now.


The Empty Chair                                                                                           77
Your kids eat at a table where you don't sit. I'm not saying that to shame you; some
of you fought like lions to stay and couldn't. I'm saying it because a man who's
honest about being the empty chair can do something about it, and a man who's
defensive about it can't. Kenny told me the day he stopped explaining the divorce to
his kids and started simply showing up was the day things turned: every exchange,
every game, every Tuesday call at 7:30, including the months when his son said
"nothing" to every question. He couldn't fill the chair at their mother's table. He
could make sure there was one at his that was never empty when they came.
    And if you're older and your father died before the conversation ever happened,
I'm sorry, and I mean that. There's a particular grief in a door that closed before you
reached it. You can still say what you needed to say. Say it to God, who knew your
father better than you did, and say it to a brother, and write it down. The
conversation you never got to have can still be had, just not with him, and it will
still do something in you.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of eighteen feels the chair on the day he moves out, when there's
nobody to help him carry the boxes and nobody to tell him what a man does with a
first apartment. He'll feel it again at his first real job when the other men talk about
what their fathers taught them, and he has nothing to say. The takeaway for him is
that the ache is accurate and not shameful, and that the Father who does not leave is
willing to teach him the things nobody taught him, mostly through older men he'll
have to go find.
   A man in his forties feels the chair at his father's funeral, or at his father's bedside,
when the conversation he'd been waiting for thirty years is suddenly never going to
happen. Anthony's father is still alive, and Anthony feels the chair every time the
phone shows his father's name and he's nine years old for a second. The takeaway
for him is that the conversation can still be had, with God and with a brother, even
when it can't be had with the man.

Anthony's doorway

  I asked Anthony what he thought his father would say if he could see the kitchen
Anthony stands outside of every night: three kids, a wife, and a table where every
chair is full.


78                                                                           The Empty Chair
   He was quiet for a long time. Then he said, "I don't know. I don't know what
he'd say. I never knew what he'd say."
  And that's the inheritance, right there: not the leaving so much as the not
knowing. A boy who never learned what his father thought of him grows into a
man who doesn't know what any father thinks of him, including the one in heaven,
and so he stands in doorways.
   Here is what is yours, Anthony. You get to walk in. You get to sit down in a chair
that's yours and stay in it. You get to be the man your kids read before they can
read, and you get to make sure what they read is different from what you read. You
didn't choose the empty chair. But you choose, every night, whether to stand in the
doorway or come to the table.
   Come to the table. The Father who does not leave is already sitting there.

Before you turn the page

   Some of you have read this chapter with a tightness in your chest that you've
carried since you were a boy, and I want to say something to that boy before we go
on. What happened to you happened, and God knows every line of it, and He isn't
shrugging. He isn't going to ask you to pretend the chair was full. He's going to ask
you to bring the empty one to Him, and then He's going to do something with it
that I've watched Him do in men of every age in this book. May the Father who sets
the lonely in families receive you the way David said He would, and may you find
the courage to walk into your own kitchen tonight and sit down.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because a chair you won't look at keeps running your house from the garage: finish
  this sentence out loud, "The chair at the end of my table was ___," and then say one
  thing it taught you.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because grief that's written down stops leaking out sideways: write your father a
  letter you won't send, telling him what you needed and didn't get and what you're
  grateful for, if anything, and keep it. You'll want it in chapter 12.




The Empty Chair                                                                          79
TELL A BROTHER
Because the sentence about your father is easier to carry once another man has heard it:
read him the sentence you named, not the letter unless you want to, and let him say "me
too" or "I'm sorry" without rushing past it.
CARRY THIS
The chair explains you. It doesn't excuse you. What sat in it is what you inherited, and
what you do now is yours.




80                                                                          The Empty Chair
CHAPTER 9



Anger, the Itch, and the Drift
   Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
  Three men sat at the same table at a Thursday breakfast in a town I won't name.
They didn't know each other well, and they would have told you they didn't have
much in common.
   The first one had thrown a drill across his shop the day before because a screw
stripped. Not at anybody; he just threw it, and then stood there breathing hard
while his apprentice pretended not to have seen. He has a wife who has started to
flinch when his voice changes pitch, and he hates that about himself, and he doesn't
know what to do about it, so mostly he gets angry about that too.
   The second one had been up until two in the morning on his phone again, and
not reading the news. He's single and twenty-six and a decent man who's in church
every Sunday, and he has lied to every accountability partner he's ever had. He tells
himself it doesn't hurt anybody. He knows it does, and he knows who.
   The third one hadn't done anything at all, which is the point. He's fifty-two,
married twenty-six years, and he can't remember the last time he felt much of
anything. He works, he comes home, he watches whatever's on, he goes to bed, and
his wife told him last month that she feels like she's living with a roommate, and the
honest truth is that he didn't feel much about that either.
   Three men, and anger, the itch, and the drift. What none of them knew, sitting
there over their eggs, was that they had the same wound. They'd simply taken three
different exits from it.

Three exits from the same pain

   Last chapter I said every man inherited something from the chair at the end of



Anger, the Itch, and the Drift                                                      81
the table. This chapter is about where that inheritance goes, because pain doesn't sit
still in a man. It moves, and it has to go somewhere, and in my experience it nearly
always goes to one of three places.
  It goes out, as anger. It goes down, as appetite. Or it goes away, as absence. Every
man I've ever sat with has a home exit, the one he takes when things get heavy, and
most of them have never noticed that it's an exit at all. They think it's their
personality, and it isn't. It's the way they learned, a long time ago, to not feel
something, and the way has become a habit they mistake for themselves.
   I want to look at each one honestly, and I want to be careful about two things
while I do. I'm not going to soften any of them, because every one of them is
destroying something in your house. And I'm not going to shame you for any of
them, because shame is fuel for all three. Honest and warm, both at once. That's the
whole book anyway.

Anger

     Anger is the exit that looks like strength, which is exactly why men like it.
   The man who threw the drill would tell you he has a temper, the way you'd say a
truck has a stiff clutch. But listen to what anger actually is underneath. Nearly every
time I've sat with an angry man long enough to get below the anger, I've found one
of three things waiting there: fear, or shame, or helplessness. He's afraid the money
isn't going to work. He's ashamed of something he did or something he is. He's
watching something he loves go wrong and he can't stop it. And instead of feeling
any of those, which would feel like weakness, he feels the one thing that feels like
power, which is anger, and he gets mad.
  I'll put it another way, and I've never found a better one. A great deal of male
anger is grief with its fists up. The Bible doesn't tell you not to be angry. It tells you
what to do with anger, and it's specific about it.

     Ephesians 4:26-27 (NIV)
     'In your anger do not sin': Do not let the sun go down while you are still angry, and do not give the
     devil a foothold.



   In your anger, the verse says, and notice that it assumes you'll have it. Then it
gives you a deadline, which is sundown, and a warning, which is that this is where


82                                                                                 Anger, the Itch, and the Drift
the enemy gets a foot inside the door of your house. Not through the big dramatic
sins. Through the anger you carried to bed and woke up with and carried to bed
again, until it was simply the weather in your home and nobody remembered a time
when it wasn't.

  Proverbs 29:11 (NIV)
  Fools give full vent to their rage, but the wise bring calm in the end.



   Full vent is the drill across the shop and the voice that changes pitch. A fool
thinks letting it all out is honesty. A wise man knows that what comes out in full
vent isn't the truth at all; it's the pressure. The truth is underneath, and it's usually
quieter and more frightened than the rage.
   If anger is your exit, here is what's yours to do. Learn your ninety seconds,
because anger in the body peaks and starts to fall within about a minute and a half if
you don't feed it. Walk out to the truck. Don't say the sentence you've got loaded.
Let it peak and let it pass. Then come back inside and say what's underneath it: "I'm
scared about the money." "I feel like I'm failing at this." "I don't know how to fix it
and that makes me crazy." Your wife can work with a scared man. She cannot work
with a storm. And whatever it takes, don't let the sun go down on it. The apology,
the conversation, the "I'm not okay but I'm not leaving." Before bed, every time.
And know that a man who conquers this doesn't become soft. He becomes the
strongest man in the room.

  Proverbs 16:32 (NIV)
  Better a patient person than a warrior, one with self-control than one who takes a city.




The itch

   The second exit goes down, into appetite, and I'm going to talk mostly about the
one that has the most men by the throat, which is sexual. But everything I say
applies to the bottle and the plate and the shopping cart and the endless scroll and
the sports book. It's all the same itch: comfort, now, without cost, and without
having to be known.
   The twenty-six-year-old on his phone at two in the morning isn't chasing



Anger, the Itch, and the Drift                                                               83
pleasure, mostly. I've talked to enough men to know that. He's chasing relief, relief
from loneliness, from failure, from a Tuesday that went badly, from a body that's
tired and a heart that hasn't been seen by anyone in a week. The screen doesn't ask
anything of him. It doesn't need him to be a man. That's the pull, and that's exactly
why it's a lie, because being needed to be a man is the very thing that would heal
him. James lays out how it works in a few lines, and I've never seen them improved
on.

     James 1:14-15 (NIV)
     but each person is tempted when they are dragged away by their own evil desire and enticed. Then,
     after desire has conceived, it gives birth to sin; and sin, when it is full-grown, gives birth to death.



   Dragged away is the feeling, isn't it. Nobody plans to be up at two. And the end
of the road is death, not usually a dramatic one, but the slow death of a man's ability
to be present, to want a real woman, to feel a real thing, to look his future wife in
the eye without a hidden room in his chest.
   Let me tell you what the itch is actually stealing, because most men have never
done the accounting. If you're single, it's stealing from the woman you haven't met
yet. Every image trains you to want something she isn't, and she's going to pay for
that training on her honeymoon and for years after it. If you're married, it's stealing
from your wife directly, and she knows something's off even when she doesn't
know what. Jesus said that what a man looks at with intent is already adultery, and
He wasn't being dramatic. He was being accurate.

     1 Corinthians 6:18-20 (NIV)
     Flee from sexual immorality. All other sins a person commits are outside the body, but whoever sins
     sexually, sins against their own body. Do you not know that your bodies are temples of the Holy Spirit,
     who is in you, whom you have received from God? You are not your own; you were bought at a price.
     Therefore honor God with your bodies.



   Flee, Paul says, and he doesn't say manage or moderate. Flee, the way Joseph ran
out of a house with a woman in it who wanted him, because there's no dignified
way to fight this one, and there's only running. If the itch is your exit, here's what's
yours. Tell one man the whole truth: not "I struggle with purity," but the actual
truth of what and how often and since when and what it's costing you. The itch
lives on secrecy and it begins to die the day a brother knows. Cut the supply, with



84                                                                                   Anger, the Itch, and the Drift
filters on every device and the password held by that brother, and the phone
charging in the kitchen at night instead of the bedroom. Yes, that feels like a rule for
a child, and you've been acting like a child at two in the morning, so a child's rule is
appropriate until you're not. Fill the hour, because the itch comes in the empty
spaces, and a man who knows where his empty spaces are can put something real in
them: a man to call, a walk, a book with pages, a prayer with words. And deal with
the loneliness, because that's what this actually is. Chapter 13 is about brothers, and
I'll tell you now what I'll tell you then. The itch shrinks in men who are known.

The drift

   The third exit is the quiet one, and it's the one I worry about most in men past
forty-five, because nobody notices it and it feels like nothing's wrong.
   The drift is a man leaving without moving. He's in the house and he goes to
work, and he hasn't blown up and he hasn't blown it. He's simply not there. His
attention lives somewhere else: the game, the news, fantasy football, a boat, the job,
the phone, the garage. His wife stopped asking him things a while ago because his
answers were "fine" and "whatever you think," and his kids learned early that Dad's
body was in the recliner and Dad was somewhere else.
   This is passivity, the thing we talked about in chapter 4, gone chronic. And
what's under it is usually one of two things. Either the man got hurt somewhere
along the way and decided, without ever saying so, that caring was too expensive.
Or he got tired, honestly tired, and rest turned into retreat, and he never came back
from it. The Bible has a picture for this that's almost funny until you see your own
house in it.

  Ecclesiastes 10:18 (NIV)
  Through laziness, the rafters sag; because of idle hands, the house leaks.



   Nobody kicked the rafters. Nobody punched a hole in the roof. The house
simply leaked, slowly, because the man who should have been on the ladder was in
the chair. The drift doesn't destroy a home in a night. It destroys it over fifteen
years, one evening at a time, and by the time anybody names it the rafters are already
bowed.
   I want to speak straight to older men here, because the drift is the exit that comes


Anger, the Itch, and the Drift                                                        85
for you hardest once the working years end. You've had a place to be and a reason to
get up for forty years, and then it stops, and there's a version of retirement that is
really just the drift with a fishing license. The television gets more of you than your
wife does. You get angry about the news, which is the drift and the anger holding
hands. Roy told me he watched three men from his old unit go that way in the first
five years after they turned in their badges, and he'd started to go too. "I was a
spectator in my own house," he said. "I'd never been a spectator in my life." What
pulled him back was a single decision: he got up before his wife every morning and
made the coffee and sat with her while she drank it. That's a small thing. It's also a
man refusing to drift, one morning at a time, and it changed the whole shape of his
retirement.
   If the drift is your exit, here's what's yours. Name it to your wife: "I've been gone.
I've been in the house and I've been gone, and I'm sorry." She knows already.
Hearing you say it will change something. Take one thing back, not your whole life
but one evening a week with the television off and your attention in the room, one
question a day to your wife or your kid that you actually wait for the answer to.
And find out what you stopped caring about and why, because somewhere back
there a man decided it wasn't worth it, and you need to go find that man and talk to
him. You might need a brother or a counselor to help you find him, and that isn't
weakness; that's a man on a ladder looking at his rafters.

The same root

  I said the three men at that breakfast had the same wound. Let me show you
how.
    The angry man is a boy who learned that feeling scared got him hurt, so he traded
it for something that felt like power. The itching man is a boy who learned he
wouldn't be seen, so he found a place that would give him something like comfort
without requiring him to be known. The drifting man is a boy who learned that
caring cost too much, so he stopped.
  Every exit is a way to not feel the empty chair. Every exit works, for a while,
which is why men keep taking them. And every exit leads out of the house.
