   I'm not going to tell you to simply stop, because you've tried that. The men who
actually get free of these things get free the same way, and it isn't by gritting their
teeth harder. It's by taking the pain somewhere it can be healed instead of


86                                                               Anger, the Itch, and the Drift
somewhere it can be numbed. That's the next chapter. There is a Father who does
not leave, and He isn't afraid of your anger, and He isn't disgusted by your itch, and
He hasn't drifted from you while you drifted from Him.
  He knows the boy under the exit. He's been waiting for the man to bring him
home.

The exits in different houses

   Let me turn toward a few of you, because the exits show up differently in
different seasons and you may not recognize yours.
   If you're young and single, the itch is probably your loudest exit, and I've said
what I have to say about it. But watch the drift too. A man can drift at twenty-six,
into games and screens and a job he doesn't care about and a church he attends
without ever being known in, and I'd rather you notice it now than at fifty.
   If there's a baby in your house, anger is going to surprise you. You didn't think
you were an angry man, and then it's three in the morning and the baby won't stop
and your wife says something and you hear a voice come out of you that you last
heard from your father. That's exhaustion opening a door your father built. Take
your ninety seconds. Go out to the truck. Then come back and say the true thing:
"I'm so tired I'm scared of myself." That sentence will save your marriage more than
once this year.
   If you're divorced, you may be taking all three exits at once in the first year or
two, and I understand why: anger at her, the itch because you're alone and you
weren't, the drift because caring is what got you here. Kenny said the first year after
his divorce he was "a mad, lonely ghost." What brought him back was one older
man at his church who called him every Tuesday night, asked him the same three
questions, and wouldn't let him hang up until he'd answered them. You'll meet that
method in chapter 13, and it works.
   And if you're older, watch the drift and the anger together, because those two
like to travel as a pair in a man past sixty, and they usually arrive by way of a
television. The remedy is the same as it was at thirty: get up, make the coffee, sit
with her, and ask a question and wait for the answer.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.



Anger, the Itch, and the Drift                                                         87
   At nineteen the itch is the loudest exit, and I've said what I have to say about it,
but the drift is already starting for a good many young men in ways they don't
recognize: a job they don't care about, a screen that eats their evenings, a church
they attend without being known in. Eli, the young man at the parts counter, told
me he'd noticed that he hadn't felt strongly about anything in a year, and that this
had frightened him more than any temptation. The takeaway for him is that
numbness at nineteen isn't peace; it's a warning light, and the fix is a brother and a
real thing to care about.
   At forty-five the drift and the itch travel together. A man's marriage has gone
quiet, the work is the only place that claps, and a woman at the office laughs at his
jokes. Walt was three years into that pattern before his wife said her sentence in the
kitchen. The takeaway for him is that the affair he's certain he'd never have starts
with the drift he's certain isn't happening.

The Man who took none of them

  Jesus got angry, and He turned over tables in the temple, and He looked at
hard-hearted men with something the Gospels are willing to call anger, and He
never once sinned in it. He knew what it was for and He aimed it and He put it
down.
   Jesus was tempted, in every way we are, and Hebrews says it flatly: He did not
sin. Every itch a man has ever felt, He felt the pull of, alone, in a desert, for forty
days, and He did not go down.
   Jesus never drifted, not once. When the time came to go to Jerusalem He set His
face and went. When His men fell asleep, He stayed awake. When everything in
Him wanted another way, He said "not as I will," and He stayed where He was.
   He took none of the exits, and He did it so that when you take one, there would
be a Man who could find you in the ditch and bring you back, without shame, and
show you the way He walked instead.
  You have a home exit. Now you know its name. Next chapter, we go to the
Father.

Before you turn the page

     You know your exit now, and if you're like most men you feel a little exposed by


88                                                                 Anger, the Itch, and the Drift
knowing it. I'd like you to hear that the exposure is a mercy. The exit was running
your house from the dark, and now it has a name, and a thing with a name can be
brought to the Father. He isn't disgusted by your anger or your itch or your drift.
He knows the boy underneath every one of them, and He's been waiting for the
man to bring the boy home. May He meet you in the truck, at the screen, and in the
recliner, and may He give you one small refusal this week that becomes a habit.

   YOUR TURN



   NAME IT
   Because an exit you've mistaken for your personality can't be closed until you see it
   as an exit: say which one is yours, anger or the itch or the drift, in a full sentence that
   starts with "When things get heavy, I."
   DO THIS WEEK
   Because each exit has a first move that fits in a week: if it's anger, take your ninety
   seconds and then say what's under it; if it's the itch, tell one man the whole truth and
   set up the filter with him holding the password; if it's the drift, give one evening to
   the room with the screens off and one real question asked and waited on.
   TELL A BROTHER
   Because the exits live on nobody knowing: tell him your exit and the last time you
   took it, and ask him his, and neither of you gets to say "I'm fine."
   CARRY THIS
   A great deal of male anger is grief with its fists up. Under every exit is a boy who's
   trying not to feel the chair.




Anger, the Itch, and the Drift                                                               89
90   The Father Who Does Not Leave
CHAPTER 10



The Father Who Does Not Leave
   Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
   Watch a boy at a Little League game and you'll see him do something between
innings, or after a hit, or after a strikeout, especially after a strikeout. He scans the
bleachers.
   He isn't looking at the crowd. He's looking for one face, and you can tell from
the dugout which boys find it. Their shoulders drop a quarter of an inch and they
stop scanning and they go back to being a kid at a ballgame. You can tell which boys
don't find it, too. They scan longer, and they scan every inning, and some of them
are still scanning at thirty-eight in a doorway between a garage and a kitchen, not
knowing anymore what they're looking for, only that they haven't found it.
   I want to tell you in this chapter about a Father you can stop scanning for. He is
in the bleachers every inning. He was there before you looked up. He isn't leaving
between the fifth and the sixth to take a call. And because I know that for some of
you the word "father" is the whole problem, I want to start there.

The trouble with the word

   If I say "God is your Father," what happens inside you depends almost entirely on
the chair at the end of your childhood table.
  If the chair was empty, "Father" means someone who isn't coming. You'll believe
God exists the way you believe your dad exists: somewhere, doing something, not
here.
  If the chair was full and silent, "Father" means someone who's in the room and
has nothing to say to you. You'll pray the way a man talks to a newspaper.
   If the chair was dangerous, "Father" means someone whose mood you have to


The Father Who Does Not Leave                                                               91
read before you speak. You'll spend your Christian life trying to work out what God
is angry about today, and you'll be fairly sure it's you.
   If the chair was good, you're ahead of most, but you've inherited limits too. Your
father couldn't be everywhere, and he got tired, and he missed things, so somewhere
in you is the quiet assumption that God gets tired and misses things.
    Here is what I want you to see. You've been reading God through your dad.
Every man does it, because the chair at the end of the table was the only picture of a
father you had, so you held it up to heaven and assumed that was what was there.
And for most men that picture has been quietly poisoning their walk with God for
decades, because they've been relating to a Father they built out of an absence or a
silence or a storm.
   The Bible does the opposite. It tells you to read your dad through God. God is
the original and your father was a copy, and copies are always worse than the
original, some of them badly. So set the copy down for a minute, and let me show
you what the original is like, from the only Man who has seen Him.

     John 14:9 (NIV)
     Jesus answered: 'Don't you know me, Philip, even after I have been among you such a long time?
     Anyone who has seen me has seen the Father. How can you say, "Show us the Father"?'



   Anyone who has seen me has seen the Father. If you want to know what God the
Father is like, you don't look at your dad. You look at Jesus, and everything Jesus
did, the Father is like.

What He is like

   He comes toward you. The most famous story Jesus ever told about a father is
about a boy who left, spent everything, and came home stinking of pigs with a
speech ready. And the father doesn't wait at the door with his arms folded.

     Luke 15:20 (NIV)
     So he got up and went to his father. But while he was still a long way off, his father saw him and was
     filled with compassion for him; he ran to his son, threw his arms around him and kissed him.



     While he was still a long way off. The father had been watching the road; that's


92                                                                             The Father Who Does Not Leave
the only way you see someone a long way off. He'd been scanning the bleachers too,
in his way, and when he saw the face he ran. Grown men in that culture did not
run. This one did, and that is your Father. He isn't waiting for you to get it
together. He's watching the road. He tells you who you are before you've done
anything. When Jesus was baptized, before He had preached a sermon or healed
anyone or done a single thing you could put on a résumé, the Father spoke from
heaven.

  Matthew 3:17 (NIV)
  And a voice from heaven said, 'This is my Son, whom I love; with him I am well pleased.'



   My Son, whom I love, with whom I am well pleased: those are three things every
boy needs to hear from his father, and Jesus heard them at the start rather than at
the finish. That's the order the Father works in. He blesses first and then He sends
you to work. Your own father may have had it backward, making you earn what
should have been given, and the Father doesn't. If you're in Christ, that voice is
speaking over you right now, on a Tuesday, before you've done a thing. He carries
you, and Moses, looking back over forty years of wilderness, reached for a picture
that any father would recognize.

  Deuteronomy 1:31 (NIV)
  and in the wilderness. There you saw how the LORD your God carried you, as a father carries his
  son, all the way you went until you reached this place.



   As a father carries his son. You've seen it: a man with a sleeping boy over his
shoulder, coming in from the truck, careful on the steps so he won't wake him. All
the way you went, Moses says, and not part of the way, and not until you got too
heavy. He knows what you need before you ask. Jesus said so to men who were
anxious about food and clothes and tomorrow.

  Matthew 6:8 (NIV)
  Do not be like them, for your Father knows what you need before you ask him.



  The man who learned from the empty chair that needing things is dangerous
needs to sit with that sentence for a long time. You don't have to hide the need. He



The Father Who Does Not Leave                                                                       93
already sees it, and He isn't put off by it, because He's a Father. He disciplines you,
because you're His. I won't pretend this one is comfortable, but a man with a
dangerous father needs to hear it more than anyone, because he has discipline and
rage confused in his memory.

     Hebrews 12:7 (NIV)
     Endure hardship as discipline; God is treating you as his children. For what children are not
     disciplined by their father?



   God is treating you as his children. The hard season isn't proof that He left. It's
proof that He stayed. A father who doesn't care lets a boy do whatever he wants.
Your Father cares enough to shape you, and He never does it in a rage, and He never
does it to make you small. He does it to make you like His Son. And He does not
leave. This is the one the whole chapter hangs on, so hear it in His own words.

     Hebrews 13:5 (NIV)
     Keep your lives free from the love of money and be content with what you have, because God has said,
     'Never will I leave you; never will I forsake you.'



  Jesus said it a different way the night before He died, to men who were about to
watch Him taken and were about to scatter.

     John 14:18 (NIV)
     I will not leave you as orphans; I will come to you.



  Orphans is the word He chose, because He knew that's what they'd feel like, and
He knows that's what some of you have felt like since you were nine years old. And
He says: not you. Not on my watch. I will come to you.

The hardest thing for a man to do

   Now I have to tell you the truth about why this chapter, which ought to be the
easiest one in the book, is actually the hardest.
   A man who has fathered himself since he was a boy doesn't know how to be
fathered. It isn't that he doesn't believe God is a Father; he'd tell you he does. It's



94                                                                            The Father Who Does Not Leave
that when it comes to actually being carried, actually being told who he is, actually
letting somebody else hold the weight, every muscle in him locks up. He has been
the corner post so long that leaning on anything feels like falling.
  Anthony said it exactly. "I know God's my Father. I just don't know what to do
with that. I don't know how to let Him be one. I never learned how to be
somebody's son."
  That's the honest sentence, and I'd rather you say it than fake something you
don't feel.
    Here is what I've watched work in men who learned to be sons late. Call Him
Father out loud. Not "God" and not "Lord," but Father, in the truck, alone, when
it's awkward. Paul says the Spirit inside you is already doing this.

  Romans 8:15 (NIV)
  The Spirit you received does not make you slaves, so that you live in fear again; rather, the Spirit you
  received brought about your adoption to sonship. And by him we cry, 'Abba, Father.'



  Abba is the word a small boy used. You're allowed to use it. It will feel strange in
your mouth for a while. Say it until it doesn't.
   Bring Him your exit. Whatever your home exit is, anger or the itch or the drift,
take it to Him first, before the brother and before the counselor, and don't clean it
up on the way. "Father, I'm about to blow up at my kids and I don't know why."
"Father, I'm reaching for my phone again and I hate it." "Father, I've been gone and
I don't know how to come back." He isn't disgusted. He's the father watching the
road.
  Let Him tell you who you are. Every day, read something He said about His sons
and say it back to Him as true about you, not as a mantra but the way a boy repeats
what his dad just told him. "You said I'm your son. You said you love me. You said
you're pleased with me because I'm in Christ." A man who was never told who he
was needs to be told many times, and the Father is patient.
    Let yourself be carried. This is the one that will cost you. Somewhere this year
something is going to be too heavy, and your instinct will be to grit it out alone the
way you always have. Don't do it. Say, out loud, "I can't carry this. Carry me." Then
let a brother help. Then let your wife see that you need help. That isn't you failing
to be a man. That's you finally learning to be a son, which every real man is first.


The Father Who Does Not Leave                                                                                95
   And stay in the house. A son who's learning to be fathered doesn't wander off.
He's in the Word, where the Father talks. He's in the church, where the Father's
other children are. He's in prayer, where the Father listens. A man who only visits
the house on holidays never learns what the Father's voice sounds like on a Tuesday.

What this does to your other father

  Here's something I didn't expect when I started walking this road with men.
Once a man begins to be fathered by God, he starts to see his own father differently.
   Not more softly, necessarily. Sometimes more clearly, which is harder. But he
stops needing his father to be something the man never was, because he's getting it
from Somebody else, and the scanning stops. A man who has stopped scanning can
look at his dad, whatever kind of chair he sat in, with a kind of steadiness. He can
honor what was good. He can name what wasn't without rage. He can forgive,
because he isn't waiting for a payment anymore. He may even be able to reach
toward the man, which is what chapter 12 is about.
  None of that is possible while you're still trying to get from your dad what only
your Father can give. The chair at the end of your table can't fill the chair in your
chest. It was never big enough, and only He is.

Learning it in your season

  Let me turn toward a few of you, because sonship gets learned at different ages
and it's never learned too late.
   If you're young, learn this now, because the men who find the Father at
twenty-five are spared thirty years of scanning. Don't wait until you have a family to
start being a son.
   If you've just had a baby, you're about to understand something about the Father
that you couldn't have understood before. You'll look at your child asleep and feel a
love that has nothing to do with anything he's done, because he hasn't done
anything; he's simply yours, and that is how the Father looks at you. Let the baby
teach you.
   If you're divorced, the Father didn't leave when your marriage did. I know it felt
as though He had. Some of you stopped praying because you figured He was as
disappointed in you as everyone else seemed to be. He was watching the road the


96                                                           The Father Who Does Not Leave
whole time, and He's watching it now. Come home.
   And if you're older, it isn't too late to be somebody's son. Roy learned to call
God Father at sixty-eight, in his truck, in a parking lot, because he'd never once
done it in sixty-eight years and decided that was long enough. "It felt like a fool," he
told me. "Then it felt like coming in out of the wind."
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty is at the best age to learn it and the least likely to think he
needs to. He's just gotten free of his parents and the last thing he wants is another
father. But the men who find the Father at twenty are spared thirty years of
scanning bleachers, and the takeaway for him is that calling God "Father" out loud
in a parked car at twenty is the single most useful thing he could do this year, more
useful than any career decision.
    A man of forty-five learns it when he finally admits that he's been praying to a
newspaper, or to a storm, or to an empty seat, and that his whole walk with God has
been shaped by a copy instead of the original. Anthony's honest sentence was that
he'd never learned to be somebody's son. The takeaway for him is that the sentence
is the start, and that a man who says it out loud to God and to a brother has begun
to be fathered.

Stop scanning

   Back to the boy in the dugout. He's looking for one face, and when he finds it he
can go back to being a kid. He can strike out. He can boot a grounder. He can get a
hit and not have to look up to see whether anybody noticed, because he already
knows Somebody did.
   That's what the Father does for a man. He lets you stop scanning and stop
auditioning. He lets you fail on a Tuesday and get up on Wednesday, because your
standing with Him was never about the Tuesday. It was about the Son, and it's
settled.

  1 John 3:1 (NIV)
  See what great love the Father has lavished on us, that we should be called children of God! And that
  is what we are! The reason the world does not know us is that it did not know him.




The Father Who Does Not Leave                                                                             97
     And that is what we are. Not what we're trying to become. What we are: sons.
   He's in the bleachers. He has been there every inning. Drop your shoulders and
go back to the game.

Before you turn the page

   I've asked you to do something in this chapter that may be the hardest thing in
the book, which is to let yourself be carried by Someone after a lifetime of carrying
yourself. I don't expect it to come easily, and neither does He. He's a Father, and
fathers know that sons learn slowly. So say the word tomorrow morning, badly, and
then again the next day, and let Him do the rest. May the Father who watched the
road for the prodigal watch it for you, and may you drop your shoulders a quarter
of an inch this week and go back to the game.

     YOUR TURN



     NAME IT
     Because you can't stop praying to a false father until you've noticed you've been
     doing it: say which picture you've been holding up to heaven, not coming or silent
     or dangerous or good with a gap, and then say what Jesus shows the Father is
     actually like.
     DO THIS WEEK
     Because a man learns to be a son by using a son's word: every morning before your
     feet hit the floor, say "Father" out loud, and then one sentence to Him about the
     day, seven days in a row.
     TELL A BROTHER
     Because being prayed over is practice at being carried: tell him which false father
     you've been praying to, ask him to pray for you out loud right there, and let him.
     CARRY THIS
     You've been reading God through your dad. Read your dad through God. Anyone
     who has seen Jesus has seen the Father.




98                                                                The Father Who Does Not Leave
CHAPTER 11



When Home Never Handed You Off
  Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
  Micah asked me a question at his grandfather's funeral that I've never forgotten.
He was twenty-six, standing at the back of a fellowship hall with a paper plate of
somebody's casserole, watching the older men from the church talk in a loose circle
by the coffee.
  "When did you know you were a man?"
   I asked him what he meant. He said he honestly couldn't tell me when it had
happened for him, or whether it had happened at all. He had a job, he paid his rent,
and he'd voted in two presidential elections, and he still felt, standing in that
fellowship hall, like a kid who had wandered into the men's section and was waiting
for somebody to notice and either send him out or tell him he could stay.
   "Nobody ever said it," he told me. "Nobody ever said, 'You're a man now.' I think
I've been waiting for somebody to say it."
   He isn't unusual; he's only more honest than most, because most men in this
country were never handed off. They went from boy to man the way a river crosses
from one county into the next: at some point they were across the line, and there
was no sign, and nobody was standing there to say so.

What a handoff is

   For most of human history, boys didn't become men by accident. They became
men because men made them men, on purpose, at a certain point, with words and a
weight and a welcome. The details changed from one people to the next, but the
shape was the same everywhere. The older men took the boy from the women, put
him through something, told him what a man was, gave him a man's responsibility,



When Home Never Handed You Off                                                    99
and brought him into their company. He went to sleep a boy and woke up a man,
not because his body had changed overnight but because the men had said so and
meant it.
 The Bible does this too. It isn't just something anthropologists write about.
Watch David, dying, hand off to Solomon.

  1 Kings 2:2-3 (NIV)
  'I am about to go the way of all the earth,' he said. 'So be strong, act like a man, and observe what the
  LORD your God requires: Walk in obedience to him, and keep his decrees and commands, his laws
  and regulations, as written in the Law of Moses. Do this so that you may prosper in all you do and
  wherever you go'



   Look at what's in that passage. A man, older, about to leave. A word: act like a
man, and here is what that means. A weight: a kingdom, and God's law to keep
inside it. Moses did the same thing with Joshua in front of the whole people. Paul
did it with Timothy, whose own father barely rates a mention in Scripture, and he
did it with his hands.

  2 Timothy 1:6 (NIV)
  For this reason I remind you to fan into flame the gift of God, which is in you through the laying on
  of my hands.



   Through the laying on of my hands. Paul had put his hands on the young man
and said, in effect, "You. You're one of us. Here's your work." And years later, when
Timothy was frightened and tired and thinking about quitting, Paul reminded him
of the moment. Remember when I handed you off. Fan it back into flame.
   That's what a handoff is, and it has four things in it: a man, a word, a weight, and
a welcome. Most of you got none of them. Or you got the weight without the
word, from a father who piled responsibility on you at twelve and never once told
you that you were doing a man's job. Or you got words without weight, from a dad
who said "you're the man of the house now" on his way out the door and never
came back to see whether you were.

The counterfeits

   A boy who isn't handed off doesn't stop wanting to be. He goes looking for it


100                                                                       When Home Never Handed You Off
somewhere else, and the world has counterfeits ready for him.
   The first is sex. For a lot of boys, the first time is the closest thing to a rite of
passage they'll ever get, and they treat it that way, and it doesn't work, because
there's no older man in the room and no word and no weight. There's only a girl
who didn't sign up to make him a man and couldn't have if she'd wanted to.
  The second is a paycheck. A job feels like manhood, and it's part of manhood,
but a boy with a paycheck is still a boy. Any foreman can tell you that.
   The third is a fight, or a truck, or a drink, or turning eighteen or twenty-one.
Every one of them is a boy trying to hand himself off, and the trouble is that you
can't hand yourself off. That's the whole point of the thing. A handoff is something
a man receives from men.
    The fourth counterfeit is the quietest, and it's the one Micah was living inside,
and it's simply waiting. A man who was never handed off can spend his entire adult
life waiting for someone to tell him he's allowed in the circle by the coffee. He
defers to men his own age. He shrinks around older men. He's forty-five and still
feels like he's auditioning for a room he has been standing in for twenty years.
   And here's what that costs. A man who doesn't know he's a man doesn't take a
man's weight, and why would he, when he's still waiting for someone to tell him it's
his? He doesn't go first, and he doesn't reject passivity, and he can't, really, because
underneath everything he's still a boy waiting for permission. Every one of the four
marks depends on this. You can't act like a man for very long if nobody, including
you, has ever said that you are one.

Jacob's night

   There's a man in Genesis who spent his whole life trying to get a handoff
through the wrong door.
    Jacob wanted his father's blessing so badly that he stole it. He dressed up as his
brother, lied to a blind old man, and got the words. And it didn't work. He spent
the next twenty years running and scheming, working for a man who cheated him,
still trying to get somewhere. The stolen blessing didn't make him a man. It made
him a fugitive.
 Then one night, alone, on the road home to face everything he'd run from, a
Man came and wrestled him until dawn. And Jacob did the one thing he had never


When Home Never Handed You Off                                                             101
done in his life. He stopped scheming and simply held on.

  Genesis 32:26 (NIV)
  Then the man said, 'Let me go, for it is daybreak.' But Jacob replied, 'I will not let you go unless you
  bless me.'



   I will not let you go unless you bless me. That's a boy who has finally stopped
stealing what he needs and started asking for it, from the only One who could
actually give it. And he got it. He got a new name, Israel, which meant he wasn't
Jacob the grabber anymore. He got a limp, which meant he'd never walk like a boy
again. And he walked toward his brother the next morning like a man.
   The handoff Jacob had spent his life trying to get from his father, he finally
received from God, on the ground, in the dark, somewhere in his forties. If you're
reading this and you're somewhere in your forties and it's dark, I want you to hear
that. It is not too late for you to be handed off.

How a man receives it now

  So how does a grown man get what should have been given to him at fourteen?
Here's what I've watched work.
   Hear it from the Father first. We said in the last chapter that the Father speaks
identity before performance, and He has already said the words over you. "This is
my son, whom I love." If you're in Christ, that isn't a hope; it's a settled fact. The
handoff you never got from your dad, you have from your Father, and it outranks
anything a man could say to you. Start there or nothing else will hold.
    Then ask an older man to say it. This is going to feel like the most awkward thing
in this book, and I'm asking you to do it anyway. Find a man older than you who's
living the four marks, and tell him the truth: nobody ever told me I was a man, and
would you? And then let him do it. Let him put a hand on your shoulder and say it.
Chapter 14 is about finding that man. You'd be surprised how many older men
have spent their whole lives waiting for a younger man to ask them for something
real.
  Be welcomed by brothers. A handoff has a welcome in it, and you need a circle of
men who know you and count you as one of them. Not a crowd; a few. Micah's
entire question at that funeral was really "am I allowed in the circle," and the honest


102                                                                       When Home Never Handed You Off
answer is that the circle should have pulled him in years before, and if it didn't, he
needed to go find one that would. Chapter 13 is about that.
   Take the weight before you feel ready. There's something in the David and
Solomon handoff that I missed for years. David says "act like a man," and it's a
command. Solomon didn't feel like a man; he was young and scared and said so to
God a chapter later. David told him to act like one anyway, and the acting was part
of how he became one. Some of you are waiting to feel handed off before you pick
up the weight, and I'd ask you to reverse the order. Pick up the weight and carry it
for a year. The men around you will begin treating you like a man because you're
doing a man's work, and one day you'll notice that the feeling has caught up.
   And mark it by writing down the date: the day you decided, the day the older
man said it, the day you asked God on the ground in the dark. Men need markers,
and Jacob got a limp; you may have to settle for a date on the inside cover of your
Bible. But have something you can point to and say, that's when. Not because the
date is magic, but because a man who can't point to when he became a man will
always half-wonder whether he did.

When the man in the chair won't hand you off

   Cole's father is alive and present and has never once, in thirty-three years, told his
son he did a good job. Cole rebuilt a transmission at seventeen, and his father found
the one bolt he'd torqued wrong. Cole got a promotion at twenty-nine, and his
father asked why it had taken so long. Cole is still trying; he calls every Sunday and
fixes things at the old man's place, and he's waiting for a sentence that isn't coming.
   If that's you, I have to tell you something hard. Some fathers will never hand you
off, not because you haven't earned it but because they never received it themselves
and don't know how, or because keeping you a boy is the only way they know to
stay a man. You can honor that father, and you should, and chapter 12 is about
how. But you cannot wait on him. Every year you spend waiting for his sentence is
a year you don't spend being the man God has already said you are.
   Cole finally heard it from a seventy-year-old deacon named Hollis who had
watched him serve for six years and one Sunday pulled him aside by his truck and
said, "Son, I've been watching you. You're a good man. I'd trust you with anything
I've got." Cole sat in the cab of his truck and cried for twenty minutes. He was
thirty-three years old, and it was the first time.


When Home Never Handed You Off                                                        103
   That was a handoff, and it didn't come from the chair. It came from a man who
was willing to stand in for the chair, and God arranges men like that for those who
ask.

The other side of this

   If you're the older man reading this, I need you to hear the rest, because you're
the other half of the problem and the other half of the solution.
  You probably never got handed off either. Most men your age didn't. And you
may be thinking that means you can't give what you didn't get, and that's wrong.
You've lived the four marks, or enough of them to know what they look like. You
have watched younger men and thought, that one's got it, and you never said so.
Saying so is the whole job.
   Roy began doing this at his Thursday table almost by accident. One of the men
in his thirties told him about a hard call he'd made at work, standing up to a boss
over something dishonest, and Roy said, "That's what a man does. You did right."
The young man didn't say anything. But he came back the next week early, and the
week after that, and a year later he told Roy that sentence was the first time any man
over fifty had ever told him he'd done right about anything.
   You don't need a ceremony for this. Chapter 29 is about ceremonies, and they're
good, and I'll tell you how to hold one. But most of the handoffs in Scripture were a
man, a word, a hand on a shoulder, and a "go." You can do that at a truck window
this week.

Handed off in every season

   If you're divorced, the divorce may have un-manned you in your own eyes. You
had a handoff of a kind, a wedding and a house and a role, and it came apart, and
now you're not sure what you are. Hear this, then: the Father's word over you
didn't get revoked in a courtroom. You're still His son, and you're still a man, and
the weight you're carrying now, children across two houses and a life rebuilt from a
one-bedroom apartment, is a man's weight, and you're carrying it. Let someone
older say so.
  If you're a young father, you're about to be the one who hands off, and you'll be
tempted to think you can't because you never got it. You can, and Part 4 will show



104                                                       When Home Never Handed You Off
you how, and you'll find that giving the handoff heals something in the giving.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of eighteen is the one the handoff was designed for, and he's the
one least likely to get it. Eli, who works the parts counter, was handed off by
accident: an older man on the church softball team watched him handle a bad call
from an umpire without losing his head, and said afterward, "That's how a man
does it," and Eli told me he thought about that sentence for a month. The takeaway
for him is that the handoff can come in pieces, from any man who's paying
attention, and that he should go find men who pay attention.
   A man of forty-four is Cole, waiting on a father who will never say it, and the
takeaway for him is the deacon by the truck: the handoff can come from a man who
stands in for the chair, and God arranges those men for those who ask.

Micah, five years on

   Micah is thirty-one now. A man named Dale, twenty years older, took him to
breakfast every other Tuesday for two years and one morning told him, plainly, that
he was a man and that it was time to stop waiting for permission to act like one.
Micah wrote the date inside his Bible. He got married last spring. And at his own
wedding, at the back of a different fellowship hall, he told me he'd caught himself
looking around at the older men by the coffee and realizing, for the first time, that
he wasn't waiting to be let in.
   He was one of them. You're allowed in the circle, and you always were, because
the Father already said so. Now go find a man who'll say it out loud, and then go be
a man who does.

Before you turn the page

   If nobody ever told you that you were a man, I'd like to be one voice that does,
though I know a page isn't a hand on the shoulder. You've carried weight and gone
first and kept getting up, and that's what a man does, and the Father who spoke
over His Son at the Jordan has spoken the same words over you. Go find a man
who'll say it out loud, and then go be a man who says it. May God give you the
awkward courage to ask, and the plain courage to tell, and may you write a date



When Home Never Handed You Off                                                    105
inside your Bible this year that you can point to for the rest of your life.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because a man who can't say whether he was handed off is still waiting on it: say
  who handed you off and when, or say plainly, "No one ever told me I was a man,"
  and name the counterfeit you went looking for instead.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because the handoff has to be asked for or given, and you're one or the other: if
  you're under fifty, ask one older man living the marks whether he sees a man in you
  and take whatever he says; if you're over fifty, tell one younger man one thing you've
  watched him do that a man does.
  TELL A BROTHER
  Because a marker nobody knows about is easy to lose: tell him whether you were
  handed off, and tell him the date you're going to write in your Bible and what it
  marks.
  CARRY THIS
  A man, a word, a weight, and a welcome. You can't hand yourself off, but the Father
  already did, and He has arranged men to say it out loud if you'll ask them.




106                                                         When Home Never Handed You Off
CHAPTER 12



Leave Without Slamming the Door
   Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
   There are two ways to fail at leaving home, and most men have done one of
them.
   The first is the one you're picturing. Eighteen or twenty-two or thirty, a fight in
the kitchen, a bag in the truck, and a door slammed hard enough to rattle the frame.
Then three states away and a phone call at Christmas if the man is feeling generous.
Some of you did it with more noise than that and some of you did it with less, but
the shape was the same. You left angry, and the anger came with you, and it's still
riding in the truck.
   The second way is quieter, and I see it more often. The man never left at all. He
moved out, maybe. He may be forty-four with a mortgage. But the center of gravity
in his life is still his parents' house. He can't make a decision without hearing his
father's voice on it. His wife has spent fifteen years competing with his mother and
losing. He calls his dad before he calls his pastor, and his dad still tells him what to
do, and he still does it. He didn't slam the door. He never walked through it. God's
design for a man is neither one, and it's in the second chapter of the Bible, before
there was a single broken family to complicate it.

  Genesis 2:24 (NIV)
  That is why a man leaves his father and mother and is united to his wife, and they become one flesh.



    A man leaves, the text says, and that's God talking, not a rebellious teenager, and
leaving is the design. Notice that it comes before "is united to his wife," which
means leaving isn't only a marriage thing. A single man has to leave too. A man's life
is supposed to have its own center, under God, and that center is not his parents'



Leave Without Slamming the Door                                                                          107
kitchen. But the same God who said "leave" also said this, and put it in the ten
commandments where nobody could miss it.

  Exodus 20:12 (NIV)
  Honor your father and your mother, so that you may live long in the land the LORD your God is
  giving you.



    Leave, and honor, both at once. A man who slams the door has left and failed to
honor. A man who never leaves has honored, after a fashion, and failed to leave.
The man God is after does both together, which is harder than either one alone, and
it's the subject of this chapter.

Why you have to leave

   Let me speak first to the man who never walked through the door, because he's
the one who doesn't think this chapter is for him.
   If you're married and your mother's opinion still outranks your wife's in your
own house, you haven't left. If your father can make you feel twelve years old with
one comment, and you arrange your decisions to avoid that comment, you haven't
left. If you're single and thirty-five and you've never made a major decision without
running it past your parents first, you haven't left.
   I'm not telling you to stop loving them, and I'm not telling you to stop listening
to them; a wise man takes counsel from his elders his whole life. I'm saying that the
man God made you to be has to stand on his own ground, answerable to God, and
that ground can't be a room in your parents' house. Your wife needs to know that
she's first. Your children need to see a father who's actually the father, not a grown
boy relaying instructions from the grandparents. And you need it. A man who
never leaves never becomes the corner post of his own place. He stays a line post in
somebody else's fence for the rest of his life.
   Leaving well looks like this. You make the decision and then tell them, instead of
asking them and then deciding. You say "I love you, and we're going to do it this
way," and you don't argue the point. You protect your wife from your mother's
criticism even when your mother happens to be right. You stop needing your
father's approval before you act, which is the work of the last two chapters. And
you do all of it with a soft voice and a steady spine, because leaving well isn't a fight.



108                                                                  Leave Without Slamming the Door
It's a man walking out of a room he has outgrown, gently, and closing the door
behind him without a sound.

Why you can't slam it

   Now the other man, the one who left hard. You may have had every reason.
Some of you left houses that no boy should have had to live in, and I'm not going to
tell you that you should have stayed, and I'm not going to tell you to go back to a
man who's still dangerous. But I am going to tell you what a slammed door does,
because I've watched it in too many men to stay quiet about it.
   A slammed door doesn't stay behind you. It comes with you. The man who cut
off his father in fury has his father in every room of his life, because fury is a kind of
attention, and the thing you're furious at is the thing your life is organized around.
He isn't free of his dad. He's more bound to him than the son who calls every
Sunday. He simply can't see it, because the binding is made of anger instead of
affection.
   And the slammed door does one other thing. It teaches your children how to
leave you.
   Kids watch how their father treats his father. That's the manual they're given. If
they see contempt, they learn that contempt is what grown sons feel for old men,
and they file that away for the day when you're the old man. If they see a father who
honors a man who didn't deserve it, they learn something about honor that no
sermon could teach them.
  So you can't slam it. Not because your father earned better; maybe he didn't.
Because you're a man, and men don't slam doors, and because the Father who did
not leave you is watching how you handle the father who did.

What honor means when he wasn't honorable

    This is where men get stuck, so let me be concrete. Honor doesn't mean
pretending. You don't have to say your father was good if he wasn't; Scripture never
asks you to lie. Honor is about how you treat the office, not how you rate the
performance. A soldier salutes the rank even when he can't respect the man wearing
it, and he isn't being a hypocrite. He's being a soldier.
   Honoring a hard father looks like telling the truth about him without mocking


Leave Without Slamming the Door                                                        109
him, because there's a difference between "my father was a hard man and he hurt
us" and the version you'd tell at a bar to get a laugh. It looks like speaking about him
carefully in front of your children, who don't need to carry your contempt for their
grandfather, and if you hand it to them, that's your sin and not his. It looks like
providing for him if he needs it in old age, even if he never provided for you; Jesus
had hard words for men who found religious reasons to skip that. It looks like not
wishing him harm and not rejoicing when harm comes. And it looks like praying for
him, honestly, even if the prayer is only "Father, I don't know how to pray for this
man, but You know him."
  And it looks like forgiving him. That's the heaviest one, so let me be careful
about what it means.

Forgiveness

  Forgiveness is not saying it didn't matter. It mattered, and God knows exactly
how much.
   Forgiveness is not trust. You can forgive a man and still not leave your children
alone with him. Trust is earned and rebuilt over time; forgiveness is given.
   Forgiveness is not necessarily a relationship. Some of you will forgive fathers you
will never sit at a table with again, because the table isn't safe, and that isn't a failure
of forgiveness.
   Here's what forgiveness is. It's a man deciding that the debt his father owes him is
going to be settled by God and not by him. He stops collecting, and he stops
waiting for the apology as the condition of his own peace. He hands the whole
account over to the only Judge who has all the facts and lets it leave his hands.

  Romans 12:19 (NIV)
  Do not take revenge, my dear friends, but leave room for God's wrath, for it is written: 'It is mine to
  avenge; I will repay,' says the Lord.



  Most men read "leave room for God's wrath" as a threat against their enemies.
Read it as a relief. You don't have to be the one who makes him pay. That was never
your job, and you can set it down. Joseph's brothers threw him in a pit and sold him
and let their father believe he was dead. Years later, when he had every power to
make them pay for it, he said something that's the clearest picture of forgiveness I


110                                                                       Leave Without Slamming the Door
know.

  Genesis 50:20 (NIV)
  You intended to harm me, but God intended it for good to accomplish what is now being done, the
  saving of many lives.



  He didn't say they hadn't intended harm. He named it: you intended to harm
me. Then he said what God had done with it anyway. That's the shape of the thing.
Full honesty about the wrong, full trust that God was working in it regardless, and
no bill presented.

The conversation

   If your father is alive and it's safe, there may be a conversation to have. Not
everyone needs to have it; some fathers can't hear it, and some conversations only
reopen a wound God is already closing. But for a lot of men it's the door they've
been standing outside of for years, and I want to give you a way through it.
  You wrote a letter in chapter 8, the one you weren't going to send. Read it again,
because it's your preparation, though it isn't your script. The conversation itself is
shorter than the letter, and it has four parts.
   First, what you needed and didn't get. Say it once, without a list of examples.
"Dad, I needed to hear you say you were proud of me, and I never did." One
sentence. Don't pile on, because he'll hear one sentence and he won't hear ten.
   Second, what you're grateful for, and you'll need to find something, because even
a hard father usually gave you something: a work ethic, a roof, a hunting trip, a
sentence at the right moment. Name it, and know that this isn't flattery; it's honor.
  Third, what you're going to do. "I'm going to raise my kids differently, and I'm
not going to carry this against you anymore." Say it as a statement and not a threat.
You're telling him what's yours.
  Fourth, the door, which sounds like this: "I'd like to know you as a man, if you're
willing." And then leave it there. Don't demand that he change. Don't wait for an
apology as the price of the door staying open. You've said your piece like a man, and
how he answers is his weight to carry, not yours.
   Cole did this with his father, the one who'd never said a kind word in thirty-three



Leave Without Slamming the Door                                                                     111
years. He drove out to the old man's place on a Tuesday and said the four things
sitting on the tailgate. His father didn't apologize, and he didn't say he was proud.
He said, "Well," and looked at the barn for a while, and then he said, "You want to
look at that pump with me?" Cole said yes. He told me later that was the closest
thing to an embrace his father had ever offered, and he took it, because he'd stopped
needing more than the man could give.
   If your father is dead, or too dangerous to approach, have the conversation
anyway. Say the four things to God, out loud, and say them to a brother, and write
them down. The words need to leave you even if they can't reach him.

About your mother

  Genesis 2:24 says a man leaves his father and mother, and I've been talking
mostly about fathers, so let me turn.
   Some of you were raised by your mother alone, and she was the whole family,
and she was strong, and you love her more than anyone on earth, and that's good.
Leave her anyway, because she raised you to be a man, and a man has his own
house. If you're married, your wife has to be first, and your mother needs to hear
you say so, kindly and clearly, the first time there's a conflict rather than the tenth.
If you're single, you still need a life whose center isn't your mother's kitchen table,
or you'll never be ready for a wife when she comes, and your mother, if she's wise,
knows that better than you do.
   If you're divorced, watch this one closely. There's a pull in the year after a divorce
to go home emotionally, to let your mother become your confidante and your
children's second mother and your reason not to build a life of your own. She loves
you and she'll let you, and you mustn't. Honor her, lean on her some, and stay a
man with his own ground.

To the older man whose son slammed the door

   Some of you are reading this from the other side, as the father. Your son left hard,
or drifted, or calls on Christmas, and you don't know what you did, or you do.
   Here is what I'd say: leave the door unlocked, because that's the whole strategy.
You can't make him come back and you shouldn't try. But you can be the father in
Jesus's story, who went out to both his sons, the one who ran off and the one who



112                                                          Leave Without Slamming the Door
stayed home bitter.

  Luke 15:28 (NIV)
  The older brother became angry and refused to go in. So his father went out and pleaded with him.



   His father went out. Twice in that story the father is the one who moves, so go
first. Make the call even if he doesn't pick up. Send the text on his birthday with
nothing in it but love. Don't keep score of who called last, and don't demand an
apology as the price of the door. And if he comes, don't relitigate anything. Just set
another plate.
   And if there's something you did, say so. Plainly, once, without excuses. "I wasn't
the father you needed. I'm sorry." A grown son who hears that from his father, even
at fifty, even at seventy, will feel something shift that he didn't know could move.
Roy did this with his daughter, who'd been distant for a decade after his years on
the road. He said the sentence in her driveway. She didn't answer for a long time.
Then she asked if he wanted to come in for supper.

Leaving at every age

   Men leave home more than once, and they fail at it in the same two ways at every
age.
   A young man of twenty is leaving for the first time, and he's tempted toward one
door or the other: the slammed one, where he cuts his parents off in the name of
freedom, or the one he never walks through, where he calls his mother before every
decision and lets his father's opinion run his life from two hundred miles away. The
takeaway for him is that leaving well at twenty looks like making his own decisions
and telling his parents afterward with love, and that this is practice for a marriage.
   A young father is leaving a second time, because the arrival of a child pulls
grandparents back into the center of a house with opinions about everything.
Tomás's mother and his wife disagreed about how the baby should sleep, and
Tomás had to say, kindly and in front of his mother, that they'd be doing it his
wife's way. The takeaway for him is that a man protects his wife from his mother's
criticism even when his mother is right.
  A man of forty-five is often leaving for the third time, because his father is aging
and the old dynamics come back. He finds himself twelve years old in the truck


Leave Without Slamming the Door                                                                       113
after every visit. The takeaway for him is the four-part conversation, which is not
too late at forty-five and may be the most important conversation of his decade.
   An older man is on the other side of the door, watching a son who left hard or
drifted, and the takeaway for him is the father in Jesus's story, who went out to both
sons. Leave the door unlocked, make the call that doesn't get answered, and say the
sentence if there's a sentence to say. Roy's supper with his daughter came after ten
years of an unlocked door.

Walk out, and don't slam

    You have to leave. That's God's design, and you can't be a man without it. Your
life has to have its own center, under Him, and it can't be in your parents' house.
   And you have to honor. That's God's command, and you can't be a man without
that either. Whatever kind of chair sat at the end of your table, the man in it was
your father, and how you treat him now is a mark that shows on you.
   So walk out, gently, with a steady spine, closing the door behind you without a
sound and leaving it unlocked. Then go build a house of your own, and make sure
the door on it is the kind a son can come home through.

Before you turn the page

   Honoring a father who wasn't honorable is one of the hardest things this book
asks, and I don't want you to read it as a demand that you pretend. God never asks a
man to lie about his father. He asks him to hand the debt over, and to salute the
office, and to walk out gently, and He asks it because He knows that a slammed
door rides in the truck for the rest of a man's life. May the Father who did not leave
you give you the steadiness to leave without slamming, and the grace to leave the
door unlocked behind you, and may your children learn from watching you how to
leave you well someday.




114                                                       Leave Without Slamming the Door
  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because both ways of failing at leaving hide from the man who's doing them: say
  which one is yours, the slammed door or the door you never walked through, and
  say what it's cost your wife or your kids or your own life.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because leaving and honoring are both done in specific acts: if you never left, make
  one decision this week without running it past your parents first and tell them
  afterward with love; if you slammed the door, pray for your father by name every
  day this week, and if he's alive and safe, pick a date for the four-part conversation.
  TELL A BROTHER
  Because a conversation with a date on it that another man knows about actually
  happens: tell him which father you have and which door you've been standing at,
  and if the conversation is coming, tell him when.
  CARRY THIS
  Honor the office, tell the truth about the man, hand the debt to God, and leave the
  door unlocked. Leave. Don't slam.




Leave Without Slamming the Door                                                        115
116   A Man With No Brothers
CHAPTER 13



A Man With No Brothers
   Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
  The year Walt almost lost the business, he sat in his truck in the parking lot of a
bank in Abilene with his phone in his hand and scrolled through every contact he
had.
   There were three hundred and some names: customers, suppliers, his accountant,
men he'd hunted with for twenty years, men from church whose kids had played
ball with his kids, a brother-in-law in Fort Worth, his pastor. He scrolled all the way
to the bottom and all the way back to the top, and then he set the phone face down
on the seat and sat there for forty minutes.
   There wasn't one name on that phone he could call and say, "I'm scared. I think
I'm about to lose everything and I don't know what to do." Not one. He'd built a
business and a family and a reputation over thirty years, and he had three hundred
contacts and no brothers.
   He told me this a long time afterward, and he told it the way men tell things like
this, flat, as if it were a fact about the weather. "I didn't have anybody to call." And
then he said the sentence I hear more than any other from men who are being
honest. "I didn't think that was a problem until it was."

Most men have no brothers

  I need to say this straight, because the men it applies to almost never think it
applies to them.
   Most men have no brothers. They have buddies, men they do things with: hunt,
fish, golf, watch the game, work on trucks. They have coworkers, some of whom
they like. They have men at church whose hands they shake and whose names they



A Man With No Brothers                                                                117
know. And if they're married they have a wife who is carrying the entire weight of
knowing them, which is a weight she was never meant to carry alone.
   Ask a man who knows his home exit, the anger or the itch or the drift we named
in chapter 9. Ask him who could call him at two in the morning and who he could
call. Ask him who's allowed to say "you're wrong" to his face and be heard. Ask him
who knows what he's actually afraid of. And then watch how many men, good
men, men with full contact lists, go quiet.
   We were not built to do this alone. That isn't a self-help line; it's the second
chapter of Genesis. The first thing God said was wrong with the world was that the
man was by himself. He fixed it with a wife, and that was the first remedy, but it
wasn't the only one He had in mind. All through Scripture, men are set in
companies. David had Jonathan and then thirty mighty men. Jesus had twelve, and
three inside the twelve, and He took those three with Him into the garden on the
night He needed them most. Paul never traveled alone if he could help it, and he
wrote most of his letters with a brother sitting next to him.

  Ecclesiastes 4:9-10 (NIV)
  Two are better than one, because they have a good return for their labor: If either of them falls down,
  one can help the other up. But pity anyone who falls and has no one to help them up.



   Pity anyone who falls and has no one to help them up. That was Walt in the
truck. He'd fallen, and he was reaching around in the dark, and there was no hand.

Why we don't have them

  I've asked a lot of men why they have no brothers, and here's what I've heard, in
more or less the order I hear it.
   They say they're fine. This is pride wearing a work shirt. You're not fine;
nobody's fine. The man who says "I'm fine" for thirty years is the man who ends up
in a bank parking lot with three hundred names.
   They say they don't have time, and they do; they simply haven't been asked for
the right amount, and I'll get to that.
  They say they don't want to be a burden. Under this is usually the empty chair. A
boy who learned that needing things was dangerous grows into a man who'd rather



118                                                                                A Man With No Brothers
go under than reach for a hand, and I understand it, and it's still killing him.
   They say men don't do that. But men did do that for most of history, and men in
Scripture did it constantly. We stopped about two generations ago, and we've been
dying quietly ever since and calling it independence.
   They say they've been burned. Some of you told a man something once and it
got around, or he used it, or he drifted off, and I'm sorry, and it happens. It's also
not a reason to never try again, any more than one bad meal is a reason to stop
eating.
   And they say they've got their wife, which is good, and I'd tell them to keep her.
But your wife is not your brother, and if she's the only one who knows you, you've
set a weight on her that will eventually bend her. She needs you to have a man. Ask
her, and she'll tell you.

What a brother is

  Let me define the word so you know what you're looking for. A brother is a man
who knows your exit. He knows whether you get angry or reach for the screen or go
quiet, and he asks about it, and you tell him the truth.
  A brother is a man who can ask you anything. Money, marriage, the bedroom,
what you looked at this week, whether you've prayed. Nothing is off the table,
because the two of you agreed that nothing would be.
  A brother is a man who will come. At two in the morning, to the hospital, to the
parking lot of the bank. Not because he has to, but because he's your brother. A
brother is a man who tells you the truth. Not the nice version but the true one, said
with love and said once, and then he lets you decide what to do with it.

  Proverbs 27:17 (NIV)
  As iron sharpens iron, so one person sharpens another.



  Sparks fly when iron sharpens iron. It isn't a comfortable picture. A brother is
going to say things that sting, and you'll be sharper for them.
  And a brother is a man who helps you find God when you can't. There's a line in
Samuel that I think is the best one-sentence description of brotherhood in the Bible.
David is on the run, hunted by the king, exhausted and hiding in the woods, and his


A Man With No Brothers                                                                   119
friend comes to find him.

  1 Samuel 23:16 (NIV)
  And Saul's son Jonathan went to David at Horesh and helped him find strength in God.



   Helped him find strength in God. Not gave him strength, and not fixed his
situation; Jonathan couldn't fix anything, since his own father was the one doing
the hunting. He came anyway, into the woods, and he helped his brother find what
only God could give. That is the job. That's what you need and that's what you're
for.
   Notice what a brother isn't. He isn't a crowd; you don't need a men's group of
forty, you need one man, maybe two. He isn't a counselor, though he might tell you
to see one. He isn't your boss or someone who reports to you. And he isn't
necessarily the man you have the most fun with. Some of your best hunting buddies
would make terrible brothers, and that's all right. Keep hunting with them, and find
a brother somewhere else.

Twenty minutes

  Here's the part I promised, and it may be the most practical thing in this book.
   The reason most men don't have a brother isn't that they don't want one. It's
that every picture of brotherhood they've been handed is too big: a weekly men's
breakfast at six in the morning, a small group with homework, a retreat, a weekend
away. Those things are good, and I've done all of them, but if you're a man with a
newborn or a business going under or a custody schedule or a wife with cancer, you
look at them and you know you can't, and so you do nothing.
   So here's a smaller picture. It's the one that finally worked for me and for most of
the men in this book, and I've never seen a season of life it couldn't survive.
    It's twenty minutes, with one man, once a week, at the same time. On the phone
is fine, and in a truck is fine, and walking is fine. It doesn't have to be face to face
and it doesn't have to be at a table. It has to be the same twenty minutes every week,
because a time that floats is a time that dies. Tuesday at 7:30 in the evening after the
kids are down. Thursday at noon on the drive to lunch. Sunday afternoon on the
porch. Pick it, and treat it like a client meeting you'd never think of canceling.



120                                                                         A Man With No Brothers
   And here's what you do with the twenty minutes. Three questions and a prayer.
The first question is, what happened this week? Two or three minutes each. Not a
report; the two or three things that actually mattered. The fight, the win, the call
from the doctor, the thing your son said.
    The second question is, what's heavy? This is the one that matters. What are you
carrying? What's the fear underneath the week? Where's your exit showing up? You
each answer, and you each tell the truth, and the other man does not fix it. He
listens, and he might ask one more question, and he does not fix it.
   The third question is, what's next? You each name one thing, one move before
next week: the conversation you're going to have, the thing you're going to stop, the
call you're going to make. Say it out loud so that a man heard you say it, because
next week the first question is going to be "did you do it?"
   Then two minutes of prayer, out loud, for each other. It'll be awkward for about
three weeks, and then it'll be the best part.
   That's the whole thing. Some weeks it runs thirty. Some weeks one of you is a
wreck and you spend all twenty on his "what's heavy," and that's fine, because that's
what it's for. But the floor is twenty minutes, once a week, at the same time, and the
floor doesn't move.
   Kenny's older man, the one who called him every Tuesday night in the first year
after his divorce, was doing exactly this. Three questions and a prayer. Kenny told
me he didn't realize until much later that the man had a method. He only knew that
every Tuesday at 7:30 his phone rang and a man wanted to know what was heavy,
and that for the first time in his adult life, somebody did.

Why twenty

  I want to defend the number, because some of you are thinking it's too small to
matter.
  Twenty minutes is small enough to survive anything. A newborn, tax season, a
twelve-hour shift, a custody weekend, chemotherapy, retirement, grief. I have never
met a man who honestly couldn't find twenty minutes a week. I've met a great
many who couldn't find two hours, and so found nothing.
  Twenty minutes with a structure does more than two hours without one. Most
men's gatherings drift toward sports and trucks and politics within eleven minutes


A Man With No Brothers                                                             121
because nobody's steering, and three questions steer. You'll go deeper in twenty
minutes with the questions than most men go in a year of breakfasts without them.
   And twenty minutes grows. Once two men have done this for six months, they
start doing other things. They show up at each other's kids' games. They help with
the move. They call on the off days. The twenty isn't the ceiling; it's the seed. But if
you try to plant the whole tree at once, you'll plant nothing.

How to find him

    This is where most men stall, so let me be specific about it. Look for a man who's
living the four marks, mostly. Not perfectly; nobody is. But you want a man who's
off the tailgate, who carries weight, who goes first sometimes, and who seems to be
working for something bigger than applause. You'll know him when you see him.
He's probably already in your church or your crew or your neighborhood, and
you've never asked him anything real.
  He can be your age or he can be older. Older is often better for this, and that's the
next chapter. But another man in your own season who's serious about being a man
makes a fine brother, and sometimes he's the only one available.
   He shouldn't be your boss, your employee, or your brother-in-law if you can
help it. You need to be able to tell him the whole truth without a job or a
Thanksgiving hanging on it.
  Then ask him, in plain words. Here's the sentence: "I'm trying to be a better man,
and I've realized I don't have a brother. Would you be willing to talk with me for
twenty minutes a week for the next three months and see how it goes?"
  Give it a time box of three months, because that makes it easy for him to say yes,
and easy for either of you to let it go if it isn't working. Most of the time it works
and nobody remembers to stop.
   Expect it to be awkward. For the first three weeks you'll both feel like you're
doing something strange, and you are. You're doing something men used to do and
stopped doing, and your body doesn't have the muscle memory yet. Push through
the awkwardness. It passes, and what's on the other side is worth it.
  And expect that he's been waiting too. Almost every man I've ever asked has said
some version of "I've been wanting somebody to ask me that." Men are lonely.
They're not going to go first. You're going to have to, which is chapter 6.


122                                                                 A Man With No Brothers
Brothers in every season

   If you're single, don't assume your roommates or your buddies are brothers,
because proximity isn't brotherhood. You might already have a brother and only
need to add the twenty minutes and the three questions. Or you might have a house
full of men and not one who knows you. Find out this week.
   If you've got a baby in the house, twenty minutes is all you've got, and that's
exactly why the number is twenty. Do it on the phone at nine at night while you do
the dishes. Your wife will bless you for it, because a man with a brother is a better
husband, and she'll see the difference inside a month.
   If you're divorced, your friends probably vanished. Couple friends do that; they
were mostly her friends, or they didn't know what to do with you. You need one
man more than any other man reading this does, so find him. If the church men's
group feels too big and too married right now, skip it for the moment and get one
man on the phone on Tuesday nights.
   And if you're older, the working years gave you a built-in company of men, and
when the work stopped, so did they. Roy said the first year of retirement was the
loneliest year of his life, and he'd worked alone in a patrol car for thirty years. He
built the Thursday breakfast table because he needed it, not out of generosity, and it
turned out a dozen other men needed it too. If you're a widower who lost the
couple friends along with your wife, the same is true of you. You need one man.
Mr. Pruitt found his in a retired schoolteacher who also drank his coffee alone, and
the two of them have met every Wednesday morning at a gas station for four years
now.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty-two has a house full of roommates and buddies and may
have no brother at all, for the reason I gave above. Eli realized this when he broke his
hand at work and couldn't think of one man among the dozen he saw every week
who'd know what to do with the fact that he was scared about the medical bills.
The takeaway for him is to take one of the dozen and add the twenty minutes and
the three questions, and to find out whether a buddy can become a brother.
   A man of fifty has three hundred contacts and no one to call, which is Walt in
the bank parking lot, and the takeaway for him is that the loneliest years of a man's


A Man With No Brothers                                                               123
life are the middle ones, that this isn't his imagination, and that the cure is one
sentence to one man he's known for twenty years and never asked anything real.

The garden

   The night before He died, Jesus took three men into a garden. He knew they'd
fall asleep. He knew one of them would deny Him before sunrise. He took them
anyway, and He asked them for something.

  Mark 14:34 (NIV)
  'My soul is overwhelmed with sorrow to the point of death,' he said to them. 'Stay here and keep
  watch.'



  Stay here and keep watch. The Son of God, who needed nothing, asked three
men to stay with Him in the worst hour of His life. If He didn't do it alone, you
don't get to either.
   Walt has a brother now. It took him a year to ask, and he asked a man from
church he'd known for twenty years and never once had a real conversation with.
They talk every Thursday at noon for twenty minutes on the drive to lunch. Walt
told me that for the first month he thought it was the dumbest thing he'd ever
agreed to. In the sixth month, when the business hit another rough patch, he made
a call from the same parking lot in Abilene, and this time somebody picked up.
   So find a brother, and give him twenty minutes at the same time every week.
Start with the sentence, and go first.

Before you turn the page

   I know the sentence I've asked you to say to another man feels like the most
awkward sentence in the English language. Every man I've ever asked to say it has
said the same thing. I also know that nearly every man who's said it has been
answered with some version of "I've been wanting somebody to ask me that,"
because men are lonely and they aren't going to go first. The Son of God took three
men into a garden on the worst night of His life and asked them to stay. May the
Father give you the nerve to ask one man for twenty minutes, and may He give you,
on the other end of that phone, a brother born for a time of adversity.



124                                                                               A Man With No Brothers
A Man With No Brothers   125
126   Ask the Man
CHAPTER 14



Ask the Man
   Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
   When I was twenty-three I asked a man named Buck how he'd stayed married for
forty years. He was standing at the back of the church parking lot with his hands in
his pockets, looking at nothing in particular the way old men do, and I don't know
what made me ask; I just did.
  He looked at me for a second as if he were deciding something. Then he said,
"Come by Tuesday. I'll show you."
   I thought he was going to tell me. I pictured his kitchen table and a pot of coffee
and the whole thing laid out. That wasn't what happened. When I showed up
Tuesday he handed me a paintbrush and pointed at a fence, and we painted for
three hours, and somewhere in the third hour I realized that his wife had brought us
tea twice and that he'd stopped both times to watch her walk back to the house. He
never gave me a speech. But I went back the next Tuesday, and the one after that,
and by the time the fence was done I'd learned more about marriage than I'd
learned in twenty-three years of watching everybody else's.
   That's what this chapter is about. There is a man about twenty years ahead of
you on the road you're walking, and he knows things you need to know, and he's
standing at the back of some parking lot with his hands in his pockets waiting for
somebody to ask, and I want you to be the one who asks him.

The two men who are waiting

  Here's the situation as I've come to see it after years of watching both sides of the
parking lot.
   The younger man doesn't ask. He figures the older man is busy, or that he'd be



Ask the Man                                                                          127
bothering him, or that he doesn't know him well enough, or that he should be able
to work this out on his own. Or he figures his own father should have told him, and
since his father didn't, there's something shameful about asking anyone else. So he
stands on his side of the lot and doesn't move.
   The older man doesn't offer. He figures the younger man doesn't want an old
guy's opinion, or that offering would be presumptuous, or that he has nothing to
offer because he made too many mistakes himself, or that nobody asked him when
he was young and he turned out all right. So he stands on his side with his hands in
his pockets.
   And a whole generation of men grows up with nobody twenty years ahead of
them telling them what's coming.
   This isn't how it's supposed to work. Scripture's picture of manhood is a chain:
Moses handed to Joshua, Elijah handed to Elisha, Naomi walked Ruth through a
whole culture she didn't understand. Paul spent years on Timothy and Titus and
then told them to go do the same with others.

  2 Timothy 2:2 (NIV)
  And the things you have heard me say in the presence of many witnesses entrust to reliable people who
  will also be qualified to teach others.



   Four generations in one sentence: Paul, to Timothy, to reliable people, to the
others they'll teach. That's the chain, and it's broken in most churches and most
families in this country, and the break is on both sides of the parking lot. The last
chapter was about brothers, men beside you. This one is about a man ahead of you,
and you need both. A brother knows what you're going through because he's going
through it too. The man ahead knows what you're going through because he went
through it and came out the other side, and he can tell you what's on the other side,
which is a thing a brother can't do.

What to ask for

   Don't ask him to mentor you, and I mean that literally. "Mentor" is a large,
formal, frightening word, and when you say it to a sixty-year-old man he hears a
commitment he isn't sure he can keep and a role he isn't sure he's qualified for, and
he'll probably say no, or say yes and then dodge you. The word gets in the way of
