128                                                                                          Ask the Man
the thing.
   Ask him something specific instead. That's what I did with Buck without
knowing I was doing it. "How did you stay married forty years?" is a question a man
can answer, or can say "come by Tuesday" to. There are a hundred others like it.
How did you decide when to leave that job? What do you wish you'd done
differently with your kids when they were small? How do you handle it when you
and your wife aren't talking? How did you get out of debt? What did you do the
first year after the divorce? How did you keep going after she died? Can I come
watch you do that?
  One question is the whole ask. If it goes well, ask another one the following
week. If he says "come by Tuesday," go by Tuesday and bring your hands, because
some of the best things I've ever learned from older men I learned while we were
both doing something else.
   And if you want to give it a shape, use the twenty minutes from the last chapter.
"Could I call you for twenty minutes on Thursdays for the next couple of months?
I've got some things I'm trying to figure out and I think you've already figured them
out." Older men can say yes to twenty minutes and a time box. They struggle to say
yes to "will you mentor me."

How to pick him

    You're looking for a man about twenty years ahead of you who's living the four
marks. Not a famous man, and not the most impressive man at church. A man
who's actually walked the stretch of road you're on and is still standing at the end of
it.
   Here's how to tell. Watch his wife's face when he talks. A woman who's been
married to a real man for thirty years looks at him a particular way, and a woman
who's been married to a performer for thirty years looks at him a different way, and
you can learn to tell the difference in about a minute. Listen to how his grown kids
talk about him, if you can. Notice whether he tells the truth about his failures. Be
careful of the man who only has wins, because he's either lying or he hasn't been
paying attention, and either way he can't help you. What you need isn't his wins. It's
his scars, and his Tuesday habits.
  He doesn't have to be in your line of work, and he doesn't have to share your
personality. He does have to be a man you'd want to be like at his age in the things


Ask the Man                                                                         129
that matter. And he has to be reachable: a man you could see face to face once a
month, or call.
   If you can't think of one, look harder. He's in your church. He's the deacon
nobody asks anything of. He's the man three pews back who's been married
forty-five years and shows up every week and has never had a real conversation with
anybody under fifty. He's the retired man at the hardware store. He's the older man
at the Thursday breakfast sitting alone. There are more of them than you think, and
nearly all of them are waiting.

What to do once you've asked

  Come with a question every time. An older man will give you the rest of his life if
you come with a real question, and he'll drift away if you come to hang out. The
question is the respect.
  Listen more than you talk. You're there to learn what he knows, not to tell him
what you know, so ask a follow-up and let him finish.
   Then do what he says, at least once. If he tells you to sit down with your wife and
ask her a particular question, go do it, and come back and tell him what happened.
That report is how you earn the next hour. Older men have wasted a great deal of
advice on young men who nodded and did nothing, and they've learned to stop
giving it. Be the young man who does the thing and comes back, and he'll give you
everything he has.
  Don't make him your father. That's a weight he can't carry, and it isn't fair to ask.
He's a man ahead of you on the road, and he'll give you a lot, but he can't give you
what the empty chair didn't, and if you go looking for that from him you'll wear
him out and end up disappointed. That was chapter 10's work.
  And say thank you, out loud and specifically. "What you told me about how you
handled your dad, I did that, and it changed something." Older men don't hear that
enough. Some of them have never heard it.

To the man who's ahead

   Now I turn, because half of you reading this are the man in the parking lot with
his hands in his pockets.




130                                                                         Ask the Man
   You're the man, and I don't care that you don't feel finished, because nobody
finishes. Paul was writing about his own unfinished race the week he died. You've
walked a stretch of road that a younger man is just starting, and you know where
the washouts are, and that knowledge is worth more to him than you can imagine.
He doesn't need you to have gotten it all right. He needs you to tell him the truth
about where you got it wrong, and what you'd do differently, and what you did on
Tuesdays that held.

  Titus 2:2 (NIV)
  Teach the older men to be temperate, worthy of respect, self-controlled, and sound in faith, in love and
  in endurance.




  Titus 2:6-8 (NIV)
  Similarly, encourage the young men to be self-controlled. In everything set them an example by doing
  what is good. In your teaching show integrity, seriousness and soundness of speech that cannot be
  condemned.



   Paul's letter to Titus is largely a set of instructions for older men and older
women to hand off to younger ones. That's the plan, and it isn't a program, but
older men teaching younger men by example and by word, in the ordinary, on
Tuesdays. Here's how to start. Go first, because he isn't going to ask. He thinks
you're busy, and he thinks he'd be bothering you, and he's probably a little afraid of
you. So you walk across the parking lot. "I've watched you handle a few things
around here and I think you're ready for more. Would you want to get breakfast
sometime?" Or, "I'm fixing a fence Saturday and could use a hand, if you want to
learn how." Give him something he can say yes to.
   Don't lecture, though the temptation for a man with forty years of experience is
to download all of it at once. Ask questions instead. "What's going on with work?"
"How's it going with her?" "What's heavy right now?" Young men will tell an older
man things they'd never tell a peer, if the older man asks and then actually waits for
the answer.
   Tell the truth about your failures. This is what makes the whole thing work.
When a young man hears a man he respects say, "I nearly lost my marriage at
thirty-five, and here's what I did wrong," something unlocks in him. He stops
performing and starts asking real questions. Your scars are the curriculum.


Ask the Man                                                                                              131
  Give him work by inviting him into something you're doing: a job, a project, the
Thursday table. Men learn beside each other more than across from each other.
Buck taught me marriage with a paintbrush.
   Keep it small and steady. Twenty minutes a week, or a breakfast a month, or a
job every other Saturday, something he can count on. The steadiness is the teaching.
A young man who has never had a reliable man in his life learns more from you
showing up eleven Tuesdays in a row than from anything you say on any one of
them.
    And expect to get more than you give. Every older man I know who's done this
says the same thing. Roy told me that the two men at his Thursday table gave him
back a reason to get up in the morning that he'd lost when he turned in the badge. "I
thought I was helping them," he said. "I was, some. Mostly they were saving my
life." That isn't a side effect; it's the design, because God sets the lonely in families,
and He does it in both directions at once.

  Psalm 71:18 (NIV)
  Even when I am old and gray, do not forsake me, my God, till I declare your power to the next
  generation, your mighty acts to all who are to come.



   Till I declare your power to the next generation. That's a prayer for a job, not for
rest, and it's a job that lasts until the end. If you've turned in your badge or your
keys or your hard hat and you're wondering what a man does with his mornings
now, this is what. The younger men are in the parking lot, and it's time to walk
over.

The man ahead, in every season

   If you're single, find a married man ahead of you and ask him about marriage
before you're in one. You'll learn more in a year of asking than in your first three
years of doing, and your future wife will thank you for it without ever knowing
why.
   If there's a baby in your house, find a man whose kids are grown and ask him
what he wishes he'd done when they were small. He'll tell you that it goes fast and to
be there, and he's right. Hear it from him now, while there's still time to do
something about it.



132                                                                                         Ask the Man
   If you're divorced, you need a man ahead of you more than most, and you may
be tempted to skip this because you're embarrassed, and you shouldn't. Find a man
who's been through it, if you can. If you can't, find a man who's been married a
long time and isn't going to judge you, and ask him to walk with you anyway.
Kenny's Tuesday-night man had never been divorced. He'd simply decided that
Kenny was worth a phone call every week, and that turned out to be enough.
  And if you're the older man, and your own father never did this for you, and no
man ever did, you can still do it. You aren't disqualified by what you didn't get.
You're equipped by what you lived through, so go find him.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty-three is standing on the young side of the lot with a real
question he's afraid to ask, the way I was with Buck. His question is about how to
stay married, or how to handle money, or how a man decides when to leave a job,
and he thinks the older men are too busy for it. The takeaway for him is that one
specific question, asked plainly, is the whole cost of entry, and that the older man is
more afraid he'll never be asked than the young man is afraid of asking.
   A man of forty-eight is standing on both sides of the lot and doesn't know it.
There's a man twenty years ahead of him he's never asked about the empty nest
that's coming, and a man twenty years behind him who's watching how he handles
his marriage and has never been spoken to. The takeaway for him is that he's in the
chain, and the chain needs him to look both directions.

Come by Tuesday

   Buck has been gone a while now. I painted his fence a few more times over the
years, and sat at his table, and asked him a few hundred more questions, and he
answered most of them by handing me a tool and starting to work. When I got
married he stood at the back of the church with his hands in his pockets, looking at
nothing in particular, and afterward he shook my hand and said, "Well. You paid
attention."
   I did, because I asked, and because he said come by Tuesday, and because neither
of us stayed on his side of the parking lot.
   Ask the man, and be the man, because the chain has been broken long enough.



Ask the Man                                                                          133
Before you turn the page

   The chain has been broken for two generations, and that isn't your fault, and it
also can't be fixed by anyone but the men reading this. If you're the younger man,
I'm asking you to be the one who walks across the lot and asks a real question, and
to feel foolish for eight seconds, and to go back Tuesday. If you're the older man,
I'm asking you to be the one who offers a paintbrush. May the Father who set men
in companies put a Buck in your life and make you a Buck in someone else's, and
may you both find that neither of you was as busy as you thought.




134                                                                       Ask the Man
Ask the Man   135
136   The Problem Inside
CHAPTER 15



The Problem Inside
   Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
   I chased a problem on an old Ford for most of one summer. It would run fine,
and then it would run hot, and then it would run fine again for a week. I did the
radiator, and then the thermostat, and then the water pump, the hoses, the fan
clutch, and the belt. Every part I replaced was a part that could have been the
problem, and every time I let the hood down I was sure I'd found it.
   The block was cracked. It had been cracked the whole time, a hairline in the
casting that let a little coolant go where it shouldn't have gone, and all summer I'd
been rebuilding everything bolted onto an engine that had a problem inside it that
no bolted-on part could touch. When the old mechanic in town finally told me, he
wasn't unkind about it. He just said, "Son, you've been fixing the wrong thing."
   This chapter is where the second part of the book turns. We've spent seven
chapters on what you inherited: the chair, the exits, the handoff you didn't get, the
brothers and the older men you didn't have. All of that is real, and all of it shaped
you, and if I stopped there I'd be leaving you under the hood replacing hoses,
because there's a problem inside that no amount of understanding your father is
ever going to fix.
   The problem is you, and it's me, and it's every man who has ever lived, and
Scripture has a plain word for it, and until you're willing to say the word you'll
spend your life fixing the wrong thing.

Even with a perfect father

   Here's a thought experiment I've put to a lot of men. Suppose the chair at your
table had been full every night. Suppose the man in it was kind and present and



The Problem Inside                                                                   137
strong, told you he loved you, and handed you off at fourteen with a ceremony and
a blessing. Suppose you had brothers and a mentor and a church that knew your
name.
    Would you be a good man? You'd be better, probably, and I'm not going to
pretend those things don't matter; I just spent seven chapters saying they do. But
would you be good? Would you never lie to your wife, never look at what you
shouldn't, never lose your temper, never nurse a grudge, never quietly decide you're
better than the man in the next pew? Would you never once put yourself first when
it cost somebody else?
    You know the answer, and the reason you know it is that the first man had all of
it. Adam had a perfect Father who walked with him every day. He had a perfect
place and a perfect work and a perfect wife and no wound of any kind. Nobody had
ever left him, and nobody had ever failed him. And he still reached for the fruit, or
stood silent while it was reached for, and then hid and blamed. The problem inside
a man didn't come from his father. It came through his father, from a father before
that, all the way back to a garden, and it would be in you if you'd been raised by
saints.

  Jeremiah 17:9 (NIV)
  The heart is deceitful above all things and beyond cure. Who can understand it?



  Beyond cure is the block. You can't bolt a part onto it. Jesus said the same thing
when the religious men of His day were busy managing their outsides.

  Mark 7:23 (NIV)
  All these evils come from inside and defile a person.



   From inside, Jesus says, and not from your father or your culture or your wound.
Those things opened doors and handed you tools and taught you which exit to
take. But what walked through the doors was already in you.

Why this is good news

  I know that doesn't sound like good news, so let me tell you why it is.
  If your problem is entirely what was done to you, then you have no way out. You


138                                                                                 The Problem Inside
can't un-have your father. You can't go back and get handed off at fourteen. You
can understand it and grieve it and forgive it, and you should, but understanding a
crack doesn't seal it. A man who believes his whole problem is his inheritance has
found a way to never repent, because you can't repent of what somebody else did.
He'll spend his life in a sophisticated version of Adam's excuse: the father you gave
me, he did it.
  But if the problem is inside you, if it's yours, then it can be dealt with, because
God deals with what's yours. He doesn't offer to fix your father. He offers to fix
you. That's the whole gospel. Jesus didn't come for men whose circumstances were
bad. He came for men whose hearts were, which is all of us, and it means nobody is
outside the reach of the cure.

  Romans 5:8 (NIV)
  But God demonstrates his own love for us in this: While we were still sinners, Christ died for us.



  While we were still sinners. Not while we were victims, and not while we were
misunderstood or under-fathered or doing our best with what we'd been given, but
while we were sinners. He named the real problem and then died for it, and if you'll
name it too, you get the cure.

The word

   The word is sin. I've been circling it and I want to land on it, because men avoid
the word for two opposite reasons and both of them are wrong.
   Some men avoid it because it sounds too harsh. They'd rather say struggles, or
issues, or brokenness, or baggage. Those words aren't wrong exactly; they're just soft
in a place where you need something hard. A struggle is something that happens to
you. Sin is something you do. The first one you manage and the second one you
repent of, and only one of those roads leads anywhere.
  Other men avoid the word because it sounds too familiar. They grew up in
churches that used it like a club, and every time they hear it they're eight years old
and in trouble, and I understand that. But the word doesn't belong to the men who
misused it. It belongs to God, and in His mouth it's a diagnosis, not a beating. A
doctor who tells you the block is cracked isn't attacking you. He's the first person all
summer who's told you the truth.


The Problem Inside                                                                                     139
   Here's what sin is. It's the bend in a man toward himself. The bend that makes
him hide when he should stand up, blame when he should own, take when he
should give, and go quiet when he should speak. It's what Adam did in the garden
and what you did last Tuesday. God spoke to Cain about it before Cain ever raised
a hand against his brother, and the picture He used is the best one I know.

  Genesis 4:7 (NIV)
  If you do what is right, will you not be accepted? But if you do not do what is right, sin is crouching at
  your door; it desires to have you, but you must rule over it.



   Crouching at your door. It's an animal, and it wants you, and God says you must
rule over it, which tells you two things at once. You're responsible, and you're not
helpless. Cain didn't rule over it, and it had him, and his brother was dead in a field
by the end of the chapter. Same animal, same door, every man.

What it feels like from inside

   Paul, who was as serious a man as ever lived, described the problem inside from
the inside, and every honest man I've ever read it to has gone quiet.

  Romans 7:15 (NIV)
  I do not understand what I do. For what I want to do I do not do, but what I hate I do.



   That's it, isn't it. You want to be patient with your kids and you're not. You want
to be present with your wife and you reach for the phone. You want to tell the truth
and something smoother comes out of your mouth. You want to pray and you fall
asleep. You know what a man does, and you do the other thing, and you don't
understand it, and neither did Paul. And then he says the thing every man says at
the bottom of himself, once he's finally quit fixing hoses.

  Romans 7:24-25 (NIV)
  What a wretched man I am! Who will rescue me from this body that is subject to death? Thanks be
  to God, who delivers me through Jesus Christ our Lord! So then, I myself in my mind am a slave to
  God's law, but in my sinful nature a slave to the law of sin.



   Who will rescue me. Not what will I do; who. He stopped looking for a part and


140                                                                                         The Problem Inside
started looking for a Person, and that's the turn, and the Person is named in the very
next breath.

What to do with the problem inside

  I'm not going to give you a program for defeating sin. Men have written libraries
on the subject, and most of them come down to trying harder, which is a hose.
Here's what I've actually watched work in men, and it's older than any program.
   Say the word about yourself. Not "I've got some stuff," but "I sinned," out loud,
to God, and specifically. "I lied to her about the money." "I looked at what I
shouldn't have on Thursday." "I've been nursing a grudge against my brother for
two years and enjoying it." David tried the other way for a while, and he described
what it did to him.

  Psalm 32:3-5 (NIV)
  When I kept silent, my bones wasted away through my groaning all day long. For day and night
  your hand was heavy on me; my strength was sapped as in the heat of summer. Then I acknowledged
  my sin to you and did not cover up my iniquity. I said, 'I will confess my transgressions to the LORD.'
  And you forgave the guilt of my sin.



  When I kept silent, my bones wasted away. That's what a hidden crack does to a
man. Then I acknowledged it, and you forgave. The whole distance between
wasting away and being forgiven is one honest sentence.
   Know the difference between guilt and shame. Guilt says, "I did a wrong thing."
Shame says, "I am a wrong thing." Guilt is useful; it's the check-engine light, and it
tells you where to look, and once you've confessed, it goes off. Shame is useless,
because it tells you nothing true about your standing with God, and it drives men
straight back to their exits, because shame is exactly the feeling that anger and
appetite and absence were designed to numb. If you're in Christ, guilt is a signal and
shame is a liar, and you have to learn to tell them apart and not let the liar drive.
   Turn, and don't just feel bad. Repentance isn't a feeling. It's a change of
direction. A man can feel terrible about something for twenty years and never once
repent of it, because he never turned. Turning looks like this: confess it, make it
right where you can, put something in place so that you don't walk through that
door as easily tomorrow, and get back to work. Then turn again tomorrow, because
you'll need to, and that isn't failure. That's what a Christian life looks like from the


The Problem Inside                                                                                     141
inside: a man who keeps turning back toward the Father he keeps walking away
from, and a Father who keeps receiving him.
   Bring a brother into it. This is why chapter 13 comes before this one. Sin lives in
the dark, and it cannot survive being said to another man who loves you.

  James 5:16 (NIV)
  Therefore confess your sins to each other and pray for each other so that you may be healed. The prayer
  of a righteous person is powerful and effective.



   Confess to each other, and not only to God. The first time Anthony said the
word about himself to another man was on a Thursday at noon, in a truck, in the
twenty minutes. His brother asked what was heavy, and Anthony started to say
"work" and stopped, and after a long silence he said, "I scared my daughter last
night. I didn't touch her. I didn't have to. I raised my voice and she went small, the
way I used to go small, and I've been calling it my temper for nine years and it's a
sin." The other man didn't say anything for a while. Then he said, "Thank you for
telling me," and asked what Anthony was going to do next, and prayed for him.
Anthony told me afterward that he'd expected to feel exposed and instead felt, for
the first time in years, clean. Something happens when a man says the true thing to
another man's face and the other man doesn't leave. James says such a man is healed,
and he doesn't say only forgiven; he says healed. The twenty minutes was built for
this. "What's heavy?" is where the sin comes out, and "what's next?" is where the
turn gets said out loud.
   And ask for a steadfast spirit. David, after the worst sin of his life, didn't ask God
to fix his circumstances. He asked for a new inside.

  Psalm 51:10 (NIV)
  Create in me a pure heart, O God, and renew a steadfast spirit within me.



   A steadfast spirit is what he asks for, because the block can't be patched. It has to
be replaced, and only God can do that, and He does, and He keeps doing it. That's
the prayer of a man who has stopped fixing the wrong thing.

The block in different shops




142                                                                                     The Problem Inside
   If you're young, learn this now, before the stakes go up. A single man who knows
the problem is inside him and knows what to do about it walks into marriage with a
tool most husbands don't have until they've done real damage.
   If there's a baby in your house, you're going to meet parts of yourself this year
that you didn't know were there. Exhaustion strips paint off a man, and what's
under the paint is the problem inside, and it isn't new; it's simply visible now. Don't
be shocked by it. Confess it, turn, and get some sleep when you can.
   If you're divorced, there's a version of this chapter you've been dreading, where I
tell you the divorce was entirely your sin. I'm not going to tell you that, because I
don't know, and neither does anyone but God and the two of you. But I am going
to tell you that there's a part that's yours, however large or small, and you can't heal
until you've named that part without attaching her part to it. "I did this" is a
sentence you need to say, full stop, and then let God sort the rest.
   And if you're older, you may have been managing the outside for so long that
you've forgotten there's an inside. Roy said he'd spent thirty years being a man other
men respected and had never once said the word "sin" about himself out loud until
he was sixty-nine. "I'd confessed in church a thousand times," he said. "In unison.
With everybody. That isn't the same as saying it about yourself, in your truck, by
name." It isn't too late. The block can be replaced at any age.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty-one who learns to say the word about himself now, in a
parked car, to God and to one brother, walks into marriage with a tool most
husbands don't have until year eight. Eli's first honest confession to another man
was about the phone at ten o'clock, and he said it felt like taking the hood off an
engine he'd been afraid to look at. The takeaway for him is that the earlier a man
finds the crack, the less he spends on the wrong parts.
   A man of forty-five has usually built a life around managing the outside, and he's
competent enough that the outside looks fine. Anthony's confession in a truck at
noon was the first time in nine years he'd called his temper a sin instead of a
temperament. The takeaway for him is that competence is not righteousness, and
that a man who's good at his life can still have a cracked block.




The Problem Inside                                                                    143
The right thing

   The old mechanic did the job. He pulled the engine, put in a rebuilt block, and
the truck ran cool for another decade. I remember standing in his shop feeling two
things at once, and it's the feeling I want to leave you with.
  I felt foolish, because I'd wasted a summer and a good deal of money on the
wrong things.
  And I felt relieved, more relieved than I'd been in months, because finally
somebody had told me the truth and the truth had a fix.
   That's what it feels like to stop blaming the chair and say the word about
yourself. Foolish, for a minute. Then relieved all the way down, because the
problem inside has a name, and the name has a cure, and the cure has already been
paid for by a Man who knew exactly what was in you and came anyway.
  You've been fixing the wrong thing, and it's time to stop. Say the word about
yourself, and let Him replace the block.

Before you turn the page

   I've asked you to say the hardest word in the language about yourself, and I want
to remind you why it's good news before you close the chapter. If the problem were
only what was done to you, there'd be no way out. But the problem is inside you,
and God deals with what's inside you, and He's already paid for it with a Man who
knew exactly what was there and came anyway. Guilt is a check-engine light and
shame is a liar; learn to tell them apart. May the Father who forgave David the
moment he opened the drawer meet you the moment you open yours, and may you
feel, on the far side of the foolishness, the relief of a man who's finally fixing the
right thing.




144                                                                    The Problem Inside
The Problem Inside   145
146   How That Looks on a Man
CHAPTER 16



How That Looks on a Man
   Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
  I knew a man whose garage was the cleanest room in his house. There was
pegboard with every tool outlined in marker, and labeled bins, and a swept floor,
and you could have eaten off the workbench. And in the bottom drawer of the red
toolbox, the one that locked, was a life his wife didn't know about.
   Nothing dramatic at first: a second phone, a few receipts, and later, more. But
the drawer had been there for years before it held anything worse than a lie, because
the drawer wasn't really about what was in it. The drawer was a habit. It was a place
in the man that was his alone, that nobody got to see, that he'd built long before he
had anything to hide in it. The clean garage was the surface. The locked drawer was
the man.
   In the last chapter we named the problem inside. This chapter is about what it
looks like from the outside, on a man in particular, because sin wears different
clothes on us than it does on anyone else, and if you don't know what it looks like
on a man you'll walk right past it in the mirror every morning.

The shapes it takes

  Here are the shapes I've seen most often. Not all of them will be yours. One or
two will be.
    The first is the locked drawer. Every man is tempted to keep a second set of
books, a part of his life that's his alone, managed and hidden and curated. For some
it's money: an account she doesn't know about, a debt he hasn't mentioned. For
some it's the screen. For some it's a friendship that isn't what he says it is. For some
it's simply his inner life, his real fears and real thoughts, which he's decided nobody



How That Looks on a Man                                                               147
gets to see. The drawer starts small, as a boy's reasonable wish for a little privacy, and
it grows into a man's hidden room, and everything bad that ever happens to a man
happens in that room.
   The second is the fortress. This is the man who has no needs. Ask him how he's
doing and he's fine. Ask what he needs and he needs nothing. He's the corner post
for everybody and he leans on no one, and he calls that strength, and it isn't. It's
pride in a work shirt, the same pride that kept Walt in a bank parking lot with three
hundred names and nobody to call. The fortress looks a great deal like the second
mark, accepting responsibility, and that's what makes it hard to spot. But a man
who accepts responsibility can still be helped. The fortress can't be. It was built to
keep out exactly the thing the man needs.
   The third is the scoreboard. Comparison, envy, the quiet ranking of every man in
the room by his truck, his job, his wife, his kids, his body, his salary. Most men do
this without noticing, the way you'd notice the temperature, and it poisons
everything, because a man who's always measuring can never be glad for another
man's win and can never rest in his own, since somebody always has more. Saul had
this, and it drove him to try to kill the young man who'd just won a battle for him.
   The fourth is the podium. This is sin dressed as righteousness, and Jesus hated it
more than any other kind. It's the man who's generous when people are watching,
spiritual when the pastor's in the room, patient with his kids in the church parking
lot and a storm in the truck on the way home. He's performing goodness for a
human audience, and Jesus said such men have already been paid in full, which is
the cruelest thing that can happen to a man: to receive all the reward he's ever going
to get from people who don't really know him.
   The fifth is the throne. Control, harshness, the man who has to win every
conversation in his house and makes sure he does, by volume or by silence or by the
kind of sarcasm that leaves marks. He tells himself he's leading. His wife would tell
you she's managing him, and his kids would tell you they've learned to read the
truck in the driveway. Scripture speaks directly to this one and doesn't soften it.

  Colossians 3:19 (NIV)
  Husbands, love your wives and do not be harsh with them.



  God saw it coming. He knew what a man with a little authority would be



148                                                                 How That Looks on a Man
tempted to do with it, and He put a fence around it in a single sentence. The sixth is
contempt, which is the throne's quieter brother. The sneer, the eye roll at his wife's
story, the disdain for the softer man at church or the weaker man on the crew or the
son who cries. Contempt is a man deciding he's above someone, and it's the fastest
way I know to shrivel a soul, because a man who looks down at people can't look up
at God. It's the same neck.
   And the seventh is the recliner, which we've covered: sloth dressed as rest, the
drift. I'll only say that on a man it doesn't look like laziness. It looks like a fellow
who's earned his evening, and it costs his house the rafters.

The man on the roof

   There's a man in the Bible who wore about half of those shapes at once, in the
span of a single spring, and he was the best man of his generation. I want to walk
through it slowly, because it's the clearest picture in Scripture of how sin looks on a
man, and because if it could take David it can take you. It starts with passivity.

  2 Samuel 11:1 (NIV)
  In the spring, at the time when kings go off to war, David sent Joab out with the king's men and the
  whole Israelite army. They destroyed the Ammonites and besieged Rabbah. But David remained in
  Jerusalem.



   At the time when kings go off to war. That's what kings did, and it's what David
had always done. But that year he sent Joab and stayed home, and nobody would
have called it sin. He was the king, and he'd earned a spring off. But he was a man
on the tailgate with nothing in his hands, and the next verse has him walking
around on his roof in the evening with nothing to do, and you know what happens
on a roof in the evening to a man with nothing to do.
   He sees her, and here's the part men skip: he asks about her, and someone tells
him she's married, and he sends for her anyway. Every mark inverted in a single
afternoon. He didn't reject passivity; he'd been drifting since spring. He didn't
accept responsibility; he'd handed the war to Joab and now he handed his
conscience to his appetite. He didn't lead courageously; he used power, which is the
coward's substitute for courage, and took what he wanted from a woman who
couldn't say no to a king. And he wasn't living for God's reward. He was living for
the next hour.


How That Looks on a Man                                                                                  149
   Then comes the locked drawer, because she's pregnant. He calls her husband
home from the front, gets him drunk, and tries to get him to sleep with his wife so
the child can be passed off. The man won't do it; he's too honorable to sleep in a
bed while his brothers are in the field. So David sends him back to the front with a
sealed order to put him where the fighting is worst and pull back, and Uriah dies,
and David marries the widow, and everything is covered. And then comes the
sentence that ought to keep every competent man awake at night.

  2 Samuel 11:27 (NIV)
  After the time of mourning was over, David had her brought to his house, and she became his wife
  and bore him a son. But the thing David had done displeased the LORD.



   The whole kingdom kept running and the wars kept getting won and nobody
knew. David ran Israel for the better part of a year with a murdered man in his
drawer and a stolen wife in his house, and he was good at it. Competence is not
righteousness. A man can run an entire kingdom while he rots. He can run a
business, a church, a household, a crew. The surfaces stay clean while the block is
cracked.
   Psalm 32, the one we read in the last chapter, is David describing that year from
the inside: bones wasting away, strength sapped, a hand heavy on him. That is what
a locked drawer does to a man even when the drawer is working.

What exposed him

  It wasn't his conscience, and that's the hard truth. David didn't wake up one
morning and decide to come clean. God sent a man.

  2 Samuel 12:7 (NIV)
  Then Nathan said to David, 'You are the man! This is what the LORD, the God of Israel, says: I
  anointed you king over Israel, and I delivered you from the hand of Saul.'



   You are the man. Nathan told him a story about a rich man who stole a poor
man's only lamb, and David got furious, and Nathan said: that's you. A brother, or
in this case a prophet, walked into the throne room and named it to his face. That's
what it took, and it's almost always what it takes. Sin on a man is rarely exposed by
the man. It's exposed by a wife who finally says it, or a brother who asks the


150                                                                           How That Looks on a Man
question in the twenty minutes and won't accept "fine," or a doctor, or a bank, or
the hand of God getting so heavy the man can't stand up under it.
   Which is why you need a Nathan, and why you need to be one. The whole
company of brothers and older men we built in chapters 13 and 14 is, among other
things, a way of making sure somebody can walk into your throne room and say
four words.
   And look at what David did. Not what he said first; he said a great deal. Look at
the sentence that mattered.

  2 Samuel 12:13 (NIV)
  Then David said to Nathan, 'I have sinned against the LORD.' Nathan replied, 'The LORD has
  taken away your sin. You are not going to die.'



   I have sinned against the LORD. Five words, and no "but." No "she came out on
the roof." No "Uriah should have gone home when I told him." No "I'm the king
and I've carried a lot." The sentence stops at the period, which is what we said in
chapter 5 a man's confession does, and the forgiveness comes in the same breath.
There were consequences, terrible ones, for years afterward. But his standing was
restored the moment the drawer was opened.

Checking the mirror

  You're not going to see these shapes on yourself by looking harder, because sin is
deceitful and that's its nature, so you'll need tests, and here are the ones I use.
   The drawer test asks what in your life you wouldn't want your brother to see.
Your phone, your browser history, your bank accounts, your calendar, the
friendship, the account. If there's a drawer, there's a problem, whatever happens to
be in it, because the drawer itself is the problem. A man with nothing hidden has
nothing to defend, and a man with nothing to defend is free.
  The "no" test asks when you last let somebody tell you no and accepted it. Your
wife, about a purchase. A brother, about a plan. An elder, about a decision. A man
on a throne doesn't get told no, because he's arranged his life so that nobody can. If
you can't remember the last time, you're on a throne.
   The wife test is the bravest one. If you're married, ask her, sincerely and in a quiet



How That Looks on a Man                                                                        151
moment, "Where do you see me hiding? Where am I harsh?" And then don't argue.
Listen, and thank her. She has been watching you for years and she knows the
shapes better than you do. If you're single, ask your closest brother or your mother
the same thing. Somebody has been watching.
   The podium test asks what you do only when someone's watching. Where's the
gap between the parking lot and the truck, between Sunday morning and Tuesday
night? Whatever's in that gap is what you're performing, and whoever's watching is
who you're living for.
  And the throne test asks who in your house is afraid of you, not who respects
you but who is afraid. If the answer isn't "no one," you've got work to do, and the
work begins with getting off the throne and onto your knees in front of the people
who've been reading your truck in the driveway.

The shapes in different houses

   If you're single, the drawer and the podium are your largest risks. You have a lot
of unwatched hours and a lot of incentive to look good at church. Get a brother
with the passwords and a habit of asking, and kill the drawer before it has anything
worth hiding in it.
   If there's a baby in your house, watch the throne. Exhaustion makes a man harsh,
and harshness gets rationalized fast when you've slept four hours. Your wife is more
tired than you are, and she's watching what you do with your tiredness. Apologize
the same night, every time.
   If you're divorced, the scoreboard is going to call to you. Comparing your house
to hers, your new life to her new life, your Christmas to theirs. Every comparison is
a small theft of your own peace. Put the scoreboard down. You've got a Father who
sees in secret, and He isn't keeping that score.
    And if you're older, look hard at the fortress. Men past sixty have usually become
very good at needing nothing, and it has cost them. Roy said the hardest question at
his Thursday table was "what do you need from us?" because he'd never once in his
life been asked, and he honestly didn't know. It took him three weeks to come back
with an answer. When he did, the table changed.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.



152                                                              How That Looks on a Man
   A young man of twenty is most at risk of the drawer and the podium: unwatched
hours on one hand, and a strong incentive to look good at church on the other. Eli's
drawer was a second browser he'd never told anyone about, and the day he gave a
brother the passwords he said he felt lighter than he had in three years. The
takeaway for him is to kill the drawer before it holds anything worth hiding,
because the drawer itself is the habit.
   A man of fifty is most at risk of the scoreboard and the fortress together:
measuring himself against every man at church while needing nothing from any of
them. Walt had both for twenty years and didn't know it until the bank parking lot.
The takeaway for him is that a man who can't remember the last time he let anyone
tell him no is on a throne, and a man who can't name what he needs is in a fortress,
and both are pride in a work shirt.

The Man with no drawer

   Jesus had no drawer. There was nothing in Him that He hid from the Father,
nothing He managed, nothing He performed. When they came to arrest Him, He
said He had spoken openly to the world. When they searched for a charge, they had
to invent one. He was fully known, all the way down, and unashamed, because
there was nothing there that needed hiding.
   That's what He's making you into. Not a man with a cleaner garage, but a man
with no locked drawer, whose inside and outside match, who can be fully seen by
his wife and his brothers and his God without flinching, because the thing that used
to need hiding has been confessed and forgiven and is being replaced.
    The man with the red toolbox opened the drawer eventually. His wife found the
phone, which is how these things usually end when a man won't end them himself.
There were two years of wreckage. And then, slowly, there was a marriage with no
drawers in it, which he told me was the first time in his adult life he'd ever felt clean.
"I spent twenty years keeping that garage spotless," he said. "It was the drawer that
was killing me."
   Open the drawer, let a Nathan in, and say the five words.

Before you turn the page

   You've probably found your shape by now, and it isn't a comfortable thing to



How That Looks on a Man                                                                153
find. I'd ask you to remember that David found his only when a man walked into
the room and said four words, and that the five words David said back restored his
standing in a single breath. The Man you're following had no drawer at all, and He
isn't asking you to have a cleaner garage; He's asking you to let a Nathan in. May the
Father give you a brother who's willing to say "you are the man," and the plain
honesty to say "I have sinned" without a "but," and may you find that a man with
nothing to defend is free.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because sin on a man hides best from the man wearing it: say which shape is yours,
  the drawer or the fortress or the scoreboard or the podium or the throne or
  contempt or the recliner, and say one place it showed this week.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because the people who live with you already know the shapes: run the wife test or
  the brother test, ask "where do you see me hiding, and where am I harsh," and listen
  to the whole answer without defending yourself, then thank them.
  TELL A BROTHER
  Because David needed a Nathan and so do you: tell him about your drawer, even if
  it's only a habit of hiding your real thoughts, and give him permission to say "you are
  the man" when he sees it.
  CARRY THIS
  Competence is not righteousness. A man can run a whole kingdom while he rots.
  Open the drawer before someone else does.




154                                                                 How That Looks on a Man
CHAPTER 17



No North Star
   Part 2: What you inherited, and what is yours
  An old rancher taught me to find the North Star when I was a boy, standing in a
pasture outside of Post with no lights for twenty miles in any direction. He showed
me the Big Dipper first, and then how to follow the two stars on the end of the cup
up and out until you landed on a star that wasn't very bright and didn't look like
much.
  "That one doesn't move," he said. "Everything else up there goes around all night.
That one stays put. You get lost, you find that one, and you know which way's
which."
   I asked him why it didn't move. He said he didn't know and didn't need to. "It
just doesn't. That's what makes it useful."
   Years later a mason taught me something similar with a plumb line, which is
nothing more than a string with a weight on the end of it. You hang it next to a wall
and the weight settles and the string points straight down, every time, no matter
how the wall looks, because gravity doesn't have opinions. If the wall's off, the
string tells you. The wall doesn't get to argue with the string.
  Two things that don't move. That's what this chapter is about, because most
men are measuring their lives against things that do.

Everyone did as they saw fit

  There's a sentence near the end of the book of Judges that I think is the saddest
summary of a people I know.




No North Star                                                                       155
  Judges 21:25 (NIV)
  In those days Israel had no king; everyone did as they saw fit.



   Everyone did as they saw fit. Not everyone did evil; that's what makes it sad.
Some of what they did was probably decent. But nobody was measuring by
anything outside himself. Each man's own judgment was his north star, and each
man's north star moved with him, so nobody could tell they were drifting because
everybody was drifting together.
   That describes most men I know. Not bad men; drifting men. They're measuring
themselves against a reference that moves, so they don't feel the drift, and one day
they're forty miles off course in a marriage or a career or a faith and they honestly
can't tell you how they got there. Nobody can, if the thing they were steering by was
moving too.
  Here is what men use as a north star, and why every one of them moves.
    Most men's first fixed point was their father. What he did was what a man does.
Then you grew up and found out he was a moving star too, sometimes wildly, and
if he was the empty chair you never even had a star to fix on. A father is a good thing
to learn from and a terrible thing to steer by.
   Then there are other men, and this is the big one. "I'm doing better than him." I
make more than my brother-in-law. I'm home more than the guys on the crew. I've
never cheated, like the deacon did. I go to church more than my neighbor. Every
one of those comparisons feels like a measurement, and every one is a man holding
his wall up against another crooked wall. You can be the straightest wall in a
crooked town and still be off plumb.
   There's the culture, which is what everybody knows a man should be. The
trouble is that what everybody knows changes about every eight years now, and a
man steering by it is steering by a star that jumps. What made a man in your
grandfather's day and what makes one on the screens your son watches are not the
same star, and chapter 2 already told you that you aren't crazy for noticing.
   There are feelings, which sound like this: "I just don't feel like this is right
anymore." "I feel like God's leading me to." "I don't feel anything when I pray."
Feelings are real and they matter, and Part 5 gives them a whole chapter. But a
feeling is the most moving star there is. It changes with sleep and blood sugar and



156                                                                        No North Star
the weather and what you ate for lunch. A man who steers by his feelings has
decided to navigate by the clouds.
   And there's success, which reasons that if it's working, it must be right. The
business grew, so I must be doing something right with my life. The marriage hasn't
blown up, so it must be fine. David ran a kingdom very well for a year with a
murdered man in his drawer. Success isn't a plumb line. It's a wall that hasn't fallen
yet.

  Proverbs 14:12 (NIV)
  There is a way that appears to be right, but in the end it leads to death.



   Appears to be right. That's what drift feels like from inside. It never feels like
drifting. It feels like a reasonable way to go, and you only find out at the end.

The one that doesn't move

   God has a plumb line, and He told Amos about it.

  Amos 7:7-8 (NIV)
  This is what he showed me: The Lord was standing by a wall that had been built true to plumb, with
  a plumb line in his hand. And the LORD asked me, 'What do you see, Amos?' 'A plumb line,' I
  replied. Then the Lord said, 'Look, I am setting a plumb line among my people Israel; I will spare
  them no longer.'



    I am setting a plumb line among my people. God holds a string with a weight on
it against the wall of a man's life, and the string points true, and the wall doesn't get
to argue. That's what His Word is. Not a set of rules, though it has rules in it. Not a
book of inspiring thoughts, though it will inspire you. It's the fixed reference, the
thing that doesn't move so that you can tell what does.

  Psalm 119:105 (NIV)
  Your word is a lamp for my feet, a light on my path.



   A lamp for my feet, and not a floodlight for the whole valley. Enough light for
the next step, which is what a man on a Tuesday actually needs, and the reason it
can be that is that it's the one thing in your life that isn't drifting along with you.


No North Star                                                                                      157
Paul said the same thing to Timothy near the end of his own life, when Timothy
was surrounded by people doing as they saw fit.

  2 Timothy 3:16-17 (NIV)
  All Scripture is God-breathed and is useful for teaching, rebuking, correcting and training in
  righteousness, so that the servant of God may be thoroughly equipped for every good work.



  Teaching, rebuking, correcting, and training. Look at that list. It teaches you
what's true. It rebukes you when you're off, which is the string against the wall. It
corrects you, which means it shows you how to get back to plumb. And it trains
you, over years, until your hands begin to build true without checking every course.
Thoroughly equipped, Paul says. That's a man with a north star.

Why men don't use it

   I've asked, and here's what men say. They say they don't know how, because
nobody taught them. Their father didn't read it, or read it in a way that made it look
like homework. They tried starting at Genesis in January and were in Leviticus by
February and that was that. That isn't a moral failure; it's a training failure, and it
can be fixed in about a month.
   They say it feels like homework. It does, if you read it like a textbook. Read it
instead like a man listening to his Father talk. Read it out loud in the truck. Read it
with one question in your hand: what does this say a man does?
  They say they don't have time. You have twenty minutes for a brother, and you
have ten for this. Chapter 34 will put it in your week for you. A man who says he
doesn't have ten minutes for the only thing in his life that doesn't move has just told
you what he's steering by.
   And they say they don't feel anything when they read it, and I tell them that's
fine, because you aren't reading it to feel something. You're reading it to find out
which way is north. The rancher didn't feel anything about that dim star either. He
only knew it didn't move.

How a man reads

  Here's how to begin, if you never have, or if you tried and quit. Start with one



158                                                                                         No North Star
Gospel. Mark is the shortest and John is the deepest. Read a chapter a day, and
you'll be done in two or three weeks, and you'll have watched the Man you're
following do everything He did, and you'll know Him better than most men
who've been in church for thirty years.
   Then read Proverbs, a chapter a day, for a month. Dale, the man who took
Micah to breakfast every other Tuesday for two years, started doing that at forty, on
the day he realized he'd spent his whole adult life steering by his father's opinion of
him and his father had been dead for six years. He read a chapter of Proverbs out
loud in his truck on the way to work, every morning, and he's done it for fifteen
years now, which means he's read the book through something like a hundred and
eighty times. When Micah asked him once how he seemed to know what to do
about money and women and anger and friends, Dale looked a little puzzled and
said, "Chapter a day." There are thirty-one chapters, one for each day. It's a father
talking to his son about money and women and work and anger and friends, and it's
the most practical thing ever written for men, and you can read it for fifty years and
it will still be telling you where the wall's off.
   Pray the Psalms, because when you don't know how to pray, which is most
mornings for most men, open to a Psalm and read it out loud to God as your own.
David was a man with every exit you have, and he prayed his way through all of
them, and he left you the words.
   Ask one question every time: what does this say a man does? Not what does it
mean in the original language, and not what a scholar would say. What does this say
a man does, today, on a Tuesday? Then go and do it.
   Read it with your brother. In the twenty minutes, one of you reads the verse
you've been sitting on that week and says why. Iron sharpens iron better with a
plumb line in the room.
    And read it out loud when you can. In the truck, on the porch, at the table with
your kids. There's something about hearing the Word in your own voice that makes
it stop being homework and start being a Father talking.

A story bigger than your story

   Now I want to turn toward the next part of the book, because the north star is
more than a rule book, and if you take it as only that you'll miss most of what it's
for.


No North Star                                                                          159
  We've spent this whole second part on your story. The chair, the exits, the
handoff, the brothers you didn't have, the problem inside and how it looks on you.
All of that is your story, and it's real, and God knows every line of it.
  But your story is not the master story. It's a chapter inside something much older
and much larger, and if all you know is your own chapter, you'll read it wrong.
You'll think the empty chair is the beginning, and it isn't. You'll think your sin is the
end, and it isn't. You'll think the point of your life is to fix what was done to you,
and it isn't that either.
   The master story starts before the chair, in a garden, with a man who was built
for something. It turns on two men and two ways of being a man. And it ends with
a destiny that isn't a feeling. That's the next three chapters. I'm going to hold the
plumb line up against the whole story of manhood, from the first man to the last,
and I want you to see where you are in it.
   Because a man with no north star isn't only lost on Tuesday. He's lost in the
story. He doesn't know what he was made for, so he can't tell whether he's doing it.
The fixed reference isn't only a set of instructions. It's a story that tells you who you
are.

The star at every age

   Men steer by moving stars at every age, but the stars change, and I want you to
see which one is likely to be moving under you now.
   A young man of twenty is usually steering by other young men, and the crooked
walls he measures against are the ones his friends have built. Eli told me his standard
for how to treat a woman had been, for most of his teens, whatever the guys in the
truck laughed at. The takeaway for him is that a young man who reads a chapter of
Mark in the truck each morning has picked up a string that doesn't move, and that
it will save him from measuring by walls that are also on sand.
    A young father is usually steering by his feelings, because a man on four hours of
sleep has almost nothing else to steer by, and his feelings are telling him the marriage
is in trouble when the marriage is only tired. The takeaway for him is that the Word
is the one thing in his life not moving this year, and that ten minutes with it before
the phone is worth more than an hour of evaluating his marriage at three in the
morning.



160                                                                          No North Star
   A man of fifty is usually steering by his success, which is the wall that hasn't
fallen yet. Walt measured his life by eleven trucks for twenty years, and the trucks
told him he was doing fine while his wife built a life without him. The takeaway for
him is that success is not a plumb line, and that a man who's never held the string
against his marriage doesn't know whether it's straight.
   An older man has often steered by the culture he grew up in, and he's watching
that culture get mocked and wondering whether everything was wrong. Hollis, who
has read Proverbs through more times than he can count, told me that the Word
had corrected his generation's picture in some places and confirmed it in most, and
that this was the difference between a man who was shaken by the times and a man
who wasn't. The takeaway for him is that the string doesn't care which decade built
the wall.

The house that held

  Jesus ended the longest sermon He ever gave with a picture that every man in
Texas understands.

  Matthew 7:24-25 (NIV)
  Therefore everyone who hears these words of mine and puts them into practice is like a wise man who
  built his house on the rock. The rain came down, the streams rose, and the winds blew and beat
  against that house; yet it did not fall, because it had its foundation on the rock.



  Hears these words and puts them into practice. Not hears them; hears them and
does them. That's the wise man. He's building on something that doesn't move, and
when the weather comes, and it comes for every house, his house holds.
  The other man in that story built on sand, and he probably built a fine-looking
house. It probably looked fine right up until the rain. He was doing as he saw fit,
measuring by walls that were also on sand, and he never knew until the water rose.
   The rancher is dead now. The pasture outside of Post is still there and so is the
star. I've found it on a lot of nights since, from a lot of places, and it has never once
moved, and every time I look at it I think about a man who told a boy that you
don't have to understand why a thing doesn't move to be saved by it.
  Find the one that doesn't move. Hold the string against your wall. Then turn the
page and let me show you the story it measures you by.



No North Star                                                                                           161
Before you turn the page

   I've told you about a rancher and a star, and a mason and a string, and I want to
leave you with the thing the rancher said that I've never forgotten: you don't have to
understand why a thing doesn't move to be saved by it. You don't have to be a
scholar to read a chapter of Mark out loud in your truck. You only have to hold the
string against the wall and let it settle. May the Father give you a lamp for your feet
this week, enough light for the next step and no more, and may you find, as men
have for three thousand years, that the one who built the wall true to plumb is
patient with the ones who are learning to.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because a man can't stop steering by a moving star until he's admitted which one it
  is: say what you've actually been measuring your life against this year, your father or
  other men or the culture or your feelings or your success, in a full sentence.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because the Word only becomes a north star to a man who reads it with a question:
  read one chapter of Mark each day, out loud if you can, and ask each time what it
  says a man does, and write the answer in one line.
  TELL A BROTHER
  Because two men holding the same string against their walls see more than one: tell
  him which moving star you've been using, then read him one verse from this week
  and tell him what it said a man does and whether you did it.
  CARRY THIS
  You don't have to understand why a thing doesn't move to be saved by it. The wall
  doesn't get to argue with the string.




162                                                                            No North Star
                   PART 3


         The story that names you




Part 3                              163
164   Before the Fall
CHAPTER 18



Before the Fall
   Part 3: The story that names you
   Every man I've ever sat with has wanted to know the same thing underneath all
the other things. Not how to fix his marriage, or how to handle his anger, or how to
get his son back, though he asked about those. Underneath them was a question he
rarely said out loud, and it was this: what was I made for?
    Not what should I do, but what am I for. What is the shape of the thing before I
bent it? Because if you don't know what a tool was made for, you can't tell whether
it's broken. A crowbar makes a lousy hammer, and a man who doesn't know he's a
crowbar will spend his whole life driving nails and wondering why it hurts his
hands.
   So for the next three chapters we're going to leave your story and go back to the
beginning of the master story, the one your story sits inside. This chapter is about
the man before anything went wrong. Not Adam as a theology lesson, but Adam as
a blueprint: what God built when He built a man, before the fall, before the chair
was ever empty, before there was a single exit to take. If you want to know what
you're for, this is where the answer lives, and I have never met a man whose deepest
ache didn't trace back to something on this list.

He was made, on purpose, in an image

  The first thing Scripture says about you is not that you're a sinner. That comes in
chapter 3. The first thing it says is this.




Before the Fall                                                                   165
  Genesis 1:27 (NIV)
  So God created mankind in his own image, in the image of God he created them; male and female he
  created them.



   In his own image. Before you did anything, and before you failed at anything,
you were made to look like God, to represent Him, to be the one thing in creation
that reminds the animals and the angels of Him when they look at it. That isn't a
status you earn. It's a status you were born with, and it's why a man's life has weight
even on the Tuesdays when he does nothing worth mentioning. You carry the
image whether or not you're carrying anything else.
   And notice: male and female. Both carry the image, fully. Your manhood isn't a
step above your wife or your daughter. It's a distinct way of carrying the same
image, and it was God's idea rather than the culture's. When chapter 2 told you that
you aren't crazy for sensing manhood is a real thing and not a costume, this is why:
God made it, and it's in the blueprint.

  Genesis 2:7 (NIV)
  Then the LORD God formed a man from the dust of the ground and breathed into his nostrils the
  breath of life, and the man became a living being.



   Formed, and then breathed. That's a craftsman working with his hands and then
getting close enough to breathe into the thing he's made. You weren't an accident
and you weren't mass-produced. You were formed by hand, and God got close.

He was blessed and given a job before he'd done anything

  The first words God speaks to the man and the woman are a blessing and a
commission, in that order.

  Genesis 1:28 (NIV)
  God blessed them and said to them, 'Be fruitful and increase in number; fill the earth and subdue it.
  Rule over the fish in the sea and the birds in the sky and over every living creature that moves on the
  ground.'



  He blesses them first, and we saw that pattern in chapter 10, with the Father



166                                                                                           Before the Fall
speaking over Jesus at the Jordan before He'd done a thing: identity before
performance. It's here at the very beginning. God blesses the man before the man
has lifted a finger, and then He hands him something enormous to do.
   Be fruitful, fill, subdue, rule: those are large verbs, and they mean that a man was
made to take raw creation and make more of it. Not to sit in the garden and enjoy it,
though he was allowed to. To take the wild thing and bring it to order, to take the
empty thing and fill it, to take what God made good and make it more. A man is a
maker. That's why you feel most alive when you've built something or planted
something or fixed something or started something, when you've taken some piece
of chaos and brought it into order. That isn't ambition; it's the blueprint showing
through.
   Subdue and rule don't mean bully. Look at what God does with His own rule in
the first two chapters: He makes things, He blesses them, He calls them good, He
plants a garden. Ruling the way God rules means making things flourish under your
hand. A man rules his acre by making it fruitful, his crew by making it better, his
house by making it a place where everybody in it grows. Domination is what rule
looks like after the fall. Before the fall, it looked like gardening.

He was placed

   This one is easy to skip, and I don't want you to.

  Genesis 2:8 (NIV)
  Now the LORD God had planted a garden in the east, in Eden; and there he put the man he had
  formed.



   There he put the man. God didn't make Adam and let him wander around
looking for the right place to be. He put him somewhere, on purpose, in a specific
garden with specific trees and specific rivers. Place was part of the design.
   You're somewhere too, in a town and a job and a street and a church and a house,
and most men treat where they are as either an accident or a waiting room, a place
they'll leave for the real place as soon as they can manage it. The blueprint says
otherwise. God puts men in places. The place you're standing on Tuesday, with its
problems and its people and its particular dirt, is a garden God put you in, and He
expects you to work it as though you were meant to be there, because you were. A



Before the Fall                                                                                 167
man who's always looking toward the next place never tends the one under his
boots.

He was given work, and told to guard it

   Here's the verse I'd have every man write on the inside of his forearm if I believed
in that sort of thing.

  Genesis 2:15 (NIV)
  The LORD God took the man and put him in the Garden of Eden to work it and take care of it.



  To work it and take care of it. Two verbs, before the fall, before the curse, before
anything had gone wrong. A man had a job, and it was a real job with dirt in it.
  I want to be plain about this because a lot of Christian men have quietly
absorbed the idea that work is the curse, and it isn't. The curse made work hard:
thorns, sweat, ground that fights back. But work itself is in the design, and a man
who doesn't have real work to do isn't resting; he's malfunctioning. That's why a
man's soul goes sideways when he's laid off, or retires with nothing to do, or gets
hurt and can't. It isn't only the money. It's that he was made to work a garden and
somebody took the garden away.
   And the second verb matters as much as the first. Take care of it, the text says,
and the Hebrew carries the sense of keeping, guarding, watching over. A man isn't
only a worker; he's a keeper. Something has been put in his charge, and he's
supposed to watch it, protect it, and make sure nothing gets in that shouldn't.
Provider and protector are both in that one verse, and they're the same job, and the
job came before the fall.
    Hold onto that thought about guarding, because in the next chapter something
is going to come through the fence, and the man who was told to keep the garden is
going to stand there.

He was given a word, and a line

  Now the man is placed, working, keeping. And God speaks to him alone,
because there is no woman yet.




168                                                                                    Before the Fall
  Genesis 2:16-17 (NIV)
  And the LORD God commanded the man, 'You are free to eat from any tree in the garden; but you
  must not eat from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, for when you eat from it you will
  certainly die.'



  You are free, God says, and that comes first, and men forget it. The garden was
enormous and almost all of it was yes. One tree was no. That's the ratio of God's
design for a man: a wide field of freedom with one clear line, and a plain warning
about what's on the other side of it.
   A man is a creature under a word. He doesn't invent his own standard; that's
what everyone did in the days of the Judges, and we saw how that went. He receives
a word from Someone above him and lives inside it. That isn't a limitation on
manhood. It's the thing that makes manhood possible, because a man without a
word is a man with no north star, and we spent the last chapter on him.
   And notice who received the word. The man did, alone, before Eve existed. He
was the one who heard the command from God's own mouth. He was the one who
would be responsible to carry it, to teach it, to keep it, to guard the garden against
anything that would try to talk them across the line. That's going to matter
enormously in the next chapter, when the line gets crossed and he's standing right
there.

He was given words of his own

  God did something with Adam that He didn't do with anything else He'd made.
He let him name things.

  Genesis 2:19-20 (NIV)
  Now the LORD God had formed out of the ground all the wild animals and all the birds in the sky.
  He brought them to the man to see what he would name them; and whatever the man called each
  living creature, that was its name. So the man gave names to all the livestock, the birds in the sky and
  all the wild animals. But for Adam no suitable helper was found.



   Whatever the man called each living creature, that was its name. God brought the
animals to Adam and let him speak, and what he spoke stuck. That's authority
given by delegation. God, who had been naming things for six days, light and day



Before the Fall                                                                                          169
and sky and sea, handed the naming over to the man.
   A man's words carry weight. That's in the blueprint. What you call your son,
he'll become. What you call your wife, in front of the children and inside your own
head, shapes what she becomes in your house. What you name a situation, a crisis or
a challenge, a disaster or a Tuesday, is largely what it becomes. Men underestimate
this because they don't talk much, but the few words a man does say land like
hammer blows, because God built the world to listen when a man speaks. We'll
spend a whole chapter in Part 4 on giving your son a name, and it starts here.

He was told it was not good to be alone

  Now comes the sentence that changes everything.

  Genesis 2:18 (NIV)
  The LORD God said, 'It is not good for the man to be alone. I will make a helper suitable for him.'



   Not good, He says, when He had said "good" about everything so far. Light,
land, plants, stars, fish, birds, beasts: good, good, very good. And then He looks at a
man by himself, in a perfect garden, with meaningful work and God's own voice to
talk to, and He says it is not good.
   Sit with that for a moment. The man wasn't sinning. He wasn't lonely the way a
man is lonely after a divorce. He had everything, including God, and God Himself
said a man alone is not good. Your need for other people isn't weakness. It isn't a
character flaw you'd outgrow if you were stronger; it's the blueprint, because God
built you needing others, on purpose, before anything broke.
   And look at what He didn't do. He didn't bring the animals and say, "Here, one
of these will do." The text goes out of its way to tell us that no suitable helper was
found among them. The man's need wasn't for company; dogs are company. His
need was for a counterpart, someone like him and not him, someone who could
stand across from him and see him and be seen. That's what "suitable" means:
corresponding, matching, fitting the way a hand fits another hand.
  Most men read this as a marriage verse, and it is one. But hear it wider than that.
"Not good for the man to be alone" is the reason for chapter 13 and chapter 14. It's
why you need brothers and a man ahead of you and a church, and not only a wife.
The first remedy God made was a bride, but it wasn't the only kind of not-alone He


170                                                                                         Before the Fall
had in mind, and a single man isn't exempt from the verse. He's simply going to
need to find his not-alone in more than one place.

He was given a bride, and he spoke

   God put the man to sleep and built the woman from his side. Not from the dust,
as He'd made the man, but from him. Then He brought her, the way He'd brought
the animals, and the man spoke again, and what he said is the first recorded human
speech in the Bible.

  Genesis 2:23 (NIV)
  The man said, 'This is now bone of my bones and flesh of my flesh; she shall be called "woman," for
  she was taken out of man.'



   The first thing a man ever said was a poem about his wife. I want that to land,
because most husbands I know can't remember the last time they said anything to
their wife that wasn't logistics. The blueprint has a man looking at his bride and
being moved to speech: bone of my bones, flesh of my flesh, this one, finally.
   Then the text tells us what a man does with a bride: he leaves his father and
mother and is united to her, and they become one flesh. We spent chapter 12 on the
leaving. Here I only want to point at the joining. Marriage is in the blueprint before
the fall. It isn't a social invention or a legal arrangement or a religious add-on. It's
the first human covenant, spoken by God over the first two people, and the man
was given the job of holding fast. And then this, which is the last sentence before
everything breaks.

  Genesis 2:25 (NIV)
  Adam and his wife were both naked, and they felt no shame.



   No shame, and no drawer. Nothing hidden from each other or from God. That's
what a man looked like before the fall: fully seen and unafraid of it. Every locked
drawer since is a man trying to get back to the garden through the wrong door.

He was given rest

   One more, and it's the one men skip because it doesn't look like a man's verse.


Before the Fall                                                                                         171
  Genesis 2:2-3 (NIV)
  By the seventh day God had finished the work he had been doing; so on the seventh day he rested from
  all his work. Then God blessed the seventh day and made it holy, because on it he rested from all the
  work of creating that he had done.



  God rested, and not because He was tired. He rested because rest is part of how
good work goes. He built it into the week before the man ever picked up a tool, and
He called that day holy, which He didn't call any of the other six.
   A man isn't a machine. The blueprint has him working six days and stopping for
one, and the stopping isn't a break from the design; it is the design. Men who work
seven days aren't more manly. They've torn a page out of the blueprint and are
wondering why the house won't stand. The drift we talked about in chapter 9 is
very often what happens to a man who never learned to rest. He never stops, so
eventually he simply goes numb, and numb isn't rest. It's a breakdown with the
television on.

The shape of a man

   So here's the man before the fall. He was made in God's image, by hand and on
purpose, and blessed before he'd done anything, and then given a real job, which
was to make this place more than it is. He was put somewhere specific, and given
work with dirt in it, and told to guard what he'd been given. He was given a word
from above, with a wide field of freedom and one clear line, and given words of his
own that carried weight. He was told he wasn't built to be alone, and given a bride,
and moved to poetry by her. He was naked and unashamed, and he was given rest.
    That's the shape, and that's what you're for. Now go back through the book with
that list in your hand. The man on the tailgate in chapter 4 is a man who was made
to work it and keep it, sitting still. The man who won't pick up weight in chapter 5
is a man who was given a word and a garden and won't carry either. The empty
