chair in chapter 8 is a boy who was supposed to be blessed first and named by his
father, and wasn't. The itch in chapter 9 is a man who was built for one flesh and no
shame, settling for a screen and a locked drawer. The man with no brothers in
chapter 13 is a man God said it was not good to leave alone, who decided he was
fine. The drawer in chapter 16 is naked and unashamed, inverted.
  Every ache traces back. Every exit is a man walking away from something on this


172                                                                                         Before the Fall
list. And every one of the four marks is a piece of the blueprint. Rejecting passivity
is a man doing what he was made to do: working it and keeping it. Accepting
responsibility is a man carrying the word he was given. Leading courageously is a
man using the authority God delegated to him, speaking, naming, going first. And
expecting the greater reward is a man remembering that the whole garden was built
around walking with God in the cool of the day, and that nothing else in it was ever
the point.

The blueprint in every season

    If you're single, notice something about the list. Almost every item on it was
given to Adam while he was alone. The image, the blessing, the commission, the
place, the work, the keeping, the word, the naming. He was a full man before there
was a bride. God's remedy for his aloneness came, but the design was complete
without it. You don't become a man when you get married. You become a man by
living the blueprint, and a wife is one of the ways God fills the "not good to be
alone," not the thing that makes you count. Work your garden and guard it and
carry the word, and leave the rest to God's timing.
    If there's a baby in your house, you're standing in the middle of Genesis 1:28, the
part that says be fruitful. You did it, and it's harder than it sounded on the day, and
that's the curse sitting on top of the blessing: the design is still good and the ground
still fights you, and you have to hold both. The exhaustion doesn't mean you've left
the blueprint. It means you're working it.
   If you're divorced, "it is not good for the man to be alone" is still true about you,
and it hurts to read, and I'm sorry. God hasn't changed His mind about the design
because a marriage broke. He isn't asking you to rush into another one. He's telling
you that the not-alone is still in your blueprint and that it has more than one shape:
brothers first, then a man ahead of you, then a church that knows your name. The
bride question is His to answer in His time, and you can be a whole man on this list
while you wait.
   And if you're older, look at Genesis 2:15 and try to find the retirement clause,
because there isn't one. The work-and-keep design didn't expire when the paycheck
did. The garden changed; you aren't the keeper of a payroll anymore. But you're the
keeper of something: grandchildren, younger men, a wife who's carried a lot, a
church full of people who need a man who has time and has learned things. Roy



Before the Fall                                                                      173
told me the day he read Genesis 2:15 after retiring was the day he stopped feeling
like a man who'd been let go and started feeling like a man who'd been reassigned.
"Same job," he said. "Different garden."
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty is most often bent at the work and the word. He hasn't
found real work that has dirt in it, or he treats the work he has as a stopgap, and he's
inventing his own standard because nobody handed him one. Eli's garden turned
out to be the parts counter and a fatherless nine-year-old he started coaching, and
the takeaway for him is that the blueprint was complete in Adam before there was a
bride, and it's complete in him.
   A man of fifty is most often bent at not-alone, which is Walt with three hundred
contacts and nobody, and at the keeping, because he's been working the garden and
letting the fence go. The takeaway for him is that the second verb of Genesis 2:15 is
guard, and that a man who provides and doesn't guard has done half his job.

What's coming

   The man in this chapter is standing in a garden he was told to work and keep. He
has God's word in his ear, the one about the tree, the one only he heard. He has a
bride he sang over. He has nothing hidden. He has authority to speak, and
everything listens when he does.
   And something is about to come through the fence. The next chapter is about
what he did when it came, and about another Man, a long time later, who stood in a
different garden with everything on the line and did the opposite. Two men, two
ways. Everything you've ever done as a man is a version of one of them.
   But before you turn the page, sit here a minute longer. You were made for this.
Not for the exits, and not for the drawer, and not for the tailgate. For a garden, a
word, a work, a bride, a company of others, a rest, and a walk with God in the cool
of the day. That's what you're for. That's the shape you were bent from, and it's the
shape you're being bent back toward.

Before you turn the page

  You were made for this, and I'd like you to sit with that before the next chapter,


174                                                                          Before the Fall
where the garden gets broken. You were formed by hand and breathed on. You
were blessed before you'd done anything. You were placed, and given a work with
dirt in it, and told to guard it, and given a word and words of your own and a rest.
Every ache you've felt as a man traces back to something on that list, and every one
of them is a place God is bending you back toward. May the Father who made you
in His image remind you tonight what you're for, and may you wake up tomorrow
in your garden knowing it's yours.

   YOUR TURN



   NAME IT
   Because a man can't walk back toward a design he hasn't looked at: say which piece
   of the blueprint is most bent in you right now, the image or the work or the keeping
   or the word or your own words or the not-alone or the bride or the rest, in a full
   sentence.
   DO THIS WEEK
   Because the blueprint is meant to be heard and not only summarized: read Genesis 1
   and 2 out loud, start to finish, on a Tuesday, and then write one line about what it
   says a man is for.
   TELL A BROTHER
   Because another man often sees a different piece bent than the one you see: tell him
   which piece you named and what walking back toward it would look like this
   month, and ask him which piece he sees bent in you.
   CARRY THIS
   You were made to work it and keep it. The job came before the curse. You aren't off
   the blueprint; you're on it, and it still fits.




Before the Fall                                                                       175
176   Two Men, Two Ways
CHAPTER 19



Two Men, Two Ways
  Part 3: The story that names you
   There were two gardens, and two men, and two nights when everything was on
the line.
   In the first garden the man had everything and lost it in an afternoon without
saying a word. In the second garden the Man had nothing left but a choice, and He
made it with His face in the dirt, and it saved the world.
   Everything you've ever done as a man is a version of one of those two nights, and
I mean that literally. Every time you've stood silent when you should have spoken,
every time you've blamed when you should have owned, every time you've hidden
when you should have stood up, every time you've grabbed the thing in front of
you instead of trusting the thing that was promised, you were in the first garden
doing what the first man did. And every time you've done the opposite, on a
Tuesday, when it cost you something, you were following the second Man into the
second garden. The Bible puts the whole human story on two men, not on two
teams or two philosophies, but on two men.

  Romans 5:19 (NIV)
  For just as through the disobedience of the one man the many were made sinners, so also through the
  obedience of the one man the many will be made righteous.



  The one man, and the one man. This chapter is about the two of them, and
about which one you're in, and about which one's way you're going to walk this
week.

The first garden



Two Men, Two Ways                                                                                       177
  You know the story, but let me tell it the way a man ought to hear it.
   The serpent comes into the garden. He doesn't go to the man. He goes to the
woman, and he starts asking questions about the word God gave, the one about the
tree. He twists it, and he suggests God's holding out on them. He tells her she won't
die; she'll be like God. Now, where is the man?

  Genesis 3:6 (NIV)
  When the woman saw that the fruit of the tree was good for food and pleasing to the eye, and also
  desirable for gaining wisdom, she took some and ate it. She also gave some to her husband, who was
  with her, and he ate it.



   He was with her. We read that in chapter 3 of this book, and I told you then that
those three words have been indicting men ever since, and here's why. Remember
who received the command. Adam did, alone, from God's own mouth, before the
woman existed. He was the one who'd been told to work the garden and to keep it,
to guard it. And when the thing he was supposed to guard against came through the
fence and started twisting the word he'd been given, he stood there.
   He didn't argue with the serpent. He didn't say, "That isn't what God said." He
didn't take his wife by the hand and walk away from the tree. He did nothing at all.
He watched, and then, when she handed it to him, he ate. The first sin of the first
man wasn't grabbing the fruit. It was silence while the word was twisted, which is
passivity, the first mark inverted before any of the others. Then God comes walking,
and they hide, and God asks where he is, and the man answers.

  Genesis 3:10 (NIV)
  He answered, 'I heard you in the garden, and I was afraid because I was naked; so I hid.'



  I was afraid, so I hid. The man who was naked and unashamed a page earlier is
now ashamed and hiding. That's the third mark inverted: cowardice instead of
courage. He heard God's voice, the voice he'd walked with in the cool of the day,
and instead of going toward it he went into the trees.
   Then God asks what he did, and he says the sentence we've already talked about:
the woman you put here, she gave it to me. He blames his wife and he blames God
and he leaves himself out. That's the second mark inverted: responsibility refused.




178                                                                                  Two Men, Two Ways
   And under all of it is the fourth. Why did he eat? Because the serpent said "you
will be like God," and that was a reward he could have right then, in his hand,
instead of the reward of walking with God every evening for the rest of forever. He
traded the greater reward for the nearer one. He chose the fruit over the Father.
   Four marks, four inversions, in one afternoon. Passive, evasive, hiding, and
grabbing. That's the first man's way, and it's the default setting of every man since,
and if you've read the first seventeen chapters of this book you've seen your own
face in it more than once, and so have I.
   And then the curse. Thorns in the work, pain in the bearing, death at the end,
exile from the garden. The man who was made to keep the garden is put outside the
fence, with an angel guarding the gate against him.
   But even there, two things happen. God makes them clothes with His own
hands, from an animal that had to die to provide them. The first covering came at
the cost of a life; hold onto that. And God says a word to the serpent in the middle
of the curse that turns out to be a promise.

  Genesis 3:15 (NIV)
  And I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your offspring and hers; he will
  crush your head, and you will strike his heel.



   He will crush your head, which means somebody is coming: a son of the woman
who will do what the first man didn't, who will stand in a garden when the enemy
comes and will not be silent. It will cost him, since you will strike his heel. But he'll
crush the thing that got in through the fence.
  The second Man was promised in the first garden, and everything between
Genesis 3 and the Gospels is the world waiting for Him.

The second garden

   It's night, in a garden on a hillside outside Jerusalem, olive trees, a place He had
gone many times before. He's brought His men, and He's taken three of them
farther in and told them to stay and watch, and then He goes on alone and falls on
His face.




Two Men, Two Ways                                                                                 179
  Matthew 26:39 (NIV)
  Going a little farther, he fell with his face to the ground and prayed, 'My Father, if it is possible, may
  this cup be taken from me. Yet not as I will, but as you will.'



   Look at what's on the table. The first man was offered fruit that would make him
like God. The second Man is offered a cup that will make Him like a criminal, and
He doesn't want it, and He says so. Every honest fiber in Him is asking for another
way. This isn't a Man who felt nothing. This is a Man sweating blood.
    And then: not as I will, but as You will. That's the second mark. The first man
wouldn't carry the word he was given. The second Man picks up a cup He didn't
fill, for a sin He didn't commit, and says that it's His and He'll carry it. He accepts
responsibility for the whole broken garden, all of it, every locked drawer and every
slammed door and every silence, and He does it on His knees with dirt on His face.
   He prays three times. The men He asked to watch fall asleep every time. He
wakes them and they fall asleep again. He is completely alone, and He does not go
into the trees. He does the opposite.

  Matthew 26:46 (NIV)
  Rise! Let us go! Here comes my betrayer!



  The first man heard God coming and hid. The second Man hears the mob
coming, torches and swords and a friend about to kiss Him, and He stands up and
walks toward it. John tells it even more sharply.

  John 18:4 (NIV)
  Jesus, knowing all that was going to happen to him, went out and asked them, 'Who is it you want?'



   He went out, John says. He didn't wait for them to find Him among the trees;
He went out to meet them, knowing. That's the third mark. That's going first, into
the worst thing, with nobody following and the men who'd promised to die with
Him about to run. Courage isn't the absence of fear; we said that back in chapter 6,
and here's the proof. He was afraid enough to sweat blood, and He went out
anyway. And while they're arresting Him, He does something the first man never
thought to do. He covers His men.


180                                                                                      Two Men, Two Ways
  John 18:8 (NIV)
  'I told you that I am he,' Jesus answered. 'If you are looking for me, then let these men go.'



   Let these men go. The first man, caught, pointed at his wife. The second Man,
caught, points at Himself and tells them to leave everybody else alone. That's what
responsibility looks like when it's finished: a man standing between the danger and
the people he's been given, saying "me, not them."
   And why did He do it? What was He working for? The first man grabbed for a
reward he could have that afternoon. The second Man had one in view too, but it
lay on the other side of a cross. The writer of Hebrews says He endured it for the joy
set before Him. And when it was done, when He'd carried the whole thing to the
end, He said one last thing about the work.

  John 19:30 (NIV)
  When he had received the drink, Jesus said, 'It is finished.' With that, he bowed his head and gave up
  his spirit.



   It is finished, He said, and notice the contrast. The first man's last recorded
words before the exile were an excuse. The second Man's last words before death
were a completion report. The work I was given, I did, all of it. That's the fourth
mark. He wasn't living for the crowd, which had turned on Him. He wasn't living
for His men, who had fled. He was living for the Father's "well done," and He got it
three days later, and then some.

  Philippians 2:9-11 (NIV)
  Therefore God exalted him to the highest place and gave him the name that is above every name, that
  at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, in heaven and on earth and under the earth, and every
  tongue acknowledge that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father.



  Therefore, Paul says, and the word means because He did it, because He picked
up the cup and went out to meet them and covered His men and finished. The
reward came, and it was the greatest reward ever given, and it was given to a Man
who looked like a loser to everyone who watched Him die.




Two Men, Two Ways                                                                                     181
Two ways, laid side by side

   Let me set them next to each other, because you're going to be standing in a
garden this week and you need to see the two paths clearly.
   The first man was silent when the word was twisted. The second Man spoke,
even to the mob: "Who is it you want?"
  The first man let the danger come to his wife and watched. The second Man
went out to meet the danger before it reached His men.
    The first man, caught, said "she did it." The second Man, caught, said "take me,
let them go."
  The first man hid from God's voice in the trees. The second Man sought the
Father's face in the dirt, three times, and then got up.
  The first man grabbed the reward in his hand. The second Man trusted the
reward on the far side of the cross.
  The first man's last words were an excuse. The second Man's were "it is finished."
    And here's what I want you to notice about the four marks now. A real man
rejects passivity, accepts responsibility, leads courageously, and expects the greater
reward, God's reward. When we laid those out in chapter 3, they might have
sounded like a list of qualities, the kind of thing you'd find on any shelf of books
about men, next to the ones about wildness and adventure and finding your heart.
They aren't that at all. They are the shape of the second Man in the second garden,
written out so a man can carry it in his pocket. Rejecting passivity is "Rise, let us
go." Accepting responsibility is "not as I will, but as you will." Leading courageously
is "went out and asked them." Expecting the greater reward is "for the joy set before
him." The marks aren't a personality profile. They're a portrait of Jesus in the worst
hour of His life, and every time you live one of them on a Tuesday, you're wearing
His face.

Which man you're in

  Now the part that matters most, and it's the part men skip when they turn the
two gardens into a pep talk. The Bible doesn't only say "be like the second man and
not the first." It says something stranger and better. It says you're in one of them.



182                                                                   Two Men, Two Ways
  1 Corinthians 15:22 (NIV)
  For as in Adam all die, so in Christ all will be made alive.



  In Adam, and in Christ. You were born in the first man. His way is your default.
That's why passivity comes so easily, why blame is the first thing out of your
mouth, why hiding feels natural and grabbing feels right. It isn't only that you copy
Adam. You were in him when he did it, the way a family is in a father. His garden is
your inheritance, and there's no amount of trying that gets you out of it.
    But God isn't asking you to try your way out. He's offering to move you. Into
the second Man, into Christ, so that what He did in His garden counts as yours the
way what Adam did counted as yours. His obedience becomes your record. His "it
is finished" becomes the last word over your life instead of the excuse. That's the
gospel, and it's the only reason a man can read the first half of this chapter without
despair.

  Galatians 2:20 (NIV)
  I have been crucified with Christ and I no longer live, but Christ lives in me. The life I now live in the
  body, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me.



   Christ lives in me. That isn't a metaphor for trying hard. It's a change of location.
A man in Christ has the second Man's Spirit in him, the same Spirit that got up off
the ground in Gethsemane, and that's why he can get up off his own. Not because
he's stronger than Adam, but because he isn't in Adam anymore.
   So if you've never done it, do it now, not at the end of the chapter. Tell God
you've been in the first man's garden your whole life, doing his silence and his blame
and his hiding and his grabbing, and that you're done. Tell Him you want to be in
the second Man, that you're trusting what He did in His garden to cover what you
did in yours. That's the door, and it's the only one, and it's open, and it's the
beginning of everything else in this book actually working.
   And if you've already done it, then hear the rest, because you're in Him, and the
four marks aren't a standard you're trying to reach so that He'll accept you. They're
the family resemblance showing up, because you're His.




Two Men, Two Ways                                                                                          183
Tuesday's garden

   I told you you'd stand in a garden this week, so let me tell you what it will look
like, because it won't have olive trees in it.
   It'll be a kitchen. Your wife says something that isn't quite fair, and every fiber in
you wants to go quiet and let it sit, or to fire back with the thing you've been saving.
That kitchen is the garden. The first man's way is silence or blame. The second
Man's way is a soft, clear word: "That landed hard. Can we talk about it after the
kids are down?" Rise, let us go.
   It'll be a phone at eleven at night with nobody watching, and that phone is the
garden. The first man grabbed what was in his hand. The second Man trusted the
reward on the far side of a hard night. Put it on the charger in the kitchen and go to
bed, for the joy set before you.
   It'll be a mess at work that's partly your fault and mostly someone else's, with
everybody waiting to see who gets blamed, and that meeting is the garden. The first
man said "she did it." The second Man said "let these men go." Own your piece first,
out loud, before anyone asks.
   It'll be two in the morning with the baby crying, and you can hear your wife
starting to move, and you could simply lie there, and that dark bedroom is the
garden, and the sentence for it is not as I will.
   It'll be your father's driveway and a conversation you've been avoiding for ten
years, and that driveway is the garden, where the second Man went out and asked
them.
   The gardens are small, and that's the point of the Tuesday spine this whole book
runs on. Adam's afternoon looked small too; it was one piece of fruit. Gethsemane
looked like one man praying alone in the dark. The size of the garden has nothing to
do with the size of what's decided in it.

The two men in every season

   If you're single, notice that the second Man was single. He stood in His garden
without a wife, and He was the fullest man who ever lived. Don't let anyone,
including yourself, tell you that your manhood is on hold until you're married. The
garden is wherever you're standing, so stand in it.


184                                                                    Two Men, Two Ways
    If there's a baby in your house, Gethsemane is a two-in-the-morning story. A
Man who didn't want to, who asked for another way, who got up and did it anyway
because the Father asked. You're going to pray "may this cup be taken" a great deal
this year, about sleep and about your marriage and about your own patience. Pray
it; He did. Then say the second sentence and get up.
   If you're divorced, look hard at "let these men go." The first man's way after a
marriage breaks is to point: at her, at the lawyers, at the God who put her here. The
second Man's way is to stand between the wreckage and the people you've been
given and say "me, not them." Your children should never have to carry your war, so
cover them, and own your part out loud, and let God sort hers.
   And if you're older, think about last words. The first man's were an excuse. The
second Man's were "it is finished." You have a stretch of road left, and you get to
decide what the completion report says: not "it wasn't my fault," but "the work You
gave me, I did." Roy told me he'd started praying for exactly that. "Let me finish.
Whatever's left, let me finish it." That is a Gethsemane prayer from a man in his
seventies, and it's exactly right.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty is most tempted by the first man's grabbing: the reward
he can have tonight instead of the one that's promised. Eli's ten o'clock phone is
Adam's fruit, and the takeaway for him is that the second Man trusted a reward on
the far side of a hard night, and that putting the phone in the kitchen is a
Gethsemane-sized act done in a one-bedroom apartment.
   A man of forty-five is most tempted by the first man's blame, pointing at his wife
or his boss or his father when the mess is partly his. Anthony's kitchen was Adam's
excuse until the night he laid the sentence down. The takeaway for him is that the
second Man said "let these men go" while being arrested, and that a husband who
says "that's mine" first has walked out of the trees.

The last Adam

  Paul gave Jesus a title I've come to love.




Two Men, Two Ways                                                                 185
  1 Corinthians 15:45 (NIV)
  So it is written: 'The first man Adam became a living being'; the last Adam, a life-giving spirit.



  The last Adam, Paul calls Him, and not the second in a series but the last, the one
who finally did what the first was supposed to do, in a garden, when the enemy
came through the fence. There will never be another one, because there doesn't
need to be, because He finished it. And He isn't asking you to be Him. He's asking
you to be in Him, and then to walk His way through your Tuesday gardens with
His Spirit in you and His record over you. Two men, two ways. You were born in
one. You've been moved into the other. Now go stand in your garden like the Man
you're in.

Before you turn the page

   I've laid the two men side by side, and I know the first one looks a great deal like
you, because he looks a great deal like me. I want you to hear the gospel one more
time before we leave this garden. You were born in the first man, and his way is your
default, and no amount of trying gets you out of him. But God has offered to move
you into the second Man, so that His "it is finished" becomes the last word over
your life instead of the excuse. If you've never asked Him to, ask Him tonight. If
you have, then go stand in your Tuesday garden as a man who's already been
moved. May the Father who raised the last Adam raise you up off the ground this
week, and may you go out to meet what's coming with His Spirit in you and His
record over you.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because the two ways only become visible when you see them in a specific garden:
  think of the last one you stood in this week, the kitchen or the phone or the two in
  the morning, and say plainly which man's way you took.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because the second Man's sentences are meant to be said and not only admired: each
  morning, name the garden you're most likely to face that day and say His sentence
  for it out loud, "Rise, let us go," or "Not as I will," or "Let these men go," and then
  walk into the day.


186                                                                                    Two Men, Two Ways
TELL A BROTHER
Because the question of which man you're in is the most important question in this
book: tell him which one, in the deepest sense, and if you've never trusted Christ to
move you out of Adam, tell him that and let him walk you through it; if you have, tell
him one garden you walked the second Man's way through.
CARRY THIS
The four marks are not a list of qualities. They are the portrait of Jesus in the worst hour
of His life. Every time you live one, you're wearing His face.




Two Men, Two Ways                                                                       187
188   Destiny Is Not a Vibe
CHAPTER 20



Destiny Is Not a Vibe
   Part 3: The story that names you
   A young man told me over coffee that he'd left his fourth job in three years
because it didn't feel like his calling. He was twenty-seven. He was sharp, and
hardworking when he worked, and a believer, and completely stuck, because
somewhere along the way he'd been taught that God had one specific thing for him
to do with his life and that he'd know it when he felt it. He'd been waiting for the
feeling since he was nineteen, and it hadn't come, so he kept leaving, and every time
he left he was a little more certain the next thing would be the one, and a little less
able to stay anywhere long enough to find out.
   Across town that same week I sat with Hollis, the deacon who told Cole he was a
good man. Hollis is seventy-four. He's worked the same land his father worked,
taught the same Sunday school class for forty-one years, buried a wife and a son, and
never once in his life, he told me, had anything he would call a calling: no voice, no
lightning, no moment where he simply knew.
   "I just did the next thing God put in front of me," he said. "For about fifty-five
years."
  I asked him whether he thought he'd found his destiny. He looked at me the way
you'd look at a man who had asked whether you'd found your hands.
   Here's what I want to say in this chapter, and I'm going to say it straight because
a whole generation of men has been sold the opposite. Destiny is not a vibe. It isn't a
feeling of significance. It isn't a sense that you're meant for something large that
hasn't shown up yet. It's an assignment, given by God, carried out in a place, in a
generation, mostly on Tuesdays, and finished. Hollis has one, and the young man
across town has one too, and he has been walking past it four jobs in a row.




Destiny Is Not a Vibe                                                                   189
The verse that ends the argument

   There's a sentence in Acts that I think is the best one-line definition of a man's
destiny in the Bible. Paul is preaching in a synagogue, walking through the history
of Israel, and when he gets to David, the greatest king they ever had, he says this.

  Acts 13:36 (NIV)
  Now when David had served God's purpose in his own generation, he fell asleep; he was buried with
  his ancestors and his body decayed.



   He served God's purpose in his own generation. That's the whole thing. Not
"discovered his passion" or "found what he was born to do" or "lived his best life."
He served God's purpose, in his own generation, meaning among the people and in
the place and time God had put him rather than some other. And then he died and
his body decayed, which Paul includes so you'll understand that even David's
destiny wasn't about David lasting. It was about the purpose lasting.
   Notice the word served. Destiny is a service word. It's a man handed a job by
Someone above him, and doing it. It's the corner post, not the parade. And notice
that it's God's purpose and not David's. David had plenty of purposes of his own,
and the ones that came from him nearly wrecked everything. The one that came
from God is the one Paul is still talking about a thousand years later.
 That is your destiny too. To serve God's purpose in your own generation.
Whether or not it feels like anything is beside the point.

Three lies

  Let me name what most men have absorbed about destiny, because you can't put
down a lie you haven't looked at.
   The first lie is that you'll know it when you feel it. This is the one that had the
young man changing jobs. The idea is that somewhere out there is a life that will
finally feel right, and that the not-feeling-right is proof you haven't found it yet. But
feelings are the most moving star in the sky; we said so in chapter 17. Moses didn't
feel called; he argued with God for an entire chapter. Jeremiah felt too young.
Gideon asked for signs three times. Jonah felt so strongly about his calling that he
got on a boat going the opposite direction. If feelings were the test, none of them


190                                                                                Destiny Is Not a Vibe
would have gone. The feeling, when it comes, comes after the going, and sometimes
it never comes at all, and the work counts anyway.
   The second lie is that it's big. The culture has taught you that a real destiny has a
platform, a visible impact, a story people tell. So a man who has spent thirty years
welding, raising three kids, teaching a Sunday school class, and showing up for his
neighbors looks at his life and concludes he missed it, because nobody is telling his
story. Meanwhile Jesus told a story about a master who came back and praised a
servant for being faithful with a few things, only a few, and the reward for
faithfulness in small things was more things to be faithful with. There's no version
of Scripture where God's purpose for most men is a stage. His purpose for most
men is a garden, and gardens are worked in obscurity.

  Zechariah 4:10 (NIV)
  Who dares despise the day of small things, since the seven eyes of the LORD that range throughout
  the earth will rejoice when they see the chosen capstone in the hand of Zerubbabel?



  Who dares despise the day of small things. God's eyes range over the whole earth,
and they rejoice at a man laying a stone. The small day isn't the waiting room for
destiny. It's where destiny happens.
   The third lie is that it's somewhere else: the next job, the next town, after I get
married, after the kids are gone, after I retire. Men live their whole lives with destiny
one move away, and the move keeps moving. But remember chapter 18: God placed
the man. He put him in a specific garden and told him to work that one. Your
generation is this one, and your place is where your boots are. Paul told the
Athenians, who thought a great deal about destiny, that God marks out men's times
and places on purpose.

  Acts 17:26 (NIV)
  From one man he made all the nations, that they should inhabit the whole earth; and he marked out
  their appointed times in history and the boundaries of their lands.



  Appointed times and boundaries, Paul says, which means you aren't in your
town and your century by accident. The purpose is here, and it won't be more here
somewhere else.




Destiny Is Not a Vibe                                                                                 191
Three truths

   Now the other side. The first truth is that destiny is assigned, not discovered.
You don't find it the way you'd find a good fishing spot. It was prepared before you
arrived, and it's handed to you.

  Ephesians 2:10 (NIV)
  For we are God's handiwork, created in Christ Jesus to do good works, which God prepared in advance
  for us to do.



   Prepared in advance, the text says, which means the works are already laid out,
and your job isn't to invent them. It's to do the next one. And here's what that frees
you from: the terror of missing it. A man who believes he has to discover his one
true purpose lives in fear that he'll choose wrong and waste his life. A man who
knows the works were prepared in advance simply gets up and does what's in front
of him, trusting that the One who prepared them will keep putting them there.
That's Hollis, fifty-five years of the next thing, and he never once worried he'd
missed his destiny, because he wasn't looking for it. He was doing it.
  The second truth is that it's local. Your purpose has an address. It's your wife's
name and your children's names and your street and your crew and your church
and the men at the Thursday table. God's purpose for David was Israel and not the
whole world; He handled the whole world through a great many Davids in a great
many places. Esther's cousin said it to her when she was deciding whether to risk her
neck for her people.

  Esther 4:14 (NIV)
  For if you remain silent at this time, relief and deliverance for the Jews will arise from another place,
  but you and your family will perish. And who knows but that you have come to your royal position for
  such a time as this?



   For such a time as this. Not for some other time, and not for a bigger position.
This one, now, here. And look at the warning that comes with it. If you stay silent,
God will do it another way, and you'll have missed your part. That's the cost of
treating your generation as a waiting room. God's purpose gets served regardless. It
just gets served by somebody else, while you spent your one life waiting for a feeling.



192                                                                                     Destiny Is Not a Vibe
   The third truth is that destiny is finished by faithfulness, not by feeling. It's a
work you complete, not a state you achieve. Jesus described His own purpose in
exactly those terms.

  John 4:34 (NIV)
  'My food,' said Jesus, 'is to do the will of him who sent me and to finish his work.'



   To finish his work. Not to feel fulfilled by it, but to finish it, and He did; "it is
finished" were the last words. Paul at the end of his life didn't say "I found my
passion." He said he'd fought the fight, finished the race, kept the faith. Those are
completion words, said by a man in a cell who had spent most of his ministry in
places he didn't choose, doing work that looked like failure to anyone watching.
   That's the shape of a man's destiny: assigned, local, and finished by showing up.
Every day of it looks like a Tuesday, and at the end of it there's a "well done" from
the only One whose opinion of your life will last.

How you actually find it

  I've told you that you don't find it, and now I'm going to tell you how to find it,
and both are true.
   You don't find it by searching for a feeling. You find it by looking at what God
has already put in your hands and doing that, faithfully, until He puts something
else there. Here's where to look.
   Look at the blueprint from chapter 18. You were made to work a garden and
keep it, to carry a word, to speak, to not be alone, to love a bride if you have one,
and to rest. Whatever else your destiny is, it's inside that shape. A man who ignores
the blueprint while he searches for his calling is a man looking for his truck keys
while he's sitting in the truck.
  Look at your place. Where has God put you? What town, what job, what street,
what church? That's your garden, and you work it. If God moves you, He'll move
you; He isn't shy about it. Until He does, the purpose is here.
   Look at your people. Who has God given you? A wife, children, a crew, brothers,
a widow down the road, two young men at a breakfast table. Those names are your
destiny, more than any job title ever will be. Most of what God's purpose in your



Destiny Is Not a Vibe                                                                     193
generation looks like is what you do for the people whose names you already know.
Look at your hands and ask what you can do, because Peter tells you what to do
with that.

  1 Peter 4:10 (NIV)
  Each of you should use whatever gift you have received to serve others, as faithful stewards of God's
  grace in its various forms.



   Whatever gift you have received. Not the one you wish you had; the one you've
got. If you can fix things, fix them for people who can't. If you can teach, teach. If
you can listen, God knows there's a shortage. If you can make money, make it and
give it. Your gift is a clue to your assignment, and using it to serve is how the
assignment gets done.
   And look at your season. Destiny changes shape as you go. A twenty-six-year-old
single man's assignment and a seventy-one-year-old widower's assignment are not
the same, and both are real. Ask what God's purpose looks like in this stretch, not
the one you've left or the one you're waiting for.
    Do those five things, and one day, probably not a dramatic one, you'll look up
and realize you've been serving God's purpose in your generation for years without
a single lightning bolt, the way Hollis did, with nothing but your hands.

The story that names you

   This whole part of the book is called "the story that names you," and I want to
close the part by saying what that means.
   Everybody has a name that his story gave him. The guy whose dad left. The guy
who blew up his marriage. The guy who never got the promotion. The guy who's
just a welder. The old guy nobody asks anything. Those names are real, in the sense
that the events happened. But they aren't your name. They're the first man's names,
the ones that come out of the first garden: naked, afraid, hiding, blaming. The
master story gives you a different name: made in the image, placed, given a work,
son of the Father who does not leave, in Christ, servant of God's purpose in your
generation. And there's one more that I've saved, because it finally answers the
question underneath all the questions.




194                                                                                     Destiny Is Not a Vibe
  Revelation 2:17 (NIV)
  Whoever has ears, let them hear what the Spirit says to the churches. To the one who is victorious, I
  will give some of the hidden manna. I will also give that person a white stone with a new name
  written on it, known only to the one who receives it.



   A new name, known only to the one who receives it. God has a name for you
that nobody else knows yet: not your father, not your ex-wife, not the men who've
written you off, not even you. It's the name of the man He made you to be and is
making you into, and one day He'll hand it to you on a stone, and everything your
story called you will be gone.
  That's what it means to be named by the story. Not by what happened to you,
but by what God said about you before it happened, and by what He'll say when it's
done.

Destiny in every season

   If you're single and waiting for your calling to arrive along with a wife or a career,
hear the young man with four jobs. Your destiny isn't on the far side of a wedding
or a promotion. It's the next assigned thing. It might be the young men at church
who need a single man with time. It might be the job you've been thinking of
leaving because it doesn't feel like enough. Stay there and work it, and serve God's
purpose in this stretch of your generation, and let Him handle the next one.
   If there's a baby in your house, your destiny currently weighs about twelve
pounds and doesn't sleep, and I mean that with total seriousness. There's no larger
assignment God will ever give you than the one crying in the next room, and the
fact that it doesn't feel like a calling is exactly the point of this chapter.
   If you're divorced, you may think the story is over, or at least the good part of it,
and it isn't. Joseph's destiny ran straight through a prison. Peter's ran straight
through the worst failure of his life, and Jesus told him ahead of time what would
come after.

  Luke 22:32 (NIV)
  But I have prayed for you, Simon, that your faith may not fail. And when you have turned back,
  strengthen your brothers.




Destiny Is Not a Vibe                                                                                     195
   When you have turned back, strengthen your brothers. Peter's failure didn't end
his purpose. It became part of the equipment for it. Kenny told me that the men
who listen to him now at his church are the divorced ones, the ones who won't talk
to anybody else, and he said, "I never would have chosen it. But I'm the only one
they'll let in." That's a man serving God's purpose in his generation with the very
thing he thought had disqualified him.
  And if you're older, "his own generation" is still yours. Not the one you led in
your fifties; this one. The purpose isn't behind you.

  Psalm 92:14 (NIV)
  They will still bear fruit in old age, they will stay fresh and green,



    They will still bear fruit, the psalm says, and I've watched it. Roy's destiny at
seventy-two is a Thursday breakfast table and two men in their thirties. Mr. Pruitt's
is forty pecan trees and a Wednesday coffee at a gas station. Hollis's is a Sunday
school class and a deacon's word to a young man by a truck. None of those is a vibe.
All of them are God's purpose, being served, in a generation, by men who stopped
waiting for a feeling and did the next thing.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty-two is most tempted by the first lie, that he'll know it
when he feels it, and he changes jobs and churches and girlfriends waiting for the
feeling. Eli's assignment turned out to be a parts counter he stopped leaving and a
nine-year-old he started coaching, neither of which felt like a calling and both of
which were God's purpose for him in that stretch. The takeaway for him is that the
assignment is found in what's already in his hands.
    A man of fifty is most tempted by the second and third lies together, that destiny
is big and somewhere else, and he's tempted to blow up a good life looking for a
bigger one. Walt nearly sold the whole business to "do something that mattered"
before an older man asked him whether being home for dinner might be the thing
that mattered. The takeaway for him is that the purpose is here, and that it won't be
more here somewhere else.

Where it gets done



196                                                                        Destiny Is Not a Vibe
   I'll tell you what happened with the young man. He stayed at the fifth job. Not
because it felt like his calling, because it didn't. He stayed because an older man told
him to stay for three years no matter what and see what God did with a man who
stopped leaving. He's been there four years now. He runs a crew. Two of the men
on it have started coming to church because of how he treats them, and he's told
me, more than once, that he still doesn't have a "calling." He simply has a purpose,
and it turned out to be wherever he stopped running.
   Most of your destiny is going to be carried out inside a house. That's where the
next part of this book goes: the wife, the kids, the bed, the table, the ceremony. If
you're looking for where God's purpose in your generation gets served, it's mostly
going to be in rooms with your family's names on them, on days with nothing
special about them.
   Destiny is not a vibe. It's a man doing the next thing God put in his hands, in the
place God put him, for the people God gave him, until it's finished. Go do the next
thing.

Before you turn the page

    If you've been waiting for a feeling, I hope this chapter gave you permission to
stop. Not permission to stop caring, but permission to stop searching for a
lightning bolt and to look instead at your place and your people and your hands and
your season, because that's where the assignment has been the whole time. God has
a name for you that nobody knows yet, written on a stone, and He's going to hand
it to you when the work is finished. Until then, there's a next thing in front of you.
May the Father who prepared your good works in advance show you the next one
this week, and may you do it, without a vibe, and find that it counted.

   YOUR TURN



   NAME IT
   Because a lie about destiny can steer a man for decades unnoticed: say which one
   you've been living under, that you'll know it when you feel it, or that it's big, or that
   it's somewhere else, and then say in one sentence, with names in it, what God's
   purpose in your own generation looks like.
   DO THIS WEEK
   Because the assignment is found in what's already in your hands: write the five clues


Destiny Is Not a Vibe                                                                     197
on one card, your place, your people, your hands, your season, and the blueprint,
with one line under each, and then do one thing from the card before Tuesday.
TELL A BROTHER
Because another man can often see your purpose more clearly than you can from inside
it: tell him what you've been waiting to feel and for how long, then read him the card
and ask him what he sees God's purpose looking like in your life right now.
CARRY THIS
Destiny is not a vibe. It's serving God's purpose in your own generation, in the place He
put you, for the people He gave you, until it's finished.




198                                                                     Destiny Is Not a Vibe
            PART 4


         The house




Part 4               199
200   If There Is No Wife Yet
CHAPTER 21



If There Is No Wife Yet
   Part 4: The house
   Every wedding has a table for the single men. It's usually near the back, next to
the cousins nobody could quite place, and the men at it are various ages, and every
one of them has been told some version of the same sentence by some well-meaning
aunt on the way to his seat. Your time will come.
   Micah sat at that table for about six years. He told me that at one wedding, when
he was twenty-eight, he counted: five men at the table, three of whom had been at
the same table at the last four weddings. A married friend came over between the
toasts, put a hand on Micah's shoulder, and said, "Hang in there, man. God's got
somebody for you." And Micah said he sat there thinking, what if He doesn't, and
what if that isn't actually the point?
   That's the right question, and it's the one this chapter is about. Because I'm
about to spend nine chapters on the house, on wives and beds and daughters and
sons, and if you're a single man you could reasonably wonder whether you should
skip ahead to Part 5. I'd ask you not to, because this chapter is for you first, and
then I'm going to ask you to stay for the other eight, and I'll tell you why.
   But first I have to say the thing the church mostly doesn't.

You are not in a waiting room

   The way most churches treat a single man, whether they intend to or not, is as a
man-in-waiting. Half a man, whose real life starts at the altar. The singles group is a
holding pen. The sermons about manhood assume a wife. The men's retreat is
about being a better husband and father. And the single man sits there, twenty-six
or thirty-four or forty-one, and absorbs the message that he's in the lobby of his



If There Is No Wife Yet                                                              201
own life.
   Scripture doesn't talk like that. Jesus was single, and the fullest man who ever
lived, the second Man in the second garden, never married, and nobody in the New
Testament seems to think that made Him less of a man. Paul was single for the years
we know about. John the Baptist was single. Some of the most useful men in the
Bible had no wife and no children and served God's purpose in their generation
with everything they had.
   And go back to chapter 18. Nearly the whole blueprint was handed to Adam
while he was alone: the image, the blessing, the commission to make the world more
than it was, the place, the work, the guarding, the word, the naming. He was a full
man with a full assignment before there was a bride. The bride was God's remedy
for a specific not-good, and it was a good remedy, and I'm going to spend four
chapters on it. But she wasn't what made him a man. He was one before she woke
up.
   So here's the truth I want to put under your feet before anything else. If there is
no wife yet, you are not waiting to start. You're a man with a garden, and it needs
working today, and a wife, if she comes, will walk into a house you've already built
rather than a lot you've been meaning to clear.

Three men at the table

   I said "single man" as if it were one man. It's three, and they have very different
griefs, and I want to look each of them in the eye.
   The first is the man who has never married. You're somewhere between
twenty-two and forty-five. You may want a wife badly, or you may be ambivalent,
or you may have given up. What you share with the other two men at the table is
that the culture has told you your life is on hold. What you don't share is that
you've never had the thing everyone assumes you're waiting for, so you don't fully
know what you're waiting for, and that makes it easy to turn her into something no
real woman is.
   The second is the man who's single again because a marriage ended, which is
Kenny's seat at the table. You aren't waiting for something you've never had. You're
grieving something you lost, or something you broke, or both, and you're doing it
while managing a custody schedule and a one-bedroom apartment and a church
that isn't sure what to do with you. Everything I say to the first man is true of you


202                                                                   If There Is No Wife Yet
too, and you have a whole extra layer on top of it, and I'm going to speak to that
layer.
   The third is the man who's single again because his wife died, which is Mr.
Pruitt's seat. You built the house and lived in it for forty or fifty years, and then the
other chair emptied, and you're a single man in your seventies with a garden you
don't know how to work alone. Nobody's telling you your time will come. Mostly
they aren't telling you anything, because they don't know what to say. I do, so stay
in this chapter.
  And there's a fourth chair at that table that's easy to miss, because the man in it
doesn't think of himself as waiting for anything yet.
   At nineteen, Eli is single and expects to be for a while, and the danger for him
isn't the back table; it's that he'll treat the next ten years as a rehearsal. He decided
this year to treat his apartment like a house, which meant a table he actually eats at
and a budget he actually keeps, and he started coaching a nine-year-old whose father
left. The takeaway is that a nineteen-year-old who lives like a man is already doing
most of what a husband does, minus the wife.

What a single man builds

   The blueprint says work it and keep it. Here's what that looks like without a wife.
   He builds a house. Not a mortgage; a home. A single man's apartment should be
a place where a man lives and not a place where a boy crashes. It should be clean
enough for company, with a table people can eat at and food in the refrigerator that
you cooked, with the money handled and the bills paid on the day. I've watched too
many single men live as though their real house were somewhere in the future and
this one were a tent, and it isn't a tent; it's your garden. And if a wife comes, the
state of this house is the first thing she'll read about you, and she'll read it correctly.
   He sets a table. Hospitality is not a married gift. Some of the best hospitality I've
ever received was from single men who had decided their table was for other people.
Have the young guys over, feed the widower, and host the twenty minutes at your
place with coffee on. A single man who can set a table is doing what Adam was
doing before Eve: making a place more than it was.
  He builds a company, which was chapter 13. You need brothers more than a
married man does, because you don't have the built-in person at home who notices



If There Is No Wife Yet                                                                 203
when you go quiet. Get your twenty minutes, get a man ahead of you, and don't let
the drift or the itch get a year's head start because nobody was watching. He builds a
devotion. Paul says something about single men that usually gets read as a
consolation prize and isn't one.

  1 Corinthians 7:35 (NIV)
  I am saying this for your own good, not to restrict you, but that you may live in a right way in
  undivided devotion to the Lord.



   Undivided devotion is what he calls it, and that isn't a lesser thing. It's a thing a
married man with three kids honestly can't have in the same measure, and Paul
knows it, and he's telling you it's a gift. You have hours, and you have attention, and
you have a Tuesday evening with nobody needing you. You can pray longer and
read deeper and serve harder and give more than any married man at your church,
and if you spend those hours waiting instead, you've thrown away the one
advantage your season gives you.
    He builds a fatherhood. This is the one nobody tells single men, and it's the one
that would change a church if they heard it. There are fatherless boys in your
congregation, dozens of them, and their mothers are exhausted, and the married
men are stretched thin with their own children. You have time and a truck, and you
can coach, and you can show up, and you can be the man who teaches a
nine-year-old with an empty chair how to throw, or change a tire, or pray. Paul was
a single man who fathered Timothy so well that he called the younger man his true
son. A single man can do fatherhood. It simply has a different shape.
   And he builds a purity. Chapter 25 is called "The Bed Is a Covenant," and a
single man might assume it's for the married men, and it isn't, because the covenant
starts before the bed. Every choice you make now about what you look at and
where you put your hands is either building a covenant for a woman you haven't
met or spending it before she arrives. Paul's word to Timothy about women was
one word long at the end of the sentence.

  1 Timothy 5:1-2 (NIV)
  Do not rebuke an older man harshly, but exhort him as if he were your father. Treat younger men as
  brothers, older women as mothers, and younger women as sisters, with absolute purity.




204                                                                                   If There Is No Wife Yet
   As sisters, with absolute purity. That's the standard for how a single man treats
every woman who isn't his wife, including the one who might become his wife.
Absolute, and not mostly.

If you want a wife

   Most of you do, and it's a good thing to want, and Scripture says so.

  Proverbs 18:22 (NIV)
  He who finds a wife finds what is good and receives favor from the LORD.



   Finds is an active verb. A man who wants a wife doesn't wait to be found. He
looks, with intention and with honor, and here is what I'd tell my own sons about
how. Look at the right thing first. The culture will train you to look at her face and
her body first and her character eventually, and you'll need to reverse that order,
because Proverbs is blunt about it.

  Proverbs 31:30 (NIV)
  Charm is deceptive, and beauty is fleeting; but a woman who fears the LORD is to be praised.



   Fleeting is the word, because everything you're tempted to look at first is going to
change. What won't change is whether she fears God, whether she tells the truth,
whether she's kind when nobody's watching, how she treats her mother and the
waitress, and those are the things to watch. Attraction matters, and I'm not telling
you to marry a woman you aren't drawn to. I'm telling you that attraction is the
least reliable thing you'll feel about her, and it should be the last thing you weigh
rather than the first.
   Go first, and go clearly, which we said in chapter 6. Ask her out plainly, for a
specific evening, with your intention visible. Don't hang out for a year. Don't text
for three months and never ask. Women can tell the difference between a man
pursuing and a boy hovering, and they should never be made to guess which one
you are. If you aren't sure you're interested enough to ask, you aren't ready to ask,
and that's fine; just don't hover.
  Don't linger, either, because once you're dating, a man decides. Not in a week,
but not in four years either. If you've been with a woman long enough to know her


If There Is No Wife Yet                                                                          205
character and you're still not deciding, you aren't being careful. You're being
passive, and you're spending her years. Either move toward the covenant or release
her honorably so that somebody else can.
   Bring older men in. Let your man ahead of you meet her. Let your brothers meet
her. Ask them what they see. You're in no condition to see clearly, because nobody
in love is; borrow their eyes. And meet her people. How she is with her father and
her friends is how she'll be with you in year eight.
   Keep your hands off her. I'm going to say it because nobody else will. Sexual
involvement before the covenant does two things at once. It clouds your judgment
about whether this is the right woman, and it teaches both of you that the covenant
isn't necessary for the very thing it was made to protect. Every man I know who did
it wishes he hadn't, and every man I know who didn't is glad. That's a hundred
percent in both directions, and I've asked a lot of men.
   And marry when you can carry the weight, not when you feel ready, because
nobody ever feels ready. What you need isn't a feeling but a garden that's being
worked: a job you show up to, money that's handled, a house that's a home,
brothers, a purity that's been held, a Father you talk to. If you have that, and you've
found a woman who fears God, and the older men who know you both say go, then
go. Paul had a plain word for men who could carry the weight and were stalling
anyway.

  1 Corinthians 7:9 (NIV)
  But if they cannot control themselves, they should marry, for it is better to marry than to burn with
  passion.



   Better to marry, he says, and not "wait until you're thirty-two and own a house."
If you're a man who can carry it, carry it.

If you're single again

    Kenny, I'm turning to you now, and to every man in your seat. Everything above
is true for you. You still build a house, a table, a company, a devotion, a fatherhood
(your own this time, across two addresses), and a purity. Single again is single. The
standard doesn't have an asterisk for men who used to be married.
  But you have things the never-married man doesn't, and I want to name them.


206                                                                                  If There Is No Wife Yet
You have grief, whether you wanted the divorce or fought it with everything you
had, because something died, and it needs a funeral. Men skip this step and go
straight to rebuilding, and the grief comes out sideways as anger or the itch or the
drift, and then the rebuilding is on sand. Cry about it, tell a brother, and take a year
to be honest about what was lost.
   You have your part, which was chapter 15. You can't heal until you've said "I did
this" about the piece that's yours without attaching her piece to it. Not for her sake,
but for yours, and for God's, and for the man you're going to be next.
   You have children, probably, and they come before any new woman. I'll be blunt
about this. A divorced father who's dating hard while his kids are adjusting has his
priorities upside down. Your children need to see a father who's stable and present
and not distracted by a new relationship for a good long while. Kenny's older man
told him two years, minimum, before he even thought about it. Kenny said it felt
like a prison sentence at the time and turned out to be the best advice he ever took.
"I got to be their dad without splitting my attention," he said. "They needed that
more than I needed a girlfriend."
   You have a church that may not know what to do with you. Some churches
handle divorced men beautifully, and some don't, and the men drift off in shame.
Stay anyway, and reject the passivity of leaving. Find the men who'll have you, and
become the man who eventually welcomes the next divorced fellow who walks in
looking for the exit.
   And you have the question of remarriage, which I'm not going to settle in a
paragraph, because Scripture handles it with more care than a men's book can. Sit
down with your pastor. Read what Jesus and Paul said. Be honest about your
circumstances. And don't let anyone, including yourself, rush you, because the man
who remarries in year one to stop hurting is a man about to hurt somebody else.

If she died

   Mr. Pruitt, this is for you, and for every man in your chair. The blueprint still
says it isn't good for a man to be alone, and you know that now in a way the young
men at the table can't. You had the remedy for fifty years and it's gone, and nobody
has told you what a man does with that.
  Here is what I've watched. You grieve, out loud, for as long as it takes, and you
don't let anyone tell you it should be faster. You keep working the garden,


If There Is No Wife Yet                                                               207
whatever's left of it: the trees, the grandkids, the Wednesday coffee. You find a
brother, one man, because the couple friends fade and your children are busy and
God said not alone. You stay in the house of the Father, in the Word and in the
church, because the drift comes for widowers harder than for anyone, and it comes
disguised as rest.
   And whether you marry again is between you and God and your grown children
and the woman, if there is one. Some widowers do, and it's a gift. Some don't, and
their last years are full anyway. What you cannot do is decide that your life ended
when hers did. Mr. Pruitt planted forty trees the spring after. That's a man who
knows the garden is still his.

Why you should stay for the next eight chapters

  Now for the ask I promised. I'm about to write about knowing a wife, laying
your life down for her, what she actually needs, the bed, daughters, and sons. Here's
why a single man should read every word of it.
   Read chapters 22 through 25 as a man building the house before she moves in.
Everything you learn about knowing a woman and laying down your life and
covenant faithfulness is a tool you'll want in your hand on the wedding night, not
something you pick up in year three after the damage is done. The married men
reading those chapters are mostly repairing. You get to build it right the first time.
   Read them, too, as a man who can serve his married brothers. You're going to sit
in the twenty minutes with a man whose marriage is struggling, and if you've read
what a wife needs, you'll be able to ask him better questions than "how's it going."
   And read chapters 26 through 29 as a man who can father. The daughter and son
chapters are about what a child needs from a man, and there are children in your
church who don't have one. A single man who knows how to give a boy a name and
a hill and a day he won't forget is worth more to a fatherless kid than you can
imagine. You may end up giving a son a day he'll never forget before you have one
of your own. Some of the best fathers I know did exactly that.

Not yet is not never, and it is not nothing

  Micah got married at thirty-one, as you know. But the thing I want you to hear is
what he told me about the six years at the back table. "I wasted the first three of



208                                                                  If There Is No Wife Yet
them," he said, "just waiting. Then Dale told me to stop treating my apartment like
a tent and my Tuesdays like a lobby, and I started building. I got the twenty
minutes going, and started coaching a kid whose dad was gone, and fixed my
money, and learned to cook, and had guys over. And the strange thing is, the day I
stopped waiting for a wife was about the day I became a man a wife would want."
   Not yet is honest. Most of you will marry. But not yet isn't never, and it certainly
isn't nothing. It's a garden, with your name on it, and a work to do, and a Father
who's watching you work it, and a reward that doesn't depend on whether anybody
ever moves in.
   Get up from the table. There's a house to build.

Before you turn the page

   If you're a man without a wife, I'd like you to close this chapter without the hum
of being half a man, because you aren't one. The fullest man who ever lived never
married, and nearly everything Adam was given, he was given alone. Your garden is
real and it needs working today, and a wife, if she comes, will walk into a house
you've built. If you're single again by divorce or death, the grief is real and God
knows its size, and the garden is still yours. May the Father who said it isn't good for
a man to be alone give you brothers this year, and a man ahead of you, and a church
that knows your name, and may He handle the question of a bride in His time
while you get up from the table and build.

   YOUR TURN



   NAME IT
   Because the waiting room is invisible to the man sitting in it: say which of the three
   men you are, never married or single again by divorce or single again by death, and
   then say plainly whether you've been living as though your life hasn't started yet, and
   since when.
   DO THIS WEEK
   Because a garden gets worked one job at a time: if you've never married, pick one
   thing from the list of what a single man builds and do it before Tuesday, the house
   or the table or the twenty minutes or a fatherless kid or the plain ask; if you're single
   again, pick one thing from the same list and add one honest sentence to God about
   your grief and one about your part.



If There Is No Wife Yet                                                                    209
TELL A BROTHER
Because a man who's single needs another man's eyes on his life more than anyone: tell
him which man you are and which thing you picked, and if you're single again and
thinking about dating, tell him that too and ask him to be honest about whether you're
ready.
CARRY THIS
If there is no wife yet, you are not waiting to start. You're a man with a garden, and it
needs working today.




210                                                                      If There Is No Wife Yet
CHAPTER 22



Know Her
  Part 4: The house
   Roy can tell you the torque spec on the lug nuts of every vehicle he has ever
owned. He can tell you the make and model of the car in every serious wreck he
worked in thirty-one years on the highway. He knows the exact date he bought his
first deer rifle and what he paid for it.
   At sixty-seven, a few months into retirement, he sat across from his wife at
breakfast on a Tuesday morning and realized he didn't know her favorite hymn.
   They had been married forty-four years, and he knew her schedule, and he knew
she took her coffee with too much cream and that she'd cry at the end of a certain
kind of movie and that she had never liked his mother, though she'd never once said
so. But sitting there with nothing to do for the first time in his adult life, he began
asking himself what he actually knew about the woman across the table, and the
honest answer frightened him. He knew where she'd be. He didn't know who she
was.
  "I'd been standing next to her for forty-four years," he told me. "I thought that
was the same as knowing her."
  It isn't, and this chapter is about the difference, because the first thing a man
owes his wife, before he lays down his life for her and before he works out what she
needs, is to actually know who he's married to.

Live with her according to knowledge

   Peter wrote one sentence to husbands, and it has carried more freight than
almost anything else in the New Testament about marriage.




Know Her                                                                              211
  1 Peter 3:7 (NIV)
  Husbands, in the same way be considerate as you live with your wives, and treat them with respect as
  the weaker partner and as heirs with you of the gracious gift of life, so that nothing will hinder your
  prayers.



   Be considerate as you live with your wives. The older translations put it closer to
the original: live with them according to knowledge. Dwell with her knowing her;
study her and understand her, and make her a subject you're an expert in.
  And look at the last clause, which men skip. So that nothing will hinder your
prayers. Peter is saying that a man who doesn't know his wife, who lives next to her
without understanding her, has a problem with God and not only with her. His
prayers hit a ceiling. That is how seriously heaven takes this. The way you know
your wife is connected to the way you're heard by your Father.
   Here's what I've noticed over the years. Men study what they care about. A man
who loves fishing knows the water temperature that turns the bass on. A man who
loves his truck knows the sound it makes when the alternator is going. A man who
loves his job knows things about it that nobody else knows. We're built to learn the
things we love, deeply, over years.
   And most men stopped studying their wives somewhere around year two. They
learned enough to get along, and then they stopped, and they've been running on
that early data ever since, and she has changed completely since then, and they don't
know it, and she knows they don't know it.
   Proximity isn't knowledge, which we said about brothers in chapter 13, and it's
truer of wives. You can share a bed with a woman for forty years and not know the
woman in it.

What there is to know

  Let me be specific, because "know your wife" is the kind of line men nod at and
do nothing with.
  There's her story, to begin with, because she had a chair at the end of her table
too. How was she fathered? What did she learn about men from watching hers?
What did her mother teach her about being a wife, on purpose and by accident?
What happened to her before you that she has never fully told you? Most men



212                                                                                                Know Her
know their wife's story the way they know the plot of a movie they half watched.
Ask her to tell it again, slowly, and listen as though it were the first time. You'll learn
why she flinches at the thing you thought was nothing.
   There are her fears, and not the ones you assume. Ask her what she's most afraid
of right now, and then be quiet for a long time. Most wives, given room, will tell
their husbands something that surprises them. It's rarely money and rarely what
he'd guess. It's often about being alone, or about the children, or about becoming
her mother, or about whether he actually sees her.
   There's what fills her and what drains her. Some women come alive in a room
full of people and some are wrung out by it. Some need an hour alone to be a
person again, and some need an hour of conversation. If you don't know which
your wife is, you'll keep planning weekends that exhaust her and wondering why
she's short with you on Sunday night.
    There's how she receives love. Men tend to give love the way they'd want to get
it, which is usually by doing something: fixing, providing, handling. Some wives
receive that as love, and some don't feel loved by it at all. Some need words, and
some need time with no agenda. Some need to be touched in a way that has nothing
to do with the bedroom. Some need you to notice and do the thing without being
asked. Find out which she is. Ask her when she has felt most loved by you and what
you were doing, and then do more of that and less of what you assumed.
    There's her body, and I'm going to say this straight because too many Christian
men are squeamish about it and it costs their wives. A husband should know his
wife's body and its seasons without embarrassment. Her cycle, and what it does to
her energy and her mood and her patience, so that he can carry more in the week she
has less. Pregnancy, and what it did and is doing. Postpartum, which is a season all
its own and which I'll come back to. The changes in her forties and fifties, which are
real and hard and which most husbands sail past without a word because nobody
ever told them a man should know about it. Illness and age, too. She lives in that
body every hour. If you don't know what's happening in it, you don't know her.
  There's her walk with God. How does she pray? What is she wrestling with Him
about right now? What verse is she standing on? What's she struggling to believe?
Most Christian couples I know have never had this conversation past the surface,
and a man who knows how his wife is doing with God can pray for her with
precision instead of generalities.



Know Her                                                                                213
   There's her Tuesday, meaning what her ordinary day actually looks like, hour by
hour? If she's home with children, do you know what two in the afternoon feels
like? If she works, do you know what her boss is like and which part of the day is
hardest? Men who don't know their wives' Tuesdays make comments about them
that land like insults, and can't understand why.
  There are her people: her friends' names and what's going on in their lives, her
mother and what that relationship costs her, her sister. Knowing her people is
knowing the weather system she lives in.
   And there are her dreams. What did she give up to be where she is? What does
she still want that she's stopped saying out loud because nobody asked? A woman
who's been married fifteen years and is raising children has usually set down some
things she carried at twenty-two, and a husband who knows what they were, and
honors them, and looks for ways to hand some of them back, is a husband she'll
trust with anything.

  Proverbs 20:5 (NIV)
  The purposes of a person's heart are deep waters, but one who has insight draws them out.



   Deep waters is the picture, because she isn't going to hand you all of this on the
surface. You'll have to draw it out, and drawing out takes insight, and insight takes
time, and time takes deciding that she's worth studying.

How a man learns his wife

   Ask, and then wait. The waiting is the whole skill. Most men ask a question and
start forming their answer to it before she's finished. Ask, and then count to ten in
your head after she stops talking, because she usually hasn't stopped. The real thing
comes after the pause.

  James 1:19 (NIV)
  My dear brothers and sisters, take note of this: Everyone should be quick to listen, slow to speak and
  slow to become angry,



  Quick to listen and slow to speak. James wasn't writing a marriage book, but he
might as well have been. Listen without fixing, because when she tells you



214                                                                                                Know Her
something hard your whole body will want to solve it, and you mustn't, not at first.
Say, "That sounds heavy, tell me more." A woman who gets fixed every time she
speaks stops speaking. A woman who gets heard tells you everything.
   Watch her, too. What does she do when she's tired? What makes her laugh when
she isn't trying to? What does she reach for when she's sad? Where does she go in the
house when she needs to be alone? Men are good at watching things they care
about. Watch her the way you'd watch a piece of water you were trying to learn.
   Write it down, and I mean that literally. You keep maintenance records on the
truck; keep them on your marriage: her sizes, her friends' names and their children's
names, the thing she said she wanted in passing eight months ago. The date her
father died. What she said her biggest fear was when you finally asked. A man who
