writes down what his wife tells him is a man who has decided it matters enough to
keep. She'll notice when you remember. She'll notice more when you remember
what she said only once.
   Ask her what you get wrong. This is the bravest one. Once a season, ask, "What
do I misunderstand about you? Where do I get you wrong?" And then don't argue;
take notes instead, because you'll learn more in that conversation than in a year of
assuming.
   And take the twenty minutes home. The three questions from chapter 13, what
happened this week and what's heavy and what's next, work across a kitchen table.
Ask her every week, same night, and answer them yourself. Roy started doing this
with his wife after the hymn morning, and he said it was like meeting her for the
first time at sixty-seven.

Knowing her under a newborn

  Tomás, this is for you and for every man with a baby in the house.
   The woman you married is not the woman across from you right now, and
neither of you signed up for that, and both of you are a little frightened by it. Her
body has been through something you can't fully understand. Her hormones are
rearranging her from the inside. She hasn't slept a full night in months. Her identity
has shifted under her feet; she was a woman, and now she's a mother, and she isn't
sure where the woman went. She may be grieving her old self and feeling guilty for
the grieving. She may be scared she's doing it all wrong. She may be more tired than
she has ever been in her life and unable to explain it in a way that sounds like a


Know Her                                                                           215
reason.
   Knowing her in this season means knowing that she's carrying a load you can't
see, and treating her like a woman under a load. It means asking "what do you need
in the next hour?" instead of "what do you need?", because the second question is
too big to answer at three in the morning and the first one isn't. It means noticing
when she hasn't eaten, hasn't showered, hasn't been out of the house, hasn't
laughed. It means understanding that her not wanting to be touched right now is
about exhaustion and a body that's been someone else's property for a year, and not
about you, and not permanent.
   And it means watching for the thing nobody warns young husbands about.
Some new mothers go into a darkness that isn't only tiredness. It has a name, and it's
common, and it's treatable, and the husband is usually the first person in a position
to notice. If your wife isn't herself in a way that's lasted weeks, if she isn't sleeping
even when the baby does, if she says things about herself that frighten you, you take
her to a doctor, and you go with her, and you don't let anyone, including her, tell
you it's nothing. That isn't overreacting; that's a man who knows his wife and keeps
her.
    Tomás told me the sentence that changed his first year was one his wife said at
four in the morning: "I don't need you to fix it. I need you to know how hard this
is." He'd been trying to fix it for four months. He stopped and started knowing, and
something in the house came unclenched.

Knowing her after forty years

   Roy, back to you, and to every man past sixty. The danger for an older husband
isn't that he doesn't know his wife. It's that he knows a version of her from 1985
and stopped updating, and she has changed. Her body has changed, and she's living
in a body that's aging in ways she may not have made peace with, and she needs a
husband who knows that and still looks at her the way Adam looked at Eve. Her
fears have changed: not the children's safety anymore, but the grandchildren's, and
her own health, and yours, and being left alone. Her griefs have accumulated: her
parents, friends, maybe a child. Her dreams for the remaining years are specific, and
she has probably never said them out loud because for forty years the calendar
belonged to your work.
  Know the woman she is at sixty-eight. Ask her the questions you asked at


216                                                                             Know Her
twenty-four, and expect different answers. Ask what she wants to do with the years
that are left, and listen as though it were the most important intelligence you've ever
gathered, because it is. Roy found out his wife had wanted to see the ocean since she
was a girl and had never mentioned it because there was never a good time. They
went that fall. "Forty-four years," he said, "and I didn't know that. What else don't I
know?" That's the right question, and he's been asking it every Tuesday since.
  And one more man belongs here, the one in the middle years, who is neither
underwater with a newborn nor retired with time to study.
   A man of forty-eight has usually been running on data from 1998, and the wife
across from him has changed her fears and her body and her dreams without his
noticing. Anthony discovered, when he finally asked, that his wife's biggest fear
wasn't money at all; it was that he'd stopped seeing her the year their second child
was born. The takeaway for him is that the question he's afraid to ask is the one that
would change the house.

For the man who's single again

   Kenny, and every man in your seat, you may be reading this with a stone in your
chest, because you know now what you didn't know then. Maybe you never really
studied her. Maybe you did and it wasn't enough. Either way, hear two things.
    First, if you're honest that you didn't know her, that's a piece of "I did this," and
it belongs in your confession and your grief, not in a fight with yourself for the rest
of your life.
   Second, you still have to know her, differently. If you're raising children across
two houses, you have to know your children's mother well enough to co-parent
wisely, not intimately but civilly. What she's afraid of for the kids. What sets her off
and what calms it down. How to say the hard thing about the schedule so that it
gets heard. That's a kind of knowing too, and it's for your children's sake, and it's a
man's work.
  And if God gives you another wife someday, you'll start knowing her on day one,
with the notebook open, because you know now what it costs not to.

For the single man

  You aren't studying a wife yet. Study women anyway, the right way, as sisters.


Know Her                                                                               217
Learn to ask a question and wait for the whole answer. Practice on your mother,
who has been waiting your whole life for you to ask her something real. Practice on
your sister. Learn what it's like to listen to a woman without fixing or flirting. A
man who has learned to hear women as sisters will know how to hear a wife, and the
man who hasn't will spend his first five years of marriage learning it the expensive
way.

How He knows His bride

  Christ knows His church, not in general but specifically: every fear, every
wound, every hidden thing, the number of hairs on every head. He knows us
completely and isn't put off by what He knows, and He keeps knowing us,
patiently, over a lifetime, drawing out the deep waters.

  John 10:14 (NIV)
  I am the good shepherd; I know my sheep and my sheep know me



  I know my sheep. That's the model for a husband. Not a man who married a
woman once and has been guessing ever since, but a man who knows her, and keeps
knowing her, and lets her know that she's known.
   Roy asked his wife her favorite hymn that Tuesday morning, and she told him.
Then she asked why he wanted to know, and he told her the truth: that he'd realized
he'd been standing next to her for forty-four years and had never really studied her,
and that he was sorry, and that he'd like to start. She didn't say anything for a while.
Then she got up and got the notebook she'd kept for thirty years with all her recipes
in it, and turned to the back, and started telling him things.
  He's still writing them down.

Before you turn the page

   You may have closed this chapter feeling behind, because most men are, and I'd
like to say that being behind isn't the same as being too late. Roy started at
sixty-seven and his wife handed him a notebook. She's been waiting for you to ask.
She isn't keeping a ledger of the years you didn't; she's waiting to see whether you
will. May the Father who knows His sheep by name give you the patience to draw



218                                                                            Know Her
out the deep waters, one question at a time, and may you find that the woman
you've been standing next to is more than you knew.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because most husbands are running on data from the first two years: say honestly
  how much of what you know about your wife comes from back then, and name the
  one area from the list, her story or fears or body or walk with God or Tuesday or
  dreams, where you know the least.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because knowing is built from one real question at a time: ask her one question from
  that area and listen for the whole answer without fixing or defending, then write
  down what she said, and start the notebook if you don't have one. If you're single,
  ask your mother or sister one real question and practice the same listening.
  TELL A BROTHER
  Because two men who assume they know their wives will keep assuming until one of
  them asks: tell him what you found out that you didn't know, ask him how long it's
  been since he studied his wife, and don't let either of you say "I know her pretty
  well." If you're single, tell him what you learned asking your mother or sister, and ask
  him whether he's ever practiced that kind of listening.
  CARRY THIS
  Proximity isn't knowledge. You can stand next to a woman for forty-four years and
  not know her favorite hymn. Study her. Write it down.




Know Her                                                                               219
220   Lay It Down
CHAPTER 23



Lay It Down
   Part 4: The house
   Anthony was right, and I want to start there because it matters. It was a Tuesday
night, a fight about money in the kitchen after the kids were down, and he'd done
the math and he'd done it correctly. His wife had made a mistake with the account,
and it had cost them a late fee and a little embarrassment, and he was right about all
of it. He had the receipts and he had the numbers and he had the moral high
ground and he was standing on it, and he could feel the sentence loading that would
end the argument and prove the point.
    And he said he had a strange thought, standing there. He thought about a Man
on His knees in a garden with a cup He didn't want, and he thought: I'm right, and
it doesn't matter. He put the sentence down. He said, "I'm sorry I got loud. We'll
figure it out. It isn't a big deal." His wife looked at him as though she didn't
recognize him, and then she started crying, and it took him a while to understand
that she was crying because in nine years of marriage he had never once laid down a
fight he was winning.
  That's what this chapter is about. You've studied her, and now you lay your life
down for her, and not once, at the altar in a nice suit, but every day, in a kitchen,
when you're right.

The harder job

   Here's the sentence men love to quote from Ephesians 5, and the one they quote
less.
   The one they love is "wives, submit." Men underline that one. And I'm not going
to argue with Paul; he wrote it and it's Scripture. But the very next thing Paul writes



Lay It Down                                                                         221
is addressed to us, and it's longer, and it's heavier, and I've never once heard a man
underline it in the same tone of voice.

  Ephesians 5:25 (NIV)
  Husbands, love your wives, just as Christ loved the church and gave himself up for her



    Gave himself up for her, just as Christ did, and that is the standard, which is
something more than "love her a lot." Love her the way Jesus loved the people He
died for, which is to say all the way to death, on purpose, while they were still failing
Him. Men want the authority of Ephesians 5 without the cross of Ephesians 5.
They want the headship without the giving up. But in Scripture, headship is a cross.
It's the man in front, and the man in front is the one who takes the arrows. Paul
goes on to say the husband gives himself up to make her holy, to wash her, to
present her radiant. His whole sacrifice aims at her flourishing. That isn't a man
who gets his way. That's a man who has decided that his way is her good. And look
at what Christ actually laid down, since He's the pattern.

  Philippians 2:5-7 (NIV)
  In your relationships with one another, have the same mindset as Christ Jesus: Who, being in very
  nature God, did not consider equality with God something to be used to his own advantage; rather, he
  made himself nothing by taking the very nature of a servant, being made in human likeness.



   He did not consider equality with God something to be used to his own
advantage. He had every right. He had the high ground in a way no husband ever
has. And He didn't use it. He set it down and put on a towel. That's the mindset,
and that's what "just as Christ" means when you're standing in a kitchen with the
receipts.

What a husband lays down

  Let me be specific, because "lay down your life" sounds like something you'd do
once, dramatically, when in fact it's done mostly in pieces, on Tuesdays.
   He lays down his right to be right, which was Anthony's kitchen. You'll be right
a great deal in your marriage; you aren't stupid. And most of the time, being right is
worth exactly nothing compared with what winning costs her. Lay it down, not by
pretending you're wrong but by deciding that the point isn't the point. "You may


222                                                                                         Lay It Down
be right about that. Let's figure it out together." The second half of that sentence is
the whole marriage.
   He lays down his preferences. Where to eat, what to watch, how the dishwasher
gets loaded, what temperature the house is, how the weekend goes. A thousand
small things a day where you'd rather it went your way. A husband who lays down
his preferences daily, cheerfully, without a scoreboard, is building something she
can feel even if she can't name it. A husband who fights for every preference is
telling her, in a thousand small ways, that his comfort outranks her.
   He lays down his phone. It's the biggest competitor for your attention in your
house, and she knows it. On the counter, face down, when you walk in. In the
kitchen, charging, at night. Not in your hand while she's talking. I've watched men
who would never dream of reading a newspaper while their wife spoke to them look
at a screen through an entire conversation and honestly not know they'd done it,
but she knows.
   He lays down his Saturday, or whatever his day is. The hunting lease, the golf, the
shop, the boat, the game. None of those is a sin. But when the house needs you and
the lease is calling, the lease loses, and it loses without you sulking about it. A man
who lays down his Saturday for his wife without making her pay for it in mood has
given her something better than the Saturday.
    He lays down his first fifteen minutes. After work you want to decompress, and
she's been waiting for an adult all day, or she's coming in from her own job, or she's
been carrying a baby for nine hours. Lay down the decompression. The first fifteen
minutes when you walk in belong to her and not to the recliner. Tomás does this
with his boots off and the baby on his shoulder. It costs him fifteen minutes, and
it's worth the whole evening.
   He lays down his side of the story. In front of the kids, in front of your parents,
in front of your friends, take her side, even when the story is complicated, and
especially then. A wife whose husband defends her in public can survive almost any
fight in private. A wife whose husband makes her the punch line at a barbecue, even
gently, learns that she's alone in the room.
    He lays down his mother's vote, which was chapter 12. When your mother and
your wife disagree, your wife wins, and your mother hears you say so. Lay down the
boy who still wants his mother's approval, because you left, and it's time to act like
it.



Lay It Down                                                                          223
    He lays down his sleep. This is the newborn chapter and I'll come back to it, but
it applies past the newborn. When she's sick, when she's grieving, when the kid is
throwing up at two in the morning, you get up. A husband who guards his sleep
like a right while his wife runs on four hours isn't giving himself up for her. He's
giving her up for himself.
  He lays down his anger. You have a right to be angry sometimes. Lay it down
anyway, not by stuffing it but by taking it to the truck and to God and to your
brother, and bringing back to her the thing that was under it, as chapter 9 described.
A man who lays down his anger before it reaches his wife has given her a safe house.
   He lays down his money. If you've held the money as if it were yours because you
earned most of it, lay it down. It's the house's money. She has full standing, full
knowledge, full say. The husband who keeps his wife on an allowance and in the
dark isn't leading; he's ruling, and Colossians told him not to be harsh.
   And he lays down his plans. The retirement you've imagined, the move you've
wanted, the way you pictured the next ten years. If those plans were made in your
own head without her in the room, lay them down and make new ones with her in
the room. A man who unveils a plan for both their lives that he built alone has told
her she's a passenger.

What laying down isn't

   I have to be careful here, because there's a counterfeit, and it's the first mark
inverted, and I've watched it ruin marriages from the other direction.
   Laying down your life is not abdicating. A husband who stops leading, stops
deciding, stops going first, and calls it "letting her have her way" isn't giving himself
up for her. He's disappearing on her, which is passivity with a kinder name, and
she'll feel it as abandonment even while she gets everything she asks for. Christ gave
Himself up and never once stopped leading. He led from the towel and from the
cross. Laying down is a strong man choosing her good over his comfort. It is not a
weak man choosing quiet over responsibility.
   Laying down is not becoming a doormat. Jesus was no doormat. He said hard
things to the people He loved. A husband who never tells his wife the truth because
he's "laying down his life" is actually laying down his job, because love speaks. It
speaks gently and at the right time and after it has listened, but it speaks.



224                                                                            Lay It Down
   And laying down is not self-erasure. She married a man. She needs a man to lean
on, one who has convictions and a spine and a direction. If you dissolve yourself
into her wishes until there's nobody home, you've robbed her of a husband. Lay
down your preferences, your rights, your comfort, your pride. Don't lay down
yourself, because she needs you.
    Here's the test, then. After you've laid something down, is she stronger or
weaker? Christ's sacrifice made the church radiant. If your sacrifice is making your
wife stronger, more secure, more herself, more radiant, it's the real thing. If it's
making her carry more because you've quietly checked out and called it humility,
it's the counterfeit.

Under a newborn

    Tomás, and every man in the first year. You're about to lay down more than
you've ever laid down in your life, and you're going to do it exhausted, and nobody
is going to thank you, and it's the most Christlike stretch of your marriage so far.
Here's what it looks like.
   You lay down sleep. You take a shift, not "I'll help when she asks," but a shift, on
the schedule, every night: the two-to-five, or the first stretch so that she can go
down at eight and get five hours in a row for the first time in months. Tomás did
the late shift for six months and worked ten-hour days on top of it. "I was a
zombie," he said. "She was a person again. Fair trade."
  You lay down the bedroom. Chapter 25 will say more, but for now, her body has
been through a war and it's still occupied territory, and your claims on it are laid
down until she's ready. That isn't a season of deprivation. It's a season of giving
yourself up for her.
  You lay down the gym, the lease, the game, the guys, for this year. Not forever,
but this year, the things that were yours get smaller so that the house can survive,
and they'll come back. If you fight for them now, something else won't.
  You lay down your expectation that she'll be who she was. She isn't, right now.
Lay down the wife you married and love the one who's here, and she'll come back,
changed, and better, and she'll remember that you loved her when she was
underwater.
   And you lay down your need for credit, because nobody's clapping, and chapter



Lay It Down                                                                            225
7 told you why that's all right. Your Father sees the three o'clock. Let that be
enough for this year.

After forty years

  Roy, and every man past sixty, you have laying down to do that the young men
can't imagine yet.
   Lay down the retirement you planned alone, the one where the days were yours.
She's been sharing a calendar with your work for forty years and now the calendar is
finally open, and it's hers as much as yours. Ask her what she wants the years to be.
Then make them that.
  Lay down the remote, and the news, and the recliner's claim on the evenings.
Roy said the hardest thing he laid down after the badge was the television, because it
was the only thing that felt like rest. "Turned out rest was sitting on the porch with
her, and I'd never tried it."
   Lay down the control. If you've run the money, the decisions, the direction for
forty years, it's time to hand her the reins on some of it. Not because you can't hold
them, but because she can, and she's waited.
   And I have to say this one, because some of you are living it. When she gets sick,
laying down your life stops being a metaphor. A friend I'll call Mr. Alvarez has
bathed his wife every morning for three years since the dementia took most of her.
She doesn't always know him, but he knows her. He said, "I said 'in sickness' in
1968. I meant it. I just didn't know what it would look like." That's Ephesians 5:25
at seventy-eight. That's a man giving himself up for a bride who can't thank him,
and it's the most beautiful thing I've ever watched a man do, and there is a Father
who sees it every single morning.

For the man who's single again

   Kenny, you may be reading this chapter and thinking about what you wouldn't
lay down when you had the chance. Hear it, grieve it, confess your part, and don't
stay there, because there's laying down for you to do right now.
   Lay down your right to win with your children's mother. Every exchange, every
schedule negotiation, every text about the school thing. You're going to be right
sometimes, and it doesn't matter. Your kids need two parents who can be in a room


226                                                                           Lay It Down
together, and that requires at least one of them to lay down the war, and it has to be
you, because you're the man and you're the one reading this. Kenny said the
sentence on the soccer sideline, the one from chapter 6, was a laying down. "I had a
whole list of things I could've said. I laid the list down. Best thing I ever did for my
kids."
  And lay down the narrative, the story where it was all her. Even if most of it was,
you don't have to tell it anymore. Let God hold it.

For the single man

   You aren't laying down your life for a wife yet. Practice anyway, because a man
who has never lost a preference for anyone will not suddenly become good at it on
his wedding night.
   Lay down where you eat when the guys want somewhere else. Lay down your
Saturday for the widower who needs his gutters cleaned. Lay down your first fifteen
minutes for a roommate who's had a bad day. Lay down your side of the story when
your mother is wrong about something small. Small deaths, daily, on purpose.
That's the training ground. Micah told me that Dale made him practice losing
arguments with his roommate for a year. "I thought it was ridiculous. Then I got
married and realized I'd been in spring training."

The reward for the man who loses

  Jesus said something about laying down your life that sounds like a loss and is a
promise.

  Matthew 16:25 (NIV)
  For whoever wants to save their life will lose it, but whoever loses their life for me will find it.



   Whoever loses their life will find it. Every husband who has actually done this
knows what Jesus meant. You lay down your right to be right and you find a wife
who trusts you with the truth. You lay down your Saturday and you find a marriage
that's still alive at fifty. You lay down your sleep and you find a woman who
remembers, thirty years later, that you got up. You lay down your plans and you
find plans you'd never have made that are better than yours.



Lay It Down                                                                                              227
  And the Father sees all of it. The laid-down fight in the kitchen that nobody
witnessed, the two o'clock shift, the remote set down, the bath at seventy-eight:
none of it is wasted. It's stored where the weather can't reach it, and one day the
Man who laid down everything is going to look at a husband who laid down a little
and say: well done. You did it the way I did.
   Anthony was right that night. He laid it down anyway. His wife told me, months
later, that it was the night she started trusting him with her fears, because a man
who'd give up a fight he was winning was a man who wouldn't use what she told
him against her.
  You'll be right this week, more than once. Lay it down.

Before you turn the page

   I've asked you to give up a good deal in this chapter, and I want to remind you
what's on the other side of it. Whoever loses his life will find it, Jesus said, and every
husband who's actually laid something down knows what He meant: you lay down
being right and find a wife who trusts you, you lay down sleep and find a woman
who remembers thirty years later that you got up. None of it is wasted. May the
Father who watched His Son lay down everything give you the strength to lay down
one thing this week, and may you find, as Anthony did, that the house changes
when you do.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because the thing you grip hardest is the thing she feels most: say which one it is,
  being right or your preferences or your phone or your Saturday or your first fifteen
  minutes or your side of the story or your mother's vote or your sleep or your anger
  or your money or your plans, in a full sentence.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because laying down is a daily habit and not a one-time gesture: lay that one thing
  down every day this week, on purpose, without announcing it or keeping score; and
  if you're single, pick the practice version and lose a small preference daily for
  someone in your life.
  TELL A BROTHER
  Because another man can see what you're gripping when you can't: tell him what


228                                                                            Lay It Down
you're laying down and how it went, and ask him what he sees you holding onto
that you don't see yourself.
CARRY THIS
Headship is a cross. Men want the authority of Ephesians 5 without the giving up of
Ephesians 5. You can be right and lay it down, and that's the whole marriage.




Lay It Down                                                                           229
230   What She Actually Needs
CHAPTER 24



What She Actually Needs
   Part 4: The house
  I've asked a lot of wives the same question over the years, in a lot of rooms,
usually on an index card so that nobody had to say it out loud. What do you most
need from your husband that you aren't getting?
  I have never once gotten a card that said money. Not a single one, not from the
wife of a man who'd been laid off, not from the wife of a man working two jobs,
not from the wife of the man who made more than anybody else in the room.
Here's what the cards say instead, and I've read enough of them now that I could
write them myself.
   To know he's not going anywhere. For him to be here when he's here. To not be
afraid when he's angry. For him to pray with me, just once a week, out loud. For
him to want to know me. For him to lead so I don't have to carry all of it. For him
to ask me out again. To hear him say it.
   I've shown some of those cards to husbands, without names, and watched their
faces change. Most of them are working themselves to the bone to provide
something their wives never asked for, and starving them of the thing they did.

More is not the thing

    Walt's wife said it in a kitchen, and I've quoted it already, and I'm going to quote
it again because it's the whole chapter. "I don't need more. I need you."
    Here's why men get this wrong, and it isn't because they don't love their wives.
It's because provision is measurable and presence isn't. A man can see the paycheck.
He can see the house, the truck, the college fund, the fixed faucet. Those are things
he did, and they count, and Scripture says they count; a man who won't provide has



What She Actually Needs                                                              231
denied the faith. But they aren't what she needs most, and if he pours himself into
them because they're the part he can see, he'll build her a beautiful house and leave
her alone in it.
  So here's what she actually needs. Not a list from a seminar, but the cards, sorted,
with Scripture underneath them.

Steadiness

  Before anything else, she needs to know that you aren't going anywhere. Not
only physically, though that too. She needs to know that the covenant is a wall she
can lean her whole weight against and it won't move.

  Malachi 2:15 (NIV)
  Has not the one God made you? You belong to him in body and spirit. And what does the one God
  seek? Godly offspring. So be on your guard, and do not be unfaithful to the wife of your youth.



   Do not be unfaithful to the wife of your youth. That isn't only about adultery,
though it includes adultery. It's about a man whose commitment has no exit clause
and who lives as though it doesn't. A wife can tell when her husband keeps a door
cracked, when "if this doesn't work out" lives somewhere in the back of his mind,
when he'd leave if it got bad enough. She'll never fully rest in a house with a cracked
door, so close it. Say it out loud, more than once: "I'm not going anywhere, ever,
and whatever this is, we're doing it together." Then live like a man who meant it.

  Psalm 15:4 (NIV)
  who despises a vile person but honors those who fear the LORD; who keeps an oath even when it hurts,
  and does not change their mind;



  Keeps an oath even when it hurts, the psalm says, and that's a description of a
husband. The oath is going to hurt sometimes. Keep it anyway, and let her watch
you keep it.

Presence

    She needs you to be here when you're here. This is the card I get most often, and
it's the one that has grown fastest in the years I've been asking, because the screens


232                                                                             What She Actually Needs
got better.
   A man can be in the room and gone; that's chapter 9's drift and chapter 5's Walt.
And a wife whose husband is home every night and present on none of them is
lonelier than a wife whose husband travels, because at least the traveling wife knows
why she's alone.
   Presence means your attention is in the room. The phone is down. The game is
off, or it's on and you're watching it with her rather than instead of her. You know
what she said five minutes ago. You ask a question and wait for the answer. You're a
person she's with, and not a body she's near.

Providing without disappearing

   I promised in chapter 5 to come back to this, and this is the place, because it's the
trap that catches the best men.
   A good man provides. He works hard, carries the weight, and makes sure the
house has what it needs. And somewhere along the way the work starts asking for
more, and it always has a good reason, and the man gives it, because giving to the
work feels like giving to the family. Then one day he's Walt, with eleven trucks and
a wife who has made a whole life in the spaces he isn't in.
   Here's how you know you've started to disappear. She stopped asking when
you'll be home, because the answer stopped being reliable. The kids tell her things
and she tells you. You find out about your own house secondhand. You've missed
the last three things and had a good reason for each one. You're too tired when you
get home to be anybody. And you've told yourself, more than once, that she wants
the lifestyle, and you've never actually asked her.
   Ask her, because that's the first move. "If you could have less of what I provide
and more of me, would you take that trade?" Walt asked. His wife said yes before
he'd finished the sentence. She said she'd rather have had him and a smaller house,
and the smaller house would have been fine, and she'd been trying to tell him for
twenty years. He sold off part of the business the next year. Not all of it, but enough
that he was home for dinner four nights out of five. He told me the money was less
and the marriage was more and he'd never once regretted the math.
  Most men can't sell off a business. Here's what most men can do. Keep the floor,
which is chapter 5's word for the minimum that doesn't move no matter what the



What She Actually Needs                                                              233
job does: dinner at the table four nights, Sunday morning, the bedtime prayer with
the kids in person or by phone from the hotel. The floor tells her the family is the
corner post and the work is the wire.
  Tell the truth about the season. Busy seasons happen: tax season, harvest, the big
project, the deployment. A man who says "the next six weeks are going to be brutal,
here's the end date, here's what I'm going to protect, here's what I need from you"
has given her something she can hold. A man who vanishes into the season and
expects credit when he comes out has made her carry the not knowing.
    Say when you'll be home and be home then. Not "I'll try to be home by six," but
"I'll be home at 6:40," and then be home at 6:40. If it changes, call before it changes
and not after. This is a small thing, and wives will tell you it's the whole thing.
Reliability is presence at a distance.
  Bring your best hours and not your leftovers. If the job gets the first fourteen
hours of you and she gets what's left, she's getting a man who's already spent. Find a
way to give her some of your good: a lunch, a morning, the first fifteen minutes
when you're still a person.
   If you travel, build a road practice. The bedtime call every night, whatever the
time zone. Knowing what's on the calendar at home on Tuesday so you can ask
about it Tuesday night. A note in her drawer before you leave. Men who travel for
work can be more present than men who don't, if they decide to be.
   And watch the scoreboard. Work claps and home mostly doesn't. If you notice
that you're drifting toward the place that claps, you've noticed the whole problem,
and chapter 7 told you the cure. You aren't working for the clapping. You're
working for a Father who sees the dinner table.

Safety

   She needs to not be afraid of you, and I don't mean fists, because most of you
never would. I mean the voice that changes pitch, and the silence that lasts three
days, and the sarcasm that draws blood, and the door that gets slammed, the truck
that leaves fast, the thing you said that you didn't mean and that she's still carrying.
A wife who has learned to read her husband's weather before she speaks isn't safe in
her own house, and she can't be fully herself with a man she has to manage.




234                                                                 What She Actually Needs
  Proverbs 15:1 (NIV)
  A gentle answer turns away wrath, but a harsh word stirs up anger.



   A gentle answer is the instrument. Not a weak one; a gentle one, from a man
strong enough not to need volume. Chapter 9 gave you the ninety seconds and the
truck and the true thing under the anger, and this is where you use them. A wife
who knows her husband's anger will never land on her has a kind of freedom that
most wives never get.

To be pursued, still

   The man who asked her out should still be asking her out. Something happens to
men after the wedding. The pursuit that got her stops, because the pursuit was for
getting her, and now he has her, and he turns his attention to the next thing to get.
She feels it as being a thing acquired and shelved. And she'll rarely say so, because it
sounds needy, and she doesn't want to be needy.

  Proverbs 5:18 (NIV)
  May your fountain be blessed, and may you rejoice in the wife of your youth.



   Rejoice in the wife of your youth, the proverb says, and it doesn't say tolerate or
manage. The blueprint had a man moved to poetry by his bride, and I don't care
whether you can write poetry. You can plan a Thursday. You can ask her out with a
specific evening and a plan and a shirt you ironed. You can look at her across a room
the way you did the first year and let her catch you doing it. Pursuit isn't a stage a
man passes through. It's how a husband tells his wife, every year, that he'd choose
her again.

Leadership she can rest in

      This card comes from tired women. For him to lead so I don't have to carry all of
it.
   She isn't asking to be ruled. She's asking not to be the only adult in the house.
She's asking for a man who will say "here's where we're going, here's how we'll get
there," so that she can stop holding the whole map. A woman married to a man who


What She Actually Needs                                                              235
won't lead ends up leading by default, and she's exhausted, and she's resentful, and
she isn't sure she's allowed to say so, because he's a nice man who lets her have her
way.
   Leading her means direction. It means going first into the hard decision and
owning the outcome. It means she can bring you a problem and it becomes your
problem too, not a thing you'll get to. And it means spiritual leadership, which
most Christian husbands treat as a nice idea and most Christian wives would trade
almost anything for. The card said "pray with me, just once a week, out loud," and
that's the whole ask: once a week, out loud. Chapter 6 gave you the eleven-second
prayer. Say it with her on Sunday night, holding her hand. You will be amazed at
what it does to a woman to hear her husband talk to God about her.

To be heard and known

  Chapter 22, and I won't repeat it. She needs a man who studies her and lets her
know she's known. The card says "for him to want to know me." Not know me;
want to.

Words

   She needs to hear it. Men think it's understood, and it isn't; it's needed, out loud,
in specific words, regularly.

  Proverbs 31:28-29 (NIV)
  Her children arise and call her blessed; her husband also, and he praises her: 'Many women do noble
  things, but you surpass them all.'



  Her husband also, and he praises her. In front of the children, and with specifics.
"You surpass them all" is not "you're a good wife." It's a man telling his wife she's the
best one there is, and meaning it, and saying it where people can hear. What you call
her, she'll become, as we said in chapter 18. Call her the best thing that ever
happened to you, out loud, this week.

Help that isn't "helping"

  She needs the load shared, and she needs you to stop calling it helping. It isn't her



236                                                                             What She Actually Needs
house that you help with; it's your house, and the dishes are your dishes. The kids'
schedules are your schedules. A husband who "helps out" has told his wife that the
whole thing is hers and he's a volunteer. A husband who owns the house with her
has told her she isn't alone in it. Eve was called a helper, and it's a title of strength.
Christ came to serve. Both are true, and neither one lets a man sit while his wife runs
the house around him.

Tenderness

   She needs to be touched without it being about the bedroom. A hand on her
back in the kitchen. Her feet after a long day. Being held when she cries without
you trying to stop the crying. Most men touch their wives for exactly two reasons:
to get past them in a hallway and to initiate. She needs a third kind, and she needs it
more than the second, and if she gets the third, the second will go better than it ever
has.

A man who's growing

   The last card is the one men don't expect. She needs to see you moving: toward
God, toward being a better man, toward the four marks. A husband who's stuck,
who's the same man at forty-five he was at thirty with more weight and less
patience, is a weight she carries. A husband who's growing, even slowly, even with
failures, is a man she can follow, because she can see where he's headed. Your
reading Mark in the truck is for her too. Your twenty minutes with a brother is for
her too. A wife married to a man who's becoming something has hope in the house.

What she needs in her seasons

   Under a newborn, she needs sleep more than anything you can buy, and you can
give her that. She needs food she didn't make. She needs one adult conversation a
day with someone who isn't asking her for milk. She needs to be told she's doing
well, specifically, because she's sure she isn't. She needs to not be alone with it: you
in the room at three in the morning even when you can't feed him. And she needs
to be touched without agenda by a man who isn't keeping score of how long it's
been.
   After forty years, she needs to be seen as a woman and not as a fixture or a



What She Actually Needs                                                               237
caretaker. She needs plans for the years that are left, made together. She needs you to
handle your health, because her greatest fear may be finding you on the floor, and
every appointment you skip is a weight on her. She needs gentleness as her body
changes, and a husband whose eyes haven't wandered to younger women on a
screen while hers aged. She needs your presence now more than ever, because the
kids are gone and the work is gone and it's just you, and if you're in the recliner she's
alone in the house you built. Roy said his wife told him, after the coffee and the
porch and the ocean trip, "This is what I wanted the whole time. I just wanted you
to want to be here."
   If you're single again, take an honest look at the cards and at what she needed and
didn't get. Not to bury yourself, but to learn, so that whatever comes next, you
aren't the same man. And know that your children's mother still needs some of this
from you: reliability, civility, and your support of her authority with the children. A
father who undermines the mother across two houses is hurting his kids to win a
point. Back her up in front of them, even when you'd have done it differently.
   If you're single, this whole chapter is a blueprint of the man you're building.
Every card is something she'll need in year one, and the man who has practiced
steadiness, presence, gentleness, pursuit, leadership, words, shared work, tenderness,
and growth before he meets her will hand her a husband instead of a project. And
your mother needs a few of these from you now, as a grown son: presence, words, a
call that's about her.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty-three has no wife to read a card from, and the takeaway
for him is that every card in this chapter describes the man he's building now:
steadiness, presence, gentleness, pursuit, words. Eli's version of pursuit this year was
calling his grandmother every week; his version of presence was putting his phone in
the other room when his roommates were talking. It sounds small, and it's the
training.
   A man of fifty's wife would write "for him to be here when he's here," which is
Walt's card, and the takeaway for him is the question he finally asked: would you
take less of what I provide and more of me, and her answer before he'd finished the
sentence.




238                                                                 What She Actually Needs
What He gives His bride

   Everything on the cards, Christ gives the church. Steadiness: never will I leave
you. Presence: surely I am with you always. Safety: gentle and humble in heart.
Pursuit: He came looking for us. Leadership: follow me. Being known: I know my
sheep. Words: this is my body, given for you. Help: He washed their feet.
Tenderness: He wept with the grieving. A man who's growing: He grew in wisdom
and stature and favor, so that even the Son of God showed His people what it looks
like to move.
  She isn't asking for anything He doesn't give. She's asking you to give her, in a
house, on a Tuesday, a small picture of what He gives you. That's the job, and it
doesn't cost money. It costs you, which is the only thing she ever wanted.

Before you turn the page

    If you read the cards and felt the floor drop, because you've been working
yourself to the bone for something she never asked for, I want you to hear that this
is one of the most fixable things in the book. She isn't asking for a different man.
She's asking for the one she married to be in the room. The Father who gives His
bride steadiness and presence and words and tenderness is offering you the same
things to give, in a house, on a Tuesday, and He isn't asking you to buy anything.
May He give you the courage to ask her the question this week, and the grace to
hear the answer, and may you find that presence costs less and gives more than you
feared.

240                                                                    What She Actually Needs
CHAPTER 25



The Bed Is a Covenant
   Part 4: The house
  Two ranchers I knew traded a stretch of grazing land on a handshake in 1979.
No lawyer and no paper. One of them told me about it forty years later, and I asked
what would have happened if the other man had backed out.
   He looked at me as though I'd asked what would happen if the sun came up in
the west. "He gave me his word," he said. "That's not a thing you back out of. That's
a thing you are."
   That's the difference between a contract and a covenant, and it's a difference
most men never learn about marriage. A contract protects you if the other party
fails; it's built for the exit. A covenant binds you regardless; it's built for the staying.
When those two men shook hands they weren't agreeing to terms. They were
putting themselves into each other's keeping, and the handshake was the sign of it,
the visible thing that stood for the invisible thing.
   The bed is that handshake. This chapter is about what that means, because
almost nobody has ever told you, and the culture has been telling you the opposite
loudly and for your whole life.

What the culture says, what the church said, what God says

    The culture says sex is recreation, a thing two adults do for fun, like bowling,
where the only rules are consent and enthusiasm and anything else is repression. Or
it says sex is self-expression: it's about who you are, your identity, your authenticity,
your needs. Either way, it's about you, and the other person is the equipment.
   The church, for a long stretch, said the opposite and got it just as wrong. Sex was
dirty and dangerous, the thing good boys didn't think about and good husbands did



The Bed Is a Covenant                                                                    241
quickly and quietly and with a little shame. A lot of men in their fifties and sixties
were raised on that and are still carrying it into their bedrooms.
   God says something different from both. He says the bed is a covenant act. It's
the one-flesh sign of a promise made before Him. It's the physical enactment of "I
am yours and you are mine, all of me, permanently." That's why the very first thing
the Bible says about a husband and wife, before the fall, is this.

  Genesis 2:24-25 (NIV)
  That is why a man leaves his father and mother and is united to his wife, and they become one flesh.
  Adam and his wife were both naked, and they felt no shame.



   One flesh, and no shame. Sex in the blueprint isn't recreation and it isn't dirt. It's
two people becoming one thing, in the sight of God, with nothing hidden, and it's
called good. And it's held in honor rather than embarrassment.

  Hebrews 13:4 (NIV)
  Marriage should be honored by all, and the marriage bed kept pure, for God will judge the adulterer
  and all the sexually immoral.



  The marriage bed kept pure. Not kept quiet, and not kept minimal. Kept pure,
which means kept for what it's for, kept between the two people it belongs to, and
kept honored. God isn't embarrassed by the bed. He built it, and He's as fiercely
protective of it as that rancher was of a handshake.
  Once you see the bed as a covenant sign, every question about it gets clearer.
What a husband does in it. What he does with his eyes outside it. What a single man
does before it. What a man does when the bed goes quiet for a season or for good.
All of it comes back to a single question: am I keeping the covenant, or breaking it?

What the covenant means in the bed

  Let me speak to husbands first, because most of you have never had a man talk to
you about this anywhere but a locker room. The covenant is exclusive, all the way
down. Proverbs has a picture of this worth memorizing.




242                                                                                 The Bed Is a Covenant
  Proverbs 5:15 (NIV)
  Drink water from your own cistern, running water from your own well.



   Your own cistern and your own well. Not the neighbor's, not the screen's, not
the memory's, not the coworker's. Exclusivity in the covenant isn't only about
where your body goes. Jesus took it all the way inside.

  Matthew 5:28 (NIV)
  But I tell you that anyone who looks at a woman lustfully has already committed adultery with her
  in his heart.



  Already committed adultery, Jesus says. A husband who's faithful with his body
and unfaithful with his eyes is a man with a cracked door in his covenant, and his
wife can feel the draft even if she can't say where it's coming from. The cistern is
hers alone, and that includes the part of you nobody sees. The covenant is generous.
Paul wrote a sentence about the marriage bed that turned the ancient world upside
down and still turns most men's assumptions upside down.

  1 Corinthians 7:3-4 (NIV)
  The husband should fulfill his marital duty to his wife, and likewise the wife to her husband. The
  wife does not have authority over her own body but yields it to her husband. In the same way, the
  husband does not have authority over his own body but yields it to his wife.



    In the same way. The husband doesn't own his own body either; it's hers. This
isn't a verse about a wife's obligation to a husband, though men have twisted it that
way for centuries. It's a verse about mutual self-giving, and the husband's line is the
surprising one. Your body is for her. Which means the bed isn't about your release.
It's about her delight. A husband who understands the covenant goes into the bed
asking what she needs and how she's made and what would bless her, and the
strange, backward, biblical thing is that a man who does that gets more out of the
bed than a man who takes.
   The covenant is present. A man can be absent in bed the same way he's absent in
the recliner. He can be there in body and somewhere else in his head: on a screen he
looked at last week, on a fantasy, on the clock. A wife knows the difference. She
knows when her husband is with her and when he's using her to get somewhere else.


The Bed Is a Covenant                                                                                  243
The covenant means you're there, with her, this woman, in this bed, and no one else
is in the room, including the ones who live in your memory.
   The covenant is patient and tender. She isn't a screen, and she isn't you. Her
body works differently and on a different clock, and a man who has studied his
wife, as chapter 22 asked, knows this and honors it. Hurry is a kind of theft.
Tenderness is a kind of worship. The men I know whose wives speak warmly about
their marriages after thirty years are without exception men who learned to go slow.
    The covenant is never a weapon. Not withheld as punishment, not demanded as
payment, not used as leverage in a fight or as a scoreboard in a marriage. The
moment the bed becomes a tool for getting your way, it stops being a covenant sign
and becomes a contract negotiation, and everything Adam and Eve had in Genesis 2
is gone. If the bed has gone quiet in your marriage, and that happens, the answer is
never to make her pay for it. The answer is to find out what's actually wrong, which
is almost never about the bed.
    The covenant is honest. Most couples never talk about it. They do it or don't do
it and never say a sentence about what they need, what hurts, what they're afraid of,
what they'd love. A covenant is a spoken thing; the ranchers said words before they
shook. Talk to your wife about the bed, gently, not in the bed and not after a
disappointment. Ask what she'd want more of and less of. Tell her the truth about
yourself. Two people who can talk about the bed can keep it for fifty years.
   And the covenant is protected. Beds go quiet from neglect long before they go
quiet from age. The pursuit from chapter 24, the presence, the tenderness that has
nothing to do with the bedroom: those are how a husband keeps the well from
running dry. A man who only touches his wife to initiate will find one day that she
flinches at his hand, and he'll wonder why, and the answer is that he taught her his
touch always wanted something.

The screen, in the covenant frame

   Chapter 9 talked about the itch as an exit and gave you tools: one brother with
the whole truth, filters with his password, the phone in the kitchen, the hour filled,
and you should use them. But I want to say it differently here, because the frame
changes the fight.
  You aren't fighting pornography because it's against a rule. You're fighting it
because you made a covenant with a real woman, and every image is a small


244                                                                  The Bed Is a Covenant
breaking of it. It isn't a victimless private habit. It has a victim, and she sleeps next to
you. It trains your appetite for novelty inside a covenant built on one well. It makes
your wife a means to an end instead of the end. It rewires what your body responds
to until a real woman in a real bed can't compete with a screen, which is why so
many men in their thirties are failing in bed and don't know why. It puts a locked
drawer in the middle of the one relationship that was supposed to have none. And it
lies to you that the problem is her not being enough, when the problem is you not
being present.
   For the husband, the fight against the screen is covenant-keeping, in the same
category as not sleeping with the coworker. Fight it like that. Not with white
knuckles, but with a brother, a filter, a habit, and a clear picture in your head of the
woman you shook hands with, and of the Father who sees what's done in secret and
keeps every faithful Tuesday on the books.

In her seasons, and in yours

    The covenant holds through every season, and the seasons change what keeping
it looks like. This is where husbands do the most damage by not knowing, so let me
be plain.
   After a baby, her body has been through a war. The doctor will say six weeks; her
body and her heart may say longer, and she won't be able to explain why in a way
that sounds like a reason. Your claims are laid down. Not your love; your claims.
She's exhausted, she's touched out, she's self-conscious about a body that doesn't
look or feel like it did, she may be sore in ways she won't describe, and she may be
quietly terrified that you're disappointed in her. You aren't keeping count. You're
telling her she's beautiful and meaning it when nothing's going to happen. You're
touching her in the third way, the way that wants nothing. And when she's ready,
you go slow, and you ask, and you stop if she needs you to. Tomás told me the
sentence that mattered was his own: "I'm not in a hurry. I'm not going anywhere.
You tell me." He said the bed came back stronger than before, months later, because
she'd learned she could trust him with her body when it was weakest.
  In illness, hers or yours, the covenant doesn't pause when the body does; it
changes shape. A husband whose wife is going through chemotherapy keeps the
covenant by tenderness that has nothing to do with the bed, by being present in the
bathroom at three in the morning, by telling her she's still his and meaning it when



The Bed Is a Covenant                                                                   245
she's lost her hair. That's the bed as covenant sign with the sign changed. The
covenant is the same.
   In the middle years, something happens to a woman in her forties and fifties that
most husbands know nothing about and have never asked about, and it changes her
body and her desire and her comfort in ways she may be embarrassed to name. A
man who has studied his wife knows this is coming and doesn't take it as rejection.
He asks, and he's patient, and he goes to the doctor with her if there's a doctor to go
to. He makes the bed a safe place for a woman whose body is doing something new,
and she'll remember for the rest of her life that he did.
   After forty years, Roy told me, with a grin I didn't expect from a former state
trooper, that the bed at seventy was better than the bed at thirty. "Less often.
Slower. I actually know her now." That's the covenant paying out. Men who kept
the well their whole lives get to drink from it in old age, and it's sweet.
  And when it's no longer possible, for one or both, the covenant holds. Mr.
Alvarez, bathing his wife every morning while she doesn't know his name, is
keeping the marriage bed pure at seventy-eight. Nothing is happening in it. He's still
one flesh with her. He said "in sickness" in 1968 and he meant the whole thing.

If there is no wife yet

  I told you in chapter 21 that the covenant starts before the bed. Here's what I
mean.
   If you're single and you intend to marry, every choice you make right now about
your body is a choice about a covenant you haven't made yet with a woman you
may not have met. You're either building it or spending it. A man who has kept
himself for his wife walks into the bed on his wedding night with something to give
her that can't be given twice: all of him, unshared. A man who hasn't walks in with
a comparison in his head and a well that's been drunk from.

  1 Thessalonians 4:3-5 (NIV)
  It is God's will that you should be sanctified: that you should avoid sexual immorality; that each of
  you should learn to control your own body in a way that is holy and honorable, not in passionate lust
  like the pagans, who do not know God;



  Learn to control your own body, Paul says, and the word is learn, because it's a



246                                                                                  The Bed Is a Covenant
skill, and it's acquired, and men have acquired it in every century including this one,
whatever the culture tells you about impossibility. The tools are the ones from
chapter 9. And the frame is this: you aren't keeping a rule. You're keeping a
covenant early, with a wife you're praying for and with Christ, whose body you are.
    Practically, don't date for years without moving toward marriage; long
courtships are where good men fall. Don't be alone with her in bedrooms and at
midnight and tell yourselves you're strong, because you aren't, and nobody is, and
that's why the wise man doesn't test it. Tell your brother the truth every week. And
if you can carry the weight and you've found the woman, marry her. Paul said it's
better than burning, and he wasn't being subtle.

If you're single again

   Kenny, and every man in your seat: single again is single. I know that's harder to
hear than it is for the man who has never married, because you know what you're
missing in a way he doesn't. The itch is going to come at you like a freight train in
the first two years, and the culture is going to tell you that a grown man who has
been married has earned the right to do as he pleases, and he hasn't. The covenant
standard has no clause for men who used to have a bed.
   That means no sex outside a new covenant. It means not moving in with
someone because "we're basically married." Basically married is not married; there's
no handshake, and the ranchers would tell you a handshake is not a formality. It
means the brother with the whole truth and the phone in the kitchen, the same as
the twenty-six-year-old, with more at stake, because your children are watching how
a man handles a body when it's hard.
   And if you're a widower, the same is true, and I'm sorry, because you kept the
covenant for fifty years and now the bed is empty and nobody warned you about
the loneliness of it. God knows about it, and He doesn't ask you to pretend the
emptiness isn't there. He asks you to keep the bed pure in the empty years the way
you kept it in the full ones, and to let your brothers carry some of the weight, and to
trust that the Father who saw every faithful night sees these too.

What the bed preaches

   Paul says the strangest thing about marriage at the end of Ephesians 5. He quotes
the one-flesh verse from Genesis, and then he says it's a profound mystery, and that


The Bed Is a Covenant                                                               247
he's actually talking about Christ and the church.

  Ephesians 5:31 (NIV)
  'For this reason a man will leave his father and mother and be united to his wife, and the two will
  become one flesh.'



   Christ and the church. That's what the one-flesh covenant between a husband
and wife is finally about. It's a picture of the covenant between Jesus and His
people. Which means the bed preaches. When a husband is exclusive, generous,
present, patient, never weaponizing, honest, and protective in the bed, he's showing
his wife and the watching world a small, real picture of how Christ loves the church:
faithfully, self-givingly, with delight, forever. And when a man breaks the covenant,
with his body or his eyes, he's preaching a lie about God.
   That's how serious it is, and that's how good it is. The bed isn't recreation and it
isn't dirt. It's a handshake between two people who have put themselves in each
other's keeping before God, renewed as often as they're able, for as long as they both
live, and it stands for the largest thing that's true.
   The rancher was right. A covenant isn't a thing you back out of. It's a thing you
are.

Before you turn the page

   This is the chapter men skim, and if you didn't, I'm glad, because the bed is
where a great many good men have quietly broken faith and never told anyone. If
that's you, the drawer can be opened; David's was, and his standing was restored in a
breath. The covenant isn't a rule you've failed; it's a handshake you can keep starting
tomorrow, with a real woman or with the one you're praying for. May the Father
who honors the marriage bed give you a well of your own to drink from and the
faithfulness to drink from no other, and may your bed preach the truth about Him.




248                                                                                  The Bed Is a Covenant
   YOUR TURN



   NAME IT
   Because a crack in the covenant is easiest to see once it's been said to God: name
   where yours is, if there is one, the eyes or the screen or the hurry or the scoreboard or
   the silence about it or the neglect; and if you're single, say plainly whether you've
   been building the covenant or spending it.
   DO THIS WEEK
   Because the covenant is a spoken thing before it's anything else: if you're married,
   have one honest and gentle conversation with your wife about the bed, not in the
   bed, asking what she'd want more of and less of; if you're single or single again, set
   up the filter with a brother's password and put the phone in the kitchen tonight.
   TELL A BROTHER
   Because the itch lives on secrecy and dies when a brother knows: tell him the crack
   you named, and if it's the screen, tell him the whole truth, what and how often and
   since when, and let him hold the password.
   CARRY THIS
   The bed is a handshake before God. A covenant isn't a thing you back out of. It's a
   thing you are.
