CHAPTER 26



Your Daughter Is Watching
   Part 4: The house
   Anthony's daughter is seven, and she watches his face when her mother walks
into a room.
   He didn't know it until his wife told him. She'd been noticing it for months: the
girl at the kitchen table, or on the floor with her crayons, glancing up at her father
the moment her mother came through the door, not at her mother but at him,
watching what his face did.
  "She's learning what a man's face does when a woman walks in," his wife said.
"She's learning it from you."
   Anthony told me he didn't sleep much that night, because he knew what his face
had been doing for a couple of years, which wasn't cruelty but nothing at all, a
glance up and a glance back down at the phone. And his daughter, who isn't going
to remember a single sermon she hears before she's twelve, was memorizing that.
   Your daughter is watching. That's the whole chapter, and I could stop there, but
I want to tell you what she's watching for, because she's learning three things from
you that she'll carry to her grave, and she's learning all of them before you say a
word.

The three lessons

   The first thing she's learning is what a man is. Not from books or movies or her
friends' fathers; from you. You are the definition. Whatever you are, that's what
"man" means to her, and every man she meets for the rest of her life will be
measured against it, for better or for worse. If you're gentle, she'll expect gentleness
and be startled by harshness. If you're absent, she'll expect absence and be



Your Daughter Is Watching                                                             251
suspicious of a man who shows up. If you're a storm, she'll either marry a storm or
spend her life flinching at weather.
   The second thing she's learning is what a woman deserves, and she's learning it
from how you treat her mother, every single day. How you speak to her. How you
look at her. Whether you defend her, thank her, touch her gently, listen when she
talks. Whether you roll your eyes. Whether you help without being asked. Whether
you go quiet for three days. Your daughter is taking notes on what a woman can
expect from a man, and she's going to choose a husband with those notes in her
hand. If you want to know what kind of man your daughter will marry, look at how
you treated her mother this week. That's the man.
   The third thing she's learning is what she's worth, and she's learning it from how
you look at her. Whether you're delighted in her or merely tolerating her. Whether
you see her or see past her. Whether your attention has to be earned by
performance, by grades and goals and pretty, or whether it's given simply because
she's yours. A girl who is delighted in by her father grows up knowing her worth
isn't up for negotiation. A girl who isn't will spend her teens and twenties looking
for a man to tell her what her father didn't, and the men who volunteer for that job
are almost never the ones you'd choose.
   Three lessons, all taught before breakfast, every day, whether or not you mean to
teach them.

What she needs from you

   Let me get practical, because "be a good father to your daughter" is a sentence
everybody agrees with and nobody knows how to do on a Tuesday.
   She needs delight, and she needs to see it on your face when she walks in, and
delight is different from approval. Approval is for what she did; delight is for who
she is. God does this with His people, and it's the picture I'd give every father of a
daughter.

  Zephaniah 3:17 (NIV)
  The LORD your God is with you, the Mighty Warrior who saves. He will take great delight in you;
  in his love he will no longer rebuke you, but will rejoice over you with singing.



  He will take great delight in you. He will rejoice over you with singing. That's a


252                                                                        Your Daughter Is Watching
Father whose face changes when His child walks in, and yours can change too. Look
up, and put the phone down, and let her see that you're glad she exists. You don't
have to say anything; it's on your face or it isn't, and she's reading it.
   She needs to see her mother honored. I've said it, and it's the biggest one, so I'll
say it plainer. The most important thing you will ever do for your daughter is love
her mother where your daughter can see it. Speak to your wife the way you want a
man to speak to your girl in twenty years. Thank her at the table, take her side,
touch her shoulder in the kitchen. Apologize to her in front of the children when
you were wrong. Every one of those is a lesson your daughter is writing down.
   If you're divorced, this lesson doesn't stop. It gets harder and more important at
the same time. Your daughter is watching how you treat her mother across two
houses, at the exchange, on the phone, when her mother is late. She's learning
whether a man honors a woman even when it's over, even when it hurts, even when
he has reasons not to. Kenny said the hardest thing he ever did for his daughter was
speak well of her mother in his own truck when the girl was trying to get him to
take her side. "I said, 'Your mom loves you and she's doing her best and I'm not
going to talk about her that way.' My daughter looked at me like I'd grown a second
head. Then she relaxed. I think she'd been afraid she had to choose."
   She needs protection, and here you should remember the second verb in Genesis
2:15, which is to keep and guard the garden. A father is a keeper, and his daughter is
the most precious thing in the garden.
   Protection is not control. A father who locks his daughter in a tower to keep her
safe teaches her that she's fragile and that men are jailers. Protection is a fence with a
gate, and a father standing at the gate who knows who's coming through. It means
you know her friends' names and their parents. It means you know what's on her
phone and who's texting her, and you've told her why. It means the boy who wants
to take her somewhere meets you first, looks you in the eye, and understands
without your saying it that she is not unaccompanied in this world. It means you've
told her, plainly, before she needs it, what a man does when a boy puts his hands
where he shouldn't: she says no, she leaves, she calls you, and you come. And
protection means protecting her from you: from your temper, from your sarcasm,
from your absence, from your disappointment landing on her because you had a
bad day. Paul told fathers exactly what not to do.




Your Daughter Is Watching                                                              253
  Ephesians 6:4 (NIV)
  Fathers, do not exasperate your children; instead, bring them up in the training and instruction of
  the Lord.



   Do not exasperate, Paul says, which means don't wear them out with your
unpredictability, your harshness, your rules that change with your mood. A
daughter exasperated by her father learns that men are exhausting, and she'll either
avoid them or resign herself to one. She needs your words. What you call her, she'll
become. Chapter 18 said God gave men the power to name, and nowhere does that
power land harder than on a daughter. Call her beautiful, and mean her whole self
and not only her face. Call her strong, and call her kind when she's been kind, and
call her yours. Say "I'm proud of you" when she did nothing to earn it, simply
because you are. Say "I love you" out loud every day. There are fathers who have
never said those three words to their daughters and think it's understood, and it
isn't; it's needed, and if she doesn't hear it from you, there's a boy at fifteen who'll
say it to get something, and it will work, because she's been starving for it. Job did
something at the end of his life that was unheard of in his world.

  Job 42:15 (NIV)
  Nowhere in all the land were there found women as beautiful as Job's daughters, and their father
  granted them an inheritance along with their brothers.



   He granted them an inheritance along with their brothers, in a culture that
didn't. He looked at his girls and said: you count, fully, the same as the boys, and
I'm putting my name and my wealth behind that. That's a father using his words
and his weight to tell his daughters what they're worth.
    She needs your time. Delight without time is only a sentiment. She needs you,
regularly, on the calendar, with nothing else in the room. A breakfast on Saturday,
or a drive to school with the radio off, or a walk. Tomás started taking his baby
daughter for a walk every evening at six, just the two of them, when she was three
months old, and his wife thought it was sweet and a little pointless. He's still doing
it; she's two now. "She talks the whole time," he said. "She's been talking to me since
before she could talk." That's a girl who's going to grow up knowing her father
wants her company.
  She needs your steadiness when she pushes you away. Somewhere between


254                                                                            Your Daughter Is Watching
twelve and sixteen, most daughters go through a stretch when they don't want
much to do with their fathers. She'll roll her eyes and prefer her room and be
embarrassed by you. And a lot of fathers, hurt, withdraw, which is the worst thing
they could do. She isn't pushing you away because she doesn't need you. She's
testing whether you'll stay, so stay. Keep showing up at the door. Keep the Saturday
breakfast even when she sits there in silence. Keep saying "I love you" to a closed
door. When she comes out the other side, she'll remember who stayed.
   She needs your faith, lived where she can see it. Pray for her by name and tell her
you do. Pray with her at bedtime, even when she's fifteen and pretends to hate it.
Let her see you read the Bible at the kitchen table. Let her see you go to church
when you don't feel like it. Let her see you apologize to God out loud. A daughter
who has watched her father kneel knows what a man on his knees looks like, and
she'll know to look for one.
   And she needs your eyes to stay home. She notices where they go: at the pool, at
the restaurant, on the screen. A father whose eyes wander teaches his daughter two
things, that women are to be looked at and that she'll be compared. A father whose
eyes stay home teaches her that a man can be faithful and that she can expect it.

If she's grown

   Roy's daughter was forty-one when he said the sentence in her driveway, the one
from chapter 12: "I wasn't the father you needed. I'm sorry." She's a grown woman
with children of her own, and it still mattered.
   A daughter never stops watching. She's watching at forty to see whether her
father has changed. She's watching how he treats her mother in retirement. She's
watching whether he'll show up for her children the way he didn't for her. And a
grown daughter still needs her father's blessing, his words, his delight. Most men
think that window closed at eighteen, and it didn't. Roy's daughter told him, a year
after the driveway, that the thing she'd needed most in her twenties was to hear him
say he was proud of her, and that she'd given up on it, and that hearing it at
forty-one had "fixed something I didn't know was still broken."
   If you're a grandfather, you have a second chance, and it isn't only about the
grandchildren. Your daughter is watching how you grandfather. Every time you
show up for her children, you're telling her something about how you wish you'd
shown up for her, and she hears it. Be the man with time now, because you've got it.


Your Daughter Is Watching                                                           255
If you're the man in the truck on Tuesday night

  Kenny, and every divorced father. You can't be at the kitchen table every
morning, and I know that. Here's what you can do, and it's more than you think.
   Never speak against her mother, not once, not when your daughter baits you,
not when it's true, not when you're tired. Your daughter is half her mother, and
every word against the mother lands on the girl.
   Be there for everything you're allowed to be there for, and some things you have
to fight for. The recital, the game, the parent night. She's counting, and she won't
say so.
   Call on a schedule. Tuesday at 7:30, every Tuesday, even when she says "nothing"
to every question. Kenny's daughter said "nothing" for about eight months. Then
one Tuesday she talked for forty minutes. "It was because I'd called the other
thirty-four Tuesdays," he said.
  Make your house a home she lives in and not a bachelor pad she visits. Her own
room, her own things, her drawings on the refrigerator. When she's with you, she's
home.
   And show her a man who's steady when the world isn't. Your divorce shook her
world. A father who's steady, present, honorable, and growing is the ground she
stands on while it shakes. You may be the empty chair at her mother's table. You
can be the full one at yours.

If you have no daughter

   Single man, or father of sons only, or man whose daughter is grown and gone:
there are girls in your church who don't have any of this. Their fathers left, or
they're there and they're storms, or they're there and gone. And those girls are
learning what a man is from whoever happens to be around.
   Be a man they can watch. That means honoring the women in your church
where the girls can see it. It means coaching, teaching Sunday school, being the
uncle who shows up, and doing all of it in the light: never alone with a girl who isn't
yours, always visible, always above reproach, so that what she learns from you is that
a good man is safe and honorable and keeps his distance appropriate. That isn't a
restriction on your usefulness. It's part of what you're teaching her.


256                                                              Your Daughter Is Watching
   I've watched a single man in his thirties become the reason three fatherless girls in
a small church know what a man looks like when he's kind to a woman. He didn't
do anything dramatic. He treated their mothers with respect, showed up when he
said he would, and never once made any of them uncomfortable. They're watching
him the way Anthony's daughter watches her father. He knows it, and he carries it
like a man.

The face at the door

  Anthony changed what his face does. He told me it was harder than it sounds,
because he had to actually feel something when his wife walked in, and he'd been
numb for a couple of years, and you can't fake delight in front of a seven-year-old,
because she'll know. So he had to go back to chapter 22 and study his wife again,
and to chapter 23 and lay some things down, and the delight came back slowly, the
way feeling comes back to a hand that's been asleep.
   His daughter still watches his face. He said she's started smiling when she does.
Jesus was tender with daughters. When a synagogue leader's little girl died, He went
into the room and took her by the hand.

  Luke 8:54 (NIV)
  But he took her by the hand and said, 'My child, get up!'



  My child, He called her, and He took her hand. That's the Man you're following,
and He was gentle with a girl who wasn't His, and He gave her back to her father.
Your daughter is watching. Give her something worth seeing.

Before you turn the page

   If you've read this chapter and realized your face has been teaching your daughter
the wrong thing, I don't want you to leave it in shame, because shame is exactly
what a father shows on his face when he's ashamed, and she'll read that too.
Anthony's delight came back slowly, the way feeling comes back to a hand that's
been asleep, and it came back because he went and did the work with his wife first.
The Man you're following took a little girl by the hand and gave her back to her
father. May the Father whose face changes when His children walk in change yours
this week, and may your daughter catch you being glad she exists.

  NAME IT
  Because she's learning from your face whether you mean to teach her or not: say
  what your face has been teaching your daughter when her mother walks in, and

  Because delight and honor have to be seen to be learned: tell your daughter out loud

258                                                               Your Daughter Is Watching
CHAPTER 27



Give Your Son a Name
   Part 4: The house
   The first weight a boy ever carries is his father's name. He didn't pick it. It was on
the mailbox before he was born. And for the rest of his life, when he says it, people
who knew his father will hear the father in it, and the boy will feel that, and he'll
learn early whether the name is a thing to stand on or a thing to live down. I've
known men who spent forty years trying to make their last name mean something
other than what their father made it mean. I've known others who could walk into a
feed store two counties over and get credit on a handshake because of what their
father's name meant there.
    That's what this chapter is about, but it's about more than the last name. A boy
needs his father to name him. Not only to hand him a word on a birth certificate,
but to tell him, over and over, in a thousand ordinary moments, who he is. Because
if his father doesn't, somebody else will, and the world is full of names for boys, and
almost none of them are good.

The names he's getting anyway

   Before I tell you what to call your son, let me tell you what he's being called.
   He's being called things at school. Some of them are fine, and some of them will
stick for life if nobody gives him something heavier to stand on: lazy, stupid, weird,
too small, too much.
   He's being called things by the culture, which has decided that boys are a
problem to be managed. He'll hear that his energy is a disorder, that his aggression is
toxic, that his wanting to build and climb and fight and win is something to be
medicated or lectured out of him. Some of that has a grain of truth in it. Most of it



Give Your Son a Name                                                                  259
is a world that doesn't know what boys are for, naming them out of fear.
   He's being called things by his own failures. Every boy fails, constantly, at
everything, because that's what learning is, and a boy with no one to tell him
otherwise will begin to believe the failures are his name, and the name sounds like
this: I can't, I'm bad at this, I always mess it up.
   And he may be getting called things at home, by you, that you don't hear
yourself saying: the sigh, the eye roll, "Why can't you be more like your sister."
"What is wrong with you." "You're just like your father," said by a mother who
didn't mean it kindly, or "you're just like your mother," said by you in anger, which
he'll carry. Nicknames that started as jokes and landed as verdicts. I sat with a man
in his fifties who could still tell me the exact word his father used for him at nine,
and his voice changed when he said it.
   Chapter 18 said that God gave men the power to name and that the world listens
when a man speaks. On a son, that power is nuclear. What you call him, he
becomes. What you don't call him, he'll go looking for somebody to call him, and
the somebodies who volunteer are coaches with agendas, older boys with worse
names, and a screen that will call him anything he wants to hear.

The three names

  Here's what a boy needs to hear from his father: three names, given over and
over, from the first week of his life to the last week of yours.
   The first name is mine, and it's the name of belonging. It says: you are my son,
and you're mine, and I'm not going anywhere. Before he's good at anything, before
he's earned anything, before he's a man or a success or anything else, he's yours, and
that's settled, and nothing he does can unsettle it. This is the name Jesus received
first, at the Jordan, before He'd done a single thing.

  Mark 1:11 (NIV)
  And a voice came from heaven: 'You are my Son, whom I love; with you I am well pleased.'



   You are my Son, whom I love, with you I am well pleased: three things in one
sentence, and the first of them is possession. Every boy needs to hear "mine" from
his father so many times that it stops being information and becomes the floor he
stands on. A boy who knows he belongs can fail at anything and come home. A boy


260                                                                               Give Your Son a Name
who isn't sure he belongs will spend his life auditioning, and we spent chapter 8 on
what that does to a man.
  The second name is what I see in you. That's the name of character, and it's
about who he is rather than what he did. When your son tells the truth about the
broken window, you say, "That's honesty. You're an honest man." When he stands
up for the kid getting picked on, you say, "That's courage. You've got courage in
you." When he helps his mother without being asked, you say, "You're a protector.
That's what protectors do." When he keeps going on something hard, you say, "You
don't quit. That's who you are."
    You're doing two things when you say that. You're catching him being what God
made him and naming it so he knows what it's called. And you're speaking the name
a little ahead of the boy, prophetically, so that he has something to grow into. He
isn't fully honest yet; no boy is. But a boy who has been called honest by his father a
hundred times will feel a lie sit wrong in his mouth in a way that a boy who has only
been called a liar never will. Paul described his own fathering in a sentence I'd put
on every father's bathroom mirror.

  1 Thessalonians 2:11-12 (NIV)
  For you know that we dealt with each of you as a father deals with his own children, encouraging,
  comforting and urging you to live lives worthy of God, who calls you into his kingdom and glory.



  Encouraging, comforting, and urging. That's the second name in three verbs.
Encouraging says, I see it in you. Comforting says, when you fail, you're still mine.
Urging says, now live up to what I see. A father who only urges, without
encouraging and comforting, produces a boy who can never rest. A father who only
comforts, without urging, produces a boy who never grows. All three, over and
over, is how a son gets named.
   The third name is man. That's the handoff word, and it's the one chapter 11 said
most men never got. At some point, and in a way he'll remember, your son needs to
hear his father say: you are becoming a man, and here is what that means. Not
"you're the man of the house now" tossed over a shoulder on the way out the door.
A deliberate, weighted, spoken word from a father to a son that says, I see you
crossing over, and I'm going to tell you what's on the other side, and I'm going to
walk you across.
   The next two chapters are about how to do that with a hill and a day. Here I only


Give Your Son a Name                                                                                  261
want to say that the word itself is a name, and the boy needs it from you. Every boy
is asking whether he's a man yet. He'll ask it of a girl, a fight, a paycheck, a truck, if
his father doesn't answer it first. Answer it, on purpose and out loud.

How to give a name

   Naming isn't a speech. It's a habit, and here's the habit. Every day, catch him and
call it. Once a day, find one thing your son did that a man does and name it. "That's
what a man does." Roy said that sentence to a thirty-year-old at a breakfast table and
the man came back every week for a year. Imagine what it does to a nine-year-old
from his own father. It takes five seconds, and it's the highest-return five seconds in
fathering.
   Every week, give the blessing. Pick a night; Sunday works. Put your hand on his
head and say three sentences. Who he is: "You're my son and I love you and I'm not
going anywhere." What you see: "This week I saw you tell the truth when it cost
you. That's courage." What God says: "God made you on purpose and He's got
work for you, and I'm going to help you find it." Then a short prayer. It will feel
strange the first three times, and you keep going anyway, because boys who get
blessed weekly grow up with a floor under them that nothing can take out.

  Deuteronomy 6:6-7 (NIV)
  These commandments that I give you today are to be on your hearts. Impress them on your children.
  Talk about them when you sit at home and when you walk along the road, when you lie down and
  when you get up.



   When you lie down and when you get up. The blessing isn't a ceremony. It's
what you say at bedtime, in the truck, at breakfast. Impress them, the text says, and
that's a word about pressure over time, like a mark pressed into leather. That's how
names get given.
   Every year, write the letter. On his birthday, write your son a letter of one page.
Who he is this year, what you've watched him become, what you see coming, and
that you love him. Put it in a box. Give him the box when he's eighteen, or at the
day we'll talk about in chapter 29. A man with eighteen letters from his father, in his
father's handwriting, telling him who he is, can walk into any room on earth.
   In public, praise him; in private, correct him. When he's done well, tell people in



262                                                                               Give Your Son a Name
front of him: his grandparents, his coach, your friends at church. "This is my son.
He finished the whole fence with me Saturday and didn't quit." Let him hear his
father brag. When he's done wrong, take him aside. Never name his failure in front
of others. Shame in public is a name that sticks, and praise in public is a name that
lifts.
   When he fails, separate the act from the name. This is the one that saves boys.
Suppose he told a lie. You say, "That was a lie. You are not a liar. You're my son,
who told a lie, and here's what my son does next: he goes and tells the truth to the
person he lied to." The act gets named accurately, and the boy gets named
accurately, and they aren't the same name. A father who says "you're a liar" has
handed his son a name he'll either fight or fulfill. A father who says "you're my
honest son who did a dishonest thing" has handed him a way back.
   And always, tell him whose he is. Under your name is a bigger one. Tell your son
he was made by God on purpose. Tell him the Father who does not leave is his
Father too. Tell him what God calls him: made in the image, loved, called. Teach
him to pray "Father" the way you learned in chapter 10. Your name over him is
good and it isn't enough; you'll fail him and you'll die. God's name over him is the
one that holds, and the best thing your name can do is point at that one.

What a boy does with a name

   Cole's father never named him, not once in thirty-three years of criticism, and
the boy grew into a man who was competent and hollow, always waiting for a
sentence that didn't come. Then a seventy-year-old deacon said "you're a good man"
by a truck, and Cole sat in the cab and cried for twenty minutes. It was one
sentence, one name from a man who wasn't even his father, and it fixed something
that thirty-three years hadn't.
   That's how hungry boys are for this, and that's how much power your words
have over your son. If a stranger's sentence can do that to a grown man, think what
a father's daily naming does to a boy who is still soft enough to take the impression.
  Cole has a son now, three years old. He names him every night. "I'm not going to
make him wait thirty-three years to hear it from a stranger," he said.

Naming in every season




Give Your Son a Name                                                                   263
   If you have a baby boy, start now. He doesn't understand the words yet, but he
understands the voice. Hold him and say "you're my son" over him every day, and
talk to him, and pray over him out loud. New fathers often feel useless with an
infant because they can't feed him and they don't know what to do, and here's what
to do: name him. The words seep in before comprehension does, and a boy who has
heard his father's voice say "mine" ten thousand times before he's two has been
named in a way he will never be able to un-hear.
   If you're divorced, your son may be getting other names at another house, from a
stepfather, or from his mother's grief, or from the silence about you. You can't
control that. You can control the names he gets from you, and consistency is
everything. The Tuesday call is where you name him. The weekend is where you
name him. Never make him a messenger or a spy between houses; that gives him a
name nobody wants. Tell him he's not in the middle. Tell him he's yours and that
isn't up for negotiation no matter where he sleeps on Wednesday. Kenny's son said
"nothing" for eight months on the Tuesday calls, and Kenny ended every single one
of them with the same sentence: "You're my son. I love you. I'll call you Tuesday."
That's naming, and it worked.
   If you're an older man, you have grandsons and you have grown sons, and both
need naming. A grandfather's "that's what a man does" carries a weight that a
father's sometimes can't, because the boy knows Grandpa has seen a lot of men, so
use it. And if your grown son never got the third name from you, if you never told
him he was a man and what that meant, it isn't too late. Roy told his son at
forty-four, on a porch, that he was proud of the man he'd become and that he was
sorry he'd never said so when it would have been easier to hear. His son didn't say
much. Three weeks later he called and asked Roy to come teach his grandson to
fish.
   And if you're a single man, or a man with no sons, there are boys in your church
and on your street with no man to name them. Coach them, teach them, show up
for them, and when you see one of them do what a man does, say so, out loud, in
front of the others. You have no idea what one sentence from an unrelated man can
do to a fatherless boy, and if you doubt it, ask Cole.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
  A son of fifteen needs the third name most, the one that says he's becoming a
man and here's what that means, and he needs the second name spoken in front of


264                                                                Give Your Son a Name
other men. Cole's son is three, but Cole told me he's already planning the sentence
for fifteen. The takeaway for the father of a teenager is that a boy who's called
honest by his father a hundred times feels a lie sit wrong in his mouth, and that the
naming has to be louder in the years when the world's names get loudest.
   A man of forty-five with a son of seventeen is often locked in a standoff, and the
takeaway for him is to separate the act from the name: the boy did a dishonest thing,
and he is not a liar, and here is what my son does next. That sentence has saved more
teenage boys than any lecture I know.

The Name that holds

  Every name you give your son is a small copy of the Name God gives His sons:
mine, what I see in you, man. God says all three over every man who's in Christ, and
He said them over you, whether or not your father ever did.

  Psalm 127:3 (NIV)
  Children are a heritage from the LORD, offspring a reward from him.



   A reward is what the psalm calls him. Your son is God's reward to you, and
you're God's gift to your son, and the gift you give him back is his name. There's a
boy in your house who's asking who he is. He asks every day, in every mess and
every win, in the way he watches your face, and you get to answer him. Give him
the first weight worth carrying: a name, from his father, that he can stand on for the
rest of his life.

Before you turn the page

   If you're the son who never got a name, and you've read this chapter with a stone
in your chest, I want you to know that you can give what you didn't get, and that
giving it does something in you that receiving it would have. Cole didn't wait for his
father to change before he started naming his own boy. And if you've been giving
your son the sigh and the eye roll without hearing yourself, the fix starts tonight
with a hand on his head and three sentences. May the Father who called His Son
beloved before He'd done a thing put the same words in your mouth, and may your
son grow up with a floor under him that nothing can take out.




Give Your Son a Name                                                               265
  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because a boy hears what his father says without meaning to as clearly as what he
  says on purpose: say what your son has actually been hearing from you, the three
  names or the sigh and the eye roll and the verdicts; and if you're the son who never
  got a name, say that too.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because naming is a daily habit before it's anything else: every day before Tuesday,
  catch your son doing one thing a man does and name it, "that's honesty," "that's
  courage"; then pick a night and give the three-sentence blessing with your hand on
  his head. If you have no son, find one fatherless boy and name one thing you saw
  him do.
  TELL A BROTHER
  Because fathers need to say out loud what they've been calling their sons: tell him the
  names you've been giving without meaning to, ask what his father called him, and
  ask what he's calling his own son.
  CARRY THIS
  What you call him, he becomes. If you don't name your son, the world will, and the
  world has no good names for boys.




266                                                                    Give Your Son a Name
CHAPTER 28



Give Your Son a Hill
   Part 4: The house
   Hollis was twelve the day his father sent him up the caprock alone after a stray
calf.
   They'd been checking fence in the truck when they spotted it, way up in the
rocks where the pasture broke off into a wall of caliche and cedar. His father
stopped the truck, looked at the hill for a minute, and said, "Go get him." Not "let's
go get him." Go. Hollis remembers standing at the bottom looking up and being
certain he couldn't. The calf was scared and half wild, the rocks were loose, there
were rattlesnakes in that country, and he was twelve years old.
   "I looked back at the truck," he told me, sixty years later. "He was just sitting
there with the window down, watching. He didn't get out."
   It took Hollis an hour and a half, and he fell twice, and he got the calf cornered
against a ledge and got a rope on it and got kicked in the thigh and got it down the
hill anyway, and when he walked up to the truck with the calf on a lead and his
jeans torn and blood on his shin, his father looked at him for a long moment and
said, "Well. I figured you could."
   "I knew he was watching the whole time," Hollis said. "He never got out of the
truck. That was the point. If he'd come, I'd have learned I couldn't do it without
him. He stayed in the truck, and I learned I could."
  Give your son a hill. That's this chapter, because the last one was about the name
you give him, and a name without a hill is only a compliment. A boy becomes what
you called him by climbing something that proves it.

Boys need difficulty



Give Your Son a Hill                                                                   267
   Here's a thing about boys that the culture has mostly forgotten and every father
knows in his bones. Boys don't become men by being protected from difficulty.
They become men by being handed difficulty that's slightly beyond them, with a
man watching who doesn't rescue too soon. That's how it has always worked. Isaac
carried the wood up the mountain.

  Genesis 22:6 (NIV)
  Abraham took the wood for the burnt offering and placed it on his son Isaac, and he himself carried
  the fire and the knife. As the two of them went on together,



   Placed it on his son. There's a father giving a boy a weight to carry up a hill.
Abraham carried the fire and the knife, the dangerous things, and gave his son the
load, and the two of them went on together: a father and a son, one carrying and
one watching, going up. David's father gave him the sheep. That doesn't sound like
much until you read what David did with them.

  1 Samuel 17:34-35 (NIV)
  But David said to Saul, 'Your servant has been keeping his father's sheep. When a lion or a bear
  came and carried off a sheep from the flock, I went after it, struck it and rescued the sheep from its
  mouth.'



   I went after it. A teenage boy alone in the hills with his father's flock, and a lion
comes, and he goes after it. Nobody sent him a lion. But his father sent him to the
hills with something real to keep, and the lion found him there, and by the time
Goliath showed up David had already been up a few hills. He told Saul as much.
The lion and the bear were the training, and the sheep were the hill his father gave
him.
   And Jesus grew up in a carpenter's shop. We don't have the details, but we have
the word: the boy who spoke the world into being spent His growing years with
tools in His hands under His father's eye, and Luke says He grew in wisdom and
stature. That's a boy with a hill.
  Now look at what happens to a boy without one. He's comfortable and he's
protected, and every difficulty gets removed before it reaches him, every failure gets
cushioned, every risk gets managed by adults who love him, and he's starving,
because boys are built for hills, and a boy who isn't given one will go find one, and



268                                                                                      Give Your Son a Hill
the hills he finds on his own are almost always worse: the fight, the dare, the video
game with ten thousand hills in it that cost nothing and build nothing, the drugs,
the recklessness in a truck at seventeen. Or he'll do the other thing, which is quieter
and which I see more of now. He'll never leave the basement, because nobody ever
taught him he could climb anything, and the whole world looks like caprock with
no truck at the bottom.

What a hill is

   Let me define it, because a hill isn't a chore chart and it isn't a travel ball schedule.
   A hill is real. It matters to somebody besides him. The family depends on its
getting done, or a person is actually helped, or something actually gets built. Boys
can smell a made-up job from across the yard. Feeding the dog is real if the dog
doesn't eat otherwise. Sweeping a clean garage to "learn responsibility" is a lie, and
he knows it.
  A hill is slightly beyond him. Not what he can already do, and not what a man
can do; the next thing. The calf was beyond a twelve-year-old and only barely not,
and that's the zone. Too easy and he learns nothing. Too hard and he learns that he
can't. The father's whole skill is finding the next hill, and it's a skill you acquire by
watching your son closely, which is chapter 22 applied to a boy.
   A hill has real risk, bounded. He can fail, and he can get hurt a little, and he can
lose money, break the thing, come down without the calf. If there's no way to fail, it
isn't a hill; it's a ride. But the risk is bounded by a father who has chosen the hill
with care. Hollis's father knew that hill. He knew the snakes were mostly gone that
time of year. He was in the truck. Bounded risk is how a boy learns courage without
learning recklessness.
   A hill has an outcome he owns, and I mean outcome rather than effort. "I tried"
is what a boy says. "It's done," or "it isn't done yet, and here's what I'm doing," is
what a man says, and he learns to say it on hills where nobody accepts "I tried." The
calf came down or it didn't.
   And a hill is followed by a name. When he comes down, whatever happened, the
father speaks. "I figured you could." That's the name from chapter 27 landing on
the hill from chapter 28, and together they make something a compliment alone
never can. He was called capable, and then he climbed, and now he knows it's true.



Give Your Son a Hill                                                                     269
The hills, by age

  Fathers ask me what hills look like at what age, so here's the range I've seen work.
Your son is his own boy; adjust.
   From four to six, real jobs a small boy can do that actually matter. Carrying the
groceries in, all of them, even when it takes four trips. Feeding the animal that
depends on it. Handing you tools and learning their names. Picking up the whole
yard before the mower. He isn't helping; he's working, and you should tell him so.
   From seven to ten, ownership. Something that's his to keep alive or keep
working: a garden bed, a pet's whole life, the trash on trash day without reminders, a
small tool set he's responsible for. First real tools, with instruction and then with
trust. First fire, built himself, with you a step back. First time doing a whole task
alone from start to finish while you're somewhere else. The hill at this age is "yours,
without me standing over you."
    From eleven to thirteen, a real job with real money: mowing yards, hauling hay, a
summer working for a man who isn't you. A physical challenge that scares him a
little: the long hike, the first hunt, the build he isn't sure he can finish. Caring for
someone weaker: a younger sibling for an afternoon, the widow's yard without pay.
And at least one hill he climbs alone with you in the truck, which is his caprock.
   From fourteen to seventeen, work for someone else who'll fire him if he's lazy.
Money he earns and manages and loses some of. A project he owns from the idea to
the finish: rebuild the engine, build the deer stand, run the fundraiser. Hardship he
chooses and endures: the mission trip somewhere hard, the cold camp, the fast. A
decision with consequences that he makes and you don't override. The hill at this
age is "a man's work with a man watching from farther off."
   And from eighteen onward, the launch, which was chapter 12. He leaves,
without slamming the door, and the hill is his own life, and you're a man he can call
rather than a man who climbs it for him.
   Notice what isn't on that list. There's nothing about trophies. A boy can play
sports for a decade and never climb a hill if the sport is his father's ego and every
failure gets explained away by the coach's bad call. And there's nothing about
screens, because a screen is a machine for simulating hills without the weight. Ten
thousand levels beaten, and not one calf brought down.



270                                                                    Give Your Son a Hill
The father's part

    Give it, and don't do it. Your instinct will be to do the job yourself because
you're faster and better and it's easier. Every time you do, you've stolen a hill. Hand
it over and walk away.
  Watch, and don't rescue. Stay in the truck, and let him struggle, and let him fall
twice, because this is the hardest thing in fathering, and mothers usually find it
harder than fathers do, so you're going to have to lead here, gently, and explain why.
Rescuing a boy who isn't in danger teaches him he needs rescue. Watching a boy
who isn't in danger teaches him he doesn't.
   Let him fail small so that he doesn't fail big. A boy who's allowed to lose forty
dollars at twelve because he didn't plan learns something that will save him forty
thousand at thirty. A boy who's never allowed to fail at anything fails for the first
time at twenty-three with a wife and a mortgage and nobody in the truck.
   Debrief him afterward. When he comes down, ask what happened, what he
learned, what he'd do differently. Not a lecture; questions, and then waiting. He's
teaching himself with your help.
  Name what he did. "I figured you could." "That's what a man does." "You didn't
quit." Chapter 27. Every hill ends with a name, or it's only a hard day.
   And raise the hill. Next time, a little more. That's the whole method. A boy who
climbs a slightly bigger hill every year for fifteen years is a man by eighteen, not
because you told him so but because he has the calluses to prove it.

  Hebrews 12:11 (NIV)
  No discipline seems pleasant at the time, but painful. Later on, however, it produces a harvest of
  righteousness and peace for those who have been trained by it.



   Trained by it, the writer says, and that's what hills are: training. It isn't pleasant
for him, and it isn't pleasant for you watching from the truck. Later on, a harvest.

How fathers get it wrong

   Some fathers give no hill at all. Comfort, protection, and a screen. The boy is
loved and safe, and he has never once been asked to do something he wasn't sure he


Give Your Son a Hill                                                                                   271
could do. He'll be thirty and living in your basement, or thirty and taking stupid
risks to feel something, and both are boys who were never handed a calf.
   Some fathers give too big a hill too fast. The father reads a book about raising
men and hands his nine-year-old a man's job and crushes him. Then the boy learns
that the hill is a place where he fails, and he stops climbing. Slightly beyond, not a
mountain.
   Some fathers give their own hill instead of the son's. The sport the father wanted
to play. The business the father wants to hand down. The hill has to be real for the
boy, in the direction of who the boy is, and not a monument to who the father
wishes he'd been.
  Some fathers rescue, finishing his science project at midnight, calling the coach,
paying off the debt he ran up. Every rescue is a message: you can't. Stay in the truck.
   And some fathers shame the fall. He fails and you make him pay for it with
contempt. Now the hill is a place of fear, and he'll avoid every hill he can for the rest
of his life. Hollis fell twice on the caprock. His father didn't say a word about the
falls. He said, "I figured you could."

Hills in every season

   If you have a baby boy, the hill is years away, but the habit starts now. Don't do
for him what he can do. Let him struggle to sit up before you prop him. Let him
work at the toy before you fix it. Fathers of infants learn to rescue reflexively
because a crying baby is unbearable, and some of that is right. But a father who
catches himself doing everything for his son at six months is a father who'll be doing
everything for him at sixteen. Watch a little, from the truck, even now.
   If you're divorced, watch the weekend trap. It's easy to make your time with your
son all fun, pizza and movies and the lake and no chores, because you feel guilty and
you want him to want to come. That's a boy with no hill at your house. He needs
work at your place: his own jobs, his own responsibilities, a project you build
together across weekends so that it's always waiting for him when he comes back.
Kenny and his son built a smoker over eleven weekends. "It was the only thing at my
house that was ours," he said. "He couldn't wait to get back to it. Not because it was
fun. Because it wasn't done."
  If you're an older man, you give hills better than anyone alive. You have time,



272                                                                     Give Your Son a Hill
tools, a shop, and patience the boy's father doesn't have. Roy's grandson learned to
fish from Roy, and then to clean the fish, and then to gut a deer, and then to change
the oil on Roy's truck, and the boy's father told me his son comes home from
Grandpa's "taller." Grandfathers hand out hills that fathers are too busy or too
anxious to give. That's a job, and it's yours.
   If you're a single man, or a man with no sons, there are boys in your church who
have never been up a hill because there was nobody in the truck. Coach them, and
coach them hard. Take one to work with you. Run the church workday and give
the fatherless boys the real jobs. Hollis's father sent him up the caprock; some boys
need an unrelated man to point at a hill and say "go," because their fathers never did,
and you can be that man.
   And a word about daughters, because this chapter has a son's name on it. Your
daughter needs hills too, because she isn't fragile. She needs real work, real challenge,
real ownership, and a father who doesn't rescue her from every difficulty and who
names her strong when she comes down. The hills may look different, but the
method doesn't.
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A son of sixteen needs a hill that's a man's work with a man watching from
farther off: a job that will fire him, a project he owns to the end, a decision his father
doesn't override. The takeaway for the father of a teenager is that the hardest part of
this age is the distance; he has to move the truck farther from the hill and stay in it
anyway.
   A man of forty-five is often the father who has given no hill at all, out of love and
out of a culture that removes every difficulty, and his son is comfortable and
starving. The takeaway for him is that the boy in the basement and the boy taking
stupid risks in a truck are the same boy, and that both needed a calf to bring down.

Well, I figured you could

   Hollis has given a lot of hills in sixty years. To his own sons, to grandsons, to the
boys in his Sunday school class, to Cole by a truck. I asked him once what the secret
was, and he thought about it and said, "Pick the right hill. Stay in the truck. Say the
right thing when he comes down." Then he laughed and said, "And don't tell his
mother about the snakes." Paul told a young man he'd fathered in the faith to


Give Your Son a Hill                                                                   273
expect hills for the rest of his life.

  2 Timothy 2:3 (NIV)
  Join with me in suffering, like a good soldier of Christ Jesus.



   Like a good soldier. Not a protected boy, but a man who has been trained on
hills and can take the weight. That's what you're building. Not a boy who had a
good childhood, but a man who can carry a calf down a caprock when nobody
comes.
  Your son is standing at the bottom looking up. He isn't sure he can. He's looking
back at the truck.
   Roll the window down, and watch him, and stay where you are.

Before you turn the page

   Staying in the truck is the hardest thing in fathering, and if you've been getting
out of it for years, I don't want you to feel like you've ruined your son. Boys are
resilient, and a hill handed to a fifteen-year-old who never had one still counts, and
so does one handed to a twenty-five-year-old. The Father who put the wood on
Isaac's back and walked up the mountain beside him knows exactly how it feels to
watch a son carry something heavy. May He give you the nerve to pick the right hill
this week, and the harder nerve to roll the window down and stay, and may you get
to say "I figured you could" when he comes down.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because a boy with no hill is invisible to a father who loves him: say whether your
  son has one right now, something real and slightly beyond him with an outcome he
  owns, and if he doesn't, say whether it's because you've been protecting him or
  doing it yourself.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because the whole method is one hill at a time: hand your son one hill before
  Tuesday, scaled to his age, then give it and walk away and don't rescue, and when he
  comes down, ask what he learned and name what you saw. If you have no son, give a
  fatherless boy a real job and stay in the truck.


274                                                                   Give Your Son a Hill
TELL A BROTHER
Because fathers help each other find the right size: tell him the hill you gave and how
hard it was not to rescue, and ask what hills his father gave him and what hills he's giving
now.
CARRY THIS
A name without a hill is only a compliment. Pick the right hill. Stay in the truck. Say the
right thing when he comes down.




Give Your Son a Hill                                                                     275
276   Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget
CHAPTER 29



Give Your Son a Day He Will Not
Forget
   Part 4: The house
  Roy's grandson turned thirteen in October, and on a Saturday two weeks later
four men picked him up before dawn.
   He knew something was coming. His father had told him to be dressed and ready
at 5:30 and hadn't said why. Roy drove, and Hollis sat in the back with the boy's
father and an uncle who had come in from Amarillo the night before, and nobody
said much. They drove out past town to a stretch of pasture where Roy and the boy
had rebuilt a fence line together over the summer, and they parked, and they walked
the fence to the far corner post as the sky started to go gray, and they stood there.
   Roy read Genesis 2:15 out loud. To work it and take care of it. Then each man
spoke. The uncle told the boy about the worst mistake he'd ever made and what a
man does afterward. Hollis told him about the caprock and the calf. His father told
him the three names, the ones he'd been saying weekly for years, but this time he
said them in front of other men with his hand on the boy's shoulder, and then he
said the fourth one. "You're becoming a man. That's what today is. And here's what
that means." And he said the sentence. A real man rejects passivity, accepts
responsibility, leads courageously, and expects the greater reward, God's reward. He
handed the boy an index card with it written in his own hand.
   Then Roy reached into his coat and pulled out a brass plumb bob, about four
inches long and heavy for its size, with the boy's name and the date engraved on it,
and handed it to him on its string. And he told him what it was for.
   The boy has carried it every day since. His father says he's seen him take it out of
his pocket in the school parking lot and simply hold it.



Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget                                               277
   That's what this chapter is about. Chapter 11 told you most men never got
handed off, and spent their lives waiting for a sentence that never came. Don't let
your son wait. Give him a day. Not a party, but a marker, with men, and a word,
and a weight, and a welcome. A day he can point to for the rest of his life and say:
that's when.

Why a day

   God builds markers into His people, and He always has. When Israel crossed the
Jordan into the land, Joshua had twelve men carry twelve stones out of the riverbed
and stack them on the bank, and he told them why.

  Joshua 4:6-7 (NIV)
  to serve as a sign among you. In the future, when your children ask you, 'What do these stones mean?'
  tell them that the flow of the Jordan was cut off before the ark of the covenant of the LORD. When it
  crossed the Jordan, the waters of the Jordan were cut off. These stones are to be a memorial to the people
  of Israel forever.



   When your children ask you what these stones mean. God wanted a pile of rocks
on a riverbank so that a boy fifty years later would ask his father a question and get a
story. Jacob set up a stone at Bethel. Samuel raised one after a battle and named it.
Every Passover, a father answered a child's question about why that night was
different. Jesus stood in a river and heard His Father's voice out loud in front of
witnesses before He started His work. Markers, days, stones a man can point to.
   Some traditions still do this for boys. Most American Christian families don't.
We have birthdays, which mark time, and graduations, which mark school, and we
have no day at all that marks the thing that matters most: the day a boy's father and
the men around him say, on purpose and out loud, you are a man now, and here is
what that means, and you're one of us.
    So the boy guesses. He picks his own marker from whatever is available: the first
time with a girl, the first drunk, the first fight, the license, the truck. Chapter 11
called those counterfeits, and they are, and the reason he reaches for them is that
nobody gave him the real one. Give him the real one. Give him a stone he can point
to.




278                                                                  Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget
The design

   I'm going to lay out the shape of a day. It isn't the only shape. It's one that works,
that I've watched work, and that you can bend to your own family. Don't make it
more complicated than this, and don't make it less.
  The when is somewhere between twelve and fourteen, as he's starting to cross.
You'll know it: his voice, his shoulders, the way he has begun to watch men. If you
missed that window, do it anyway, because seventeen is fine and twenty-five is fine.
A man of forty who never got a day can be given one by his father, and it will still do
something, and I've seen it.
   The who is the father and two to four men the boy knows and respects and who
will be in his life going forward: a grandfather, an uncle, a coach who's a real man,
your brother from the twenty minutes, a man from church who has watched him
grow. Not a crowd, because a crowd makes it a performance. Four or five men
who'll still be around in ten years to say "remember the fence line." If you have no
men, this is why chapters 13 and 14 came first, and you have a year to go get them.
  The where is outside, early, somewhere with weight: a hill he has climbed, a fence
you built together, the stock tank at first light, the deer lease, the church before
anyone else arrives. Not a restaurant, and not a living room. The place should ask
something of him just to get there, even if it's only a walk in the cold and dark.
   Before the day, he should know something's coming but not what. The father
meets with the men beforehand, once, and asks each one to prepare one true thing:
a story about what a man is from their own life, one failure and what they did
afterward, and one charge for the boy. Five minutes each and not more. The father
prepares the three names and the fourth word. If you've been writing the birthday
letters from chapter 27, this is when the box gets handed over.
  And the shape of the morning goes like this. You arrive by going somewhere. Up
something, along something. A small hill to echo all the hills you've given him,
walked in quiet, so that the weight of it settles on him.
   One man reads the Word. Genesis 2:15 for the work and the keeping. Joshua 1:9
for courage. 1 Kings 2:2-3 for the handoff, David to Solomon. Or this, which I've
heard read at more of these mornings than any other verse.




Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget                                                279
  1 Corinthians 16:13 (NIV)
  Be on your guard; stand firm in the faith; be courageous; be strong.



   Four commands and four marks in a single breath, and it should be read slowly.
The men speak, each in turn. The story, the failure, the charge. The boy stands
there and takes it. He'll remember every word for the rest of his life; men have told
me at sixty what a man said to them at thirteen on a morning like this.
   The father gives the four marks. He says the sentence, the locked one, and walks
through each mark in a sentence or two, and hands the boy the card in his own
handwriting. He tells him what each mark looks like on a Tuesday. He tells him
which one he himself is weakest at. He tells him they'll be working on them
together.
   The father gives the name. He puts his hand on his son's shoulder and gives the
three names in front of the men, mine and what I see in you, and then the fourth:
you are becoming a man. He says what that means. He says that he'll walk him
across. If you've never said it before, you'll probably cry. Let them see it. A boy who
watches his father cry while naming him learns something about men that no dry
ceremony could teach.
   Then comes the token. Give him something he'll carry for sixty years, not
expensive but weighted: a knife, a Bible with the date inside, his great-grandfather's
watch, a tool. Roy chose a plumb bob, and I'll tell you why in a moment, but the
point is that it's a physical thing he can hold in a parking lot at fifteen when he has
forgotten everything else about the morning. The stones on the Jordan were only
stones. Their whole job was to be there when he needed to remember.
   Then comes the prayer. Every man puts a hand on him and every man prays,
short and out loud. The father prays last, and asks the Father who does not leave to
be this boy's Father in every place his earthly one can't reach.
   And then the welcome. Somebody says it plainly: "You're one of us now." Then
you go eat, and at breakfast, for the first time, the men talk to him like a man. They
ask his opinion, and they tell him things, and he notices.

The plumb bob

  Roy chose the token because of chapter 17, and here is roughly what he told his


280                                                                Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget
grandson at the corner post with the sun coming up. I asked him afterward to say it
again so that I'd get it right.
   "This is a plumb bob. Your great-grandfather used one like it to build the barn
that's still standing. You hang it on this string next to whatever you're building, and
you wait for it to stop swinging, and when it settles it points straight down. Every
time. It doesn't care what the wall looks like. It doesn't care what you think is
straight. Gravity doesn't have opinions.
    "You're going to build a lot of things in your life. Some of them are going to be
crooked, and you won't be able to tell, because everything around them is crooked
too. When that happens, you hang this and you wait. God's Word is your plumb
line. It doesn't move. When you can't tell what's straight anymore, you hold your
life up next to it and let it settle and see where you're off.
   "And here's the other thing. This is heavy for its size, and that's on purpose,
because a light bob swings forever. A heavy one settles fast. A man's supposed to be
like that, steady and weighted and hard to knock off true. Your name's on this so
you remember who you're supposed to be when you're not sure."
   The boy's father told me his son has put that plumb bob next to more than one
decision already. Whether to go along with something at school. Whether to tell the
truth about a grade. He takes it out, holds it, and watches it settle. It's a piece of
brass. It's also the whole book, in his pocket.
  Choose your own token. But choose one that says something, and say the
something out loud when you hand it over.

The day after

   Now the part that matters more than the day, and it's the reason this chapter sits
in a book with a Tuesday spine.
   The ceremony changes nothing by itself. I've watched fathers put everything into
the morning and then go back on Monday to treating their son like a boy, and the
son learns that the day was a nice thing his dad said once, like a birthday card. What
makes the day real is that starting Tuesday, the men treat him differently. He's given
more hill. He's asked his opinion at the table and it's actually weighed. He's handed
a man's jobs. He's invited, eventually, into the twenty minutes with his father and a
brother, and he's expected to answer "what's heavy" like a man. He's corrected like a



Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget                                              281
man, privately and with respect. He's trusted with things, and when he fails he's
held to a man's standard and given a man's grace.
  That's what a handoff is. The day is the marker. The Tuesdays are the handoff. If
you give him the day and not the Tuesdays, you've given him a stone with no story.
  And there's another day-after I need to say something about, because this book
has men in it who have turned in an actual badge.
   Roy retired from thirty-one years of wearing one, and he told me the day of the
retirement ceremony was the best day of his career and the Tuesday after it was the
worst day of his life. Because the ceremony said "well done" and then Tuesday said
nothing at all, because nobody needed him and there was no assignment. He sat by
the phone like a rookie, as he put it, until he got up and went to find the work.
   Every man who has had a marker knows something about this. The wedding is a
marker; the Tuesday after is the marriage. Baptism is a marker; the Tuesday after is
the walk. The retreat where you finally said yes to God is a marker; the Tuesday
after is when you find out whether you meant it. Markers matter, and God builds
them in. But they're stones on a riverbank, not the land. The land is Tuesday. The
land is always Tuesday. Roy knew that when he handed his grandson the plumb
bob, and that's why the first thing he did the following week was call the boy and
ask him to come help rebuild the north gate. The day said "you're a man." Tuesday
made him one.

If you never had a day

   Some of you are reading this with a stone in your throat because nobody did this
for you, and you're not sure you can give what you didn't get. Hear me on this: you
can, and giving it will heal something in you that receiving it would have.
   I've watched fathers who were never named stand at a corner post and name their
sons, and break down halfway through, and finish anyway, and drive home
different men, which is what chapter 11 promised. The handoff you give your son
hands something off to you too. You get to say the sentence you never heard, and
hearing it in your own voice does something the Father has been waiting to do in
you for forty years.
   If you want it to be complete, do this: ask one of the older men at your son's day
to say something to you afterward, privately. Let him put a hand on your shoulder



282                                                   Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget
and tell you that you're a man and you did right by your boy. It will take him thirty
seconds. It isn't too late for you either.

The day in every season

   If you're divorced, you can still do this, and you should. Coordinate with his
mother respectfully; tell her what it is and why, and let her know it's about the boy
and not about the two of you. If there's a stepfather in his life who's a good man,
consider inviting him. I know how hard that is, and it's also one of the most
honorable things a divorced father can do, and your son will never forget that you
did it. If his mother resists, do it on your time, with your men, and keep the tone
clean. Kenny gave his son a day at fourteen at a cabin on Lake Buchanan with three
men from his church and his own father. His ex-wife wasn't sure about it
beforehand. Afterward the boy came home, and she told Kenny, "Whatever you
did, do more of it."
  If you're a single man, you may be one of the men at somebody else's day, and
you should be ready for it. Have your one true thing. And there may be a fatherless
boy in your life whose mother would weep with gratitude if you and three other
men gave her son a day, so ask her, and do it with her blessing and in the light. You
may give a boy a day he'll never forget before you have a son of your own.
   If you're an older man, grandfathers often lead these because fathers aren't sure
how, and you're the one who should offer. You have the standing. You have the
token; there's something in a drawer of yours right now that a boy would carry for
sixty years. And you have the time to make the Tuesdays after mean something.
Roy's day for his grandson was Roy's idea. His son told me he'd wanted to do it for
two years and hadn't known where to start, and his father said, "I do. Let me help."
   If your son is an infant, the day is thirteen years off, and the letters start now, and
so do the Tuesdays. A boy who has been named weekly and given hills yearly walks
up to his day already knowing most of what he'll hear. The day just makes it official.
   And a word for fathers of daughters. She needs a day too. It's a different shape:
her mother and grandmother should be there, it's often a dinner rather than a dawn,
the token is often a ring or a necklace, and the word is about who she is and what
she's worth and what she should expect from a man. But the bones are the same: a
marker, people who love her, a word spoken on purpose, a weight she can hold.
Don't give your son a day and leave your daughter guessing.


Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget                                                 283
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A boy of thirteen is the one the day was designed for, and Roy's grandson is
standing at the corner post with a plumb bob in his hand. The takeaway for the
father of a boy that age is that the window is now, that the men need to be asked a
year out, and that the Tuesdays after are what make the day real.
   A man of forty-five who never got a day can still give one, and Kenny's cabin at
Lake Buchanan with three men from church and his own father is the picture. The
takeaway for him is that giving it will heal something in him that receiving it would
have, and that asking one of the older men to say a sentence to him afterward is not
too much to ask.

What do these stones mean

    Roy's grandson will ask his own son someday what the plumb bob means. He'll
tell him about a fence line before dawn, and four men, and a card in his father's
handwriting, and a great-grandfather who used a tool like it to build a barn that's
still standing. And then, if the men did their job, he'll give the boy a day.
  That's how it's supposed to work. A pile of stones on a riverbank, and a father
with a story, and a boy who knows where he came from and what he's for and who's
watching.
  Give your son a day he will not forget. Then give him the Tuesday after.

Before you turn the page

   If you never got a day, and I know a great many of you didn't, I want to say to
you what I'd want said to me: it isn't too late, and the day you give your son will
hand something back to you that you've been waiting forty years for. Stones on a
riverbank are only rocks without a story, and you're the one who gets to tell it. May
the Father who set stones in the Jordan give you the men and the morning and the
nerve to say the fourth name out loud, and may your son carry something heavy in
his pocket for sixty years that reminds him who he's supposed to be.




284                                                   Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget
   YOUR TURN



   NAME IT
   Because a man who never got a day tends to let his son guess too: say whether you
   got one, and if not, what you picked as a marker instead; then say whether your son
   has one coming or whether you've been letting him guess.
   Do this year. Because a day takes planning, this week's line stretches to a year: pick the
   date, the men, the place, and the token, write the three names and the fourth, and put
   it on the calendar before Tuesday even if the day is eleven months out.
   TELL A BROTHER
   Because the men at the fence line have to be asked: ask him to be one of them, tell
   him what to prepare, one story, one failure, one charge, and if he has a son, ask when
   his day is and offer to stand at it.
   CARRY THIS
   The day is the marker. The Tuesdays are the handoff. A stone on a riverbank with
   no story is only a rock.




Give Your Son a Day He Will Not Forget                                                      285
286   Part 5
             PART 5


         The long road




Part 5                   287
288   You Were Made for a Work
CHAPTER 30



You Were Made for a Work
   Part 5: The long road
   There's a man in a town near me who runs the wastewater plant on the night
shift. Ten at night to six in the morning, four nights a week, for nineteen years.
Nobody in that town thinks about him. Nobody knows his name. Every one of
them flushes a toilet and turns on a tap and never once wonders who's awake at
three in the morning making sure the water that comes back to them won't kill
them.
   He's a believer, and he asked me once, after church, standing by his truck,
whether his work counted. He meant for God. He'd been sitting through years of
sermons about calling and ministry and the mission field, and he'd started to feel
that his real life was the twelve hours a week he spent at church, and that the forty
he spent at the plant were a kind of necessary evil, something he did to fund the part
that mattered.
   "I'm just running a plant," he said. "It's not like I'm doing anything for the
kingdom."
   I told him a whole town drinks clean water because he stays awake, and that God
had noticed before I did. But I've thought about his question for years, because
most men are asking it in one form or another. Does my work count? Is it part of
the thing, or is it the thing I do so that I can get to the thing?
   This is the fifth part of the book, the long road, and it starts here because most of
a man's road is work. Forty or fifty years of it. If your work doesn't count, most of
your life doesn't count, and I don't believe that, and neither does Scripture.

Work came before the curse




You Were Made for a Work                                                             289
  We've been to Genesis 2:15 several times in this book, and I'm going there again,
because men keep forgetting it.
  To work it and take care of it, before the fall, before the serpent, before the
thorns, before sweat was a word in a curse. God made a man and gave him a job,
and the job was part of the good rather than a punishment added later. The curse in
chapter 3 didn't invent work. It made work hard: thorns, sweat, ground that fights
back. But the work itself is in the blueprint, and a man who thinks of his job as a
necessary evil has read Genesis 3 and skipped Genesis 2.
   Go higher than that, because God Himself works. The first thing the Bible shows
God doing is six days of labor followed by a day of rest. Jesus said it plainly when
the religious men accused Him of working on the wrong day.

  John 5:17 (NIV)
  In his defense Jesus said to them, 'My Father is always at his work to this very day, and I too am
  working.'



   My Father is always at his work. The God you serve isn't a God who sits. He
builds and tends and keeps and makes things grow, and He made you in His image,
which means among other things that He made you to work. That's why a man
who can't work feels as though he's coming apart. That's why a man laid off or
retired or disabled goes sideways in ways that aren't only about money. The image
in him is straining against a life with no garden.
   And Jesus worked, and not only for the three years. Most of His life on earth,
from boyhood to thirty, He spent in a carpenter's shop with tools in His hands,
making things for people who paid Him for them. Nobody wrote it down, because
it wasn't remarkable. It was a man working. The Son of God spent six times longer
building furniture than preaching, and He didn't consider the furniture years a
waste.
    So here's the plain answer to the man at the plant: yes, it counts. It isn't the thing
you do so you can get to the thing. It is the thing, or most of it, and the way you do
it is most of what God's purpose in your generation looks like.

What work is for

  Let me be specific, because "your work matters" can mean anything. Here are the


290                                                                             You Were Made for a Work
four things work is for in Scripture, and the three things it isn't.
   Work is for provision. You work so that your house has what it needs. We've said
this; it's a real weight and a man carries it, and a man who won't has denied the
faith. Paul was blunter than I'd dare to be.

  2 Thessalonians 3:10 (NIV)
  For even when we were with you, we gave you this rule: 'The one who is unwilling to work shall not
  eat.'



   Unwilling is the word, and not unable, because there's grace for the man who
can't. There's a rule for the man who won't. Work is for service. Your work is for
your neighbor. The plant operator's work is clean water for four thousand people.
The electrician's work is a house that doesn't burn. The teacher's work is a child
who can read. The trucker's work is groceries on the shelf. This is the piece men
miss most: your job is how you love your neighbor forty hours a week, and the
quality of the work is the quality of the love. Paul told thieves to work, and look at
the reason he gave.

  Ephesians 4:28 (NIV)
  Anyone who has been stealing must steal no longer, but must work, doing something useful with their
  own hands, that they may have something to share with those in need.



   Something useful, so that they may have something to share. Work turns a man
from a taker into a giver. That isn't a side effect. It's what work is for.
    Work is for making the world more than it is, which is Genesis 1:28. Subdue and
rule, which we said means cultivate: take the raw thing and bring it to order and
fruit. Every honest job does this. The mechanic takes a broken machine and makes
it run. The farmer takes dirt and makes food. The accountant takes chaos and
makes it legible. The nurse takes a body that's failing and holds it steady. You're
doing in small what God did in six days, and that's why a finished job feels the way
it does. It's the image showing.
  And work is for worship, which is Colossians 3:23. Whatever you do, work at it
with all your heart, as working for the Lord. The man at the plant is working for the
Lord at three in the morning whether or not anybody in the town knows it, and the
Lord knows it, and the Lord is the audience that matters. When you do a job with


You Were Made for a Work                                                                               291
all your heart because God is watching, you've turned the plant into a sanctuary.
That isn't a metaphor. That's the fourth mark applied to a time card.
   Now the three things work isn't. Work is not your identity. It's what you do and
not who you are. The man who is his job dies when the job does, and we'll come to
that. Your name is son, brother, husband, father, keeper of a garden. Your job is one
of the gardens.
   Work is not an escape, which chapters 5 and 24 have already said. Work claps and
home mostly doesn't, and a man can hide in a job and call it provision. If you're
staying late because the plant is easier than the kitchen, you've turned a good thing
into an exit.
   And work is not your salvation. You don't earn your standing with God by
working hard. You already have it, in Christ, and now you work because you're His
rather than in order to become His. A man who's trying to justify himself by his
output never rests and never finishes, because there's always more.

When you hate the job

  Some of you are reading this at a job you can't stand, and "work is worship"
sounds like a slap. Let me talk to you straight.
    The thorns are real, and Genesis 3 wasn't joking about them. There are bosses
who are fools, customers who lie, systems that grind, tasks that mean nothing to
anybody. Some of what makes work miserable is the curse, and the curse won't lift
until Jesus comes back, and pretending otherwise is a lie I won't tell you. But here's
what Solomon, who had every job and every luxury, concluded after looking hard at
all of it.

  Ecclesiastes 2:24 (NIV)
  A person can do nothing better than to eat and drink and find satisfaction in their own toil. This too,
  I see, is from the hand of God,



   Find satisfaction in their own toil, from the hand of God. Satisfaction in work is
a gift, and it's available in a great many more jobs than men think, and it comes
from three places. It comes from excellence. A man who does even a job he hates
