with skill has something the job can't take from him.




292                                                                             You Were Made for a Work
  Proverbs 22:29 (NIV)
  Do you see someone skilled in their work? They will serve before kings; they will not serve before
  officials of low rank.



    Skilled in their work, whatever the work. Be the best one at it in the building, not
for the boss, but because skill is a form of dignity and God gave you hands to be
skilled with. It comes from people. The job is a garden, and the garden has people in
it, and those people are your assignment as much as the tasks are. The apprentice
who's watching how you handle a bad day. The coworker whose marriage is coming
apart. The customer nobody else is patient with. You may hate the work and love
the people, and the people may be why God has you there.
   And it comes from the audience. If you're working for the boss, the job is as good
as the boss. If you're working for the Lord, the job is as good as the Lord, and He's
watching a man do a thing well that nobody else values, and He counts it.
   If you should leave, leave like a man. Not on a Tuesday because you had a feeling;
chapter 20 covered that. With a plan, with your wife in the room, with the next
thing lined up or the savings to bridge it, with your reasons written down and
checked by a brother. Men leave jobs badly in two directions: they quit in a huff, or
they stay in a coffin. The man in the middle plans his exit and provides through it.

When you lose the job

  This one is different, and it's coming for more of you than you think, and
nobody prepares men for it.
   You get called in on a Thursday, and by Friday you're a man with no garden.
And the first thing that happens isn't about money. It's about who you are. Because
you've been introducing yourself by your job for twenty years, and now the
sentence has nothing after "I'm."
   Here is what a man does. Tell your wife the same day. Not after you've figured
out a plan; the same day. Men hide layoffs from their wives for weeks, and it's the
worst thing they can do, because she will find out, and what she'll learn is that you
hid. Tell her, and tell her you're scared if you're scared, and tell her you're going to
handle it.
   Tell your brother that week. The twenty minutes was built for this. "What's


You Were Made for a Work                                                                               293
heavy?" has an answer now.
   Treat the search as your work. Get up at the same time, shave, dress, and sit down
at eight and work at finding work until five. A man who treats unemployment as a
vacation drifts into the recliner and doesn't come out. A man who treats it as a job
stays a man.
   Take the bridge job. Paul made tents so that he could preach. If the plant closes
and the only thing available is driving a delivery truck at two-thirds the pay, drive
the truck. It's provision, it's service, it's making the world more than it is, and it's
worship if you do it for the Lord. It isn't beneath you. Nothing that feeds your
house is beneath you.
   And kill the shame. Chapter 15: guilt says you did a wrong thing; shame says you
are a wrong thing. Losing a job is neither. It's a thorn, and it happened to you. Your
Father saw it coming, and He isn't surprised and He isn't disappointed, and He's
the one who told you not to worry about what you'd eat, because He knows what
you need.

When your work is the house

   Some of you have work that doesn't look like work to anybody, including you.
You're home with the children because of how the math worked out, and you feel
like less of a man for it, and you shouldn't. You're disabled and can't do what you
used to. You're a caregiver: Mr. Alvarez bathing his wife every morning is doing the
second verb of Genesis 2:15, keeping what he was given, and it's the hardest work in
this book. If your garden is a house and a body that needs you, that's a garden.
Work it and keep it. The Father who sees in secret is watching a man do the
unglamorous thing with all his heart, and He doesn't grade on visibility.

After the badge

   Now the longest section, because the men past sixty have been under-served on
this by a century of Christian books.
   Scripture has no retirement, and I've looked. There is no verse where a man stops
working God's purpose because he reached a number. What there is, though, is a
shift, and it's in a passage most men have never read.




294                                                                You Were Made for a Work
  Numbers 8:25-26 (NIV)
  but at the age of fifty, they must retire from their regular service and work no longer. They may assist
  their fellow Levites in performing their duties at the tent of meeting, but they themselves must not do
  the work. This, then, is how you are to assign the responsibilities of the Levites.



   Retire from their regular service, and then: they may assist. The Levites who did
the heavy lifting in the tabernacle stopped doing the heavy lifting at fifty, but they
didn't stop. They moved from carrying to assisting, from doing the work to helping
the ones who did. That's the biblical shape of retirement. Not the recliner; a change
of garden.
   Roy's first Tuesday after thirty-one years of wearing a badge was, in his words,
the worst day of his life, because nobody needed him and there was no assignment.
He'd been a man with a work since he was nineteen, and then he was a man with a
pension and a television and a wife who had built a life in the spaces he wasn't in.
   Here's what pulled him out, and it's a rhythm any retired man can build. He kept
getting up at 5:30. Not because he had to, but because a man who sleeps until he
feels like getting up will soon feel like getting up around ten, and a man who gets up
at ten is halfway to the drift, so the alarm stayed.
   He made the coffee, which was chapter 5, and then he sat with his wife while she
drank it, and that became the first work of every day: knowing her, after forty-four
years of not.
   He read, Mark first and then Proverbs, a chapter a day, out loud on the porch,
which was chapter 17 and the plumb line. For thirty-one years he'd let the badge be
his north star, and it was gone, and he needed the one that doesn't move.
    He gave himself a job every day. Not a hobby; a job, something he'd committed
to that somebody else was counting on: the north gate, the widow's gutters, the
church van, the grandson's oil change. "If I didn't have a job by breakfast," he said,
"I found one by lunch. A man with no job by noon is a man in a chair by two."
   He built the Thursday table, which was chapters 13 and 14, for two men in their
thirties who needed a man ahead of them. That became his main work, and he'll tell
you it saved his life. Retirement from the regular service, and assisting the ones still
carrying.
   He fished with his grandson on Tuesdays, giving hills the way chapter 28



You Were Made for a Work                                                                                 295
described. And he stayed in church, not as an attender but as a man with a job there.
Sunday wasn't a day off from work anymore. It was the work.
   That's a post-badge rhythm. Seven things, none of them a career, all of them
Genesis 2:15. Roy said the day he read that verse after retiring was the day he
stopped feeling like a man who'd been let go and started feeling like a man who'd
been reassigned to the same job in a different garden.
   If your health took your work before your age did, the shape is smaller but it's the
same, and the word is still assist. Mr. Alvarez, when he can get an hour away from
his wife's care, calls every widower in the church, one a day, and asks them what's
heavy. He does it from a chair at seventy-eight, and it's a work, and men are being
kept alive by it.
   The danger for the older man is the drift wearing a fishing license, and its cousin,
the anger that comes through the television. Both are what happens to a man who
was made for a work and stopped, so don't stop; shift instead. Carry less and assist
more. Declare it to the next generation, as the psalm says, until you're done.

Work in the other seasons

  If you're single, work is your main garden, and you should work it like a man
who intends to hand it to a wife someday: with excellence, with money handled,
with a reputation you'd be proud for her to hear about. And watch that you don't
make the work your wife. Men who marry their jobs at twenty-six have a hard time
un-marrying them at thirty-two.
   If there's a baby in your house, you're working exhausted, and the temptation to
stay an extra hour at the plant because it's quieter than the house is going to be real,
and you should name it for what it is. The floor from chapter 5 holds this year more
than any other. Provision is holy; disappearing isn't; and a wife with a newborn
knows the difference by 6:40.
   If you're divorced, the money is tighter than it has ever been: two households,
support, lawyers. You may need to work more. Do it without vanishing from the
children. The Tuesday call from the truck at 7:30 costs nothing, and it's the most
important work you'll do that week. And let your kids see a father who's steady at
his job. In a world that shook, a dad who goes to work every day and comes home
every night is ground they can stand on.



296                                                              You Were Made for a Work
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of nineteen is often working a job he considers beneath his real life,
which is Eli at the parts counter, and the takeaway for him is that the counter is the
garden. He started treating it that way, learning every part number and every
regular's name, and within a year the owner had made him the man who trains the
new hires, and two of them go to church with him now. The work counted before
it was noticed.
   A man of fifty has often let work become his identity, and he finds out what that
costs when the work is threatened, which was Walt in the bank parking lot. The
takeaway for him is that his name is son and husband and father and keeper of a
garden, and that his job is one of the gardens rather than the whole of him.

He finished the work

   Jesus said it in a prayer the night before He died.

  John 17:4 (NIV)
  I have brought you glory on earth by finishing the work you gave me to do.



   Finishing the work you gave me to do. That's the sentence every man wants to be
able to say at the end. Not "I had a great career," and not "I made a lot of money."
The work You gave me, I did, and I finished it, and it brought You glory. The
carpenter years and the preaching years and the cross, all one work, all finished.
   You were made for a work. It might be a plant at three in the morning. It might
be a classroom or a crew or a spreadsheet or a body that needs bathing or a
Thursday table or a north gate, and it's all Genesis 2:15. It all counts, and the way
you do it is most of what your life will have been.
   The man at the plant is still there. Nineteen years, and he told me last spring that
he'd started praying over the town while he ran his checks at three in the morning.
Four thousand people asleep, drinking his water in the morning, none of them
knowing that a man they've never met asked God to keep them while he kept the
plant.
   That counts, and so does yours. Go do your work.



You Were Made for a Work                                                            297
Before you turn the page

   If you've spent years thinking your work didn't count for God, I hope you'll
drive to it tomorrow with a different picture in your head: the Son of God at a
workbench for thirty years, making things for people who paid Him, and not one
hour of it wasted. Your plant, your classroom, your crew, your spreadsheet, your
kitchen, your chair are all Genesis 2:15. And if the work was taken from you, by a
layoff or a body that quit or a birthday, you weren't let go; you were reassigned.
May the Father who is always at His work give you a job by noon tomorrow that
somebody is counting on, and may you do it with all your heart, as for Him, and
find that it was the thing all along.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because a man can work for forty years without ever asking what the work has been
  for: say which of the four it's been for you, provision or service or making the world
  more or worship, or which of the three it's become, identity or escape or salvation,
  honestly.
  DO THIS WEEK
  Because work becomes worship one ordinary task at a time: do one task at your job
  with all your heart, as for the Lord, and tell no one; if you're between jobs, treat the
  search as your job from eight to five starting Monday; if you're retired, name your
  job for tomorrow before you go to bed tonight and do it before noon.
  TELL A BROTHER
  Because the man who's using work as an exit rarely knows it until someone asks: tell
  him whether your work has been an exit and what your house has paid for it; if
  you're retired, tell him your rhythm and ask him to check whether you're keeping it.
  CARRY THIS
  Work came before the curse. You weren't let go; you were reassigned. Same job,
  different garden. Finish the work you were given.




298                                                                 You Were Made for a Work
CHAPTER 31



Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel
   Part 5: The long road
   The old Ford with the cracked block had a temperature gauge, and it worked.
That's the part of the story I left out in chapter 15. All summer, while I was
replacing hoses and thermostats, the gauge was telling me the truth, which was that
the engine was running hot. Every time I put the hood down and told myself I'd
fixed it, the needle would climb again on the next long grade, and I'd look at it and
decide the gauge must be off.
   The gauge was the only honest thing on that truck. But here's the other side of it.
I've known men who drove into a ditch staring at the dash. A gauge tells you
something true about the state of the machine. It doesn't tell you where the road
goes. A man who ignores his gauges blows an engine, and a man who steers by them
drives off the road. You need both hands on the wheel and one eye on the dash, and
you need to know which is which.
  That's feelings, and it's this whole chapter in a sentence. Your feelings are the
dash and not the wheel. They're real, and they're telling you something, and you
should read them. And they don't get to steer.

Two ways men wreck

  There are two kinds of men who get this wrong, and most of us are one or the
other, and some of us switch depending on the week.
   The first is the man who ignores the dash. He'd tell you he doesn't do feelings.
He isn't angry; he's fine. He isn't sad; he's tired. He isn't lonely; he's busy. Walt was
that man for the entire year before the bank parking lot in Abilene. The fear gauge
had been climbing since the spring, every time he looked at the receivables, and



Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel                                                   299
every time he looked at it he told himself the gauge was off, the way I told myself the
Ford's temperature gauge was off, and he went on driving. By the time he finally
read it honestly, sitting in his truck with three hundred contacts and nobody to call,
the fear had been running his sleep and his temper and his silence at the dinner table
for eleven months, and his wife could have told him so at any point if he'd asked.
He was probably raised by a man who did the same thing, and he's proud of it, and
he believes it's strength, and it isn't. It's a man driving with tape over his gauges. The
feelings don't go away because he won't look at them; they go somewhere else, and
chapter 9 told you where: out as anger, down as appetite, away as absence. Every
one of the three exits is a gauge that got ignored until the engine did something
about it. The man who says he doesn't have feelings has feelings running his life
from under the hood where he can't see them.
   The second is the man who steers by the dash. He's the opposite and he's just as
wrecked. "I don't feel in love anymore." "I don't feel like God's here." "I feel like this
job isn't my calling." "I feel like I need to be happy, and I'm not happy here." He
reads a gauge, which is fine, and then he turns the wheel toward whatever the gauge
says, which is a ditch. He leaves marriages because a feeling changed. He leaves
churches because the feeling left. He makes his whole life a series of course
corrections toward whatever the needle is doing this week, and he ends up nowhere,
and he can't understand why, because he was so honest about his feelings.
   The Bible's way is neither. It's a man who reads every gauge honestly, says out
loud what it says, brings it to God and to a brother, and then steers by something
that doesn't move.

What the dash is telling you

   Let me give you a vocabulary, because a lot of men can't read their own dash for
the simple reason that nobody ever taught them the names of the gauges.
  Fear says that something matters to you and you aren't sure you can protect it.
Read it, and then ask what you're actually afraid of losing.
  Anger says that something is wrong, or that you think it is. Read it, and then ask
what's under it, because it's almost always fear or shame or helplessness wearing a
hard face, as chapter 9 said.
  Sadness says that something is lost. Read it, and grieve the thing. Men skip this
gauge more than any other, and the skipping is what turns sadness into the drift.


300                                                         Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel
  Loneliness says not-good-alone. It's the most accurate gauge on the dash, because
God Himself said it in Genesis 2. Read it, and don't read it as "something's wrong
with me." Read it as "go find a brother."
  Desire says you're alive. It isn't sin to want. What you do with the wanting is
where sin comes in.
   Numbness says you've been running hot so long that the gauge burned out, and
that isn't peace. It's a warning light that has stopped working, and it's the most
dangerous reading on the dash.
   Shame says "I am a wrong thing." Read it, and then check it against the plumb
line, because this gauge lies more than any other. Chapter 15: guilt is a working
gauge and shame is a broken one.
  And joy says you're where you were made to be, doing what you were made to
do. Read it, and thank God, and notice what you were doing when it came.
   The Psalms are a book of men reading their gauges out loud to God. David, who
was as hard a man as ever lived, said things to God that most men wouldn't say to
their wives. Read this one and tell me he was ignoring the dash.

  Psalm 42:5 (NIV)
  Why, my soul, are you downcast? Why so disturbed within me? Put your hope in God, for I will yet
  praise him, my Savior and my God.



  Why are you downcast. He's reading the gauge and naming it. And then, in the
same breath: put your hope in God. He's turning the wheel, not toward the feeling
but toward God. That's the whole method in two sentences, and it's why the
Psalms are the best book a man could read who has never learned to do this.

What the dash is not telling you

    Now the other half, for the man who steers by it. A feeling is not the truth about
God. "I don't feel forgiven" is a gauge reading and not a verdict. God's forgiveness is
a legal fact established at a cross, and it doesn't fluctuate with your sleep. "I don't
feel like God is here" is a reading too, and it's often accurate about you and never
accurate about Him. He said He'd never leave. The gauge describes your
perception; the wheel is His word.



Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel                                                                 301
   A feeling is not the truth about your marriage. "I don't feel in love anymore" is a
reading. Every marriage that has lasted forty years has had stretches, sometimes long
ones, when that gauge read empty. The men who steered by it are divorced. The
men who kept the covenant with both hands and let the gauge do what gauges do
woke up, often years later, with the needle back up and a marriage that had gone
somewhere the feeling could never have taken it.
   A feeling is not a command. "I feel like God wants me to" is the most dangerous
sentence in a Christian man's mouth, because it takes a gauge and gives it God's
authority, so test it by holding it against the plumb line. Does the Word say it? Do
your brothers see it? Does it look like the four marks, or like an exit? God doesn't
usually lead by feelings, and when He leads, what He leads toward looks like
Scripture and not like relief.

  Proverbs 28:26 (NIV)
  Those who trust in themselves are fools, but those who walk in wisdom are kept safe.



    Trust in themselves is a man steering by his dash. Walk in wisdom is a man with
his hands on a wheel that's fixed to something outside him. And a feeling is not
permanent. Every gauge on your dash moves with sleep, food, blood sugar,
hormones, weather, the last conversation you had, and what you watched last night.
A man who makes a permanent decision on a Tuesday-night reading has handed his
life to the weather, so wait. Read it again on Thursday, and again after you've slept,
and again with a brother in the room.

What the wheel is

   So what does a man steer by, if not the dash? Four things, and you've met all of
them in this book.
   The first is the Word, from chapter 17, which doesn't move. When your feelings
say one thing and the Bible says another, the Bible is describing reality and your
feelings are describing you.
  The second is the covenant, meaning what you promised before God and with
witnesses, to her and to the church and to your children and to the men you gave
your word to. Promises are made precisely so that they'll hold when the feeling
doesn't; otherwise you wouldn't need to make them.


302                                                                    Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel
  The third is the four marks: reject passivity, accept responsibility, lead
courageously, expect the greater reward. When you don't know what to do, do
whichever of those four is in front of you. None of them requires a feeling.
   And the fourth is the next assigned thing, from chapter 20, which is not the thing
you feel like doing but the thing God has put in your hands. Do that thing, and the
feeling will follow, or it won't, and either way you did the right thing.
   Here's how the two work together, and I'll use the truck. You're driving up a
long grade and the temperature gauge climbs. You don't ignore it, because you'll
blow the engine. You don't steer toward it, because there's nowhere to steer toward
a gauge. You read it, and you name it, "running hot," and then you make a wise
decision with the wheel. You pull over, you let it cool, you check the coolant, you
tell somebody, and then you get back on the road toward where you were already
going. The gauge changed how you drove. It didn't change where.
   That's a man reading his dash and keeping his wheel.

Elijah under the broom bush

    I want to spend some time here on the heavy end of the dash, because men die of
it and the church has been too quiet about it.
   Some of you aren't only sad. You're somewhere darker than that, and you've
been there a while, and you aren't sure how to say it, and you're a little afraid of
what saying it means about you. I want to show you a man of God who was there,
and what God did with him. Elijah had just seen the greatest victory of his life, fire
from heaven on a mountain, and a day later he was alone in the desert asking to die.

  1 Kings 19:4 (NIV)
  while he himself went a day's journey into the desert. He came to a broom bush, sat down under it
  and prayed that he might die. 'I have had enough, LORD,' he said. 'Take my life; I am no better
  than my ancestors.'



   I have had enough, take my life: that's a prophet of God, one of the greatest who
ever lived, reading his dash out loud, and the dash said "I want to die." He didn't
hide it. He said it to God. Now watch what God did. He didn't rebuke him. He
didn't tell him to have more faith. He sent an angel with bread and water and let
him sleep. Then He fed him again. Then, after the man had eaten and slept, God



Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel                                                                  303
spoke, and then God gave him a job and a companion. Food, sleep, presence, a
word, work, a friend, in that order.
  That is the most practical passage on depression in the Bible, and here is what it
means for you.
   Read the gauge and say it out loud, to God first, the way Elijah did, and then to a
man. If the gauge says "I've had enough," that sentence needs to leave your mouth in
front of another human being this week. Not because saying it fixes it, but because a
man carrying that alone is in more danger than he knows.
   Take care of the body. God's first two moves with a suicidal prophet were a meal
and a nap. Sleep, food, sunlight, movement: those aren't spiritual shortcuts, because
they're the way God made you, and He knows how we're formed.

  Psalm 103:14 (NIV)
  for he knows how we are formed, he remembers that we are dust.



  He remembers that we are dust. Your body isn't separate from your soul, and a
man who hasn't slept in a month is going to read every gauge on his dash wrong. Fix
what you can fix in the body first.
   Get help, and hear me on this: getting help is not weakness. A doctor is not a
failure of faith. A counselor is not a substitute for God; he's one of the ways God
sends bread and water. If your gauges have read dark for more than a few weeks, if
the numbness has settled in, if you aren't sleeping when you could, if you've
thought about ending it, you see a doctor and you tell him the truth. If he says
medication, you take it, the way you'd take insulin. That isn't steering by your
feelings. That's repairing a broken gauge so that you can read the dash again.
   And if you're thinking about ending it tonight, or this week, call a man now,
your brother from the twenty minutes or your pastor, and if there's no one, call 988
and talk to someone whose whole job is to sit with you in the dark. Elijah asked God
to kill him and God fed him breakfast. There's no shame in the broom bush.
There's only the question of whether you'll let somebody find you there.
  Then, when you've eaten and slept and been found, the wheel. Elijah got up and
went back to work with a new companion beside him. The feeling didn't leave first.
He moved first, in small ways, with help, and the feeling followed over time. That's
how it works for most men. Not a lightning bolt, but a meal, a nap, a brother, and


304                                                                Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel
the next small assigned thing, repeated until the gauge comes back into range.

The dash in every season

   If there's a baby in your house, every gauge on your dash is pegged, and none of
them are accurate. You're sleep-deprived in a way that changes brain chemistry, and
so is she. Do not make decisions about your marriage at three in the morning. Do
not evaluate your life at three in the morning. Sleep is a spiritual discipline this year,
and so is grace, for her and for yourself and for the gauges that lie loudest exactly
when you're most tempted to believe them. Say to each other, out loud, when it's
bad: "This is the sleep talking. We're fine. We'll talk about it after a nap."
   If you're divorced, your dash is going to do things for a year or two that you've
never seen it do. Anger, grief, relief, guilt, loneliness, in rotation, sometimes all in
one afternoon. Waves at odd times: the exchange in the parking lot, Sunday night in
an empty house, a song. Read every one of them. Say them to God and to your
man. And don't turn the wheel for a year. No moving, no marrying, no quitting the
job, no leaving the church. Kenny's older man told him, "Your gauges are broken
right now. You don't make turns with broken gauges. You drive straight and slow
until they settle." He did. He said the second year the needle started making sense
again, and he was glad of every turn he hadn't made.
   If you're an older man, you may have been raised in a generation that taped over
the dash and called it manhood. Roy said he didn't know he had feelings until he
retired and there was nothing left to drown them out. "I sat on the porch and all of a
sudden I was sad about things from 1975." That's fifty years of gauge readings
coming due. It isn't too late to learn to read them; the Psalms are the manual. And
the older man has gauges the younger ones don't: grief that accumulates as friends
die, fear of decline, the feeling of being useless. Read them out loud to a brother.
Widowers especially: the loneliness gauge isn't lying to you, and it isn't a sign that
you're weak. It's a sign that God made you for company and you've lost some. Go
find the rest.
  And if you're single, watch the loneliness gauge and read it right. It isn't telling
you something's wrong with you. It's telling you the truth Genesis 2:18 told: not
good alone. Steer toward brothers, and not toward whatever screen or woman
promises to quiet the needle fastest.
   Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this


Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel                                                     305
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty is most likely to steer by the dash, because his culture has
taught him that his feelings are the truest thing about him. Eli left a good church
because he "didn't feel it anymore," and came back a year later when an older man
asked him what he'd been steering by. The takeaway for him is that a feeling is not a
command and not a verdict, and that the Word is the only instrument on the dash
that doesn't move.
   A man of fifty is most likely to have tape over the dash, which was Walt for
eleven months before the parking lot, and the takeaway for him is that the feelings
he won't read are running his house from under the hood, and that reading them
out loud to one man is the first move.

The Man who read every gauge

  Jesus felt everything. He wept at a grave, and He was troubled in spirit, He was
overwhelmed with sorrow to the point of death, and He said so. Hebrews says He
prayed with loud cries and tears. There has never been a man who read his dash
more honestly than the Son of God.
   And He never once let a gauge turn the wheel. Not as I will, but as You will. He
read the gauge that said "let this cup pass," out loud, three times, and then He
steered by the Father's will straight into the thing the gauge was begging Him to
avoid.
  That's the model: not a man with no feelings, and not a man run by them, but a
Man who felt all of it, said all of it, and kept both hands on a wheel that was fixed to
something that didn't move.
  So read your dash, and tell the truth about what it says, to God and to a brother.
Then put your hands back on the wheel and drive where you were already going.

Before you turn the page

   If any gauge on your dash has been reading dark for a long time, I want to say the
plainest thing I know before you close this chapter: tell one man today. Not this
week; today. Elijah asked God to kill him and God fed him breakfast, and there is no
shame in the broom bush, only the question of whether you'll let somebody find
you there. For the rest of you, read your dash tomorrow morning in one honest


306                                                       Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel
sentence to God, and then put your hands back on the wheel. May the Father who
knows how we're formed and remembers we are dust give you rest for your body
and truth for your gauges, and may He steer you where you were already going.

   YOUR TURN



   NAME IT
   Because a gauge you won't read still runs the engine: say which man you are, the one
   with tape over his gauges or the one steering by them, and then read your dash right
   now in one word per gauge, fear or anger or sadness or loneliness or numbness or
   shame or joy.
   DO THIS WEEK
   Because reading the dash is a morning habit and not a crisis skill: each morning, read
   one gauge out loud to God in one sentence, "Father, I'm ___ today," and then say
   the wheel, "and I'm going to ___ anyway." If any gauge has read dark for weeks,
   make a doctor's appointment before Tuesday.
   TELL A BROTHER
   Because the gauge you've never said out loud is the one that's most dangerous: read
   him your dash, all of it, and if you've been under the broom bush, tell him today
   rather than this week.
   CARRY THIS
   Feelings are the dash, not the wheel. Read every gauge. Steer by the Word.




Feelings Are the Dash, Not the Wheel                                                   307
308   Every Season Still Counts
CHAPTER 32



Every Season Still Counts
   Part 5: The long road
  A cotton field in February looks dead. Bare dirt, stubble, a few tumbleweeds
caught on the fence. A man who didn't know farming would drive past it and think
nothing was happening.
  Everything is happening, though. The ground is resting, and the stubble is going
back into the soil. The moisture from the winter rains is working its way down to
where the roots will need it in July. The farmer is fixing equipment, planning rows,
watching the market, praying about rain. February isn't the off-season. It's a season
with its own job, and a farmer who tried to skip it, who plowed and planted in
February because he couldn't stand the waiting, would lose the whole year.
Solomon said it plainer than I can.

  Ecclesiastes 3:1 (NIV)
  There is a time for everything, and a season for every activity under the heavens:



   A season for every activity. Not one season that counts and a great deal of waiting
around it. Every season has an activity. Every season has an assignment.
   I told you in chapter 3 that I wasn't going to stop every chapter and sort you by
age, and I've tried to keep that promise, turning toward you inside the paragraphs
when your season changed the counsel. This is the chapter where we do it on
purpose: all the seasons, in one place, each with its job, because the culture has
taught men to rank them, and the ranking is a lie that costs men decades.

The lie of the peak




Every Season Still Counts                                                              309
   Here's the ranking most men carry without knowing they carry it. The twenties
are for freedom. The thirties are for building. The forties are the peak. The fifties
are for coasting. Sixty and beyond is done, a long slow exit with golf in it. And any
season that doesn't fit the arc, divorce or illness or a wife dying, is a detour to get
through so that you can get back on the arc.
   Scripture doesn't know this arc. Scripture knows a man who serves God's
purpose in his own generation, and his generation includes every season he lives
through, and every season has a job, and none of them is a detour. Moses started his
real work at eighty. David was anointed as a boy and did his best writing as an old
man. Caleb asked for a hill country at eighty-five.

  Joshua 14:10-12 (NIV)
  So here I am today, eighty-five years old! I am still as strong today as the day Moses sent me out; I'm
  just as vigorous to go out to battle now as I was then. Now give me this hill country that the LORD
  promised me that day.



    Give me this hill country. That's an eighty-five-year-old man asking for the
hardest assignment on the map, because his season still counted and he knew it. So
let me walk the seasons with you. For each one I'll tell you what it's for, what it's
tempted by, what its assignment is, and which man from this book is standing in it.
You'll find yours, and you may find that you're standing in two at once, because
seasons overlap, and that's all right. God's purpose in your generation is God's
purpose in your season, whichever one it is.

The young single man

  This is the season of strength and open hours, and it's the most wasted season in
most men's lives.
   Micah stood in it for six years at the back table, and he told you in chapter 21
that he wasted the first three of them waiting. That's the temptation: to treat this
season as the lobby, to wait for a wife or a career or a calling or a feeling before you
start. And the other temptation, the one that fills the waiting, is the itch, because a
young man with open hours and no one watching has more exposure to that exit
than he'll ever have again.
  The assignment is to build. Chapter 21 gave you the list: a house, a table, a



310                                                                                 Every Season Still Counts
company of brothers, a devotion, a fatherhood toward somebody else's fatherless
boy, a purity. Paul told a young man in this season not to let anyone discount him
for it.

  1 Timothy 4:12 (NIV)
  Don't let anyone look down on you because you are young, but set an example for the believers in
  speech, in conduct, in love, in faith and in purity.



   Set an example, Paul says, and he doesn't say to prepare to set one when you're
older; he means now. And Solomon, looking back from old age, gave the young
man one instruction above all the rest.

  Ecclesiastes 12:1 (NIV)
  Remember your Creator in the days of your youth, before the days of trouble come and the years
  approach when you will say, 'I find no pleasure in them'



   Remember your Creator in the days of your youth. The men who find the
Father at twenty-five are spared thirty years of scanning the bleachers. This season
counts, not as a warm-up, but because the man you build now is the man every later
season gets.

The first year of marriage, and the first year with a baby

   Nobody warns you about this one. The wedding gets the attention, and then
two people who were dating are suddenly one flesh in one apartment with one bank
account, and the season that decides the whole marriage begins the day after the
honeymoon, when everyone has stopped watching.
  The temptation is to believe the wedding did the work, and it didn't. The
wedding was the marker; the Tuesdays are the marriage, and the first year of
Tuesdays sets the floor for the next forty. The assignment is chapters 22, 23, and 24,
run hard: know her, because you don't yet, whatever you think; lay it down, every
preference you've never had to lay down before; find out what she actually needs;
and keep the twenty minutes with your brother going, because a newly married
man who drops his brothers has handed his wife a weight she can't carry.
   And if a baby comes in that first year or two, you're in a season inside a season,



Every Season Still Counts                                                                            311
and it's the one that breaks more young marriages than any other, and it's the one
the church talks about least.
   Tomás's daughter was four months old when we first met him in these pages,
and here is what he'd tell you. The marriage takes the hit when the baby comes. Not
because either of you did anything wrong, but because a third person who needs
everything has moved in, and the two of you are running on fumes, and every gauge
on both your dashboards is pegged, and you've stopped talking about anything but
logistics, and the bed has gone quiet, and you're both a little afraid this is what it's
going to be now.
   It isn't going to be this forever. But it's what it is this year, and the assignment in
this season is to protect the marriage inside the chaos rather than after it. Ten
minutes at the kitchen counter after the baby's down, phones face down, is the
season's whole liturgy. Chapter 4 said it: that isn't waiting for a better season. It is
the season. Lay down sleep and take a shift. Know her postpartum body and heart
the way chapter 22 described, and watch for the darkness that has a name. Keep the
floor from chapter 5 even when the job is hard and the house is harder. Don't
evaluate your marriage at three in the morning; chapter 31. And keep your brother.
Twenty minutes on the phone at nine while you do the dishes is what brotherhood
looks like this year, and it's enough.
   This season counts, and not as survival until the real marriage starts. The floor
you pour in this year, exhausted and unthanked, is the floor the marriage will stand
on when the child is sixteen. Tomás said, at the end of his first year, "We didn't have
a great year. We had a year where I found out I could be a man when I was too tired
to be anything, and she found out she could count on me. I think that's better than
a great year."

The middle years

   Thirty-five to fifty-five, children at home, career at full throttle, the mortgage,
the schedule. This is the season the culture calls the peak, and it's the season where
most men lose the house while building the career.
   Anthony is in it, standing in a doorway between a garage and a kitchen. Walt was
in it when he built eleven trucks and disappeared. The temptations here are the
strongest and the best disguised: disappearing into provision, the drift that looks
like being tired, the scoreboard against every other man at church, and, for a good


312                                                                   Every Season Still Counts
many men in their forties, the itch coming back with a middle-aged face, when the
marriage has gone quiet and a woman at work laughs at your jokes.
   The assignment is presence, and it's harder here than anywhere. It means the
floor held, and the marriage tended: date her, pursue her, know her again, because
the woman you married at twenty-six is gone and the one who's here needs
studying. Brothers, because the middle years are the loneliest of a man's life; men
between forty and fifty-five have fewer real friends than at any other age, and the
men with none are the ones who end up in bank parking lots. Hills for your sons
and daughters, and the day, chapters 28 and 29. And a hard look at what you're
actually working for, because the fourth mark gets tested in this season more than in
any other. If the reward you're chasing is the scoreboard, you'll win it and lose
everything that mattered.
   This season counts, but not the way the culture says. It isn't your peak. It's the
season where you find out whether you're a corner post or a line post, and your
children are watching to find out too.

The divorced man

   Nobody plans this season, and Kenny certainly didn't. And when it comes, most
men treat it as a detour, a thing to get through so they can get back to the real story,
and that's the first mistake, because it isn't a detour. It's a season with a job, and it
lasts longer than anyone tells you.
   Here's the shape I've watched. The first year or two is survival. Grief that comes
in waves at the exchange and on Sunday nights. Anger at her, at God, at yourself.
The itch, hard, because you're alone and you weren't. The drift, because caring is
what got you here. Money you don't have. A custody schedule that runs your life. A
church that doesn't know what to do with you. The temptation in this stretch is to
make a turn: move, marry, quit, leave the church, anything to make the gauges stop
screaming. Don't make the turn; chapter 31 told you why. Drive straight and slow.
  The assignment in the survival years is short and hard. Grieve out loud, and own
your part without attaching hers. Children first, before any new woman and before
your own comfort. One brother, on the phone, every Tuesday. The Tuesday call to
your kids, even when they say nothing. Stay in the house of God even when the
house is awkward about you.
   Then, somewhere in years three to five, something shifts, and the gauges settle.


Every Season Still Counts                                                               313
You've built a house, a real one, with your children's drawings on the refrigerator.
You have a smoker you built across eleven weekends. You've become the man in
your church that the other divorced men will talk to. And you begin to see what
God was doing, which is what Kenny saw: a purpose he never would have chosen,
served with the very thing he thought disqualified him. Peter, after the worst failure
of his life, was told: when you have turned back, strengthen your brothers.
   I'm not going to promise you remarriage. That's between you and God and your
pastor and a woman who may or may not come. I'm going to promise you that this
season counts. That the Father who does not leave didn't leave in the courtroom.
That a man who walks through a divorce the second Man's way, covering his
children, owning his part, refusing the exits, comes out of it a man other men will
follow. And that your children, who are watching how a man handles the worst
thing, are learning something they'll need.

The empty nest

   The last one leaves for college or the Army or an apartment across town, and the
house goes quiet, and two people who've spent twenty years talking about children
look at each other across a kitchen and aren't sure what's left.
   The temptation is two strangers in a house. The drift, because the thing you
organized your life around is gone. And the affair, because a fifty-two-year-old man
who feels invisible at home and visible somewhere else is in real danger.
   The assignment is to court her again, and I don't mean it as a figure of speech.
Ask her out, and find out who she is now; chapter 22 at fifty-two. Make plans for
the years that are left that are hers as much as yours. Walt did this after he sold off
part of the business, and he said the empty nest turned out to be the best stretch of
their marriage, "because for the first time since 1994 it was just us and I actually
showed up for it." And the assignment is to turn outward, because the younger men
need you now, chapter 14, and the grandchildren are coming, and there are hills to
give.
  This season counts because it's the one where you find out whether you built a
marriage or a co-parenting arrangement, and if it was the second, this is the season
God gives you to build the first.




314                                                                Every Season Still Counts
The retired man

    Chapter 30 gave you Roy's whole rhythm, so I'll only say the shape here.
Retirement in Scripture is a shift from carrying to assisting, not from working to
sitting. The temptation is the drift with a fishing license and the anger that comes
through the television, and both are what happens to a man who was made for a
work and stopped.
   The assignment is Numbers 8, which is to assist: the younger men, the
grandsons, the church, the widow's gutters, your wife who has waited forty years
for you to be home. And the assignment is Caleb: ask for a hill. At sixty-eight, at
seventy-five, ask God for something hard, and expect Him to give it.
  Roy's season counts more than his badge years did, by his own accounting. "I
kept the peace for thirty-one years," he said. "I've been making men for five. I know
which one God's going to ask me about."

The widower

   Mr. Pruitt buried his wife of fifty-one years and planted forty pecan trees the
next spring. That's the assignment in one sentence, and it took him a year of grief to
get there, and the grief was part of the assignment too.
   The temptation is to decide that life ended when hers did. To let the couple
friends fade and not replace them. To drift into the recliner and let the television
keep you company. To let the loneliness gauge, which is telling the truth, convince
you that you're done.
   The assignment is to grieve as long as it takes and not let anyone rush you. To
find one brother, because God said not alone and He didn't add an exception for
men past seventy. To keep working whatever's left of the garden: trees,
grandchildren, a Wednesday coffee at a gas station. To stay in the house of the
Father. And to hold onto the promise God made specifically to old men.

  Isaiah 46:4 (NIV)
  Even to your old age and gray hairs I am he, I am he who will sustain you. I have made you, and I
  will carry you; I will sustain you and I will rescue you.




Every Season Still Counts                                                                             315
   I will carry you. It's the same word from Deuteronomy, a father carrying his son,
and God says it to the gray-haired. This season counts, because somebody's
grandson is going to eat those pecans.

The man in weakness

  Some of you are sick. Some of you are caring for someone who is. Mr. Alvarez
has bathed his wife every morning for three years. Others of you have the illness
yourselves, and your body won't do what it did, and you've concluded that a man
who can't work is a man who's done. He isn't done. Paul had a thorn he begged
God to remove, and God said no, and told him why.

  2 Corinthians 12:9 (NIV)
  But he said to me, 'My grace is sufficient for you, for my power is made perfect in weakness.' Therefore
  I will boast all the more gladly about my weaknesses, so that Christ's power may rest on me.



    Made perfect in weakness. Not in spite of it; in it. The temptation in this season
is uselessness, and it's a lie. The assignment is Numbers 8 again: assist. Pray, and
pray specifically, for people by name; a man in a chair with a list is a power in a
church. Call people, write to them, bless them. Mr. Alvarez calls a widower a day. A
homebound man I know has prayed for every young man in his church by name
every morning for eleven years, and those young men know it, and it has done
something to them. That's a work, and it counts.

The last season

  There's one more, and men don't like to talk about it, and Scripture talks about it
constantly.

  Psalm 90:12 (NIV)
  Teach us to number our days, that we may gain a heart of wisdom.



   Number our days, the psalm says, which means know that they're finite, and live
like a man who'll give a completion report. The assignment in the last season is to
finish well: to say what needs saying, to bless your children the way Jacob did from
his bed, to hand off the way David did to Solomon, to reconcile what can be


316                                                                                Every Season Still Counts
reconciled, to leave your plumb bob to someone with a story attached to it. Paul
wrote his last letter from a cell, knowing.

  2 Timothy 4:7 (NIV)
  I have fought the good fight, I have finished the race, I have kept the faith.



    Finished the race is the sentence, and notice that it isn't won but finished, and
that he kept the faith. Every man in this book is going to stand in this season, and
the ones who have lived the four marks in every other one will find that the last
season has a job too, and that they know how to do it, because they've been doing it
all along.

The marks don't change

   Here's what I want you to see, having walked the seasons. The four marks look
different at twenty-five and at seventy-five. Passivity in a young man is a tailgate; in
an old one it's a recliner. The weight a new father picks up is a two o'clock feeding;
the weight a widower picks up is a post-hole digger and forty bare-root trees. Going
first at thirty is an apology in a kitchen; at seventy it's walking across a church
parking lot to a young man who won't ask. And the greater reward is the same in
every season, because it was never in the season. It's on the other side of all of them.
   The marks don't change; the garden does. A man who has learned to reject
passivity, accept responsibility, lead courageously, and expect God's reward in one
season already knows how to do it in the next. He only has to find the new garden
and work it.
   There is no off-season, and February counts. The field that looks dead is doing
the work that July depends on. Whatever season you're standing in, it has an
assignment with your name on it, and the Father who set the seasons is watching
you work it.
   Find your season, and then work it like it counts, because it does.

Before you turn the page

  You've walked every season in this chapter, and I'd guess you found yourself in
one of them and maybe saw your father or your son in another. I'd like you to close



Every Season Still Counts                                                            317
it without the ranking, because the ranking was a lie, and it has cost men decades of
treating a real season as a waiting room. February counts. The field that looks dead
is doing the work July depends on, and the Father who set the seasons is watching
you work yours. May He give you the assignment of this season in one plain
sentence with a verb in it, and may you find, whether you're nineteen or
seventy-nine, that the garden you're standing in has your name on it.

  YOUR TURN



  NAME IT
  Because the ranking of seasons is a lie a man carries without knowing it: say which
  season you're standing in, or which two, and then say the temptation of that season
  out loud, the one you've been calling by a kinder name.
  Do this season. Because a season has a job and this chapter's line is a season's line: write
  the assignment of your season in one sentence with a verb in it, put it where you'll see it
  every Tuesday until the season changes, and do the first piece of it before next Tuesday.
  TELL A BROTHER
  Because a man in a different season can see yours from outside it: tell him your
  season and its assignment, ask him his, and ask what he sees that you can't from
  inside yours.
  CARRY THIS
  There is no off-season. February counts. The marks don't change; the garden does.
  Find yours and work it.




318                                                                       Every Season Still Counts
CHAPTER 33



What You Are Building When Nobody
Claps
   Part 5: The long road
   In July in Texas, foundation crews pour at four in the morning. The concrete has
to set before the heat gets to it, so the trucks roll in while it's still dark and the men
work under portable lights, and by the time the sun is up the slab is down and the
crew is gone.
  Nobody photographs a slab. Nobody drives past a finished house and says,
"Look at that foundation." The homeowners will live there thirty years and never
once think about what's under their feet. And every wall, every window, every room
where a child learns to walk and a couple fights and makes up and a family eats ten
thousand dinners, all of it sits on what those men did in the dark before anyone was
awake.
  That's what this chapter is about. Most of what a man builds, he builds when
nobody is watching, and the part nobody sees is the part that everything else stands
on.

Most of your life is unwitnessed

   Do the math on your own week: the two o'clock feeding, the Tuesday call from
the truck, the temptation you turned away from in a hotel room, the tithe check,
the coffee you've made for your wife every morning for eleven years, the apology in
the kitchen, the lie you didn't tell at work when the true answer cost you, the hour
with the Word before the phone, the twenty minutes with a brother, the widow's
gutters, the bedtime prayer, the plumb bob you handed a boy at a fence line with
four men watching and nobody else on earth.



What You Are Building When Nobody Claps                                               319
    Nobody clapped for any of it, and nobody is going to. Most of the men in your
life don't know it happened, and they never will.
   The culture has an answer to that, and the answer is poison. If nobody saw it, it
doesn't count. That's the whole logic of the scoreboard from chapter 16. It's why
men perform, why they tell the story so that someone will think well of them, why
they drift toward the place that claps. If being witnessed is what makes a thing real,
then the unwitnessed life is a wasted one, and a man had better get himself seen.
Scripture says the exact opposite, and says it in Jesus's own mouth, over and over, in
one sermon.

  Matthew 6:6 (NIV)
  But when you pray, go into your room, close the door and pray to your Father, who is unseen. Then
  your Father, who sees what is done in secret, will reward you.



    Who sees what is done in secret. The unwitnessed thing isn't the wasted thing.
It's the one with the highest return, because it's the only kind where you can be sure
you weren't doing it for the crowd. The applause you don't get here is the applause
that's stored somewhere the weather can't touch. That's the fourth mark in its
purest form: a man who expects the greater reward can do a lifetime of unwitnessed
good and not feel cheated, because he knows exactly who saw.

What you're actually building

  Let me be concrete, because "faithfulness in obscurity" is a phrase that floats.
Here's what a man is actually building with his unwitnessed Tuesdays.
   He's building a floor for his children. The thousand dinners nobody remembers
become, in a child, the settled sense that the world is stable and somebody's in
charge and they're safe. Your kids won't remember a single Tuesday. They'll
remember, in their bones, whether the Tuesdays were there. A grown man who can
handle a crisis without falling apart is usually a man whose father was at the table
ten thousand times without anyone counting.
   He's building a marriage that holds at seventy. Roy's marriage didn't survive
forty-four years because of anniversaries. It survived because of coffee, and it's
better now because of the coffee he started making after he retired. Ten thousand
unclapped mornings build something no vacation can. When a couple in their



320                                                          What You Are Building When Nobody Claps
seventies still likes each other, you're looking at a slab poured in the dark for fifty
years.
   He's building a name that arrives before he does. A reputation isn't built by the
things people saw. It's built by the things people found out later that you did when
they weren't looking.

  Proverbs 22:1 (NIV)
  A good name is more desirable than great riches; to be esteemed is better than silver or gold.



   A good name is what the proverb calls it, and chapter 27 said a man's last name is
the first weight his son carries. What you do when nobody claps is what makes that
name a thing to stand on. The feed store credit two counties over comes from forty
years of paying bills nobody knew about on time.
   He's building a church. Every church runs on men nobody thanks: the ones who
stack chairs, unlock the doors, fix the van, sit with the old man in the hospital, teach
the fourth-grade boys for twenty-two years. The pastor gets the platform. The men
who built the floor under the platform are in the parking lot afterward with their
hands in their pockets, and the church would fall over without them, and they
know it, and they're fine.
   He's building a town. The man at the wastewater plant. Four thousand people
drinking clean water because a man they've never heard of stayed awake. Most of
what holds a town together is men doing unwitnessed work. He's building a self,
which is the one men forget. Character is what gets built when nobody's watching,
and it can't be built any other way, and Jesus said so.

  Luke 16:10 (NIV)
  Whoever can be trusted with very little can also be trusted with much, and whoever is dishonest with
  very little will also be dishonest with much.



   Trusted with very little. The little is the unwitnessed. The man who tells the
truth when a lie would cost nothing and no one would know is building a man who
can tell the truth when it costs everything and everyone's watching. You don't get
the second man without the first. The hotel room at midnight is where the man
who'll one day stand in a boardroom gets made.



What You Are Building When Nobody Claps                                                                  321
    And he's building treasure in heaven, which was chapter 7. Every unclapped act
of faithfulness is stored, by a Father who doesn't lose track, in a place where it can't
be stolen or eaten by moths. That isn't a metaphor for feeling good about yourself.
It's a claim about where your work went. It went into an account, and it's still there.

The fire

  Paul had a picture for this that every man who has ever built anything will
understand.

  1 Corinthians 3:12-13 (NIV)
  If anyone builds on this foundation using gold, silver, costly stones, wood, hay or straw, their work
  will be shown for what it is, because the Day will bring it to light. It will be revealed with fire, and
  the fire will test the quality of each person's work.



   The fire will test the quality. Every man is building; the only question is what
with. And here's what I've come to believe about that passage after a good many
years. What's built for applause is straw. It looks fine, and it may look better than
the gold on the day, because straw is showy and gold is heavy. But when the fire
comes, and Paul says it's coming for every man, the work done for the crowd burns
off in a second, and what's left is what was done for the Father in the dark.
   Which means the slab poured at four in the morning is gold. The two o'clock
feeding is gold. The Tuesday call is gold. And the sermon preached for the sermon's
own sake, the gift given so the giver's name would go on the wall, the fatherhood
performed at the ballpark and abandoned in the truck: those are straw. The fire is
going to sort it out, and I'd rather be a man with a small pile of gold than a large pile
of straw.

The Tuesday after the marker

   Now the most practical thing in this chapter, and perhaps the most practical
thing in the second half of this book.
   You've had markers: a wedding, a baptism, a retreat where you finally said yes to
God with tears on your face, the day you gave your son at the fence line, a
retirement ceremony where they handed you a plaque and shook your hand and
said well done. Chapter 29 said it: the marker is the stone on the riverbank, and the


322                                                              What You Are Building When Nobody Claps
Tuesdays are the land. Every man who has ever had a marker has learned that the
Tuesday after is where it gets decided, and the Tuesday after is exactly the day when
nobody's clapping.
   So here's a Tuesday rhythm for the man after the marker. It's the unwitnessed
structure that holds the witnessed moments up. Roy's is the model, because he built
his after the hardest marker of his life, but I'll show you how it scales.
   The alarm doesn't move. Whatever time you got up when you had to, keep
getting up then. A man whose wake-up time is negotiable has a life that's negotiable.
Roy still rises at 5:30.
  The first hour is the plumb line. Before the phone and before the news: the
Word, a chapter, out loud if you can, and a prayer that starts with "Father."
Chapters 17 and 10. This is the hour that makes every other hour straight. If you
only have fifteen minutes, take fifteen, but take them first.
  The floor, which is the two or three family things that don't move no matter
what: dinner at the table, the bedtime prayer, Sunday. Chapter 5 gave you this one,
and you should write them down, because they're the corner posts.
  The twenty minutes with your brother, same time every week, three questions
and a prayer, from chapter 13. The man who drops this after the marker has gone
back to being alone, and alone is where markers go to die.
   One secret good thing a week, for someone, that no one will ever know about,
from chapter 7. It recalibrates you toward the Father who sees in secret and away
from the crowd that doesn't.
  A job by noon, something real that somebody is counting on, every day, from
chapter 30. For the retired man this is the difference between assisting and drifting.
For the working man it's already there; make sure it's done for the Lord and not
only for the boss.
   The blessing, from chapter 27, which is the weekly hand on the head and three
sentences if you have children at home. If they're grown, the call. If they're across
two houses, Tuesday at 7:30.
   The notebook from chapter 22: what she told you this week, a line a day.
   Sundown, which means nothing carried to bed: the apology made, the true thing
said, the anger handed to God, as chapter 9 and Ephesians 4:26 asked.



What You Are Building When Nobody Claps                                                 323
   And Sunday, in the house of the Father, with His other children, as a man with a
job there and not a visitor.
  That's ten things, and none of them is dramatic and none of them will get you
noticed, and together they're a life, and they're the life that makes the markers mean
something.
    Now watch it scale. Tomás, with a four-month-old and a ten-hour shift, can't do
Roy's version. His looks like this: the alarm is set by the baby, so he takes the first
fifteen minutes of the two-to-five shift for a Psalm on his phone in the dark while
she sleeps. The floor is the twenty minutes after his boots come off and the bedtime
prayer over the crib. The twenty minutes with his brother happens on the phone at
nine while he does the dishes. The secret good thing is letting his wife sleep an extra
hour Saturday and not mentioning it. The blessing is "you're my daughter and I
love you" said over a baby who doesn't understand words yet. The notebook is a
note on his phone. Sundown is "I'm so tired I'm scared of myself, and I'm sorry, and
I'm not going anywhere." Sunday is church, exhausted, in the back row, with the
baby. That's a whole rhythm. It fits in the cracks of a life that has no room, and it's
gold.
   Kenny's looks different again: the first hour before the kids wake on his
weekends and before work on the others; the floor is the Tuesday call and the
every-other-weekend that never gets traded away; the job by noon is showing up at a
business he owns whether he feels like it or not; the secret good thing is often
something for his children's mother that she'll never know he did. Micah's, when he
was single, had more room in it and more danger, and his floor was the roommates'
dinner and the twenty minutes and the phone in the kitchen at night.
    Build yours, with ten things or seven or five, whatever fits, and write it on a card.
It's the slab, and everything visible sits on it.

When you're weary

   I have to say something about the long middle, because this chapter can sound
easy, and it isn't.
   There's a stretch in every unwitnessed work when nothing grows. You pour the
slab and there's no house yet, only a flat gray thing in the dirt, for months. You
make the coffee for a year and she's still distant. You call your son every Tuesday for
eight months and he says "nothing." You teach the fourth-grade boys for a decade


324                                                 What You Are Building When Nobody Claps
and can't name one thing that's changed. And the thought comes, in the language
of Isaiah: I have labored for nothing.

  Isaiah 49:4 (NIV)
  But I said, 'I have labored in vain; I have spent my strength for nothing at all. Yet what is due me is
  in the LORD's hand, and my reward is with my God.'



   Yet what is due me is in the LORD's hand, and that's the turn. The servant in
Isaiah felt exactly what you've felt, that nothing was happening and he'd wasted his
strength, and he didn't argue himself out of the feeling. He moved the accounting
somewhere else. My reward is with my God. Not with the results, and not with the
son who said "nothing." With God, who saw every one of the thirty-four Tuesdays
before the one when the boy talked for forty minutes.

  Galatians 6:9 (NIV)
  Let us not become weary in doing good, for at the proper time we will reap a harvest if we do not give
  up.



   At the proper time. Not your time; the pecan tree's time. Mr. Pruitt's trees are
knee-high and he won't see them bear, and he isn't weary, because he never believed
the reward was the pecans. Don't give up, because the harvest is coming on a
schedule you don't control, and some of it is coming after you're gone, and the
Father is keeping the books.

The barn nobody remembers

  Roy's grandson carries a plumb bob like the one his great-grandfather used to
build a barn. The barn is still standing. Nobody in the county remembers the man's
name, and there's no plaque. The family that owns the land now didn't know who
built it until Roy told them.
   That's what legacy is: not being remembered, but something standing. The older
men reading this need to hear that, because the reward of being remembered comes
for good men late, and it's a small reward dressed up as a large one. The town will
forget you. The church will get a new pastor and the young families won't know
what you built. Your grandchildren will move to Dallas. If you were building for
the remembering, you'll be disappointed. If you were building for the standing,


What You Are Building When Nobody Claps                                                                     325
you'll have a barn.
   Roy told me the hardest thing about the years after the badge was realizing that
thirty-one years of work had already been forgotten by the people he'd done it for.
Then he said, "And then I realized that was fine. I didn't do it for them. I thought I
did. I didn't. I did it because it was mine to do and God was watching." That's a
man who found the fourth mark at seventy. His Thursday table won't be
remembered either. The two men at it will stand.

Building unseen at every age

  Men pour slabs in the dark at every age, and the temptation to resent the dark
changes shape as they go.
   A young man of twenty is building a self, mostly, and he's tempted to think
nothing counts until somebody sees it. Eli's unwitnessed work this year was telling
the truth to a customer when a lie would have cost nothing, and putting the phone
in the kitchen when nobody would have known, and the takeaway for him is Luke
16:10: the man who'll one day stand in a hard room is being built in the ones
nobody sees.
  A young father is building a floor for a child who won't remember a single
Tuesday of it, and the takeaway for him is that his kids will remember, in their
bones, whether the Tuesdays were there.
   A man of fifty is building a marriage that will hold at seventy, or isn't, and he's
tempted toward the podium because work claps and home doesn't. The takeaway
for him is the fire in 1 Corinthians 3: what's built for applause is straw, and what's
built for the Father in the dark is gold.
   An older man is building something that will stand after his name is forgotten,
which is the barn and the plumb bob, and the takeaway for him is that legacy is not
being remembered but something standing, and that Roy's Thursday table won't be
remembered either while the two men at it will stand.

Thirty years in a shop

   Jesus spent thirty years unwitnessed, and Luke gives the whole stretch one
sentence.




326                                                What You Are Building When Nobody Claps
  Luke 2:51 (NIV)
  Then he went down to Nazareth with them and was obedient to them. But his mother treasured all
  these things in her heart.



   He went down to Nazareth and was obedient to them. Thirty years of that. The
Son of God, planing boards in a town nobody thought anything of, obedient to a
carpenter and his wife, and no one saw, and no one clapped, and it was the
foundation everything else stood on. When He walked into the Jordan at thirty, the
Father spoke out loud in front of witnesses: this is my Son, whom I love. One
sentence of applause, and then three years of being misunderstood, doubted, and
finally killed, and He never went looking for the crowd's version. The Father had
seen the thirty years. That was enough.
  It's enough for you too. Nobody is going to clap for your slab. The Father
watched you pour it in the dark, and He isn't going to forget.

  Hebrews 6:10 (NIV)
  God is not unjust; he will not forget your work and the love you have shown him as you have helped
  his people and continue to help them.



  He will not forget your work, so build. Pour the slab and make the coffee and
make the call. Nobody's watching except the only One who counts, and He's
keeping every Tuesday.

Before you turn the page

   If you've been building in the dark for a long time and the silence has started to
wear on you, I'd like you to hear the servant's sentence in Isaiah one more time: yet
what is due me is in the LORD's hand, and my reward is with my God. Not with
the results. Not with the son who says "nothing." With God, who saw every one of
the thirty-four Tuesdays before the one when the boy talked. He will not forget
your work. May the Father who watched His Son plane boards in Nazareth for
thirty years watch you pour your slab this week, and may you run your Tuesday
rhythm for seven days without telling anyone and find that He kept every one of
them.




What You Are Building When Nobody Claps                                                                327
328   What You Are Building When Nobody Claps
CHAPTER 34



Write the Next Move
   Part 5: The long road
   Hollis carries a notebook in his shirt pocket. It's about the size of a deck of cards,
spiral-bound at the top, and he's carried one like it since he was nineteen. Every
night before bed he writes down tomorrow's first three jobs, and that's all: not a
to-do list, three things, in order, that he's going to do before anything else.
  I asked him once why he still does it at seventy-four, when he has nowhere to be
and nobody to answer to.
   "Because if it isn't written down, it isn't going to happen," he said. "I learned that
at nineteen and it hasn't stopped being true. A man who knows what he's going to
do tomorrow gets up and does it. A man who's going to figure it out in the morning
figures out that he'd rather have another cup of coffee."
    He flipped the notebook open and showed me: fix gate latch, call Cole, read
Proverbs 14. Then he closed it and put it back in his pocket, and I thought: that's
fifty-five years of the next thing, written down the night before, and it's why he's
Hollis.
    You've read thirty-three chapters. If you close this book with a feeling and
nothing written down, you'll have a marker with no Tuesday after it, and by
Christmas you won't be able to tell anyone what the book said. So before the last
chapter, I want you to write the next move, and I mean the next move rather than a
life plan.

Make it plain

   God tells men to write things down. He told Habakkuk to, in the middle of a
vision that was too big to carry in his head.



Write the Next Move                                                                    329
  Habakkuk 2:2 (NIV)
  Then the LORD replied: 'Write down the revelation and make it plain on tablets so that a herald
  may run with it.'



    Write it down, make it plain, and make it so that someone can run with it: that's
a plan in one sentence. Not a feeling in your chest that you'll act on when the time
is right, but a thing written on something, plain enough that a man could pick it up
and run. Men fail at plans in two directions. Some write nothing, and they leave the
retreat or the conference or the book or the sermon with tears in their eyes and
resolve in their gut, and they go home, and the resolve lasts until the first hard
Tuesday, because nothing was ever written and so nothing was ever real. Others
write too much. A forty-page life vision with values and mission statements and
ten-year goals, and it goes in a drawer and dies of its own weight, because no man
can run with forty pages.
   The Bible's version is a tablet. One surface, plain words. Nehemiah, who rebuilt a
wall in fifty-two days, did it because he'd prayed for months and then walked the
wall at night and looked at exactly what was broken and then asked the king for
exactly what he needed: timber, letters, safe passage, all of it specific and written.
Then he organized every family by its section of the wall and went to work, and
when men tried to pull him off it, he had a sentence ready.

  Nehemiah 6:3 (NIV)
  so I sent messengers to them with this reply: 'I am carrying on a great project and cannot go down.
  Why should the work stop while I leave it and go down to you?'



  I am carrying on a great project. A man who has written his plan knows what the
project is, and knows what's a distraction from it, and can say so.

One page

   Here's what I want you to write. One page, not a notebook; a page, or an index
card if you write small. It pulls the whole book onto a single surface you can run
with, and every line of it is something you've already been asked to name in a closer
somewhere in these thirty-three chapters. You've done the thinking, and now you
make it plain.



330                                                                                    Write the Next Move
   Start with the mark you're faintest on, from chapter 3, in one line. "Of the four,
the one I'm missing most is _." Rejecting passivity, accepting responsibility, leading
courageously, or expecting the greater reward. Pick one, because you can't fix four
at once.
   Then your exit, from chapter 9, in one word: anger, the itch, or the drift. Written
down where you'll see it, so that when you reach for it you'll know what you're
reaching for.
   Then your season and its assignment, from chapter 32, in one sentence with a
verb in it. "I'm a new father under a baby, and my assignment is to protect the
marriage inside the chaos." "I'm retired, and my assignment is to assist: two younger
men and a grandson." "I'm single again in year one, and my assignment is to drive
straight and slow and call my kids every Tuesday."
  Then your brother, from chapter 13, as a name and a day and a time: "Dale,
Thursdays at noon, by phone." If you don't have a name yet, the line reads, "Ask _
by Tuesday." That's the move.
  Then your man ahead, from chapter 14, as a name and the first question: "Hollis,
how did you handle it when your kids stopped talking to you?" If you're the older
man, this line flips: the younger man's name and what you'll offer him.
  Then your Tuesday rhythm, from chapter 33: five to ten unwitnessed things that
don't move, scaled to your season: the alarm, the first hour, the floor, the twenty
minutes, the secret good thing, the job by noon, the blessing, the notebook,
sundown, Sunday. This is the longest part of the page, and it's still short.
   Then your house's next move, from chapters 21 through 29. One thing for her,
or for the wife who isn't here yet, or for the woman you're building a covenant
toward in your singleness. One thing for each child, or for a fatherless kid if you
have none. And a date, if you have a son or daughter approaching the crossing, for
the day.
  Then your work's next move, from chapter 30, in one line. "Do the plant with all
my heart for the Lord." "Tell my wife the truth about my hours." "Name my job for
tomorrow before bed." "Take the bridge job."
   Then the drawer you're opening, from chapter 16, in one line with a name
attached. What's hidden, and which Nathan you're telling. This is the line men skip,
and you mustn't.



Write the Next Move                                                                 331
