   And then the next move. One thing, before next Tuesday. The single first action
from everything above that you're going to do this week. Not the most important
thing; the first. "Ask Dale." "Put the phone in the kitchen tonight." "Say the
eleven-second prayer at dinner." "Call my father and pick a date."
  At the bottom, sign it and date it. Chapter 11 said men need markers, and this is
one. The day you wrote the next move is a day you can point to.

What a move is

  I keep saying "move" rather than "goal," and the difference matters. A goal is a
destination. "Be a better husband." "Get closer to God." "Fix my relationship with
my dad." Goals are fine and they're useless, because you can't do a goal. You can
only do a move.
    A move has three parts: a verb, a person, and a day. "Ask my wife what she most
needs from me, Thursday, after the kids are down." "Call my father Sunday and tell
him one thing I'm grateful for." "Pray out loud with my roommate tonight." "Text
Dale before noon tomorrow and ask about twenty minutes a week." Every one of
those you either did or you didn't, and there's no arguing with it. That's what makes
it a move.
   Hollis's notebook is all moves: fix gate latch, call Cole, read Proverbs 14, each one
a verb and an object with the day always tomorrow. He has never written "be a
better man" in fifty-five years. He has written something like twenty thousand small
moves, and the better man is what they added up to. Solomon knew the difference
too.

  Proverbs 21:5 (NIV)
  The plans of the diligent lead to profit as surely as haste leads to poverty.



  The plans of the diligent, and not the dreams of the inspired. Plans, made by men
who show up. Diligence is a Tuesday word.

Why plans die

  I've watched a lot of men write a page like this, and I've watched what kills it.
Here's the list, so that you can build against it.



332                                                                               Write the Next Move
  Plans die when they're too big. He wrote forty things and did none. Write ten, or
write five. The page should be something you can read in ninety seconds in a truck.
    Plans die when nobody sees them. A plan that lives in a drawer is a wish.
Habakkuk's was written so that a herald could run with it. Yours needs to be read
by one other man. Show it to your brother and give him the right to ask you about
it.
  Plans die when there's no date. "Soon" is not a day. "This week" is barely a day.
Tuesday is a day. Put dates on the moves.
   Plans die when they were made in a feeling. The retreat high, the last chapter of a
book. Chapter 31: the gauges were pegged when you wrote it and you steered by
them. That's all right, as long as you review the page on a plain Tuesday when the
feeling has gone and see what still holds. Most of it will. Some of it was the feeling.
Cross that part out.
  Plans die when there's no floor, when everything was aspiration and nothing was
non-negotiable. The Tuesday rhythm is the floor. Without it the rest floats.
   Plans die when nobody reviews them. A page written once and never read again
was written for the feeling of writing it. Every ninety days, sit down with the page
and your brother and ask what got done, what's changed, and what the next move is
now. Then write a fresh page. Hollis writes a fresh three every night. You can
manage a fresh page every season. And plans die when God isn't in them, which is
the one that matters most and the one men miss most. James had a word for men
who plan without Him.

  James 4:13-15 (NIV)
  Now listen, you who say, 'Today or tomorrow we will go to this or that city, spend a year there, carry
  on business and make money.' Why, you do not even know what will happen tomorrow. What is your
  life? You are a mist that appears for a little while and then vanishes. Instead, you ought to say, 'If it is
  the Lord's will, we will live and do this or that.'



    If it is the Lord's will. That isn't a disclaimer to tack onto the bottom of the page.
It's the posture of the whole page. You're writing moves, and you're handing the
page to God, and He's allowed to change it. Nehemiah planned everything and
prayed through all of it and still watched God rearrange his days. A plan held loosely
in an open hand is a plan God can use. A plan gripped in a fist is an idol with bullet
points.


Write the Next Move                                                                                          333
  Proverbs 16:9 (NIV)
  In their hearts humans plan their course, but the LORD establishes their steps.



  Humans plan, and the LORD establishes. Both halves are true. You plan, on
paper, in plain words. He establishes the steps, which means He decides which of
your moves actually land and when. Write the page, and then pray over it and let
Him hold it.

The page in every season

   If you're single, your page leans on chapter 21's list: the house, the table, the
company, the devotion, the fatherhood, the purity. Pick one from each for the
season. And if you want a wife, one move: a specific ask of a specific woman on a
specific day, or a specific question to a specific older man about how to find one.
   If there's a baby in your house, your page is three lines, and that isn't failure; it's
wisdom. The counter time with her, the shift you take, the brother on the phone at
nine, and everything else waits until you've slept. A young father who writes a
ten-point plan in the first year is a young father who'll feel like a failure by February.
Three lines, done every week, is the whole plan for this season.
   If you're single again, your page for year one has a rule at the top: no turns. Then
the floor: the Tuesday call to the kids, the every-other-weekend that never gets
traded, the brother on the phone, church even when it's awkward. Then the
drawer: your part, named, to your brother. That's all for year one. Year two's page
can have the rebuild on it.
   If you're the older man, your page is the rhythm from chapter 30 and then three
lines a younger man's page can't have: the hill you're asking God for, like Caleb; the
younger man you're walking across the parking lot toward; and the conversation
you've been putting off with a grown child, or the blessing you've never written,
with a date. Roy's page had "Say it to my son on the porch" on it for two months
before he did it. Then he did it, because it was written and it had a date and Hollis
asked him about it every Thursday.
   And if you're a widower, your page may be the shortest of all, and the most
important: a brother's name, a job for each morning, and one thing for the garden
that's left. Mr. Pruitt's first page after his wife died said, in its entirety, "Coffee with


334                                                                                 Write the Next Move
Ed Wednesdays. Trees."
  Two more men belong in this section, because they're the ones a chapter like this
most often leaves out.
   A young man of twenty writes a page that leans on the house he's building alone:
one line each for the apartment, the table, the brother, the fatherless kid, and the
phone in the kitchen, and one move toward a woman if there's a woman. Eli's first
page had five lines and a date, and he told me the date was the first time his life had
ever felt like it belonged to him.
   A man of fifty writes the drawer line first, because it's the one his age skips, and
then the brother line, because he has three hundred contacts and no name to put
there. Walt's first page had "Ask Dale about Thursdays" at the bottom, and it sat
there for three weeks before he did it.

As for me and my household

   Joshua, at the end of his life, gathered the whole nation and made them choose,
and then told them what he'd chosen, in a sentence men have been putting over
their doors for three thousand years.

  Joshua 24:15 (NIV)
  But if serving the LORD seems undesirable to you, then choose for yourselves this day whom you will
  serve, whether the gods your ancestors served beyond the Euphrates, or the gods of the Amorites, in
  whose land you are living. But as for me and my household, we will serve the LORD.



   Choose for yourselves this day, he says, and not this season or when you're ready,
but this day. And then: as for me and my household. That's a man writing his next
move in front of witnesses. He isn't describing a feeling. He's declaring a direction,
for himself and for everyone under his roof, and he's doing it out loud where men
can hear it and hold him to it.
  Your page is your version of that sentence. It doesn't need to be eloquent. It
needs to be plain, dated, signed, shown to a brother, and held loosely in an open
hand before God. Then it needs to be run with.

Tomorrow's three




Write the Next Move                                                                                     335
   Hollis put the notebook back in his pocket, and we sat on his porch a while
longer, and I asked him what the hardest move he'd ever written down was.
   He thought about it. "Probably 'Tell Cole he's a good man.' That was on the
page for about three weeks before I did it. Kept moving it to the next day. Finally I
thought, if it isn't written down it isn't going to happen, and it was written down,
so I'd better." He shrugged. "Took thirty seconds. He sat in his truck and cried for
twenty minutes. I'd have put it off another year if I hadn't had to look at it every
night."
  That's the whole method: write the move, look at it every night, do it because
you wrote it, and let God establish the step.
   You have one page to write and one move to make before Tuesday. Go make it
plain.

Before you turn the page

   I've given you a page to write and I know that writing it will feel, for some of you,
like the most exposed thing in this book, because a written move can be checked.
That's the point of it, and it's also a mercy, because a man who writes his move
down and hands it to a brother has stopped carrying his intentions alone. Hold the
page loosely, in an open hand, and let God rearrange it. May the Father who
establishes a man's steps establish yours this week, and may you do the move at the
bottom before Tuesday and discover, as Hollis has for fifty-five years, that the next
thing was always enough.

338   Write the Next Move
CHAPTER 35



These Men Are My Brothers
   Part 5: The long road
   It's a Tuesday as I write this. There's nothing special about it: a gray sky, a little
wind out of the north, the kind of day nobody remembers. I've been thinking about
the men in this book and where they are today, on this particular unremarkable
Tuesday, and I want to tell you before I let you go.
   Not because they're impressive. None of them is famous, and none of them will
be. If you passed any of them at a gas station you wouldn't look twice. I want to tell
you because they're the argument of the whole book, and the argument is this: the
four marks are not for exceptional men. They're for men, ordinary ones, in every
season, on Tuesdays, and I've watched them hold.

Where they are

   Roy is seventy-two now. He got up at 5:30 this morning and made the coffee
and sat on the porch with his wife while she drank it, and she told him about a
dream she'd had, and he listened to the whole thing and asked a question at the end.
Then he read Proverbs 12 out loud. Then he drove to the church to look at a leak in
the fellowship hall roof that nobody had asked him to look at. On Thursday he'll sit
at a table with two men in their thirties and ask them what's heavy, and they'll tell
him. His grandson has a plumb bob in his pocket at school right now. His daughter
calls on Sundays. He turned in a badge five years ago and thought his work was
over, and he'll tell you, if you ask, that the last five years have been the most
important of his life, and that nobody has clapped once.
  Tomás's daughter is two. He walked her around the block at six this evening the
way he has since she was three months old, and she talked the whole way. His wife
got five hours of sleep in a row last night because he took the first shift, and she's a


These Men Are My Brothers                                                             339
person again, and the bed came back months ago, better than before, because she
learned in the worst year that she could trust him with her body when it was
weakest. He's tired, and he works ten hours on a pipeline crew, and he's
twenty-nine, and most days he doesn't feel like a man. He's more of one than he
knows. His brother calls at nine while he does the dishes.
    Kenny's children are with their mother this week. He called his son at 7:30
tonight, as he does every Tuesday, and the boy talked for twenty minutes about a
smoker they built over eleven weekends and a girl he isn't sure about. Before they
hung up Kenny said, "You're my son. I love you. I'll call you Tuesday." His
daughter texted him a picture of a school project. Three years ago he was a mad,
lonely ghost in a one-bedroom apartment who had stopped praying because he
figured God was as done with him as everybody else seemed to be. An older man
called him every Tuesday night for a year and asked three questions and wouldn't
let him hang up until he'd answered. Now the divorced men at his church talk to
Kenny, because he's the only one they'll let in. He didn't choose that. He's serving
God's purpose in his generation with the thing he thought disqualified him.
   Micah is married now, and he and his wife had a younger couple over for dinner
tonight, and he cooked. He still meets Dale every other Tuesday for breakfast, and
he's started meeting a twenty-four-year-old on Thursdays who reminds him of
himself at the back table. He wrote a date inside his Bible a few years ago, the day a
man told him he was a man and to stop waiting for permission. He looks at it
sometimes.
   Walt sold off part of the business. He was home for dinner tonight, as he is four
nights out of five, and he and his wife are planning a trip for the fall that she picked.
He has a brother now; they talk Thursdays at noon on the drive to lunch. He told
me the empty nest turned out to be the best stretch of his marriage, because for the
first time since 1994 it was just the two of them and he actually showed up for it.
He still thinks about the bank parking lot in Abilene. He calls it the day he found
out he had three hundred contacts and nobody.
    Anthony walked into his kitchen tonight. He didn't stand in the doorway. His
daughter looked up from her homework to see what his face would do when her
mother came in from the car, and it did the right thing, and the girl smiled and went
back to her fractions. He lost a fight he was winning last week. He's been working
on that. He says the word "Father" in the truck every morning, and it still feels a
little foolish, and he does it anyway.


340                                                               These Men Are My Brothers
   Cole names his son every night: three sentences, hand on the boy's head. He
drove out to his father's place on Sunday and they looked at a pump together and
didn't say much, which is the closest thing to an embrace his father has, and Cole
took it, because he stopped needing more than the man could give. A
seventy-four-year-old deacon told him he was a good man by a truck four years ago,
and he sat in the cab and cried for twenty minutes. He's thirty-seven, and he isn't
waiting anymore.
   Hollis, who is seventy-four, wrote tomorrow's three in a notebook tonight before
bed. He has never had a calling, and he has served God's purpose in his generation
for fifty-five years by doing the next thing God put in front of him. One of
tomorrow's three is "Call Roy re: roof."
   Mr. Pruitt's pecan trees are a little past knee-high. He drank coffee with Ed at the
gas station this morning as he has every Wednesday for four years, and they talked
about the price of cattle and Ed's grandson and nothing in particular, and it was the
most important twenty minutes of Mr. Pruitt's week. He's seventy-six, and he won't
eat one of those pecans. He's the most settled man in the county.
  Mr. Alvarez bathed his wife this morning, and she didn't know his name, and he
knew hers. Then he called a widower from church and asked him what was heavy.
He's seventy-eight, and he said "in sickness" in 1968 and he meant the whole thing.
  The man at the wastewater plant is on shift right now, as I write, running his
checks and praying over four thousand sleeping people who don't know he exists.
   And the young man with four jobs in three years stayed at the fifth. He runs a
crew now. Two of the men on it started coming to church because of how he treats
them. He still doesn't have a calling. He has a purpose, and it turned out to be
wherever he stopped running.

What they have in common

   Look at that list. A retired trooper, a pipeliner, an HVAC man, a help desk tech,
a business owner, a man who used to stand in doorways, a mechanic, a farmer, a
widower, a caregiver, a plant operator, a crew chief. Twenty-four to seventy-eight.
Single, married, divorced, widowed, newborn in the house, empty nest, retired, sick.
Every season in the book.
   Not one of them is remarkable, and that's the point. I didn't choose them



These Men Are My Brothers                                                           341
because they were the best men I know. I chose them because they're the men I
know, and because every one of them, in his own season, on his own Tuesdays, is
doing the four things.
   Every one of them got off the tailgate. Roy went and found the work. Tomás
turned toward his wife inside the chaos instead of waiting for it to calm down.
Kenny made the call before the anger cooled. Every one of them rejected passivity.
   Every one of them picked up weight that was theirs, and some that wasn't. Walt
sold trucks to be home. Cole reached toward a father who never reached back. Mr.
Alvarez picks up a washcloth every morning. Every one of them accepted
responsibility.
   Every one of them went first. Anthony into the apology. Micah into the sentence
at a young man's table. Hollis across a parking lot to a man by a truck. Roy into his
daughter's driveway. Every one of them led courageously.
   And every one of them is doing it for a Father who sees in secret. Nobody's
clapping for any of them. They know exactly who's watching. They expect the
greater reward, God's reward, and it's the only reason the other three have held.

  Psalm 112:7 (NIV)
  They will have no fear of bad news; their hearts are steadfast, trusting in the LORD.



   Steadfast is the word this book is named for, and that's what it looks like. Not a
man who never fails, but a man whose heart is fixed to something that doesn't
move, so that when the bad news comes, and it comes for every one of these men,
he doesn't come apart. He reads the gauge, he says the true thing to God and a
brother, and he puts his hands back on the wheel.

These men are my brothers

  I've called them brothers throughout, and I want to say what I mean by it at the
end, because it's the last thing I want to leave with you.
   I don't mean they're my friends, though they are. I mean that we're in something
together. We've said the true thing to each other at tables and in trucks and on
phones at nine at night over dishes. We've asked each other what's heavy and waited
for the answer. We've told each other "you are the man" when we needed to hear it



342                                                                            These Men Are My Brothers
and "that's what a man does" when we needed to hear that. We've stood at fence
lines before dawn and put our hands on each other's sons. We've buried each other's
wives and held each other's babies and called each other every Tuesday when there
was nothing to say. Solomon said it in one line.

  Proverbs 17:17 (NIV)
  A friend loves at all times, and a brother is born for a time of adversity.



   A brother is born for a time of adversity. He's the man who's there when it goes
bad. Not the hunting buddy, and not the three hundred contacts. The one who
picks up. Every man in this chapter has one now. Most of them didn't when I met
them, and the difference between the man they were and the man they are is mostly
that.
   And here is what I want you to understand about the company they're in: it isn't
closed, and it isn't a club for men who've got it together. Every one of them walked
in as a man who didn't: Walt from a parking lot, Kenny from a ghost's apartment,
Cole from thirty-three years of waiting, Roy from a recliner, Anthony from a
doorway. They walked in because somebody asked them to, or because they finally
asked somebody. The door was open, and it still is.

  Hebrews 10:24 (NIV)
  And let us consider how we may spur one another on toward love and good deeds,



   Spur one another on. That's a company of men. Not a crowd; a company. Two
or three, a Thursday table. Men who know each other's exits and ask about them.
Men who go first for each other. Men who are building something nobody claps for
and who know that the men beside them see it even when no one else does.
   These men are my brothers. And if you've read this far, if you've named the
things and done the things and told a brother and carried the lines, then whether we
ever meet or not, you're one of them too. That isn't a sentiment. It's what the four
marks make. A man who lives them isn't alone in it. He's in a company that
stretches back to a garden and forward to a table that hasn't been set yet, and every
man in it is ordinary, and every man in it is steadfast, and every man in it got there
on Tuesdays.



These Men Are My Brothers                                                          343
The last time

  I've said the sentence a great many times in this book. I'm going to say it once
more and then get out of your way.
  A real man rejects passivity, accepts responsibility, leads courageously, and
expects the greater reward, God's reward.
   Four marks, not four moods. Four things you can see on a man the way you
could see the fence stretcher on my grandfather's palm. They aren't a personality,
and they aren't a feeling you find in a deer blind. They're the portrait of a Man in a
garden on the worst night of His life, who got up off the ground and went out to
meet what was coming and covered His men and finished the work, for the joy set
before Him. Every time you live one of them on a Tuesday, you're wearing His face.
    You're going to fail at them. So did every man in this chapter, this week. That
isn't the question. The question is whether you're in the first man or the second,
and if you're in the second, whether you'll get up tomorrow and turn back toward
the Father who does not leave and go stand in your garden again. That's the whole
life: you get up, you turn, you stand, and you repeat it until He says "well done."

  1 Corinthians 15:58 (NIV)
  Therefore, my dear brothers and sisters, stand firm. Let nothing move you. Always give yourselves
  fully to the work of the Lord, because you know that your labor in the Lord is not in vain.



   Let nothing move you. Your labor is not in vain. Not the slab, not the coffee, not
the Tuesday call, not the pecans, none of it. He saw all of it, and He's keeping the
books, and the reward is with Him, and it lasts.

A quiet Tuesday

   This book started on a quiet Tuesday, and it ends on one, because that's where a
man's life is actually decided. Not at the altar, not at the fence line, not at the
retirement ceremony, not in the last chapter of a book. On a gray day with a little
wind out of the north, when nobody's watching and nothing's at stake except
everything.
  Tomorrow is a Tuesday somewhere. There's a smoke detector chirping in a



344                                                                           These Men Are My Brothers
hallway near you. There's a woman who needs you to know her, a child who needs a
name and a hill, a father who needs a call, a brother who needs to be asked, a job
that needs doing with all your heart, a drawer that needs opening, a Father who's
watching the road for you. You know what a man does. You've known since
chapter 3. There's nothing left to learn from me. There's only the getting up. So I'll
leave you the way the old priests left the people, with the words God gave them to
say over men who were about to walk back out into ordinary days.

  Numbers 6:24-26 (NIV)
  The LORD bless you and keep you; the LORD make his face shine on you and be gracious to you; the
  LORD turn his face toward you and give you peace.



   The LORD bless you and keep you. Keep: the second verb of Genesis 2:15, the
one you were told to do for the garden, said back over you by the One who does it
for you. So go work it, and go keep it. Reject passivity, accept responsibility, lead
courageously, and expect the greater reward.
   These men are my brothers, and there's a chair open at the table. Come be one.

Before you close the book

   I've told you where the men in this book are on this particular Tuesday, and I've
told you the four marks one last time, and I've said the blessing the old priests said.
There's one more thing I'd like to say to you, and I'd like to say it the way I'd say it
across a table.
   You're going to fail at this. So am I. The men in this chapter failed at it this week,
and next Tuesday they'll get up and turn back toward the Father and stand in their
gardens again, and that's the whole life. If you've read this far you have a page
written and a brother asked and a mark you're working on, and that's more than
most men do with most books, and the Father who sees in secret has counted every
line of it. May He keep you, the way He asked Adam to keep the garden, and may
He make His face shine on you on the ordinary days, and may you find one day, at a
table that hasn't been set yet, that these men were your brothers all along.
